=== disc-01 | Propositions and truth tables
--- teach
You have finished the Python courses and ORBIT's algebra modules. You can write functions, loops and comprehensions, and you can solve equations. This course is the mathematics underneath computer science: logic, proof, counting, number theory and graphs. Every later course leans on it. Algorithms are proved correct with it, running times are counted with it, and cryptography is built from it.

Python plays two parts in this course. It is your **calculator**: it builds tables, counts, and searches thousands of cases in a second. It is also your **proof assistant**: it checks each step of an argument and hunts for cases where a claim breaks. But checking a million cases is not a proof about infinitely many. You will learn both sides, and exactly where the line between them is.

The first topic is logic: the rules for what follows from what.

### Sentences that are true or false

A **proposition** is a sentence that is either true or false, and not both. Some propositions:

- "The hatch is closed." It depends on the hatch, but it is definitely one or the other.
- "7 is a prime number." True.
- "2 + 2 = 5." False. A false sentence is still a proposition.

Some sentences that are **not** propositions:

- "Is the hatch closed?" A question is not true or false.
- "Close the hatch." Neither is a command.
- "x + 1 = 3." Whether it is true depends on x, and x has not been given a value. Sentences like this are the subject of lesson 3.
- "[[This sentence is false|liar]]." If it were true it would be false, and if it were false it would be true.

Every proposition has a **truth value**: true, written T, or false, written F. In Python these are `True` and `False`, the two values of the [[type bool|boole]].

In logic you name propositions with letters such as p, q and r, the way algebra names numbers with x and y. These letters are **propositional variables**: each one stands for some proposition, so it can be either T or F.

### Not

Logic builds new propositions from old ones with **connectives**, the way arithmetic builds numbers with + and ×.

The simplest connective is **negation**. The negation of p is written ¬p (the symbol is read "not") and it is true exactly when p is false.

A **truth table** lists every possible combination of truth values for the variables, one combination per row, and gives the value of the formula in each row:

| p | ¬p |
|---|---|
| F | T |
| T | F |

In Python, negation is `not p`.

### And, or

The **conjunction** p ∧ q (read "p and q") is true when both p and q are true. The **disjunction** p ∨ q (read "p or q") is true when at least one of them is true, and that includes when both are.

| p | q | p ∧ q | p ∨ q |
|---|---|---|---|
| F | F | F | F |
| F | T | F | T |
| T | F | F | T |
| T | T | T | T |

This "or" is the **inclusive or**. "You may bring a laptop or a tablet" allows both. In Python the two connectives are `and` and `or`.

### Exclusive or

Sometimes "or" means "one or the other, but not both", as in "soup or salad comes with the meal". That connective is the **exclusive or**, written p ⊕ q and often called XOR. It is true when exactly one of p and q is true, which is the same as saying p and q have different values. For two booleans in Python, that is `p != q`.

### If, then

The **conditional** p → q (read "if p, then q", or "p implies q") is the most important connective in this course. Every theorem you will prove has this shape. p is the **hypothesis** and q is the **conclusion**.

Think of it as a promise: "If the cabin pressure drops below 90 kPa, the alarm sounds." When has the promise been broken? Only when the pressure drops and the alarm stays silent. If the pressure never drops, the promise has not been broken, whatever the alarm does. So p → q is false in exactly one row, where p is true and q is false:

| p | q | p → q |
|---|---|---|
| F | F | T |
| F | T | T |
| T | F | F |
| T | T | T |

When p is false, p → q is true no matter what q is. It is then called **vacuously true**: true because the hypothesis never happened. The [[two top rows|vacuous]] surprise almost everyone at first.

English has many ways to say p → q, and you need to recognise them all:

- "if p, then q", "q if p", "q whenever p";
- "p only if q": "you may launch only if the weather is clear" is launch → clear;
- "p is sufficient for q" and "q is necessary for p".

Python has no arrow. But `(not p) or q` is false in exactly the row where p is true and q is false, so it has the same table. Check it row by row.

### If and only if

The **biconditional** p ↔ q (read "p if and only if q", often shortened to "p iff q") is true when p and q have the same truth value. It says p → q and q → p together. In Python, for booleans, it is `p == q`.

### Bigger formulas

Connectives combine into formulas such as (p ∨ q) → ¬r. So that you can leave out some brackets, there is an order, just as × comes before + in arithmetic: ¬ first, then ∧, then ∨, then →, then ↔. So ¬p ∧ q means (¬p) ∧ q, and p ∨ q → r means (p ∨ q) → r. When a formula could be misread, write the brackets anyway.

Python has an order too: `not` before `and`, and `and` before `or`. So `not a and b or c` means `((not a) and b) or c`.

A formula becomes a Python function: its variables are the parameters, and it returns the formula's truth value.

```python
def fire_allowed(armed, abort, pressure_ok):
    return armed and not abort and pressure_ok

print(fire_allowed(True, False, True))   # True
print(fire_allowed(True, True, True))    # False
```

### How many rows

With one variable a table has 2 rows. Adding a variable doubles the rows: every old row appears twice, once with the new variable false and once with it true. So a formula with n variables has 2ⁿ rows (2 to the power n): 4 for two variables, 8 for three, 1024 for ten.

This course always lists rows in the same order. Write F as 0 and T as 1, read each row as a binary number with the first variable as the leftmost digit, and count up from 0. For three variables:

| row number | p | q | r |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | F | F | F |
| 1 | F | F | T |
| 2 | F | T | F |
| 3 | F | T | T |
| 4 | T | F | F |
| 5 | T | F | T |
| 6 | T | T | F |
| 7 | T | T | T |

### Every row in Python

The `itertools` module has a tool called **`product`** that hands out every combination of choices. `product([False, True], repeat=2)` means "choose from `[False, True]`, two times over", and it gives the combinations as tuples, in exactly the course's order:

```python
from itertools import product

for choice in product([False, True], repeat=2):
    print(choice)
# (False, False)
# (False, True)
# (True, False)
# (True, True)
```

With `repeat=3` you get the 8 rows above. With `repeat=0` you get one row, the empty tuple `()`: with no variables there is exactly one way to choose nothing.

To call a formula's function with a row, spread the tuple into the arguments with `*`, as in the lesson on spreading arguments:

```python
def exactly_one(a, b):
    return a != b

pair = (True, False)
print(exactly_one(*pair))   # True, the same as exactly_one(True, False)
```

### Always true, always false

Three words sort formulas by their table:

- A **tautology** is true in every row. p ∨ ¬p is one: whatever p is, one of the two parts is true.
- A **contradiction** is false in every row, like p ∧ ¬p.
- A **contingency** is true in some rows and false in others, like p → q.

A formula is **satisfiable** when at least one row makes it true; in other words, when it is not a contradiction.

### Why the table method is right

A truth table does more than test a formula. It proves things about it, and here is why.

1. **The value depends only on the variables.** Each connective's table fixes its value from the values of its parts. Work from the innermost brackets outwards: the variables' values fix each small part, those fix the bigger parts, and so on up to the whole formula. No other fact about the world can change the answer.
2. **The table lists every assignment exactly once.** Each of the n variables is F or T, chosen independently, which gives 2 × 2 × … × 2 = 2ⁿ combinations. Counting in binary from 0 to 2ⁿ − 1 visits each of them once.

Together: "true in every row" means "true however the world turns out". Testing a program can never try every input, because there are too many. A formula has only finitely many cases, and the table checks all of them. So a table is a complete proof.

### What it costs

A table has 2ⁿ rows. Working out a formula with m connectives in one row takes about m steps, so the whole table takes Θ(m · 2ⁿ) time: it grows in proportion to m times 2ⁿ. Ten variables is 1024 rows, instant. Thirty is about a billion rows. Sixty is more than 10¹⁸ rows, which would take centuries. Each extra variable doubles the work: that is **exponential** growth.

Is there a fundamentally faster way to tell whether a formula is satisfiable? That is the [[SAT problem|sat]], and nobody knows.

For memory, `product` hands out one row at a time, so going through the table needs room for only one row. Keeping the whole table in a list takes room for all 2ⁿ rows.

### Where it is used

- Every `if` and `while` condition is a formula. Knowing its table is how you make sure it says what you mean.
- Computer hardware is built from [[logic gates|gates]], tiny circuits for ∧, ∨ and ¬. The Computer Organization course builds a working processor out of them.
- **SAT solvers**, programs that find a satisfying row for formulas with millions of variables, check chip designs, choose compatible package versions for installers, and plan schedules.
- Flight software interlocks are formulas: "fire only if armed, not aborted, and pressure nominal".
- A database's `WHERE` clause is a formula checked against each row of a table.

**Watch out:** three traps catch nearly everyone.

- The logical "or" is inclusive: p ∨ q is true when both are true. For "exactly one", use ⊕.
- p → q is true whenever p is false, and it does not mean p causes q. "If 2 + 2 = 5, then the Moon is made of cheese" is a true conditional.
- Python's `and` and `or` do not always give `True` or `False`: they hand back one of their operands. `0 or 7` is `7`, and `3 and ""` is `""`. On booleans you get booleans; on anything else, wrap the result in `bool(...)`. Also, `not p == q` means `not (p == q)`, because `==` binds more tightly than `not`.

::: context liar The liar's sentence
"This sentence is false" is the liar paradox, known since ancient Greece. It shows that not every grammatical sentence can be given a truth value, which is why logic insists that a proposition must be true or false and not both. In the 1930s, Kurt Gödel and Alan Turing turned sentences that talk about themselves into precise tools, and got deep results out of them: there are true statements no proof system can prove, and there are questions no program can answer. You will meet Turing's version, the halting problem, in the Theory of Computation course.
:::

::: context boole Why it is called bool
Python's type is named after George Boole, an English mathematician who in 1854 published "The Laws of Thought". He showed that reasoning with true and false could be done as algebra, with rules as exact as those for numbers. For decades it was a curiosity. Then in 1937 Claude Shannon, a student at MIT, showed that Boole's algebra describes electrical switching circuits exactly. Every digital computer since has been built on that idea, and nearly every programming language names its true-or-false type after Boole.
:::

::: context vacuous Why a false hypothesis makes it true
It helps to see p → q as a claim about every case: "in every case where p holds, q holds too". If p holds in no case, there is no case that can break the claim, so the claim stands. "Every astronaut on Mars has visited Jupiter" is true today for the same reason: there is no astronaut on Mars to be a counterexample. This choice is also what makes the rest of logic work. With it, p → q has the same table as ¬p ∨ q, and later lessons lean on that.
:::

::: context sat A million-dollar question
SAT, short for satisfiability, asks: given a formula, is there a row that makes it true? Trying every row takes 2ⁿ steps. Nobody has found a method that is always fast, and nobody has proved that none exists. In 1971 Stephen Cook showed that a fast method for SAT would give fast methods for thousands of other hard problems, from scheduling to protein folding. Whether it exists is the P versus NP question, one of the Clay Institute's million-dollar prize problems. In practice, modern solvers handle huge formulas from industry anyway, by clever search.
:::

::: context gates The connectives in hardware
Each connective is a small circuit called a gate. Wires carry a high or low voltage, standing for T and F, and the gate's output wire carries the connective's value. A modern processor has billions of them. The small circle on the NOT gate is the symbol engineers use for negation.

```svg

  <line x1="10" y1="42" x2="40" y2="42" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="10" y1="68" x2="40" y2="68" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <path d="M40 30 L62 30 A25 25 0 0 1 62 80 L40 80 Z" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>

  <text x="62" y="106" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">AND: p ∧ q</text>
  <line x1="130" y1="42" x2="162" y2="42" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="130" y1="68" x2="162" y2="68" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
```

--- task
A formula is given to you as a Python function `f` that takes `n` booleans and returns the formula's value. Write two functions.

- `truth_table(f, n)`: return a list with one pair `(row, value)` for each of the 2ⁿ rows, in the course's order (binary counting, all `False` first, all `True` last). `row` is a tuple of `n` booleans, and `value` is `bool(f(*row))`: always a real `True` or `False`, even if `f` returns something else. With `n = 0` the list has one pair, `((), bool(f()))`.
- `classify(f, n)`: return the string `"tautology"`, `"contradiction"` or `"contingency"`, using `truth_table`.

For example, `truth_table(lambda p, q: p and not q, 2)` is `[((False, False), False), ((False, True), False), ((True, False), True), ((True, True), False)]`, and `classify` of the same formula is `"contingency"`.
--- starter
def truth_table(f, n):
    return []


def classify(f, n):
    return ""
--- solution
from itertools import product


def truth_table(f, n):
    table = []
    for row in product([False, True], repeat=n):
        table.append((row, bool(f(*row))))
    return table


def classify(f, n):
    values = [value for row, value in truth_table(f, n)]
    trues = values.count(True)
    if trues == len(values):
        return "tautology"
    if trues == 0:
        return "contradiction"
    return "contingency"
--- hint
Build the rows with `product([False, True], repeat=n)` from `itertools`, and call the formula on each row with `f(*row)`.
--- hint
Append `(row, bool(f(*row)))` for each row. The `bool(...)` matters: `True and 5` is `5`, not `True`.
--- hint
In `classify`, count how many values are `True`. All of them means a tautology, none means a contradiction, anything in between a contingency.
--- check case | The conditional, row by row
truth_table(lambda p, q: (not p) or q, 2)
=> [((False, False), True), ((False, True), True), ((True, False), False), ((True, True), True)]
--- check case | Three variables come in binary counting order
[row for row, value in truth_table(lambda a, b, c: a, 3)]
=> [(False, False, False), (False, False, True), (False, True, False), (False, True, True), (True, False, False), (True, False, True), (True, True, False), (True, True, True)]
--- check case | No variables: one row, the empty tuple
(truth_table(lambda: True, 0), truth_table(lambda: 0, 0))
=> ([((), True)], [((), False)])
--- check case | Values are real booleans
truth_table(lambda p: p and 5, 1)
=> [((False,), False), ((True,), True)]
?? `True and 5` is `5`. Wrap the value in `bool(...)`.
--- check test | Ten variables: 1024 rows, half of them odd
(lambda t: len(t) == 1024 and len(set(r for r, v in t)) == 1024 and [v for r, v in t].count(True) == 512)(truth_table(lambda *xs: sum(xs) % 2 == 1, 10))
--- check case | p or not p is a tautology
classify(lambda p: p or not p, 1)
=> "tautology"
--- check case | p and not p is a contradiction
classify(lambda p: p and not p, 1)
=> "contradiction"
--- check case | The conditional is a contingency
classify(lambda p, q: (not p) or q, 2)
=> "contingency"
--- check case | Peirce's law, ((p → q) → p) → p, is a tautology
(lambda imp: classify(lambda p, q: imp(imp(imp(p, q), p), p), 2))(lambda a, b: (not a) or b)
=> "tautology"
--- check case | True only in the last row, or only in the first
(classify(lambda a, b, c, d: a and b and c and d, 4), classify(lambda a, b, c: not (a or b or c), 3))
=> ("contingency", "contingency")
?? Make sure your table really has every row, including the all-True one.
--- check case | No variables
(classify(lambda: True, 0), classify(lambda: False, 0))
=> ("tautology", "contradiction")

+++ practice | Flight rules as formulas
--- task
Write four functions. Each takes booleans and returns the truth value of one rule, written as a formula.

- `rule_a(sealed, nominal)`: "The hatch is sealed only if the pressure is nominal."
- `rule_b(pump_on, tank_full)`: "The pump is on whenever the tank is full."
- `rule_c(radio1, radio2)`: "Exactly one of the two radios is transmitting."
- `rule_d(armed, ready, abort)`: "The engine is armed if and only if the vehicle is ready and there is no abort."

Each function must return `True` or `False`. For example, `rule_a(True, False)` is `False`: the hatch is sealed, but the pressure is not nominal.
--- starter
def rule_a(sealed, nominal):
    return sealed and nominal


def rule_b(pump_on, tank_full):
    return pump_on and tank_full


def rule_c(radio1, radio2):
    return radio1 or radio2


def rule_d(armed, ready, abort):
    return armed and ready and not abort
--- solution
def rule_a(sealed, nominal):
    return (not sealed) or nominal


def rule_b(pump_on, tank_full):
    return (not tank_full) or pump_on


def rule_c(radio1, radio2):
    return radio1 != radio2


def rule_d(armed, ready, abort):
    return armed == (ready and not abort)
--- hint
"p only if q" is p → q, and "q whenever p" is also p → q. Find which part is the hypothesis in each sentence before you write anything.
--- hint
p → q is `(not p) or q`. "Exactly one" is the exclusive or, and "if and only if" is `==` on booleans.
--- hint
In `rule_b`, the tank being full is the hypothesis: `(not tank_full) or pump_on`. In `rule_d`, compare `armed` with the whole formula `ready and not abort`.
--- check case | rule_a in all four rows
[rule_a(s, n) for s in (False, True) for n in (False, True)]
=> [True, True, False, True]
?? "Sealed only if nominal" is sealed → nominal.
--- check case | rule_b in all four rows (pump_on first, then tank_full)
[rule_b(p, t) for p in (False, True) for t in (False, True)]
=> [True, False, True, True]
?? The rule is broken only when the tank is full and the pump is off.
--- check case | rule_c in all four rows
[rule_c(a, b) for a in (False, True) for b in (False, True)]
=> [False, True, True, False]
--- check case | rule_d in all eight rows
[rule_d(a, r, x) for a in (False, True) for r in (False, True) for x in (False, True)]
=> [True, True, False, True, False, False, True, False]

+++ practice | A truth table as text
--- task
Write `table_text(f, names)`. `f` is a formula as a function, and `names` is a list of its variable names, with at least one name. Return the truth table as one string, with lines joined by `"\n"` and no newline at the end:

- The first line is the names joined by single spaces, then ` | out`.
- Then one line per row, in the course's order: each value as the letter `T` or `F`, joined by single spaces, then ` | `, then `T` or `F` for the formula.

For example, `table_text(lambda p, q: (not p) or q, ["p", "q"])` returns this string:

```text
p q | out
F F | T
F T | T
T F | F
T T | T
```
--- starter
def table_text(f, names):
    return " ".join(names) + " | out"
--- solution
from itertools import product


def letter(value):
    return "T" if value else "F"


def table_text(f, names):
    lines = [" ".join(names) + " | out"]
    for row in product([False, True], repeat=len(names)):
        cells = " ".join(letter(v) for v in row)
        lines.append(cells + " | " + letter(f(*row)))
    return "\n".join(lines)
--- hint
The number of variables is `len(names)`. Use `product([False, True], repeat=len(names))` for the rows.
--- hint
A small helper that turns a value into `"T"` or `"F"` keeps this tidy. Use it on each value in the row and on `f(*row)`.
--- check case | The example from the task
table_text(lambda p, q: (not p) or q, ["p", "q"])
=> "p q | out\nF F | T\nF T | T\nT F | F\nT T | T"
--- check case | One variable
table_text(lambda x: not x, ["x"])
=> "x | out\nF | T\nT | F"
--- check case | Three variables and longer names
table_text(lambda armed, abort, ok: armed and not abort and ok, ["armed", "abort", "ok"])
=> "armed abort ok | out\nF F F | F\nF F T | F\nF T F | F\nF T T | F\nT F F | F\nT F T | T\nT T F | F\nT T T | F"
--- check case | A formula that returns something other than a boolean
table_text(lambda a: a and 0, ["a"])
=> "a | out\nF | F\nT | F"
?? `True and 0` is `0`. Decide on the letter by whether the value is true, not by comparing it with `True`.

+++ practice | The rows that make it true
--- task
Write `models(f, names)`. `f` is a formula as a function and `names` lists its variables in order. Return a list with one dict for each row that makes `f` true, in the course's row order. Each dict maps every name to its value in that row.

For example, `models(lambda a, b: a != b, ["x", "y"])` is `[{"x": False, "y": True}, {"x": True, "y": False}]`. A contradiction gives `[]`. With no names, the one empty row gives `[{}]` if `f()` is true and `[]` if not.
--- starter
def models(f, names):
    return []
--- solution
from itertools import product


def models(f, names):
    found = []
    for row in product([False, True], repeat=len(names)):
        if f(*row):
            found.append(dict(zip(names, row)))
    return found
--- hint
Loop over the rows with `product`, and keep only the rows where `f(*row)` is true.
--- hint
`dict(zip(names, row))` pairs each name with its value in the row.
--- check case | The example from the task
models(lambda a, b: a != b, ["x", "y"])
=> [{"x": False, "y": True}, {"x": True, "y": False}]
--- check case | A contradiction has no models
models(lambda p: p and not p, ["p"])
=> []
--- check case | A tautology: every row
models(lambda p, q: p or not p, ["p", "q"])
=> [{"p": False, "q": False}, {"p": False, "q": True}, {"p": True, "q": False}, {"p": True, "q": True}]
--- check case | An interlock with three variables
models(lambda armed, abort, ok: armed and not abort and ok, ["armed", "abort", "ok"])
=> [{"armed": True, "abort": False, "ok": True}]
--- check case | No variables
(models(lambda: True, []), models(lambda: False, []))
=> ([{}], [])

+++ practice | Row numbers
--- task
In the course's order, row number i is the row whose values, read as a binary number with `False` as 0 and `True` as 1, give i. The first variable is the leftmost digit.

Write `minterms(f, n)` that returns the list of row numbers, from smallest to largest, of the rows where the formula `f` (with `n` variables) is true.

For example, `minterms(lambda p, q: (not p) or q, 2)` is `[0, 1, 3]`: the conditional is false only in row 2, where p is true and q is false. Watch the edges: a contradiction gives `[]`, a formula true only when every variable is true includes the last row, 2ⁿ − 1, and with `n = 0` the only row is row 0.
--- starter
def minterms(f, n):
    return [i for i in range(2 ** n - 1)]
--- solution
from itertools import product


def minterms(f, n):
    rows = product([False, True], repeat=n)
    return [i for i, row in enumerate(rows) if f(*row)]
--- hint
Rows from `product([False, True], repeat=n)` come out in row-number order, so a row's number is its position. `enumerate` gives you both.
--- hint
Keep position `i` exactly when `f(*row)` is true. There are 2ⁿ rows in all, numbered 0 to 2ⁿ − 1.
--- check case | The example from the task
minterms(lambda p, q: (not p) or q, 2)
=> [0, 1, 3]
--- check case | Odd parity on three variables
minterms(lambda a, b, c: (a != b) != c, 3)
=> [1, 2, 4, 7]
--- check case | Only the last row
minterms(lambda a, b, c: a and b and c, 3)
=> [7]
--- check case | A contradiction and a tautology
(minterms(lambda p: p and not p, 1), minterms(lambda p, q: True, 2))
=> ([], [0, 1, 2, 3])
--- check case | No variables
(minterms(lambda: True, 0), minterms(lambda: False, 0))
=> ([0], [])

+++ practice | Debug: the tautology checker that says yes too often
--- task
`is_tautology(f, n)` should return `True` exactly when the formula `f` with `n` variables is true in every row. `rows(n)` builds the rows itself: row number `i` is turned into its binary digits, first variable first.

It gets some formulas wrong. For example, `is_tautology(lambda p, q: not (p and q), 2)` returns `True`, but the formula is false when p and q are both true, so the answer should be `False`. Find the bug and fix it. It is one small change.
--- starter
def rows(n):
    out = []
    for i in range(2 ** n - 1):
        row = tuple(i // 2 ** (n - 1 - k) % 2 == 1 for k in range(n))
        out.append(row)
    return out


def is_tautology(f, n):
    for row in rows(n):
        if not f(*row):
            return False
    return True
--- solution
def rows(n):
    out = []
    for i in range(2 ** n):
        row = tuple(i // 2 ** (n - 1 - k) % 2 == 1 for k in range(n))
        out.append(row)
    return out


def is_tautology(f, n):
    for row in rows(n):
        if not f(*row):
            return False
    return True
--- hint
Print `rows(2)`. How many rows should a table with two variables have, and how many are there?
--- hint
The row numbers go from 0 to 2ⁿ − 1, which is 2ⁿ numbers. `range` stops one before its end.
--- check case | The example from the task
is_tautology(lambda p, q: not (p and q), 2)
=> False
?? Which row is missing from rows(2)?
--- check case | rows(2) has all four rows
rows(2)
=> [(False, False), (False, True), (True, False), (True, True)]
--- check case | Real tautologies still pass
(is_tautology(lambda p: p or not p, 1), is_tautology(lambda p, q: (not (p and q)) == ((not p) or (not q)), 2))
=> (True, True)
--- check case | False only when all three are true
is_tautology(lambda a, b, c: not (a and b and c), 3)
=> False
--- check case | No variables: one row
(is_tautology(lambda: True, 0), is_tautology(lambda: False, 0))
=> (True, False)

+++ practice | A formula for any table
--- task
Every truth table has a formula. Here is one way to build it. For each row where the table says `True`, write a **clause** that is true in that row and in no other: join, with ` and `, each variable's name if it is `True` in the row, or `not ` and its name if it is `False`, and put the clause in brackets. Then join all the clauses with ` or `. This shape is called **disjunctive normal form**.

Write `dnf(f, names)` that returns this formula as a Python expression string. Clauses come in the course's row order, and names in the order given. If `f` is false in every row, return `"False"`.

For example, `dnf(lambda p, q: p != q, ["p", "q"])` is `"(not p and q) or (p and not q)"`. With one variable, `dnf(lambda x: True, ["x"])` is `"(not x) or (x)"`. `names` has at least one name.

A check turns your string back into a formula with `eval` and compares it with `f` in every row.
--- starter
def dnf(f, names):
    return "False"
--- solution
from itertools import product


def clause(names, row):
    parts = []
    for name, value in zip(names, row):
        parts.append(name if value else "not " + name)
    return "(" + " and ".join(parts) + ")"


def dnf(f, names):
    clauses = []
    for row in product([False, True], repeat=len(names)):
        if f(*row):
            clauses.append(clause(names, row))
    if not clauses:
        return "False"
    return " or ".join(clauses)
--- hint
Write a helper that makes the clause for one row: walk `zip(names, row)` and collect `name` or `"not " + name`, then join with `" and "` inside brackets.
--- hint
Collect one clause for every row where `f(*row)` is true. Join them with `" or "`, and return `"False"` if there are none.
--- check case | The example from the task
dnf(lambda p, q: p != q, ["p", "q"])
=> "(not p and q) or (p and not q)"
--- check case | One variable, always true
dnf(lambda x: True, ["x"])
=> "(not x) or (x)"
--- check case | A contradiction
dnf(lambda a, b: a and not a, ["a", "b"])
=> "False"
--- check case | True in one row only
dnf(lambda a, b, c: a and not b and c, ["a", "b", "c"])
=> "(a and not b and c)"
--- check test | The string means the same as f in every row (majority of three)
(lambda f, names: all(eval(dnf(f, names), {}, dict(zip(names, row))) == f(*row) for row in __import__("itertools").product([False, True], repeat=3)))(lambda a, b, c: (a and b) or (a and c) or (b and c), ["a", "b", "c"])
--- check test | The string means the same as f in every row (a four-variable rule)
(lambda f, names: all(eval(dnf(f, names), {}, dict(zip(names, row))) == bool(f(*row)) for row in __import__("itertools").product([False, True], repeat=4)))(lambda w, x, y, z: (w or not x) and (y != z), ["w", "x", "y", "z"])

+++ question | When the conditional is false
--- ask
In which rows is p → q false?
--- choice
Whenever p is false.
--- choice correct
Only when p is true and q is false.
--- choice
Whenever q is false.
--- choice
Whenever p and q have different values.
--- why
p → q is a promise about what happens when p holds. It is broken only when p holds and q does not. When p is false the conditional is vacuously true, and when p and q differ the other way round (p false, q true) it is true as well.

+++ question | How many rows
--- ask
How many rows does the truth table of a formula with 6 variables have? Type a number.
--- answer
64
--- why
Each variable doubles the number of rows, so 6 variables give 2⁶ = 64 rows.
=== disc-02 | Logical equivalence
--- teach
Last lesson you built truth tables and sorted formulas into tautologies, contradictions and contingencies. Often two formulas that look different say exactly the same thing. "It is not the case that both valves are open" and "at least one valve is closed" are one fact in two sets of words. This lesson makes "say the same thing" exact, gives you the laws for rewriting one formula into another, and shows how Python settles any such question by brute force.

### The same in every row

Two formulas A and B are **logically equivalent**, written A ≡ B (read "A is equivalent to B"), when they have the same truth value in every row of the truth table. In everyday words: however the world turns out, they are both true or both false. Like two recipes that give the same cake from any set of ingredients, you can always swap one for the other.

Another way to say it: A ≡ B exactly when A ↔ B is a tautology. The ↔ is true in a row when both sides agree there, so "true in every row" is "agree in every row".

Note that ≡ is not a connective. p ∧ q is a formula, which is true or false in each row. A ≡ B is a claim *about* two formulas, and it is either right or wrong once and for all.

You already know one equivalence: p → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q. Their columns in the truth table are identical.

### De Morgan's laws

The two most useful equivalences are **[[De Morgan's laws|de-morgan]]**:

- ¬(p ∧ q) ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q: "not both" is the same as "at least one is not".
- ¬(p ∨ q) ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬q: "not either" is the same as "neither".

Pushing a ¬ inside brackets flips ∧ into ∨ and ∨ into ∧. Here is the table for the second law:

| p | q | p ∨ q | ¬(p ∨ q) | ¬p | ¬q | ¬p ∧ ¬q |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| F | F | F | T | T | T | T |
| F | T | T | F | T | F | F |
| T | F | T | F | F | T | F |
| T | T | T | F | F | F | F |

The fourth and last columns match in all four rows, so the law holds. That table *is* the proof.

In code, De Morgan's laws are how you negate a condition. The opposite of `fuel_ok and link_ok` is `not fuel_ok or not link_ok`. With comparisons, the negation of `x < 5` is `x >= 5`.

### Not equivalent: one row is enough

To show two formulas are **not** equivalent, you need only one row where they differ. That row is a **counterexample**.

Is ¬(p ∧ q) equivalent to ¬p ∧ ¬q? Try p = T, q = F. Then p ∧ q is F, so ¬(p ∧ q) is T. But ¬p is F, so ¬p ∧ ¬q is F. They differ, so the answer is no. (This is the mistake De Morgan's laws exist to prevent.)

### Checking in Python

Python can build both columns and compare them. Here are the columns for ¬(p ∧ q) and ¬p ∨ ¬q:

```python
from itertools import product

left = [not (p and q) for p, q in product([False, True], repeat=2)]
right = [(not p) or (not q) for p, q in product([False, True], repeat=2)]
print(left)            # [True, True, True, False]
print(left == right)   # True
```

### The laws

Each of these is proved by a truth table, once. After that you can use them without drawing tables. T and F here stand for a formula that is always true and one that is always false.

| name | law | and its partner |
|---|---|---|
| identity | p ∧ T ≡ p | p ∨ F ≡ p |
| domination | p ∨ T ≡ T | p ∧ F ≡ F |
| idempotent | p ∧ p ≡ p | p ∨ p ≡ p |
| double negation | ¬¬p ≡ p | |
| negation | p ∨ ¬p ≡ T | p ∧ ¬p ≡ F |
| commutative | p ∧ q ≡ q ∧ p | p ∨ q ≡ q ∨ p |
| associative | (p ∧ q) ∧ r ≡ p ∧ (q ∧ r) | the same with ∨ |
| distributive | p ∧ (q ∨ r) ≡ (p ∧ q) ∨ (p ∧ r) | p ∨ (q ∧ r) ≡ (p ∨ q) ∧ (p ∨ r) |
| De Morgan | ¬(p ∧ q) ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q | ¬(p ∨ q) ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬q |
| absorption | p ∨ (p ∧ q) ≡ p | p ∧ (p ∨ q) ≡ p |
| conditional | p → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q | |
| biconditional | p ↔ q ≡ (p → q) ∧ (q → p) | |

One difference from ordinary algebra: both distributive laws hold. In arithmetic, × spreads over + (2 × (3 + 4) = 2 × 3 + 2 × 4), but + does not spread over ×. In logic, ∧ spreads over ∨ **and** ∨ spreads over ∧.

### Proving an equivalence with the laws

Here is the rule that lets you use laws like algebra. **Substitution:** if you replace a part of a formula with an equivalent part, the whole formula stays equivalent. It holds because a formula's value depends only on the values of its parts (last lesson), and an equivalent part has the same value in every row.

So you can prove an equivalence as a chain, naming the law at each step. Here is a proof that ¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q:

1. ¬(p → q) ≡ ¬(¬p ∨ q), by the conditional law.
2. ≡ ¬¬p ∧ ¬q, by De Morgan.
3. ≡ p ∧ ¬q, by double negation.

This is worth remembering by itself: "if p then q" is false exactly when p holds and q does not. You will use it every time you disprove a claim.

### Converse, inverse, contrapositive

Every conditional p → q has three relatives:

- the **converse** q → p (the arrow turned round);
- the **inverse** ¬p → ¬q (both parts negated);
- the **contrapositive** ¬q → ¬p (turned round **and** negated).

| p | q | p → q | q → p | ¬p → ¬q | ¬q → ¬p |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| F | F | T | T | T | T |
| F | T | T | F | F | T |
| T | F | F | T | T | F |
| T | T | T | T | T | T |

Read the columns: a conditional is **[[equivalent to its contrapositive|square]]**, and the converse is equivalent to the inverse. But a conditional is **not** equivalent to its converse: the row p = F, q = T tells them apart.

An example. "If a part is a rocket nozzle, it is heat-resistant" is true. Its contrapositive, "if a part is not heat-resistant, it is not a rocket nozzle", says the same thing. Its converse, "if a part is heat-resistant, it is a rocket nozzle", is false: an oven dish is heat-resistant. Mixing up a statement with its converse is so common that the mistake has a [[name|fallacies]].

The contrapositive also follows from the laws: ¬q → ¬p ≡ ¬¬q ∨ ¬p ≡ q ∨ ¬p ≡ ¬p ∨ q ≡ p → q, using the conditional law, double negation, commutativity, and the conditional law again.

### Why brute force is right

Two formulas over the same n variables are equivalent if and only if they agree in all 2ⁿ rows. Comparing every row is therefore a complete proof, for the same reason as last lesson: the rows are every possible case, and there are finitely many.

What if the formulas use different variables, like p ∨ (q ∧ ¬q) and p? Compare them over all the variables that appear in either one. A formula that does not mention q simply ignores it. Here the two agree in all four rows, so they are equivalent.

### What it costs

Checking equivalence this way looks at up to 2ⁿ rows and works out both formulas in each, so it takes time in proportion to 2ⁿ times the size of the formulas. It can stop early: the first row where they differ settles "not equivalent". But "equivalent" needs every row.

A chain of laws can be far shorter than a table. The distributive law for 50 variables would need a table with 2⁵⁰ rows, about 10¹⁵, but it takes one line with the laws. The catch is finding the chain: there is no known recipe that always finds a short one quickly. Deciding equivalence is [[as hard as SAT|as-hard]].

### Where it is used

- **Compilers** rewrite conditions with these laws to make them cheaper to test.
- **Refactoring:** `while not (done or timed_out)` becomes `while not done and not timed_out`, which many people find easier to read.
- **Chip design:** engineers simplify circuits to use fewer gates, and then run an [[equivalence checker|equivalence-checkers]] to prove the small circuit computes exactly what the big one did.
- **Databases** rewrite `WHERE` clauses into equivalent forms that can use an index.

**Watch out:** negating a condition by hand is where the bugs live.

- `not (a and b)` is `not a or not b`, **not** `not a and not b`. The ∧ must flip to ∨.
- The negation of `x < 5` is `x >= 5`, not `x > 5`. The value 5 itself belongs to the negation.
- The negation of `lo <= t <= hi` is `t < lo or t > hi`. Writing `and` there gives a condition that is never true.
- A conditional and its converse are different claims. Proving one does not prove the other.

::: context de-morgan Augustus De Morgan
Augustus De Morgan was a British mathematician and a friend of George Boole. In 1847 he published the laws that carry his name, though logicians in the Middle Ages, such as William of Ockham, already knew them in words. De Morgan also first stated mathematical induction carefully under that name, the proof method of lesson 7. His laws come back often: in set theory (the complement of a union is the intersection of the complements), in circuit design, and in every negated `if` you will ever write.
:::

::: context square Four statements, two pairs
The four relatives of a conditional fall into two pairs. Each pair says the same thing. The two pairs do not say the same thing as each other.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="20" width="130" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="85" y="37" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">original</text>
  <text x="85" y="53" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">p → q</text>
  <rect x="210" y="20" width="130" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="275" y="37" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">converse</text>
  <text x="275" y="53" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">q → p</text>
  <rect x="20" y="110" width="130" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="85" y="127" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">contrapositive</text>
  <text x="85" y="143" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">¬q → ¬p</text>
  <rect x="210" y="110" width="130" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="275" y="127" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">inverse</text>
  <text x="275" y="143" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">¬p → ¬q</text>
  <line x1="85" y1="60" x2="85" y2="110" stroke="#1f7a3d" stroke-width="3"/>
  <text x="95" y="90" font-size="13" fill="#1f7a3d">≡</text>
  <line x1="275" y1="60" x2="275" y2="110" stroke="#1f7a3d" stroke-width="3"/>
  <text x="285" y="90" font-size="13" fill="#1f7a3d">≡</text>
  <line x1="150" y1="40" x2="210" y2="40" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2" stroke-dasharray="5 4"/>
  <text x="180" y="33" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">not ≡</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context fallacies Affirming the consequent
Reasoning "p → q, and q is true, so p is true" is called affirming the consequent. "If the engine is firing, the chamber is hot. The chamber is hot. So the engine is firing." Wrong: it may be cooling down after a burn. It treats p → q as if it were its converse. Its twin, denying the antecedent, reasons "p → q, and p is false, so q is false", which treats the conditional as its inverse. Both look convincing in a hurry. The valid pair is modus ponens (from p → q and p, conclude q) and modus tollens (from p → q and ¬q, conclude ¬p), which is the contrapositive at work.
:::

::: context as-hard Why equivalence is as hard as SAT
Two formulas A and B are not equivalent exactly when some row makes them differ, which is exactly when the formula A ⊕ B (exclusive or) is satisfiable. So a fast equivalence checker would give a fast satisfiability checker, and the other way round. That is why no one expects a method that is always fast. Real equivalence checkers rely on the same clever search as SAT solvers, and work very well on the formulas that come up in practice.
:::

::: context equivalence-checkers Proving two circuits match
In 1994, a flaw in the division unit of Intel's Pentium processor gave slightly wrong answers for rare inputs. Replacing the chips cost Intel about 475 million dollars. Since then, chip makers have used formal verification much more. An equivalence checker takes the circuit as designed and the circuit after optimisation, turns both into formulas, and proves they agree on every input, which is far more than testing could ever cover.
:::
--- task
A formula is given as a Python function of `n` booleans, as in the last lesson. Write two functions.

- `counterexample(f, g, n)`: return the **first** row, in the course's row order, where `f` and `g` have different truth values, as a tuple of `n` booleans. If they agree in every row, return `None`. Compare truth values, so a formula that returns `0` agrees with one that returns `False`.
- `equivalent(f, g, n)`: return `True` if the two formulas are logically equivalent and `False` if not, using `counterexample`.

For example, `counterexample(lambda p, q: (not p) or q, lambda p, q: (not q) or p, 2)` is `(False, True)`: the conditional and its converse differ there. For a conditional and its contrapositive it is `None`, and `equivalent` is `True`.
--- starter
def counterexample(f, g, n):
    return None


def equivalent(f, g, n):
    return False
--- solution
from itertools import product


def counterexample(f, g, n):
    for row in product([False, True], repeat=n):
        if bool(f(*row)) != bool(g(*row)):
            return row
    return None


def equivalent(f, g, n):
    return counterexample(f, g, n) is None
--- hint
Go through the rows with `product([False, True], repeat=n)`, in order, and return the first row where the two truth values differ.
--- hint
Compare `bool(f(*row))` with `bool(g(*row))`, so that `0` and `False` count as the same. If the loop ends without returning, return `None`.
--- hint
`equivalent` is one line: the formulas are equivalent exactly when there is no counterexample, so test `counterexample(f, g, n) is None`.
--- check case | A conditional and its converse
counterexample(lambda p, q: (not p) or q, lambda p, q: (not q) or p, 2)
=> (False, True)
--- check case | A conditional and its contrapositive agree everywhere
(counterexample(lambda p, q: (not p) or q, lambda p, q: q or not p, 2), equivalent(lambda p, q: (not p) or q, lambda p, q: (not (not q)) or (not p), 2))
=> (None, True)
--- check case | Both of De Morgan's laws
(equivalent(lambda p, q: not (p and q), lambda p, q: (not p) or (not q), 2), equivalent(lambda p, q: not (p or q), lambda p, q: (not p) and (not q), 2))
=> (True, True)
--- check case | The classic wrong negation, and where it fails first
(equivalent(lambda p, q: not (p and q), lambda p, q: (not p) and (not q), 2), counterexample(lambda p, q: not (p and q), lambda p, q: (not p) and (not q), 2))
=> (False, (False, True))
--- check case | Both distributive laws, on three variables
(equivalent(lambda p, q, r: p and (q or r), lambda p, q, r: (p and q) or (p and r), 3), equivalent(lambda p, q, r: p or (q and r), lambda p, q, r: (p or q) and (p or r), 3))
=> (True, True)
--- check case | Absorption
equivalent(lambda p, q: p or (p and q), lambda p, q: p, 2)
=> True
--- check case | Truth values are compared, not raw values
(equivalent(lambda p: p and 0, lambda p: False, 1), counterexample(lambda p: p and 7, lambda p: p, 1))
=> (True, None)
?? `True and 0` is `0`, and `True and 7` is `7`. Compare `bool(...)` of each side.
--- check case | No variables
(counterexample(lambda: True, lambda: 1, 0), counterexample(lambda: True, lambda: False, 0))
=> (None, ())
--- check case | Ten variables that differ only in the last row
counterexample(lambda *xs: True, lambda *xs: not all(xs), 10)
=> (True, True, True, True, True, True, True, True, True, True)
--- check case | The negation of a conditional
(equivalent(lambda p, q: not ((not p) or q), lambda p, q: p and not q, 2), equivalent(lambda p, q: not ((not p) or q), lambda p, q: (not p) and q, 2))
=> (True, False)

+++ practice | The four relatives
--- task
Write `relatives(p, q)`. It takes two booleans and returns a dict with the truth values of the conditional "if p then q" and its three relatives, under these keys: `"original"`, `"converse"`, `"inverse"` and `"contrapositive"`. Every value must be `True` or `False`.

For example, `relatives(False, True)` is `{"original": True, "converse": False, "inverse": False, "contrapositive": True}`.
--- starter
def relatives(p, q):
    return {"original": True, "converse": True, "inverse": True, "contrapositive": True}
--- solution
def implies(a, b):
    return (not a) or b


def relatives(p, q):
    return {
        "original": implies(p, q),
        "converse": implies(q, p),
        "inverse": implies(not p, not q),
        "contrapositive": implies(not q, not p),
    }
--- hint
A small `implies(a, b)` helper that returns `(not a) or b` makes each entry one call.
--- hint
The converse swaps the two parts. The inverse negates both. The contrapositive swaps them and negates both.
--- check case | The example from the task
relatives(False, True)
=> {"original": True, "converse": False, "inverse": False, "contrapositive": True}
--- check case | p true and q false
relatives(True, False)
=> {"original": False, "converse": True, "inverse": True, "contrapositive": False}
--- check case | Both true, and both false
(relatives(True, True), relatives(False, False))
=> ({"original": True, "converse": True, "inverse": True, "contrapositive": True}, {"original": True, "converse": True, "inverse": True, "contrapositive": True})
--- check test | In every row, original matches contrapositive and converse matches inverse
all((lambda d: d["original"] == d["contrapositive"] and d["converse"] == d["inverse"])(relatives(p, q)) for p in (False, True) for q in (False, True))

+++ practice | Simplify with the laws
--- task
Each formula below can be simplified, using the laws of this lesson, to one that uses **at most one** connective (`and`, `or` or `not`). Write the simplified version of each as a function. Each function's `return` line may hold at most one of the words `and`, `or` and `not`, and each must be equivalent to its formula.

- `simple_a(p, q)` for `(p and q) or (p and not q)`
- `simple_b(p, q)` for `not ((not p) or (not q))`
- `simple_c(p, q)` for `(p or q) and not ((not p) and (not q))`
- `simple_d(p, q, r)` for `p or (p and q and r)`

For example, `(p or not p) and q` simplifies to plain `q`: by the negation law, `p or not p` is always true, and by the identity law, "always true" `and q` is `q`.
--- starter
def simple_a(p, q):
    return None


def simple_b(p, q):
    return None


def simple_c(p, q):
    return None


def simple_d(p, q, r):
    return None
--- solution
def simple_a(p, q):
    return p


def simple_b(p, q):
    return p and q


def simple_c(p, q):
    return p or q


def simple_d(p, q, r):
    return p
--- hint
For `simple_a`, use the distributive law backwards: `(p and q) or (p and not q)` is `p and (q or not q)`. For `simple_b`, use De Morgan and then double negation.
--- hint
In `simple_c`, De Morgan turns `not ((not p) and (not q))` into `p or q`, so the formula is `(p or q) and (p or q)`. The idempotent law finishes it. `simple_d` is absorption.
--- check test | simple_a is equivalent to its formula
all(bool(simple_a(p, q)) == bool((p and q) or (p and not q)) for p in (False, True) for q in (False, True))
--- check test | simple_b is equivalent to its formula
all(bool(simple_b(p, q)) == bool(not ((not p) or (not q))) for p in (False, True) for q in (False, True))
--- check test | simple_c is equivalent to its formula
all(bool(simple_c(p, q)) == bool((p or q) and not ((not p) and (not q))) for p in (False, True) for q in (False, True))
--- check test | simple_d is equivalent to its formula
all(bool(simple_d(p, q, r)) == bool(p or (p and q and r)) for p in (False, True) for q in (False, True) for r in (False, True))
--- check source absent | Every return line has at most one connective
^[ \t]*return\b[^\n#]*\b(and|or|not)\b[^\n#]*\b(and|or|not)\b
?? Use the laws until each formula has at most one `and`, `or` or `not` left.

+++ practice | Grouping equivalent formulas
--- task
Write `group_equivalent(formulas, n)`. `formulas` is a list of formulas, each a function of the same `n` booleans. Return a list of groups: each group is a list of the positions (indexes) of formulas that are all equivalent to each other, and formulas in different groups are not equivalent.

Order the positions inside a group from smallest to largest, and order the groups by their first position. For example, with `n = 2` and the formulas `p → q`, `p ∧ q`, `¬p ∨ q`, `q ∧ p` (in that order), the answer is `[[0, 2], [1, 3]]`. An empty list of formulas gives `[]`.

Work out each formula's column of truth values once, and group by it.
--- starter
def group_equivalent(formulas, n):
    return [[i] for i in range(len(formulas))]
--- solution
from itertools import product


def group_equivalent(formulas, n):
    rows = list(product([False, True], repeat=n))
    groups = {}
    for i, f in enumerate(formulas):
        column = tuple(bool(f(*row)) for row in rows)
        groups.setdefault(column, []).append(i)
    return list(groups.values())
--- hint
Two formulas are equivalent exactly when their columns of truth values, over all rows in order, are equal. A tuple of booleans can be a dict key.
--- hint
Keep a dict from column to a list of positions. Dicts remember the order keys were first added, so the groups come out ordered by first position. Use `bool(...)` on each value.
--- check case | The example from the task
group_equivalent([lambda p, q: (not p) or q, lambda p, q: p and q, lambda p, q: (not p) or q, lambda p, q: q and p], 2)
=> [[0, 2], [1, 3]]
--- check case | Nothing to group
group_equivalent([], 3)
=> []
--- check case | De Morgan, the wrong negation, and a contrapositive
group_equivalent([lambda p, q: not (p and q), lambda p, q: (not p) and (not q), lambda p, q: (not p) or (not q), lambda p, q: not (p or q), lambda p, q: (not p) or q, lambda p, q: q or (not p)], 2)
=> [[0, 2], [1, 3], [4, 5]]
--- check case | Raw values are compared as truth values
group_equivalent([lambda p: p and 0, lambda p: False, lambda p: p, lambda p: p and 3], 1)
=> [[0, 1], [2, 3]]
--- check case | All different
group_equivalent([lambda p, q, r: p, lambda p, q, r: q, lambda p, q, r: r], 3)
=> [[0], [1], [2]]

+++ practice | Formulas over different variables
--- task
Two formulas may not use the same variables. Write `equivalent_named(f, f_names, g, g_names)`. `f` is called with one boolean for each name in `f_names`, in that order, and `g` the same way with `g_names`. Return `True` if the formulas are equivalent when compared over **every** variable that appears in either list, and `False` if not.

For example, `equivalent_named(lambda p, q: p or (q and not q), ["p", "q"], lambda p: p, ["p"])` is `True`. Watch the edges: the lists may share some names, share none, or list the same names in a different order, and either list may be empty (a formula with no variables).
--- starter
from itertools import product


def equivalent_named(f, f_names, g, g_names):
    for row in product([False, True], repeat=len(f_names)):
        if bool(f(*row)) != bool(g(*row)):
            return False
    return True
--- solution
from itertools import product


def equivalent_named(f, f_names, g, g_names):
    names = list(f_names)
    for name in g_names:
        if name not in names:
            names.append(name)
    for row in product([False, True], repeat=len(names)):
        value = dict(zip(names, row))
        a = f(*[value[x] for x in f_names])
        b = g(*[value[x] for x in g_names])
        if bool(a) != bool(b):
            return False
    return True
--- hint
First build one list of all the names, with no repeats. Then go through every row of values for that combined list.
--- hint
Turn each row into a dict from name to value with `dict(zip(names, row))`. Then call `f` with the values of `f_names`, in their order, and `g` with the values of `g_names`.
--- check case | The example from the task
equivalent_named(lambda p, q: p or (q and not q), ["p", "q"], lambda p: p, ["p"])
=> True
--- check case | The same names in a different order
(equivalent_named(lambda a, b: a and not b, ["a", "b"], lambda b, a: a and not b, ["b", "a"]), equivalent_named(lambda a, b: a and not b, ["a", "b"], lambda b, a: b and not a, ["b", "a"]))
=> (True, False)
?? Pass each formula the values of its own names, in its own order.
--- check case | No names in common
(equivalent_named(lambda p: p or not p, ["p"], lambda q: q or not q, ["q"]), equivalent_named(lambda p: p, ["p"], lambda q: q, ["q"]))
=> (True, False)
--- check case | A formula with no variables
(equivalent_named(lambda: True, [], lambda x, y: x or not x, ["x", "y"]), equivalent_named(lambda x: x, ["x"], lambda: False, []))
=> (True, False)
--- check case | Absorption with an extra variable on one side
equivalent_named(lambda p, q: p and (p or q), ["p", "q"], lambda r, p: p, ["r", "p"])
=> True

+++ practice | Debug: the window that lets everything through
--- task
`in_window(readings, lo, hi)` should return a new list of the readings `r` with `lo <= r <= hi`, in their original order. Someone started from `if not (r < lo or r > hi):` and rewrote it without the `not`, but now every reading gets through. For example, `in_window([5, 12, 30, 8], 6, 20)` should be `[12, 8]`, but it returns all four.

Find the bug and fix it. It is one small change.
--- starter
def in_window(readings, lo, hi):
    kept = []
    for r in readings:
        if r >= lo or r <= hi:
            kept.append(r)
    return kept
--- solution
def in_window(readings, lo, hi):
    kept = []
    for r in readings:
        if r >= lo and r <= hi:
            kept.append(r)
    return kept
--- hint
Apply De Morgan's law to `not (r < lo or r > hi)` yourself. What happens to the `or`?
--- hint
Pushing the `not` inside flips `or` into `and`, and each comparison into its negation: `r >= lo and r <= hi`.
--- check case | The example from the task
in_window([5, 12, 30, 8], 6, 20)
=> [12, 8]
--- check case | The ends of the window are inside it
in_window([6, 20, 5, 21], 6, 20)
=> [6, 20]
--- check case | Nothing inside, and an empty list
(in_window([1, 2, 99], 10, 20), in_window([], 0, 1))
=> ([], [])
--- check case | Negative readings
in_window([-5, -1, 0, 3], -2, 0)
=> [-1, 0]

+++ practice | Everything from NAND
--- task
The connective NAND ("not and") is true unless both inputs are true. It is enough to build every other connective, which is why chips can be made from NAND gates alone.

The starter gives you `nand(p, q)`, written without any of the words `and`, `or` or `not`. Keep it as it is. Write four more functions, each built **only** from calls to `nand` (and to each other):

- `not_(p)`, equivalent to `not p`;
- `and_(p, q)`, equivalent to `p and q`;
- `or_(p, q)`, equivalent to `p or q`;
- `implies_(p, q)`, equivalent to p → q.

Your code must not use the words `and`, `or` or `not` anywhere outside comments. For example, `and_(True, False)` must be `False`.
--- starter
def nand(p, q):
    return (p, q) != (True, True)


def not_(p):
    return p


def and_(p, q):
    return p


def or_(p, q):
    return p


def implies_(p, q):
    return q
--- solution
def nand(p, q):
    return (p, q) != (True, True)


def not_(p):
    return nand(p, p)


def and_(p, q):
    return not_(nand(p, q))


def or_(p, q):
    return nand(not_(p), not_(q))


def implies_(p, q):
    return nand(p, not_(q))
--- hint
`nand(p, p)` is true unless p and p are both true, which is `not p`. And `nand` is `not` of `and`, so `and` is `not_` of `nand`.
--- hint
For `or_`, De Morgan says `p or q` is `not (not p and not q)`, which is `nand(not p, not q)`. For p → q, the lesson showed it is `not (p and not q)`.
--- check test | not_ in both rows
[not_(p) for p in (False, True)] == [True, False]
--- check test | and_ in all four rows
[and_(p, q) for p in (False, True) for q in (False, True)] == [False, False, False, True]
--- check test | or_ in all four rows
[or_(p, q) for p in (False, True) for q in (False, True)] == [False, True, True, True]
--- check test | implies_ in all four rows
[implies_(p, q) for p in (False, True) for q in (False, True)] == [True, True, False, True]
--- check test | Every function really calls nand
(lambda g, box: (g.__setitem__("nand", (lambda old: lambda p, q: box.append(1) or old(p, q))(g["nand"])), [(box.clear(), fn(), len(box) > 0)[-1] for fn in (lambda: not_(True), lambda: and_(True, False), lambda: or_(False, True), lambda: implies_(True, True))])[-1] == [True, True, True, True])(globals(), [])
--- check source absent | No and, or or not outside comments
^[^#\n]*\b(and|or|not)\b
?? Build everything from `nand`: for example `not_(p)` is `nand(p, p)`.

+++ question | Negating a condition
--- ask
A safety check fires when this is false: "the valve is open and the pump is on". Which statement is its negation?
--- choice
The valve is closed and the pump is off.
--- choice correct
The valve is closed or the pump is off.
--- choice
The valve is open or the pump is on.
--- choice
If the valve is open, the pump is on.
--- why
By De Morgan's law, ¬(p ∧ q) ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q: "not both" means "at least one fails". The first choice is the classic wrong negation: it covers only the case where both fail.

+++ question | Which one says the same thing
--- ask
Which statement is logically equivalent to "if the engine fires, the valve is open"?
--- choice
If the valve is open, the engine fires.
--- choice
If the engine does not fire, the valve is not open.
--- choice correct
If the valve is not open, the engine does not fire.
--- choice
The engine fires and the valve is open.
--- why
The contrapositive ¬q → ¬p is equivalent to p → q. The first choice is the converse and the second is the inverse; those two are equivalent to each other, but not to the original.
=== disc-03 | Predicates and quantifiers
--- teach
In lesson 1, "x + 1 = 3" was not a proposition, because its truth depends on x. Mathematics is full of sentences like that: "n is prime", "every reading is below the limit", "for every input there is an output". This lesson gives them a precise form. It also shows how to check them in Python whenever the values come from a finite list.

### Sentences with a gap

A **predicate** is a sentence with one or more variables that becomes a proposition as soon as you choose values for them. Write P(x) for "x > 3". Then P(5) is a true proposition, and P(2) is a false one. P(x) on its own is neither.

The set of values the variable may take is the **domain** (also called the universe of discourse). It is part of the meaning: "x has a square root" is true for every x when the domain is the non-negative numbers, and false for some x when it is all the integers.

A predicate can have several variables. Q(x, y) could be "x + y = 10". Then Q(3, 7) is true and Q(3, 3) is false.

In Python, a predicate is a function that returns a boolean:

```python
def over_limit(temp):
    return temp > 100

print(over_limit(120), over_limit(80))   # True False
```

### For all, there exists

There are two ways to turn a predicate into a proposition without choosing a value. They are called **quantifiers**, because they say *how many* values make it true.

- The **universal quantifier**: ∀x P(x), read "for all x, P(x)". It is true when P(a) is true for **every** a in the domain.
- The **existential quantifier**: ∃x P(x), read "there exists an x such that P(x)". It is true when P(a) is true for **at least one** a in the domain.

The symbols are an upside-down A (for "all") and a backwards E (for "exists").

Once x is quantified it is no longer a gap. ∀x (x > 3) does not depend on any particular x; it is simply true or false (false over the integers, since 0 > 3 is false). The x is now a **bound variable**, like the loop variable in `for x in ...`: its name does not matter outside.

When the domain is a finite list a₁, a₂, …, aₙ, the quantifiers are long versions of ∧ and ∨:

- ∀x P(x) means P(a₁) ∧ P(a₂) ∧ … ∧ P(aₙ).
- ∃x P(x) means P(a₁) ∨ P(a₂) ∨ … ∨ P(aₙ).

### all and any

Python has two built-in functions for exactly this. `all(items)` is `True` when every item is true, and `any(items)` is `True` when at least one item is:

```python
temps = [21, 25, 19, 23]
print(all([t < 30 for t in temps]))   # True: every one is below 30
print(any([t > 24 for t in temps]))   # True: 25 is above 24
```

You can leave out the square brackets. Then Python works out the items one at a time and stops as soon as the answer is settled: `all` stops at the first false item, and `any` at the first true one.

```python
readings = [3, -1, 4, 1, 5]
print(all(r > 0 for r in readings))   # False, and it never looked past -1
```

### The empty domain

What is "every astronaut on Mars wears a red suit" when there are no astronauts on Mars? The long ∧ has no parts, and nothing can make it false, so ∀ over an empty domain is **true** (vacuously, as in lesson 1). ∃ over an empty domain is **false**: there is nothing to be the example. Python [[agrees|empty-all]]:

```python
print(all([]), any([]))   # True False
```

### Witnesses and counterexamples

To prove ∃x P(x), one value a with P(a) true is enough. That value is a **witness**.

To disprove ∀x P(x), one value a with P(a) false is enough. That value is a **counterexample**.

The other two jobs are harder. Proving ∀x P(x) needs every value. Disproving ∃x P(x) needs every value too. When the domain is infinite, such as all integers, no amount of checking can finish those jobs. A computer search can find a witness or a counterexample, but it cannot prove a "for all" over infinitely many values. That takes a proof, which is what lessons 4 to 7 are about.

### Negating a quantifier

What is the opposite of "every sensor reads below 50"? It is **not** "every sensor reads 50 or more". It is "**some** sensor reads 50 or more". The rules are:

- ¬∀x P(x) ≡ ∃x ¬P(x): "not all" is "at least one fails".
- ¬∃x P(x) ≡ ∀x ¬P(x): "none" is "every one fails".

For a finite domain these are De Morgan's laws applied to the long ∧ and ∨: ¬(P(a₁) ∧ … ∧ P(aₙ)) ≡ ¬P(a₁) ∨ … ∨ ¬P(aₙ). For an infinite domain they follow from the meanings: "it is not true that every x works" says exactly that some x does not work.

So negation flips the quantifier **and** negates the predicate. Both changes are needed.

### Two quantifiers

With a predicate of two variables you can stack quantifiers. The order matters when they are of different kinds.

- ∀x ∃y Q(x, y): "for every x there is some y", and the y may be different for each x.
- ∃y ∀x Q(x, y): "there is one y that works for every x at once".

Over the integers, ∀x ∃y (y > x) is true: for any x, take y = x + 1. But ∃y ∀x (y > x) is false: it claims there is a largest integer. The [[grid picture|grid]] shows the difference.

In Python, nest `all` and `any` in the same order as the quantifiers, outer one first:

```python
digits = range(10)
print(all(any(x + y == 9 for y in digits) for x in digits))   # True
print(any(all(x + y == 9 for x in digits) for y in digits))   # False
```

The first line says every digit x has a partner y that makes 9 (y = 9 − x). The second says one single y makes 9 with every x, which is false.

Two quantifiers of the same kind can swap: ∀x ∀y means the same as ∀y ∀x, and ∃x ∃y the same as ∃y ∃x. And ∃y ∀x Q always implies ∀x ∃y Q (the one y that works for all x is a partner for each), but not the other way round.

### Negating nested quantifiers

Move the ¬ inward one quantifier at a time, flipping each one as it passes:

¬∀x ∃y Q(x, y) ≡ ∃x ¬∃y Q(x, y) ≡ ∃x ∀y ¬Q(x, y).

In words: "not every x has a partner" means "some x has no partner at all". That x is the witness for the negation. It is exactly what you would report when a claim like "every request gets a free slot" fails.

### "Every P is Q" and "some P is Q"

Most real claims are about part of a domain: "every prime above 2 is odd", "some reading from station B is too high". These are **restricted quantifiers**, and each has one correct translation:

- "Every P is Q" is ∀x (P(x) → Q(x)).
- "Some P is Q" is ∃x (P(x) ∧ Q(x)).

The wrong translations are common. ∀x (P(x) ∧ Q(x)) says that *everything* is P and Q. ∃x (P(x) → Q(x)) is true as soon as one thing is not P, because a conditional with a false hypothesis is true.

Negating a restricted "every" uses last lesson's rule for a negated conditional: ¬∀x (P(x) → Q(x)) ≡ ∃x ¬(P(x) → Q(x)) ≡ ∃x (P(x) ∧ ¬Q(x)). "Not every P is Q" means "some P is not Q".

In Python, the `if` part of a comprehension does the restricting:

```python
data = [4, 7, 12, 3, 8]
print(all(n < 10 for n in data if n % 2 == 0))   # False: 12 is even and not below 10
print(any(n > 5 for n in data if n % 2 == 1))    # True: 7 is odd and above 5
```

### Exactly one

"There is exactly one x such that P(x)" is written ∃!x P(x). Spelled out with the two basic quantifiers, it is ∃x (P(x) ∧ ∀y (P(y) → y = x)): some x works, and anything that works is that same x.

### Why this is right

Over a finite domain, the quantifiers are the long ∧ and ∨, so everything follows from lessons 1 and 2. The negation rules are De Morgan's laws. The nested versions apply the same rule once per quantifier, because the inner part ∃y Q(x, y) is itself just a predicate of x. And `all` and `any` compute exactly the long ∧ and ∨, including the empty cases.

### What it costs

Checking ∀x P(x) over a domain of N values costs up to N evaluations of P. Stopping at the first counterexample can make it much less, but in the worst case (when the claim is true) every value is checked. Two nested quantifiers over domains of sizes N and M cost up to N × M evaluations, and k nested quantifiers over one domain of size N cost up to Nᵏ. This is why a claim with many quantifiers gets expensive to check fast.

### Where it is used

- **Specifications** are full of quantifiers. "The output is sorted" is ∀i (out[i] ≤ out[i + 1]) over the valid positions i.
- The definition of **big-O** from DSA I is [[a stack of quantifiers|big-o]].
- **SQL** has `EXISTS` and `NOT EXISTS`, and a "for all" query is written as "not exists … not" using the negation rule.
- **Property-based testing** tools check a "for all" claim on hundreds of random inputs and report a counterexample.
- **Coverage questions** in mission planning: "at every minute some ground station sees the satellite" (∀ minute ∃ station) is a weaker demand than "some station sees it at every minute" (∃ station ∀ minute).

**Watch out:** four ways to get quantifiers wrong.

- Swapping ∀x ∃y and ∃y ∀x. They say different things.
- Translating "every P is Q" with ∧, or "some P is Q" with →.
- Negating only half: the negation of ∀x P(x) is ∃x ¬P(x), with the quantifier flipped **and** the predicate negated.
- In code, returning `True` inside the loop as soon as one item passes. That checks ∃ for the first item, not ∀ for all of them. Only a failing item may end the loop early.

::: context empty-all Why all([]) is True
Think of `all` as starting from `True` and switching to `False` at the first item that fails; with no items, nothing ever fails. `any` starts from `False` and switches at the first item that passes. This is the same reason that a sum of no numbers is 0 and a product of no numbers is 1: each starts from the value that changes nothing. Mathematicians made the same choice for ∀ and ∃ long before Python, and it makes rules like ¬∀x P(x) ≡ ∃x ¬P(x) hold even for an empty domain.
:::

::: context grid Rows, columns and ticks
Draw a grid with one row for each x and one column for each y, and tick the square where Q(x, y) is true. ∀x ∃y Q says every row has at least one tick. ∃y ∀x Q says some column is ticked all the way down. Here every row has a tick, but no column is full.
