:::

::: context big-o Big-O is a quantifier sentence
In DSA I, f(n) is O(g(n)) when there are constants c > 0 and n₀ such that f(n) ≤ c · g(n) for every n ≥ n₀. In symbols: ∃c ∃n₀ ∀n (n ≥ n₀ → f(n) ≤ c · g(n)). The order matters: c and n₀ are chosen first, once, and must then work for all large n. Negating it with this lesson's rules gives what you must show to prove f is not O(g): for every c and n₀, some n ≥ n₀ has f(n) > c · g(n). Limits in calculus have the same shape: for every ε > 0 there exists a δ > 0 such that for all x, and so on.
:::
--- task
A predicate `p(x, y)` of two variables is given as a Python function. `xs` is the domain for x and `ys` the domain for y; both are lists. Write three functions.

- `forall_exists(xs, ys, p)`: return the truth value of ∀x ∃y p(x, y): every x in `xs` has at least one y in `ys` with `p(x, y)` true.
- `exists_forall(xs, ys, p)`: return the truth value of ∃y ∀x p(x, y): some single y in `ys` has `p(x, y)` true for every x in `xs`.
- `no_partner(xs, ys, p)`: the witness for the negation of ∀x ∃y. Return the first x in `xs` for which **no** y in `ys` makes `p(x, y)` true, or `None` if every x has one.

Each must stop as soon as its answer is settled: a check counts the calls to `p`. Mind the empty lists; follow the definitions exactly. For example, `forall_exists([1, 2, 3], [1, 2, 3], lambda x, y: x == y)` is `True`, `exists_forall` of the same is `False`, and `no_partner([0, 5, 9], [1, 6], lambda x, y: y > x)` is `9`.
--- starter
def forall_exists(xs, ys, p):
    return None


def exists_forall(xs, ys, p):
    return None


def no_partner(xs, ys, p):
    return None
--- solution
def forall_exists(xs, ys, p):
    return all(any(p(x, y) for y in ys) for x in xs)


def exists_forall(xs, ys, p):
    return any(all(p(x, y) for x in xs) for y in ys)


def no_partner(xs, ys, p):
    for x in xs:
        if not any(p(x, y) for y in ys):
            return x
    return None
--- hint
Write the quantifiers from the outside in. In ∀x ∃y the outer one is `all` over x, and inside it is `any` over y. In ∃y ∀x the outer one is `any` over y.
--- hint
Leave out the square brackets inside `all` and `any`, so they stop early. For `no_partner`, loop over `xs` and return the first x where `any(p(x, y) for y in ys)` is false.
--- hint
Empty lists: `all` of nothing is `True` and `any` of nothing is `False`. So with no y at all, no x has a partner, and ∃y ∀x is false even when `xs` is empty.
--- check case | Every x has an equal partner, but no single y equals all of them
(forall_exists([1, 2, 3], [1, 2, 3], lambda x, y: x == y), exists_forall([1, 2, 3], [1, 2, 3], lambda x, y: x == y))
=> (True, False)
--- check case | Bigger partners: 4 has none among 0 to 4
(forall_exists(list(range(5)), list(range(5)), lambda x, y: y > x), no_partner(list(range(5)), list(range(5)), lambda x, y: y > x))
=> (False, 4)
--- check case | With one more y, a single y beats them all
(forall_exists(list(range(5)), list(range(6)), lambda x, y: y > x), exists_forall(list(range(5)), list(range(6)), lambda x, y: y > x), no_partner(list(range(5)), list(range(6)), lambda x, y: y > x))
=> (True, True, None)
--- check case | Opposites: every x has one, no y is everyone's
(forall_exists(list(range(-3, 4)), list(range(-3, 4)), lambda x, y: x + y == 0), exists_forall(list(range(-3, 4)), list(range(-3, 4)), lambda x, y: x + y == 0))
=> (True, False)
--- check case | The y in ∃y ∀x is the outer variable
(exists_forall([1, 2], [1, 2, 3], lambda x, y: x <= 1), exists_forall([2, 4, 6], [3, 5, 12, 7], lambda x, y: y % x == 0))
=> (False, True)
?? ∃y ∀x asks for one y that works with every x. Check `x <= 1` for x = 2.
--- check case | The example for no_partner, and the first failure wins
(no_partner([0, 5, 9], [1, 6], lambda x, y: y > x), no_partner(["a", "b", "c"], [1], lambda x, y: x == "b"))
=> (9, "a")
--- check case | An empty domain for x
(forall_exists([], [1, 2], lambda x, y: False), exists_forall([], [1, 2], lambda x, y: False), no_partner([], [1], lambda x, y: False))
=> (True, True, None)
--- check case | An empty domain for y
(forall_exists([1, 2], [], lambda x, y: True), exists_forall([1, 2], [], lambda x, y: True), exists_forall([], [], lambda x, y: True), no_partner([1, 2], [], lambda x, y: True))
=> (False, False, False, 1)
?? With no y at all, there is nothing for ∃y to point to.
--- check test | forall_exists stops at the first x with no partner
(lambda box: forall_exists(list(range(1000)), list(range(1000)), lambda x, y: box.append(1) or (x > 0 and y > x)) is False and len(box) <= 1000)([])
?? x = 0 has no partner, so the answer is settled after 1000 calls. Leave out the square brackets so `all` and `any` stop early.
--- check test | exists_forall stops at the first y that works for all
(lambda box: exists_forall(list(range(1000)), list(range(1000)), lambda x, y: box.append(1) or y == 0) is True and len(box) <= 1000)([])
--- check test | no_partner stops as soon as it finds its x
(lambda box: no_partner(list(range(1000)), list(range(1000)), lambda x, y: box.append(1) or x > 0) == 0 and len(box) <= 1000)([])

+++ practice | Claims about a star catalogue
--- task
A star catalogue is a list of tuples `(name, magnitude, distance)`. A smaller magnitude means a brighter star, and `distance` is in light years. Write three functions.

- `all_brighter_than(stars, limit)`: is every star's magnitude less than or equal to `limit`?
- `any_within(stars, d)`: is some star's distance less than `d`?
- `none_named(stars, letter)`: does no star's name start with `letter`?

Each returns `True` or `False`. Think about what each should say for an empty catalogue before you run it. For example, with `stars = [("Sirius", -1.46, 8.6), ("Vega", 0.03, 25.0)]`, `all_brighter_than(stars, 0.5)` is `True` and `any_within(stars, 5)` is `False`.
--- starter
def all_brighter_than(stars, limit):
    return False


def any_within(stars, d):
    return True


def none_named(stars, letter):
    return False
--- solution
def all_brighter_than(stars, limit):
    return all(mag <= limit for name, mag, dist in stars)


def any_within(stars, d):
    return any(dist < d for name, mag, dist in stars)


def none_named(stars, letter):
    return not any(name.startswith(letter) for name, mag, dist in stars)
--- hint
Each is one `all` or `any` over the list, with the tuple unpacked in the loop: `for name, mag, dist in stars`.
--- hint
"No star is named with that letter" is ¬∃, which is `not any(...)`, or equally ∀¬, which is `all(not ...)`.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda stars: (all_brighter_than(stars, 0.5), any_within(stars, 5), none_named(stars, "V")))([("Sirius", -1.46, 8.6), ("Vega", 0.03, 25.0)])
=> (True, False, False)
--- check case | Limits are inclusive for brightness, strict for distance
(lambda stars: (all_brighter_than(stars, 0.03), all_brighter_than(stars, 0.0), any_within(stars, 8.6), any_within(stars, 8.7)))([("Sirius", -1.46, 8.6), ("Vega", 0.03, 25.0)])
=> (True, False, False, True)
--- check case | A longer catalogue
(lambda stars: (all_brighter_than(stars, 1.0), any_within(stars, 5.0), none_named(stars, "B"), none_named(stars, "Z")))([("Proxima Centauri", 11.13, 4.24), ("Betelgeuse", 0.5, 548.0), ("Rigel", 0.13, 863.0), ("Altair", 0.76, 16.7)])
=> (False, True, False, True)
--- check case | The empty catalogue
(all_brighter_than([], -5), any_within([], 1000), none_named([], "S"))
=> (True, False, True)
?? Every star in an empty catalogue passes (there is none to fail), no star is close, and no star has the name.

+++ practice | Every P is Q, some P is Q
--- task
Write two functions for restricted quantifiers. `domain` is a list, and `cond` and `prop` are predicates of one variable.

- `all_that(domain, cond, prop)`: "every x that satisfies `cond` satisfies `prop`", which is ∀x (cond(x) → prop(x)).
- `some_that(domain, cond, prop)`: "some x that satisfies `cond` satisfies `prop`", which is ∃x (cond(x) ∧ prop(x)).

Both return `True` or `False`. For example, over the numbers 1 to 19, "every prime above 2 is odd" is `True`, and "some prime above 2 is even" is `False`. If no x satisfies `cond`, "every" is vacuously `True` and "some" is `False`.
--- starter
def all_that(domain, cond, prop):
    return all(cond(x) and prop(x) for x in domain)


def some_that(domain, cond, prop):
    return any((not cond(x)) or prop(x) for x in domain)
--- solution
def all_that(domain, cond, prop):
    return all(prop(x) for x in domain if cond(x))


def some_that(domain, cond, prop):
    return any(prop(x) for x in domain if cond(x))
--- hint
The starter uses the two classic wrong translations: ∧ for "every" and → for "some". Swap them round.
--- hint
The `if` part of a comprehension restricts the domain: `all(prop(x) for x in domain if cond(x))` looks only at the x that satisfy `cond`.
--- check case | Every prime above 2 is odd
all_that(list(range(1, 20)), lambda n: n > 2 and all(n % d for d in range(2, n)), lambda n: n % 2 == 1)
=> True
?? "Every P is Q" is P → Q for each x. With ∧, the number 1 would already make it false.
--- check case | No prime above 2 is even
some_that(list(range(1, 20)), lambda n: n > 2 and all(n % d for d in range(2, n)), lambda n: n % 2 == 0)
=> False
?? "Some P is Q" needs one x that is P **and** Q. With →, any x that is not P makes it true.
--- check case | A counterexample makes "every" false
all_that([4, 7, 12, 3, 8], lambda n: n % 2 == 0, lambda n: n < 10)
=> False
--- check case | A witness makes "some" true
some_that([4, 7, 12, 3, 8], lambda n: n % 2 == 1, lambda n: n > 5)
=> True
--- check case | Nothing satisfies the condition
(all_that([1, 2, 3], lambda n: n > 100, lambda n: False), some_that([1, 2, 3], lambda n: n > 100, lambda n: True))
=> (True, False)
--- check case | An empty domain
(all_that([], lambda n: True, lambda n: False), some_that([], lambda n: True, lambda n: True))
=> (True, False)

+++ practice | Exactly one
--- task
Write `unique_witness(domain, pred)`. `domain` is a list of distinct values and `pred` is a predicate of one variable. If ∃!x pred(x) holds (exactly one value in the domain makes `pred` true), return that value. Otherwise, whether no value or two or more values make it true, return `None`.

For example, `unique_witness([1, 2, 3, 4], lambda n: n * n == 9)` is `3`, and `unique_witness([1, 2, 3, 4], lambda n: n % 2 == 0)` is `None`, because both 2 and 4 work. Call `pred` at most once on each value.
--- starter
def unique_witness(domain, pred):
    for x in domain:
        if pred(x):
            return x
    return None
--- solution
def unique_witness(domain, pred):
    found = [x for x in domain if pred(x)]
    if len(found) == 1:
        return found[0]
    return None
--- hint
The starter proves ∃ but not the "only one" part. It needs to know whether a second value works too.
--- hint
Collect every value that satisfies `pred` in a list, then look at its length.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(unique_witness([1, 2, 3, 4], lambda n: n * n == 9), unique_witness([1, 2, 3, 4], lambda n: n % 2 == 0))
=> (3, None)
--- check case | No value works
unique_witness([1, 2, 3], lambda n: n > 10)
=> None
--- check case | The witness is last, or the only value
(unique_witness(["lox", "rp1", "ch4"], lambda s: s.startswith("c")), unique_witness([7], lambda n: True))
=> ("ch4", 7)
--- check case | An empty domain has no witness
unique_witness([], lambda n: True)
=> None
--- check test | pred is called at most once per value
(lambda box: unique_witness(list(range(100)), lambda n: box.append(n) or n == 42) == 42 and len(box) <= 100)([])

+++ practice | Four ways to stack two quantifiers
--- task
Write `holds(pattern, xs, ys, p)`. `p(x, y)` is a predicate, `xs` is the domain of x and `ys` of y. `pattern` has two letters: the first is x's quantifier (the outer one) and the second is y's (the inner one), with `"A"` for ∀ and `"E"` for ∃. So `"AE"` means ∀x ∃y p(x, y), and `"EA"` means ∃x ∀y p(x, y).

Return `True` or `False`. For any other pattern, raise `ValueError`. The edges matter here: work out what each pattern means when `xs` or `ys` is empty, straight from the definitions, before you code.

For example, `holds("AE", [1, 2], [2, 3], lambda x, y: y > x)` is `True`, and `holds("EA", [1, 2], [2, 3], lambda x, y: y > x)` is `True` too (x = 1 is below both y values).
--- starter
def holds(pattern, xs, ys, p):
    return any(any(p(x, y) for y in ys) for x in xs)
--- solution
def holds(pattern, xs, ys, p):
    quantifier = {"A": all, "E": any}
    if len(pattern) != 2 or pattern[0] not in quantifier or pattern[1] not in quantifier:
        raise ValueError("pattern must be two letters, each A or E")
    outer = quantifier[pattern[0]]
    inner = quantifier[pattern[1]]
    return outer(inner(p(x, y) for y in ys) for x in xs)
--- hint
`all` is ∀ and `any` is ∃. Pick the outer function from the first letter and the inner one from the second, then nest them with x outside.
--- hint
A dict from letter to function, `{"A": all, "E": any}`, avoids four separate branches. Check the pattern first and raise `ValueError` if it is not two of those letters.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(holds("AE", [1, 2], [2, 3], lambda x, y: y > x), holds("EA", [1, 2], [2, 3], lambda x, y: y > x))
=> (True, True)
--- check case | All four patterns on one predicate
[holds(pat, [1, 2, 3], [1, 2, 3], lambda x, y: x <= y) for pat in ["AA", "AE", "EA", "EE"]]
=> [False, True, True, True]
--- check case | Empty xs
[holds(pat, [], [1, 2], lambda x, y: False) for pat in ["AA", "AE", "EA", "EE"]]
=> [True, True, False, False]
?? With no x, ∀x is vacuously true and ∃x is false, whatever the inside says.
--- check case | Empty ys
[holds(pat, [1, 2], [], lambda x, y: False) for pat in ["AA", "AE", "EA", "EE"]]
=> [True, False, True, False]
?? With no y, ∀y is true for each x, and ∃y is false for each x.
--- check test | A bad pattern raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: holds("AX", [1], [1], lambda x, y: True)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: holds("A", [1], [1], lambda x, y: True)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: holds("AEA", [1], [1], lambda x, y: True))

+++ practice | Debug: the check that trusts the first reading
--- task
`all_nominal(readings, lo, hi)` should return `True` when every reading `r` has `lo <= r <= hi`, and `False` otherwise. An empty list is nominal. But `all_nominal([10, 99, 12], 0, 50)` returns `True`, although 99 is out of range.

Find the bug and fix it. It is a small change inside the loop.
--- starter
def all_nominal(readings, lo, hi):
    for r in readings:
        if lo <= r <= hi:
            return True
        else:
            return False
    return True
--- solution
def all_nominal(readings, lo, hi):
    for r in readings:
        if not (lo <= r <= hi):
            return False
    return True
--- hint
Follow the loop by hand on `[10, 99, 12]`. How many readings does it look at before it returns?
--- hint
For a "for all" claim, only a failing reading can end the loop early. A passing reading must let the loop go on to the next one.
--- check case | The example from the task
all_nominal([10, 99, 12], 0, 50)
=> False
?? The loop returns on the very first reading. Only return early when a reading fails.
--- check case | A bad reading at the end
all_nominal([1, 2, 3, 4, 60], 0, 50)
=> False
--- check case | All in range, including the ends
all_nominal([0, 50, 25], 0, 50)
=> True
--- check case | An empty list is nominal
all_nominal([], 0, 50)
=> True
--- check case | Bad first reading
all_nominal([-1, 10], 0, 50)
=> False

+++ practice | Relay satellites
--- task
Ground stations talk to each other through relay satellites. `sees` is a dict from each station's name to the set of satellite names it can see, and `stations` is the list of station names, in order. Two stations can talk when **some** satellite is seen by **both**.

Write two functions.

- `unreachable_pair(stations, sees)`: the network is fully connected when ∀a ∀b (a ≠ b → ∃s (s seen by a ∧ s seen by b)). Return the first pair `(a, b)` that breaks this, with `a` before `b` in `stations`, ordered by `a` first and then by `b`. Return `None` if every pair can talk. With fewer than two stations there are no pairs.
- `common_relays(stations, sees)`: the sorted list of satellites that every station can see (∀a: s seen by a). If there are no stations, return `[]`.

For example, with `stations = ["A", "B", "C"]` and `sees = {"A": {"s1", "s2"}, "B": {"s2"}, "C": {"s1"}}`, `unreachable_pair` gives `("B", "C")`, and `common_relays` gives `[]`.
--- starter
def unreachable_pair(stations, sees):
    return None


def common_relays(stations, sees):
    return []
--- solution
def unreachable_pair(stations, sees):
    for i in range(len(stations)):
        for j in range(i + 1, len(stations)):
            a = stations[i]
            b = stations[j]
            if not any(s in sees[b] for s in sees[a]):
                return (a, b)
    return None


def common_relays(stations, sees):
    if not stations:
        return []
    candidates = {s for a in stations for s in sees[a]}
    return sorted(s for s in candidates if all(s in sees[a] for a in stations))
--- hint
For the pairs, loop `i` over the positions and `j` over the positions after `i`. The pair breaks the rule when `any(s in sees[b] for s in sees[a])` is false: that is the negation ∃a ∃b ¬∃s.
--- hint
For `common_relays`, the only candidates are satellites some station sees. Keep a candidate `s` when `all(s in sees[a] for a in stations)`.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda st, sees: (unreachable_pair(st, sees), common_relays(st, sees)))(["A", "B", "C"], {"A": {"s1", "s2"}, "B": {"s2"}, "C": {"s1"}})
=> (("B", "C"), [])
--- check case | Fully connected through one common relay
(lambda st, sees: (unreachable_pair(st, sees), common_relays(st, sees)))(["Kiruna", "Perth", "Goldstone"], {"Kiruna": {"r1", "r3"}, "Perth": {"r3", "r2"}, "Goldstone": {"r3"}})
=> (None, ["r3"])
--- check case | Connected pair by pair, with no common relay
(lambda st, sees: (unreachable_pair(st, sees), common_relays(st, sees)))(["A", "B", "C"], {"A": {"x", "y"}, "B": {"y", "z"}, "C": {"z", "x"}})
=> (None, [])
?? Every pair shares a satellite (∀∃ holds), but no one satellite is shared by all (∃∀ fails).
--- check case | The first broken pair, by a then b
unreachable_pair(["A", "B", "C", "D"], {"A": {"1"}, "B": {"1"}, "C": {"2"}, "D": {"1", "2"}})
=> ("A", "C")
--- check case | A station that sees nothing
unreachable_pair(["A", "B"], {"A": {"1", "2"}, "B": set()})
=> ("A", "B")
--- check case | One station, and none
(unreachable_pair(["A"], {"A": {"1"}}), common_relays(["A"], {"A": {"2", "1"}}), unreachable_pair([], {}), common_relays([], {}))
=> (None, ["1", "2"], None, [])

+++ question | Negating "every"
--- ask
What is the negation of "every reading in the log is below 50"?
--- choice
Every reading in the log is 50 or more.
--- choice correct
Some reading in the log is 50 or more.
--- choice
Some reading in the log is below 50.
--- choice
No reading in the log is below 50.
--- why
¬∀x P(x) ≡ ∃x ¬P(x): the quantifier flips to "some" and the predicate is negated, so "below 50" becomes "50 or more". The first choice flips only the predicate, and the third flips only the quantifier.

+++ question | Which order is true
--- ask
The domain is all integers. Which of these is true?
--- choice correct
∀x ∃y (y > x), but not ∃y ∀x (y > x).
--- choice
∃y ∀x (y > x), but not ∀x ∃y (y > x).
--- choice
Both are true, because they use the same predicate.
--- choice
Neither is true.
--- why
For each x, y = x + 1 is bigger, so ∀x ∃y holds. But ∃y ∀x would need one integer bigger than every integer, including itself, and there is none. The order of different quantifiers changes the meaning.
=== disc-04 | Direct proof and proof by contrapositive
--- teach
The last three lessons gave you the language: propositions, connectives, equivalences and quantifiers. Now you use it to prove things. A proof is how you know a claim holds for **every** case, including the infinitely many you will never test. This lesson covers the two most common ways to prove "if P, then Q": directly, and through the contrapositive.

### What a proof is

A **theorem** is a statement that has been proved. A **proof** is a finite list of statements that ends with the theorem, where each statement is one of these:

- an assumption the theorem allows you (its hypothesis);
- a definition, or a fact already proved (including ordinary algebra: the sum and the product of two integers are integers, multiplication distributes over addition, and so on);
- something that follows from earlier statements by a rule of logic.

A **lemma** is a small theorem proved to help with a bigger one, and a **corollary** is a theorem that follows quickly from another. The end of a proof is marked with ∎, or the letters [[Q.E.D.|qed]].

### Definitions you can compute with

A proof can only use what a word exactly means, so definitions come first. All letters here stand for **integers**: whole numbers, positive, negative or zero.

- n is **even** if n = 2k for some integer k.
- n is **odd** if n = 2k + 1 for some integer k.
- d **divides** n, written d | n, if n = d · k for some integer k. Then n is a **multiple** of d, and d is a **divisor** of n.

So 3 | 12 because 12 = 3 · 4, and 3 does not divide 13. When d is not 0 and d | n, the k in the definition is exactly `n // d` in Python, negative numbers included: −12 = 3 · (−4), and `-12 // 3` is `-4`. Careful: the bar is not a fraction. "3 | 12" is a statement that is true or false; 12/3 is a number. Also note what the definitions say about [[zero|zero]].

Every integer is either even or odd, and never both. **Either:** dividing n by 2 gives n = 2q + r with a remainder r of 0 or 1, and in Python that is `q = n // 2` and `r = n % 2`, which work for negative n too: −7 = 2 · (−4) + 1. **Never both:** if n = 2k and also n = 2j + 1, then 2(k − j) = 1, but 2 times an integer is 0, ±2, ±4 and so on, never 1.

### Rules you use without noticing

- **Modus ponens:** from p and p → q, conclude q.
- **Using a "for all":** from ∀x P(x), conclude P(c) for any particular c.
- **Proving a "for all":** if you prove P(c) for an **[[arbitrary|arbitrary]]** c, one you assumed nothing about except that it is in the domain, you have proved ∀x P(x).
- **Proving an "exists":** show one witness.

### Direct proof

Most theorems have the shape ∀n (P(n) → Q(n)). A **direct proof** goes: "Let n be an arbitrary integer, and assume P(n). … Therefore Q(n)."

Why that is enough: P(n) → Q(n) is false only when P(n) is true and Q(n) is false. So the only rows you need to deal with are those where P(n) is true, and in those you show Q(n) is true.

**Theorem.** If n is odd, then n² is odd.

**Proof.** Let n be an arbitrary odd integer. By the definition of odd, n = 2k + 1 for some integer k. Then

n² = (2k + 1)² = 4k² + 4k + 1 = 2(2k² + 2k) + 1.

Let m = 2k² + 2k. Since k is an integer, so is m (sums and products of integers are integers). So n² = 2m + 1, which is odd by definition. ∎

Look at the shape. The hypothesis is unpacked with its definition, then algebra, then the conclusion is packed back up with **its** definition. Most direct proofs look like this.

### A proof builds something

The proof above does more than say "n² is odd". It says exactly which m makes n² = 2m + 1: m = 2k² + 2k, where k = (n − 1)/2. That is a recipe you can run:

```python
def square_witness(n):          # n must be odd: n = 2k + 1
    k = (n - 1) // 2
    return 2 * k * k + 2 * k

for n in [1, 3, 7, -5]:
    m = square_witness(n)
    print(n, n * n, m, n * n == 2 * m + 1)
# 1 1 0 True
# 3 9 4 True
# 7 49 24 True
# -5 25 12 True
```

Running a proof's recipe is a good way to catch algebra slips, because a wrong formula shows up at once. But the loop checks four numbers. The proof is what covers every odd integer.

### Divisibility, and a new letter for each unknown

**Theorem.** If a | b and b | c, then a | c.

**Proof.** Assume a | b and b | c. Since a | b, there is an integer k with b = a · k. Since b | c, there is an integer l with c = b · l. Then

c = b · l = (a · k) · l = a · (k · l),

and k · l is an integer. So a | c by the definition of divides. ∎

Notice that the second multiplier got a **new letter**, l. The two facts each promise *some* integer, and nothing says they are the same one. Writing c = b · k would secretly add the assumption that both multipliers are equal, and the proof would then only cover that special case. This is the most common mistake in beginners' proofs.

### Proof by contrapositive

Lesson 2 showed that p → q ≡ ¬q → ¬p. So to prove "if P(n), then Q(n)" you may instead prove "if not Q(n), then not P(n)". That is a **proof by contrapositive**: assume the conclusion is false, and show the hypothesis is false.

It helps when the negated conclusion gives you something concrete to compute with.

**Theorem.** If n² is even, then n is even.

A direct attempt is stuck: from n² = 2k, what is n? A square root does not obviously give an integer formula.

**Proof, by contrapositive.** Suppose n is not even. Then n is odd, since every integer is one or the other. By the theorem above, n² is odd, so n² is not even. That proves "if n is not even, then n² is not even", which is the contrapositive of the theorem. ∎

A second example uses De Morgan's law from lesson 2.

**Theorem.** For integers a and b: if a + b ≥ 15, then a ≥ 8 or b ≥ 8.

**Proof, by contrapositive.** The negation of "a ≥ 8 or b ≥ 8" is "a < 8 and b < 8". So suppose a < 8 and b < 8. Since they are integers, a ≤ 7 and b ≤ 7, so a + b ≤ 14. Then a + b ≥ 15 is false. ∎

### Two degenerate cases

- A **vacuous proof**: if the hypothesis can never be true, the conditional holds with nothing more to show. "If n is an integer with n² < 0, then n = 42" is true.
- A **trivial proof**: if the conclusion is always true, the hypothesis does not matter. "If n is odd, then n² ≥ 0" is true because every square is at least 0.

### Examples are not proofs

The claim "n² + n + 41 is prime" holds for n = 0, 1, 2, …, 39, forty cases in a row, and fails at n = 40. No number of examples proves a claim about infinitely many integers. Examples are still useful: they suggest what to prove, they check your algebra, and a single failing example (a counterexample, lesson 6) disproves a claim for good.

### What a proof buys you

Checking a claim for the first N values costs N checks and still says nothing about value N + 1. A proof is a fixed number of lines, and it covers every value at once. The witnesses a direct proof builds are also cheap: m = 2k² + 2k takes a handful of arithmetic operations, however big n is.

### Why these methods are valid

- **Direct proof:** a conditional can only fail where its hypothesis is true, so showing the conclusion in exactly those cases proves it. Starting from an arbitrary n turns that into a proof for all n.
- **Contrapositive:** ¬Q → ¬P has the same truth table as P → Q, so proving one proves the other.

### Where it is used

- Every correctness argument in DSA I, such as a loop invariant, is a direct proof: assume the invariant before a pass, show it after.
- Compilers use small theorems like these to replace slow code with fast code that is proved to give the same answer.
- The [[parity bit|parity-bit]] on telemetry frames works because of a proof about even and odd.
- Divisibility is the foundation of the number theory lessons, and of RSA encryption.
- Engineers now check some proofs by machine, using [[proof assistants|proof-assistants]].

**Watch out:** the mistakes that sink proofs.

- **Reusing a letter.** If m and n are both even, write m = 2j and n = 2k, never m = 2k and n = 2k.
- **Proving by examples.** Ten cases, or ten million, prove nothing about all integers.
- **Assuming the conclusion.** If you start from the thing you want to prove, you prove nothing.
- **Proving the converse.** "If n² is even then n is even" and "if n is even then n² is even" are different theorems.
- **Forgetting that k must be an integer.** 3 = 2 · 1.5, but that does not make 3 even.

::: context qed Q.E.D. and the tombstone
Q.E.D. stands for the Latin "quod erat demonstrandum", meaning "which was to be shown". It translates the phrase Euclid's Greek proofs ended with, over 2,000 years ago. The black square ∎ is called the tombstone or the Halmos, after the mathematician Paul Halmos, who made it popular in the 1950s. Both say the same thing: the proof is finished, and the reader can check that the last line is the theorem.
:::

::: context zero What the definitions say about zero
0 is even, because 0 = 2 · 0. With the definition of divides above, every integer d divides 0, because 0 = d · 0. And 0 divides only 0: if 0 | n then n = 0 · k = 0. Some books leave out the case d = 0 by only defining d | n for d ≠ 0. Either way, 0 | n is not the same as dividing n by 0, which has no answer. Edge cases like these are exactly where code that tests divisibility with `n % d == 0` crashes, since `n % 0` raises `ZeroDivisionError`.
:::

::: context arbitrary Why arbitrary means all
"Let n be an arbitrary odd integer" is a promise: in the rest of the proof you will use nothing about n except that it is an odd integer. Then every step would work just as well for 7, for -1,001, or for any other odd integer. So the proof is really infinitely many proofs, one for each odd integer, all written at once. If you ever used a fact about one particular value, such as "n = 3", the promise is broken and the proof covers only that value.
:::

::: context parity-bit A proof that protects telemetry
A parity bit is one extra bit added to a block of data so that the total number of 1 bits is even. When the block arrives, the receiver counts the 1 bits again. If a single bit flipped on the way, the count went up or down by exactly 1, and an even number plus or minus 1 is odd. So any single flip is always detected. The proof also shows the limit: two flips change the count by -2, 0 or +2, which keeps it even, so two flips can go unnoticed. Stronger codes, such as the CRCs in the Networks course, are built on the same kind of reasoning.
:::

::: context proof-assistants Proofs checked by machine
A proof assistant, such as Lean, Coq or Isabelle, is a program that checks every step of a proof against the rules of logic, and accepts nothing it cannot verify. They have been used to verify famous results, like the four colour theorem, and real software: seL4 is an operating system kernel whose code is proved to match its specification, and CompCert is a C compiler proved never to change what a program means. Such tools are used where bugs are unacceptable, from aircraft to cryptography. The proofs in this course are the kind these tools check.
:::
--- task
Each function below runs the recipe of one direct proof: given numbers that satisfy the hypothesis, it returns the integer that the proof builds for the conclusion. Prove each theorem on paper first; the function should compute what your proof constructs. Every result must be an `int`.

- `odd_times_odd(m, n)`: **theorem:** the product of two odd integers is odd. Given odd `m` and `n`, return the integer k with m · n = 2k + 1. Write m = 2a + 1 and n = 2b + 1 and multiply out.
- `divides_sum(d, a, b)`: **theorem:** if d | a and d | b, then d | (a + b). Given a nonzero `d` that divides both `a` and `b`, return the integer k with a + b = d · k.
- `product_witness(a, b, c, d)`: **theorem:** if a | b and c | d, then ac | bd. Given nonzero `a` and `c` with a | b and c | d, return the integer k with b · d = (a · c) · k.

All inputs may be negative. The divisors (`d` in `divides_sum`, `a` and `c` in `product_witness`) are never 0, but the numbers being divided may be 0. For example, `odd_times_odd(3, 5)` is `7` (15 = 2 · 7 + 1), `divides_sum(3, 12, 9)` is `7`, and `product_witness(2, 6, 3, 9)` is `9` (54 = 6 · 9).
--- starter
def odd_times_odd(m, n):
    return None


def divides_sum(d, a, b):
    return None


def product_witness(a, b, c, d):
    return None
--- solution
def odd_times_odd(m, n):
    a = (m - 1) // 2
    b = (n - 1) // 2
    return 2 * a * b + a + b


def divides_sum(d, a, b):
    k = a // d
    l = b // d
    return k + l


def product_witness(a, b, c, d):
    k = b // a
    l = d // c
    return k * l
--- hint
Unpack each hypothesis with its definition, using a new letter each time: m = 2a + 1 and n = 2b + 1, or a = d · k and b = d · l. Then do the algebra until the conclusion has the defined shape.
--- hint
(2a + 1)(2b + 1) = 4ab + 2a + 2b + 1 = 2(2ab + a + b) + 1. For the sum, a + b = dk + dl = d(k + l). For the product, bd = (ak)(cl) = (ac)(kl).
--- hint
In code, the letters come from exact whole-number division: `a = (m - 1) // 2`, and `k = a // d` when d divides a exactly. Use `//`, not `/`, so the results stay `int` and exact even for huge numbers.
--- check case | Odd times odd, small cases
(odd_times_odd(3, 5), odd_times_odd(1, 1), odd_times_odd(7, 9))
=> (7, 0, 31)
--- check case | Odd times odd, with negatives
(odd_times_odd(-3, 5), odd_times_odd(-1, -1), odd_times_odd(-7, -9))
=> (-8, 0, 31)
--- check test | Odd times odd, for every odd pair from -21 to 21
all(type(odd_times_odd(m, n)) is int and m * n == 2 * odd_times_odd(m, n) + 1 for m in range(-21, 22, 2) for n in range(-21, 22, 2))
?? Every result must be an int k with m · n = 2k + 1. Use `//` rather than `/`.
--- check test | Odd times odd, with huge numbers
(lambda m, n: m * n == 2 * odd_times_odd(m, n) + 1)(10**40 + 1, 3 * 10**35 + 7)
--- check case | Divides the sum
(divides_sum(3, 12, 9), divides_sum(-4, 8, 12), divides_sum(5, 0, 0), divides_sum(7, -14, 21))
=> (7, -5, 0, 1)
--- check test | Divides the sum, for many divisors and multiples
all(type(divides_sum(d, d * i, d * j)) is int and d * i + d * j == d * divides_sum(d, d * i, d * j) for d in [1, 2, 3, -3, 7, -10] for i in range(-6, 7) for j in range(-6, 7))
--- check case | The product witness
(product_witness(2, 6, 3, 9), product_witness(-2, 6, 3, -9), product_witness(5, 0, 4, 8), product_witness(1, 7, 1, 1))
=> (9, 9, 0, 7)
--- check test | The product witness, for many cases
all(type(product_witness(a, a * i, c, c * j)) is int and (a * i) * (c * j) == (a * c) * product_witness(a, a * i, c, c * j) for a in [1, -2, 3, 6] for c in [1, 4, -5] for i in range(-5, 6) for j in range(-5, 6))

+++ practice | Sums and products with even and odd
--- task
Write the recipes of two more direct proofs. Each result must be an `int`.

- `odd_plus_odd(m, n)`: **theorem:** the sum of two odd integers is even. Given odd `m` and `n`, return the integer k with m + n = 2k, built the proof's way: m = 2a + 1 and n = 2b + 1.
- `even_times_any(m, n)`: **theorem:** an even integer times any integer is even. Given an even `m` and any integer `n`, return the integer k with m · n = 2k.

Inputs may be negative or zero. For example, `odd_plus_odd(3, 5)` is `4` and `even_times_any(6, 7)` is `21`.
--- starter
def odd_plus_odd(m, n):
    return (m + n) / 2


def even_times_any(m, n):
    return m * n / 2
--- solution
def odd_plus_odd(m, n):
    a = (m - 1) // 2
    b = (n - 1) // 2
    return a + b + 1


def even_times_any(m, n):
    j = m // 2
    return j * n
--- hint
(2a + 1) + (2b + 1) = 2a + 2b + 2 = 2(a + b + 1). For the product, m = 2j, so m · n = 2(j · n).
--- hint
Get a, b and j with `//`, which keeps them `int` and exact. The starter's `/` gives a float, and floats lose digits on big numbers.
--- check case | Small cases
(odd_plus_odd(3, 5), odd_plus_odd(1, -1), even_times_any(6, 7), even_times_any(0, 99))
=> (4, 0, 21, 0)
--- check case | Negatives
(odd_plus_odd(-3, -5), even_times_any(-4, 3), even_times_any(4, -3))
=> (-4, -6, -6)
--- check test | odd_plus_odd for every odd pair from -15 to 15
all(type(odd_plus_odd(m, n)) is int and m + n == 2 * odd_plus_odd(m, n) for m in range(-15, 16, 2) for n in range(-15, 16, 2))
--- check test | even_times_any for many pairs
all(type(even_times_any(m, n)) is int and m * n == 2 * even_times_any(m, n) for m in range(-20, 21, 2) for n in range(-9, 10))
--- check test | Huge numbers stay exact
odd_plus_odd(10**30 + 1, 10**30 + 3) == 10**30 + 2 and even_times_any(2 * 10**25, 10**25 + 1) == 10**25 * (10**25 + 1)
?? `/` turns the answer into a float, which cannot hold 30 digits exactly. Use `//`.

+++ practice | Dividing a combination
--- task
**Theorem:** if d | a and d | b, then d divides a · x + b · y for all integers x and y. (A number of the form a · x + b · y is called an integer **combination** of a and b; it comes back in the number theory lessons.)

Write `combo_witness(d, a, b, x, y)`. `d` is nonzero and divides both `a` and `b`; `x` and `y` are any integers. Return the integer k with a · x + b · y = d · k, built the way the proof builds it. It must be an `int`.

For example, `combo_witness(3, 6, 9, 2, -1)` is `1`: 6 · 2 + 9 · (−1) = 3 = 3 · 1.
--- starter
def combo_witness(d, a, b, x, y):
    return a * x + b * y
--- solution
def combo_witness(d, a, b, x, y):
    k = a // d
    l = b // d
    return k * x + l * y
--- hint
Unpack the hypotheses with two different letters: a = d · k and b = d · l.
--- hint
Then a · x + b · y = d · k · x + d · l · y = d · (k · x + l · y).
--- check case | The example from the task
combo_witness(3, 6, 9, 2, -1)
=> 1
--- check case | Zero coefficients, zero multiples, negative divisor
(combo_witness(5, 10, 15, 0, 0), combo_witness(5, 0, 0, 3, 4), combo_witness(-4, 8, 12, 1, 1))
=> (0, 0, -5)
--- check test | Many cases
all(type(combo_witness(d, d * i, d * j, x, y)) is int and (d * i) * x + (d * j) * y == d * combo_witness(d, d * i, d * j, x, y) for d in [1, 2, -3, 7] for i in range(-4, 5) for j in range(-4, 5) for x in range(-3, 4) for y in range(-3, 4))
--- check case | Big numbers
combo_witness(10**20, 3 * 10**20, 5 * 10**20, 7, -4)
=> 1

+++ practice | Find the broken step
--- task
An algebra proof is a chain of expressions, each claimed equal to the one before. You can grade such a chain by computing: if some step is wrong, the two expressions on either side of it will usually differ at some value.

Write `first_bad_step(steps, values)`. `steps` is a list of functions of one integer, the expressions of the chain in order. The step from `steps[i]` to `steps[i + 1]` is **broken** if the two differ at some v in `values`. Return the smallest i whose step is broken, or `None` if no step is broken (∀ steps ∀ values, they agree). A chain with fewer than two expressions has no steps.

For example, a proof that (2k + 1)² is odd might go (2k + 1)², then 4k² + 4k + 1, then 2(2k² + 2k) + 1. If the middle line were written 4k² + 2k + 1 instead, the step from 0 to 1 would be broken, and the answer would be `0`.
--- starter
def first_bad_step(steps, values):
    for i in range(len(steps) - 1):
        if steps[i](values[0]) != steps[i + 1](values[0]):
            return i
    return None
--- solution
def first_bad_step(steps, values):
    for i in range(len(steps) - 1):
        if any(steps[i](v) != steps[i + 1](v) for v in values):
            return i
    return None
--- hint
Look at each step in order, i from 0 to `len(steps) - 2`. A step is broken when ∃v in `values` where the two sides differ.
--- hint
The starter tests only the first value. A slip can agree at one value by accident, so use `any(...)` over every value. Also make sure an empty `values` list does not crash.
--- check case | A correct chain
first_bad_step([lambda k: (2 * k + 1) ** 2, lambda k: 4 * k * k + 4 * k + 1, lambda k: 2 * (2 * k * k + 2 * k) + 1], list(range(-10, 11)))
=> None
--- check case | The slip from the task
first_bad_step([lambda k: (2 * k + 1) ** 2, lambda k: 4 * k * k + 2 * k + 1, lambda k: 2 * (2 * k * k + 2 * k) + 1], list(range(-10, 11)))
=> 0
--- check case | A slip that agrees at 0 but not elsewhere
first_bad_step([lambda k: (k + 1) ** 2, lambda k: k * k + 2 * k + 1, lambda k: k * k + k + 1], [0, 1, 2, 3])
=> 1
?? The last step is right at k = 0 by accident. Check every value, not just the first.
--- check case | Only a later step is broken
first_bad_step([lambda n: n * (n + 1), lambda n: n * n + n, lambda n: n * n + n, lambda n: n ** 2 + n + 0 * n, lambda n: (n + 1) ** 2 - 1], list(range(-5, 6)))
=> 3
--- check case | No steps, or no values
(first_bad_step([lambda x: x], [1, 2]), first_bad_step([], [1]), first_bad_step([lambda x: x, lambda x: x + 1], []))
=> (None, None, None)

+++ practice | Divides, from the definition
--- task
Write two functions, straight from the definition: d | n when n = d · k for some integer k.

- `divides(d, n)`: return `True` or `False`. It must handle every integer, including `d = 0`: by the definition, 0 divides only 0. It must never raise an error.
- `divisors(n)`: return the sorted list of **positive** divisors of `n`, for any nonzero integer `n` (negative too: the divisors of −12 are those of 12). For `n = 0`, every positive integer is a divisor, so raise `ValueError`.

For example, `divides(3, -12)` is `True`, `divides(0, 5)` is `False`, and `divisors(-12)` is `[1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12]`.
--- starter
def divides(d, n):
    return n % d == 0


def divisors(n):
    return [d for d in range(1, n) if n % d == 0]
--- solution
def divides(d, n):
    if d == 0:
        return n == 0
    return n % d == 0


def divisors(n):
    if n == 0:
        raise ValueError("every positive integer divides 0")
    size = abs(n)
    return [d for d in range(1, size + 1) if divides(d, size)]
--- hint
`n % 0` raises `ZeroDivisionError`, so treat `d == 0` on its own: by the definition, 0 | n exactly when n = 0.
--- hint
The divisors of a negative number are the divisors of its absolute value, `abs(n)`. The number itself is one of its own divisors, so the range must include it.
--- check case | Ordinary cases, with negatives
(divides(3, 12), divides(3, 13), divides(3, -12), divides(-3, 12), divides(-4, -8))
=> (True, False, True, True, True)
--- check case | Zero on either side
(divides(0, 0), divides(0, 5), divides(7, 0), divides(-7, 0))
=> (True, False, True, True)
?? 0 = 7 · 0, so 7 divides 0. And 0 · k is always 0, so 0 divides only 0.
--- check case | Divisors, including negatives and 1
(divisors(12), divisors(-12), divisors(1), divisors(-1), divisors(13))
=> ([1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12], [1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12], [1], [1], [1, 13])
--- check test | divisors(0) raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: divisors(0))

+++ practice | Debug: halves that go wrong
--- task
`split_parity(n)` should return a pair `(k, r)` with n = 2k + r and r equal to 0 or 1, for **every** integer `n`, positive, negative or huge. It is the tool behind the definitions of even and odd. It works for small positive numbers, but `split_parity(-3)` returns `(-1, -1)`, and r = −1 is not allowed. The right answer is `(-2, 1)`, since −3 = 2 · (−2) + 1. It also goes wrong on huge numbers.

Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def split_parity(n):
    k = int(n / 2)
    r = n - 2 * k
    return (k, r)
--- solution
def split_parity(n):
    k = n // 2
    r = n - 2 * k
    return (k, r)
--- hint
`int(-1.5)` is `-1`: `int` cuts toward zero. For negative odd numbers, k must be rounded down instead.
--- hint
`n // 2` rounds down for every integer and never goes through a float, so it is exact even for huge numbers.
--- check case | The example from the task
split_parity(-3)
=> (-2, 1)
--- check case | Small cases
(split_parity(0), split_parity(7), split_parity(-8), split_parity(-1))
=> ((0, 0), (3, 1), (-4, 0), (-1, 1))
--- check test | Every n from -50 to 50
all((lambda k, r: n == 2 * k + r and r in (0, 1))(*split_parity(n)) for n in range(-50, 51))
--- check case | A huge number
split_parity(2**60 + 3)
=> (2**59 + 1, 1)
?? `n / 2` makes a float, which has only 53 bits of precision.

+++ practice | The best guarantee
--- task
Generalise the lesson's theorem "if a + b ≥ 15, then a ≥ 8 or b ≥ 8". A spacecraft has `n` fuel tanks, and each holds a whole, non-negative number of kilograms. If the total is at least `total`, how big must the fullest tank be?

Write two functions, for `n ≥ 1` and `total ≥ 0`.

- `best_guarantee(n, total)`: the largest m such that **every** list of n non-negative integers with sum at least `total` has some item ≥ m. Prove on paper, by contrapositive, that m = ⌈total / n⌉ works (⌈ ⌉ means round up): if every item were ≤ m − 1, the sum would be at most n(m − 1), which is less than `total`.
- `tight_example(n, total)`: a list of `n` non-negative integers that sum to exactly `total` and whose largest item equals `best_guarantee(n, total)`. It shows no bigger guarantee is possible. Spread the total as evenly as possible, and put the larger items first.

For example, `best_guarantee(2, 15)` is `8` and `tight_example(2, 15)` is `[8, 7]`. `tight_example(3, 7)` is `[3, 2, 2]`.
--- starter
def best_guarantee(n, total):
    return total // n


def tight_example(n, total):
    return [total // n] * n
--- solution
def best_guarantee(n, total):
    return (total + n - 1) // n


def tight_example(n, total):
    q = total // n
    r = total - q * n
    return [q + 1] * r + [q] * (n - r)
--- hint
Rounding up without floats: ⌈t / n⌉ is `(t + n - 1) // n` for positive n. The starter rounds down, which is wrong whenever n does not divide the total.
--- hint
For the even spread, let q = total // n and r = total − q · n (the remainder). Then r tanks get q + 1 and the other n − r get q. The sum is exactly `total`, and the largest item is ⌈total / n⌉.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(best_guarantee(2, 15), tight_example(2, 15), tight_example(3, 7))
=> (8, [8, 7], [3, 2, 2])
--- check case | Exact division, one tank, and an empty total
(best_guarantee(4, 20), tight_example(4, 20), best_guarantee(1, 9), tight_example(1, 9), best_guarantee(3, 0), tight_example(3, 0))
=> (5, [5, 5, 5, 5], 9, [9], 0, [0, 0, 0])
--- check test | The guarantee holds for every split, checked by brute force
all(max(split) >= best_guarantee(n, t) for n in range(1, 5) for t in range(0, 13) for split in __import__("itertools").product(range(t + 1), repeat=n) if sum(split) >= t)
--- check test | The tight example is tight, for many n and totals
all(len(tight_example(n, t)) == n and sum(tight_example(n, t)) == t and min(tight_example(n, t)) >= 0 and max(tight_example(n, t)) == best_guarantee(n, t) for n in range(1, 12) for t in range(0, 60))
--- check test | Big numbers stay exact
best_guarantee(3, 10**30 + 1) == 10**30 // 3 + 1 and sum(tight_example(7, 10**20)) == 10**20

+++ question | The flaw in a proof
--- ask
Claim: the sum of two even integers is divisible by 4.

"Proof. (1) Let m and n be even integers. (2) Then m = 2k for some integer k. (3) And n = 2k. (4) So m + n = 4k, which is divisible by 4. ∎"

Which step is wrong?
--- choice
Step 1: you cannot start by assuming m and n are even.
--- choice
Step 2: m = 2k is not what even means.
--- choice correct
Step 3: n needs its own letter, such as n = 2j.
--- choice
Step 4: 4k is not always divisible by 4.
--- why
Each hypothesis promises some integer, and nothing says it is the same one. Writing n = 2k forces n = m, so the "proof" covers only that case. With n = 2j, m + n = 2(k + j), which is even but not always divisible by 4: 2 + 4 = 6 is a counterexample to the claim.

+++ question | Starting a contrapositive proof
--- ask
You want to prove "if 3n + 2 is odd, then n is odd" by contrapositive. What do you assume at the start?
--- choice
n is odd.
--- choice correct
n is even.
--- choice
3n + 2 is even.
--- choice
3n + 2 is odd and n is even.
--- why
The contrapositive of P → Q is ¬Q → ¬P. Here Q is "n is odd", so you assume n is even and show 3n + 2 is even: n = 2k gives 3n + 2 = 6k + 2 = 2(3k + 1). Assuming both the hypothesis and the negated conclusion is a proof by contradiction, the next lesson's method.

+++ question | What the check proves
--- ask
A program checks that n² is odd for every odd n from −10⁹ to 10⁹, and finds no exception. What has it established?
--- choice
That n² is odd for every odd integer n.
--- choice correct
That the claim holds for those odd n only; the other odd integers need a proof.
--- choice
Nothing, because computers make rounding errors.
--- choice
That the converse holds too.
--- why
A finite check covers exactly the cases it checked. Infinitely many odd integers lie outside that range, and only an argument like the lesson's proof, which works for an arbitrary odd n, covers them all. Integer arithmetic in Python is exact, so rounding is not the issue.
=== disc-05 | Proof by contradiction
--- teach
Last lesson you proved "if P, then Q" directly and through the contrapositive. Some claims have no obvious P to start from. "√2 is irrational" says a number is *not* a fraction, and "there are infinitely many primes" says a list *never* ends. For claims like these there is a third method, the most powerful of the three: assume the claim is false, and show that this leads to something impossible.

### Assume the opposite, reach the impossible

Sudoku players do this all the time. "If this square were a 5, then row 3 would have two 5s. That cannot happen. So this square is not a 5."

A **proof by contradiction** of a statement S goes like this:

1. Suppose, for contradiction, that S is false: assume ¬S.
2. Reason from ¬S, using definitions and known facts, until you reach a **contradiction**: some statement R together with its negation ¬R.
3. Conclude that ¬S cannot hold, so S is true. ∎

Why is that valid? Two facts from lesson 1:

- Every proposition is true or false (p ∨ ¬p is a tautology). So if ¬S is impossible, S is true.
- The formula (¬S → (R ∧ ¬R)) → S is a tautology. You can check its four rows: R ∧ ¬R is always false, so ¬S → (R ∧ ¬R) is true only when ¬S is false, which is when S is true.

To prove a conditional P → Q by contradiction, assume its negation. Lesson 2 showed ¬(P → Q) ≡ P ∧ ¬Q, so you assume P **and** ¬Q, and derive a contradiction.

### Rational and irrational

A real number is **rational** if it equals p/q for some integers p and q with q ≠ 0. Otherwise it is **irrational**.

A fraction p/q is in **lowest terms** when no integer greater than 1 divides both p and q. **Lemma:** every rational number can be written in lowest terms. Take any p/q, let g be the greatest common divisor of p and q (the biggest integer dividing both, Python's `math.gcd`), and divide both by g. If some d > 1 divided both p/g and q/g, then d · g would divide both p and q and be bigger than g, which is impossible. So the result is in lowest terms. Notice that this little lemma is itself a proof by contradiction.

### √2 is irrational

**Theorem.** √2 is irrational.

**Proof.** Suppose, for contradiction, that √2 is rational. By the lemma, √2 = p/q for some integers p and q with q ≠ 0, in lowest terms.

1. Squaring both sides gives 2 = p²/q², so p² = 2q².
2. So p² is even. By last lesson's theorem ("if n² is even, then n is even"), p is even: p = 2r for some integer r.
3. Put that into p² = 2q²: 4r² = 2q², so q² = 2r².
4. So q² is even, and by the same theorem q is even.
5. Now 2 divides both p and q. That contradicts "p/q is in lowest terms".

So √2 is not rational. ∎

The contradiction here is R = "p/q is in lowest terms" (assumed in the first line) together with ¬R (derived in step 5). The [[story of this proof|hippasus]] is old.

### What Python can and cannot add

Python can hunt for a fraction p/q equal to √2, which means p² = 2q², and find none. For this you need an exact square root for integers. `math.isqrt(n)` gives the **integer square root**: the largest integer r with r² ≤ n. It works exactly on integers of any size, which `n ** 0.5` does not.

```python
from math import isqrt

print(isqrt(50), isqrt(49), isqrt(10**40))   # 7 7 100000000000000000000
hits = [q for q in range(1, 100001) if isqrt(2 * q * q) ** 2 == 2 * q * q]
print(hits)   # []
```

That is evidence for 100,000 denominators. The proof covers all of them, and all the ones after.

The same fact explains a [[floating point|float-sqrt]] surprise:

```python
print((2 ** 0.5) ** 2)   # 2.0000000000000004
```

### Primes

An integer p is **prime** if p ≥ 2 and its only positive divisors are 1 and p. An integer n ≥ 2 that is not prime is **composite**: it has a divisor d with 1 < d < n. So 2, 3, 5, 7 and 11 are prime, and 1 is neither prime nor composite.

**Lemma.** Every integer N ≥ 2 has a prime divisor. In fact, its smallest divisor greater than 1 is prime.

**Proof.** N has divisors greater than 1 (N itself, at least), so it has a smallest one; call it d. This uses the **[[well-ordering principle|well-ordering]]**: every non-empty set of positive integers has a smallest member. Suppose, for contradiction, that d is not prime. Then d = a · b for some a with 1 < a < d. Now a | d and d | N, so a | N (last lesson's transitivity theorem). So a is a divisor of N, greater than 1 and smaller than d. That contradicts d being the smallest. ∎

### There are infinitely many primes

**Theorem.** There are infinitely many primes.

**Proof.** Suppose, for contradiction, that there are only finitely many primes. List them all: p₁, p₂, …, pₖ. Let

N = p₁ · p₂ · … · pₖ + 1.

N ≥ 3, so by the lemma N has a prime divisor p. Every prime is on the list, so p is one of the pᵢ. Then p divides the product p₁ · p₂ · … · pₖ, and p divides N, so p divides their difference, N − p₁ · … · pₖ = 1. (If p | a and p | b, then a = pk and b = pl, so a − b = p(k − l).) But no prime divides 1, since every prime is at least 2. That is a contradiction, so the list cannot hold every prime. ∎

This proof is from [[Euclid|euclid]], written around 300 BC.

**N itself need not be prime.** The proof says only that N's prime factors are missing from the list. Using the first six primes:

```python
n = 2 * 3 * 5 * 7 * 11 * 13 + 1
print(n, n % 59, n // 59)   # 30031 0 509
```

So 30031 = 59 · 509, and both are primes not on the list 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13.

### Finding a smallest factor, and why √N is enough

To find the smallest divisor of N greater than 1, try d = 2, 3, 4, … in turn. You can stop early, by another small proof by contradiction:

**If N is composite, it has a divisor d with 2 ≤ d ≤ √N.** Suppose not: N = a · b with 1 < a ≤ b, and a > √N. Then b ≥ a > √N too, so a · b > √N · √N = N. That contradicts a · b = N.

So if no d with d · d ≤ N divides N, then N is prime. For example, 221 needs trying only up to isqrt(221) = 14:

```python
from math import isqrt

n = 221
small = [d for d in range(2, isqrt(n) + 1) if n % d == 0]
print(small)   # [13], and 221 = 13 · 17
```

### Which method to choose

A proof by contrapositive is really a special kind of contradiction proof: assume P and ¬Q, then derive ¬P, which contradicts P. So contradiction is the most general method. But when a direct proof exists it is usually clearer, and it often builds something useful, like last lesson's witnesses. Try direct first, then contrapositive, and reach for contradiction when the claim is a "not" or a "never".

Euclid's proof is also more constructive than it looks: given **any** finite list of primes, it gives a way to find a prime that is not on it.

### What it costs

Trial division up to √N takes at most about √N divisions: O(√N) time. For N around 10¹² that is a million divisions, a fraction of a second. For the 600-digit numbers used in RSA, √N has 300 digits, and trial division could not finish before the Sun burns out. No fast method for factoring is known at all, and the [[security of RSA|rsa]] depends on that. Euclid's numbers grow very fast too, which is why nobody can factor them far along.

### Where it is used

- **Impossibility results** are nearly always proofs by contradiction: no program can decide whether every program halts (Theory of Computation), and no comparison sort can beat n log n comparisons (DSA I).
- **Correctness proofs** of algorithms such as Dijkstra's (DSA II) suppose a first wrong answer and show it cannot exist.
- **Cryptography** needs an endless supply of large primes, which Euclid guarantees, and relies on factoring being slow.
- **Numerical software** never tests floats with `==`, because values like √2 cannot be stored exactly.

**Watch out:**

- **Negate exactly.** The opposite of "there are infinitely many primes" is "there are finitely many primes", not "there are no primes". The opposite of "for all" is "there exists … not".
- **The contradiction must be real.** It must clash with something you assumed or already proved. If you reach "1 = 2" because of an algebra slip, you have proved nothing.
- **Euclid's N is not always prime.** 30031 is not. Only its prime factors are new.
- **Use `isqrt` on integers.** `int(n ** 0.5)` goes through a float and can be wrong for big n.

::: context hippasus An old and dangerous discovery
The Pythagoreans of ancient Greece believed that everything could be described by whole numbers and their ratios. The discovery that the diagonal of a square is not a ratio of whole numbers times its side, which is exactly the statement that √2 is irrational, broke that belief. A later story says a member named Hippasus revealed it and was drowned at sea as punishment. Historians treat the story as legend, but the proof is real, and it is the model for many later proofs of irrationality.
:::

::: context float-sqrt Why the float is off
A Python float stores a number in binary with 53 significant bits. Only numbers of the form (integer) / (a power of 2) can be stored exactly, and all of those are rational. Since √2 is irrational, the float `2 ** 0.5` must be a slightly wrong rational number, and squaring it shows the error. This is why numerical code compares floats with a tolerance, as in `abs(x - y) < 1e-9`, instead of `==`. The Computer Organization course shows exactly how floats are stored.
:::

::: context well-ordering Every set has a smallest
The well-ordering principle says: every non-empty set of positive integers has a smallest element. It sounds obvious, and for the integers it is taken as a basic fact. It is not true for other sets: the positive real numbers have no smallest element, since half of any one is smaller. In lesson 7 you will see that well-ordering is exactly what makes induction work, and that "pick the smallest counterexample" is one of the most useful moves in proofs.
:::

::: context euclid Euclid's Elements
Euclid's "Elements", written in Alexandria around 300 BC, collected the mathematics of its time into thirteen books, each result proved from a few stated assumptions. It was used as a textbook for over 2,000 years. Book IX, Proposition 20 proves that there are more primes than any given list, the proof in this lesson, though Euclid phrased it without the word infinity. Book VII gives the algorithm for the greatest common divisor that you will write in the number theory lessons.
:::

::: context rsa Factoring and RSA
RSA, the public-key system you will build in lesson 20, uses a public number N that is the product of two secret primes, each hundreds of digits long. Anyone can encrypt with N, but decrypting needs the two primes. Multiplying them takes a split second; getting them back from N would need factoring, and the best known methods would take longer than the age of the universe for numbers that size. Nobody has proved that factoring is hard, so RSA's security is an assumption, which is one reason cryptographers are moving to new systems.
:::
--- task
Write two functions that turn this lesson's proofs into programs.

- `smallest_factor(n)`: for an integer `n ≥ 2`, return its smallest divisor greater than 1. By the lemma, that divisor is always prime, and it is `n` itself exactly when `n` is prime. Try divisors d = 2, 3, 4, … and stop once d · d > n, using the √n argument. A check uses numbers around 10¹², where going all the way up to n would take far too long.
- `euclid_prime(primes)`: given a list of distinct primes (possibly empty), return a prime that is **not** in the list, the way Euclid's proof finds one: the smallest prime factor of the product of the list, plus 1. The product of an empty list is 1.

For example, `smallest_factor(91)` is `7`, `smallest_factor(97)` is `97`, and `euclid_prime([2, 3, 5])` is `31`.
--- starter
def smallest_factor(n):
    return None


def euclid_prime(primes):
    return None
--- solution
def smallest_factor(n):
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return d
        d += 1
    return n


def euclid_prime(primes):
    product = 1
    for p in primes:
        product *= p
    return smallest_factor(product + 1)
--- hint
Loop with `d` starting at 2 while `d * d <= n`, and return `d` as soon as it divides `n`. If the loop ends, no divisor up to √n exists, so `n` is prime: return `n`.
--- hint
For `euclid_prime`, multiply the primes together, starting from 1 (so an empty list gives 1), add 1, and return `smallest_factor` of that.
--- check case | Small numbers
[smallest_factor(n) for n in [2, 3, 4, 9, 15, 49, 91, 97, 221]]
=> [2, 3, 2, 3, 3, 7, 7, 97, 13]
--- check test | Agrees with a plain search for every n from 2 to 2000
all(smallest_factor(n) == next(d for d in range(2, n + 1) if n % d == 0) for n in range(2, 2001))
--- check case | Squares of primes are the edge of the √n test
(smallest_factor(25), smallest_factor(121), smallest_factor(10403), smallest_factor(1018081))
=> (5, 11, 101, 1009)
?? For n = p · p, the divisor p is exactly √n. Keep going while `d * d <= n`, including equality.
--- check case | A prime near 10¹², and a product of two primes near 10⁶
(smallest_factor(999999999989), smallest_factor(999983 * 1000003))
=> (999999999989, 999983)
?? Stop when d · d > n. Going all the way up to n would take about a trillion steps.
--- check case | Euclid's construction on short lists
(euclid_prime([]), euclid_prime([2]), euclid_prime([3]), euclid_prime([2, 3, 5]))
=> (2, 3, 2, 31)
?? The product of an empty list is 1, so N = 2.
--- check case | The first six primes give 30031 = 59 · 509
euclid_prime([2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13])
=> 59
--- check test | The new prime is prime and never on the list
all((lambda p: p not in L and smallest_factor(p) == p)(euclid_prime(L)) for L in [[2], [2, 3], [5, 7], [2, 3, 5, 7], [3, 5, 7, 11, 13], [2, 11, 101], [13, 59, 509]])

+++ practice | All the primes up to n
--- task
Write `primes_up_to(n)` that returns the list of all primes from 2 up to and including `n`, in increasing order. For `n < 2` it returns `[]`. Test each number by trial division, trying divisors only while d · d is at most the number.

For example, `primes_up_to(20)` is `[2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19]`.
--- starter
def primes_up_to(n):
    return [p for p in range(2, n) if p % 2 == 1]
--- solution
def is_prime(m):
    if m < 2:
        return False
    d = 2
    while d * d <= m:
        if m % d == 0:
            return False
        d += 1
    return True


def primes_up_to(n):
    return [m for m in range(2, n + 1) if is_prime(m)]
--- hint
Write a helper `is_prime(m)`: it is `False` for m < 2, `False` if some d with `d * d <= m` divides m, and `True` otherwise.
--- hint
Then keep every m from 2 to n inclusive, so the range must end at `n + 1`.
--- check case | The example from the task
primes_up_to(20)
=> [2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19]
--- check case | n itself is included when it is prime
(primes_up_to(2), primes_up_to(3), primes_up_to(13)[-1])
=> ([2], [2, 3], 13)
--- check case | Nothing below 2
(primes_up_to(1), primes_up_to(0), primes_up_to(-7))
=> ([], [], [])
--- check test | 5,133 primes up to 50,000, the last one 49,999
(lambda ps: len(ps) == 5133 and ps[-1] == 49999)(primes_up_to(50000))
--- check case | Squares of primes are not prime
[m for m in [4, 9, 25, 49, 121, 169] if m in primes_up_to(200)]
=> []

+++ practice | When is a square root rational?
--- task
The lesson's proof extends to every integer: for an integer n ≥ 0, √n is rational exactly when n is a perfect square (the square of an integer). So √4 = 2 is rational, and √2, √3 and √5 are not.

Write `sqrt_rational(n)` that returns `True` if √n is rational and `False` otherwise, for any integer `n ≥ 0`, however large. Use `math.isqrt`: floats cannot tell a huge perfect square from its neighbours.

For example, `sqrt_rational(49)` is `True` and `sqrt_rational(50)` is `False`.
--- starter
def sqrt_rational(n):
    r = int(n ** 0.5)
    return r * r == n
--- solution
from math import isqrt


def sqrt_rational(n):
    r = isqrt(n)
    return r * r == n
--- hint
`isqrt(n)` is the largest integer r with r · r ≤ n. The number n is a perfect square exactly when r · r equals n.
--- hint
The starter's `n ** 0.5` goes through a float, which keeps only about 16 digits, so for a 40-digit square it gives the wrong r.
--- check case | Small numbers
[n for n in range(0, 30) if sqrt_rational(n)]
=> [0, 1, 4, 9, 16, 25]
--- check case | Around a square
(sqrt_rational(143), sqrt_rational(144), sqrt_rational(145))
=> (False, True, False)
--- check case | A huge perfect square, and its neighbours
(sqrt_rational((10**20 + 1) ** 2), sqrt_rational((10**20 + 1) ** 2 - 1), sqrt_rational((10**20 + 1) ** 2 + 1))
=> (True, False, False)
?? A float cannot hold 41 digits. `math.isqrt` works exactly on integers of any size.
--- check case | A 60-digit square
sqrt_rational(123456789012345678901234567890 ** 2)
=> True

+++ practice | Euclid's sequence
--- task
Start a list with the prime 2. Then repeat: multiply everything in the list together, add 1, and append the **smallest** prime factor of the result. Euclid's proof guarantees each new number is a prime that is not already in the list. The list begins 2, 3, 7, 43, 13, … and it is called the Euclid–Mullin sequence.

Write `euclid_mullin(k)` that returns the first `k` numbers of the sequence as a list, for `k ≥ 1`. You will need a smallest-factor helper that stops at √n.

For example, `euclid_mullin(5)` is `[2, 3, 7, 43, 13]`: 2 · 3 · 7 · 43 + 1 = 1807 = 13 · 139.
--- starter
def euclid_mullin(k):
    seq = [2]
    while len(seq) < k:
        product = 1
        for p in seq:
            product *= p
        seq.append(product + 1)
    return seq
--- solution
def smallest_factor(n):
    d = 2
    while d * d <= n:
        if n % d == 0:
            return d
        d += 1
    return n


def euclid_mullin(k):
    seq = [2]
    while len(seq) < k:
        product = 1
        for p in seq:
            product *= p
        seq.append(smallest_factor(product + 1))
    return seq
--- hint
The starter appends the product plus 1 itself. That number need not be prime: 1807 = 13 · 139. Append its smallest prime factor instead.
--- hint
The smallest divisor greater than 1 is always prime, so a trial-division helper that stops once `d * d` passes n is all you need.
--- check case | The example from the task
euclid_mullin(5)
=> [2, 3, 7, 43, 13]
--- check case | One and two terms
(euclid_mullin(1), euclid_mullin(2))
=> ([2], [2, 3])
--- check case | Eight terms
euclid_mullin(8)
=> [2, 3, 7, 43, 13, 53, 5, 6221671]
--- check test | Eight distinct primes
(lambda s: len(set(s)) == 8 and all(all(p % d for d in range(2, int(p ** 0.5) + 1)) for p in s))(euclid_mullin(8))

+++ practice | Lowest terms
--- task
Write `lowest_terms(p, q)` that returns the fraction p/q in lowest terms, as a pair of integers `(a, b)`:

- `a / b` equals `p / q`, and no integer greater than 1 divides both `a` and `b`;
- the denominator `b` is always positive, so any minus sign goes on `a`;
- zero is `(0, 1)`;
- if `q` is 0, raise `ValueError`, since p/0 is not a number.

Use `math.gcd`. For example, `lowest_terms(6, -8)` is `(-3, 4)`.
--- starter
from math import gcd


def lowest_terms(p, q):
    g = gcd(p, q)
    return (p // g, q // g)
--- solution
from math import gcd


def lowest_terms(p, q):
    if q == 0:
        raise ValueError("the denominator must not be 0")
    if q < 0:
        p = -p
        q = -q
    g = gcd(p, q)
    return (p // g, q // g)
--- hint
Check `q == 0` first and raise. Then, if `q` is negative, flip the sign of both `p` and `q`: that does not change the fraction.
--- hint
`gcd(0, q)` is `q`, so dividing gives `(0, 1)` for zero with no special case. `math.gcd` always returns a non-negative number.
--- check case | The example from the task
lowest_terms(6, -8)
=> (-3, 4)
--- check case | Already in lowest terms, and whole numbers
(lowest_terms(3, 7), lowest_terms(12, 4), lowest_terms(-12, 4))
=> ((3, 7), (3, 1), (-3, 1))
--- check case | Two negatives make a positive
lowest_terms(-10, -25)
=> (2, 5)
--- check case | Zero
(lowest_terms(0, 5), lowest_terms(0, -3))
=> ((0, 1), (0, 1))
--- check test | A zero denominator raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: lowest_terms(3, 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: lowest_terms(0, 0))
--- check test | Many fractions come out right
all((lambda a, b: b > 0 and a * q == b * p and __import__("math").gcd(a, b) == 1)(*lowest_terms(p, q)) for p in range(-12, 13) for q in range(-12, 13) if q != 0)

+++ practice | Debug: the prime test that misses squares
--- task
`is_prime(n)` should return `True` exactly when `n` is prime, for any integer `n`. It uses the √n argument, but it says that 49, 9 and even 4 are prime. For example, `is_prime(49)` returns `True`, and it should return `False` because 49 = 7 · 7.

Find the bug and fix it. It is one small change.
--- starter
from math import isqrt


def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    for d in range(2, isqrt(n)):
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
    return True
--- solution
from math import isqrt


def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    for d in range(2, isqrt(n) + 1):
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
    return True
--- hint
Which divisors does the loop try for n = 49? `isqrt(49)` is 7.
--- hint
The argument says a composite n has a divisor d with d ≤ √n, and that includes d = √n itself. `range` stops before its end, so the end must be one more than `isqrt(n)`.
--- check case | The example from the task
is_prime(49)
=> False
--- check case | Other squares of primes
(is_prime(4), is_prime(9), is_prime(25), is_prime(10201))
=> (False, False, False, False)
--- check case | Primes still pass
(is_prime(2), is_prime(3), is_prime(97), is_prime(10007))
=> (True, True, True, True)
--- check case | Below 2
(is_prime(1), is_prime(0), is_prime(-7))
=> (False, False, False)
--- check test | Agrees with a plain search up to 1000
