Computing that square root with floats breaks for big numbers. A float holds about 16 significant digits, so the square root of a 40-digit number is only approximate, and rounding down an approximation can land on the wrong whole number. **`math.isqrt(n)`**, from lesson 5, gives the exact integer square root, the largest k with k · k ≤ n, for integers of any size:

```python
import math

print(math.isqrt(10 ** 40 - 1))        # 99999999999999999999
print(int(math.sqrt(10 ** 40 - 1)))    # 100000000000000000000: one too big
print(math.isqrt(50), math.isqrt(49))  # 7 7
```

### More countable sets

- **The rationals ℚ.** Every positive fraction is p/q for a pair of positive integers. Walking the pairs by diagonals and skipping fractions not in lowest terms (2/2, 2/4, …) lists every positive rational exactly once. Add 0 and the negatives by interleaving, as for ℤ. So ℚ is countable, even though between any two fractions there are infinitely many more.
- **Finite strings.** Over a finite alphabet, list the strings by length, and alphabetically within each length: "", "a", "b", "aa", "ab", "ba", "bb", "aaa", … This is **shortlex order**. There are finitely many strings of each length, so every string gets a finite position. So the set of all finite strings is countable, and so is the set of all Python programs, since each one is a finite string.
- **Countable unions.** If A₀, A₁, A₂, … are each countable, so is their union: list each Aₓ as aₓ,₀, aₓ,₁, aₓ,₂, … (repeating elements if Aₓ is finite), put aₓ,ᵧ at position π(x, y), and cross out repeats.

### Something bigger: the diagonal argument

An **infinite binary sequence** is an endless row of bits, s = s(0) s(1) s(2) …, like 0101010… or 1000… .

**Theorem (Cantor).** The set of all infinite binary sequences is uncountable.

**Proof,** by contradiction. Suppose some list s₀, s₁, s₂, … contains every infinite binary sequence. Build a new sequence d by looking down the diagonal and flipping each bit:

d(n) = 1 − sₙ(n).

That is, bit n of d is the opposite of bit n of sₙ. Now d is some infinite binary sequence, so it must be sₖ for some k. But d(k) = 1 − sₖ(k) ≠ sₖ(k), so d and sₖ differ at position k. That contradicts d = sₖ. So no list contains every sequence. ∎

| | bit 0 | bit 1 | bit 2 | bit 3 | … |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| s₀ | **0** | 1 | 1 | 0 | |
| s₁ | 1 | **1** | 0 | 0 | |
| s₂ | 0 | 0 | **0** | 1 | |
| s₃ | 1 | 0 | 1 | **1** | |
| d | 1 | 0 | 1 | 0 | |

Whatever list you are handed, d is built from it and escapes it. The argument does not say the list was badly made; it says **every** list misses something.

Consequences:

- **𝒫(ℕ) is uncountable.** A sequence s matches the subset {n : s(n) = 1}, and that is a bijection.
- **ℝ is uncountable.** Do the same with decimal digits of numbers between 0 and 1, making digit n of d a 5 if digit n of the n-th number is not 5, and a 4 if it is. Avoiding 0 and 9 sidesteps the one way two decimals can name the same number: [[0.4999… = 0.5000…|nines]].

### Cantor's theorem: no set is as big as its power set

The diagonal trick works for any set.

**Theorem.** For every set A, there is no surjection from A onto 𝒫(A). So |𝒫(A)| > |A|.

**Proof.** Take any function f: A → 𝒫(A). Each f(a) is a subset of A. Define

D = {a ∈ A : a ∉ f(a)},

the elements that are not in their own set. Suppose D = f(t) for some t ∈ A. Is t ∈ D? By the definition of D, t ∈ D exactly when t ∉ f(t), and f(t) is D. So t ∈ D exactly when t ∉ D, which is impossible. So D is not f(t) for any t, and f is not onto. Finally, a ↦ {a} is an injection from A into 𝒫(A), so 𝒫(A) is at least as big as A, and with no bijection possible it is strictly bigger. ∎

For finite sets this says 2ⁿ > n. For infinite sets it gives an endless ladder: ℕ, then 𝒫(ℕ), then 𝒫(𝒫(ℕ)), each strictly bigger. You can run the construction on a small example:

```python
chosen = {"a": {"a", "b"}, "b": set(), "c": {"a", "c"}}
d = {x for x in chosen if x not in chosen[x]}
print(d, d in chosen.values())   # {'b'} False
```

### What it costs

An enumeration is useful in code when you can jump to position n directly.

- **Cantor pairing:** π(x, y) is a few multiplications. Going back needs one `math.isqrt`, also fast. For numbers with b bits, each step costs about b² bit operations at worst, which is nothing for b in the thousands.
- **Walking a list** to reach position n costs n steps. The rationals in lowest terms have no simple position formula, so finding one's position means counting the reduced fractions on the earlier diagonals, with a gcd for each.
- **The diagonal sequence** costs one step per bit: to produce its first n bits you look at n entries of the list.

### Where it is used

- **Theory of computation.** There are countably many programs but uncountably many yes-or-no problems about natural numbers (each is a subset of ℕ). So almost every problem has no program that solves it. The [[halting problem|halting]] is a concrete one, proved with exactly this diagonal trick.
- **Pairing keys.** Cantor pairing packs two IDs into one integer key, for example a (satellite, pass) pair in a database index or a hash key.
- **Serialising data.** Encoding lists, trees or whole programs as one natural number, as [[Gödel|godel]] did with proofs, relies on these bijections.
- **Floating point.** A 64-bit float can take at most 2⁶⁴ values, a finite set. Almost every real number can never be stored exactly; floats are a finite sample of an uncountable set.

**Watch out:** four classic mistakes.

- **A list that never gets there.** Listing ℕ × ℕ row by row, or ℚ as 1/1, 1/2, 1/3, … , gives some elements no finite position. Countable means **every** element has a finite position.
- **A diagonal that is not diagonal.** d must differ from the n-th list entry **at position n**. Flipping some other bit gives no guarantee.
- **Floats for exact integer arithmetic.** `int(math.sqrt(n))` is wrong for large n. Use `math.isqrt`.
- **Countable is not finite.** ℕ is countable and infinite. And "uncountable" does not mean "you cannot count them fast"; it means no list of them exists at all.

::: context hilbert A hotel that is full but has room
David Hilbert imagined a hotel with rooms 0, 1, 2, … , every one occupied. A new guest arrives. The manager asks everyone in room n to move to room n + 1, and room 0 is free. An infinite bus of new guests arrives: everyone in room n moves to room 2n, and the new guests take the odd rooms. Nobody shares, nobody is turned away. The hotel is just ℕ, and the moves are bijections from ℕ onto proper subsets of itself.
:::

::: context triangular Counting the earlier diagonals
The d-th triangular number T(d) = 0 + 1 + 2 + … + d = d(d + 1)/2 is a sum of the kind you proved by induction in lesson 7. It counts dots arranged in a triangle with d on the bottom row. In the pairing, diagonals 0 to d − 1 hold 1, 2, …, d pairs, so T(d) pairs come before diagonal d. The formula is quadratic in d, which is why going back needs a square root.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 240 110" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <g fill="#1d6fd1">
    <circle cx="30" cy="90" r="5"/><circle cx="50" cy="90" r="5"/><circle cx="70" cy="90" r="5"/><circle cx="90" cy="90" r="5"/>
    <circle cx="30" cy="70" r="5"/><circle cx="50" cy="70" r="5"/><circle cx="70" cy="70" r="5"/>
    <circle cx="30" cy="50" r="5"/><circle cx="50" cy="50" r="5"/>
    <circle cx="30" cy="30" r="5"/>
  </g>
  <text x="130" y="45" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">T(4) = 1 + 2 + 3 + 4</text>
  <text x="130" y="65" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">= 4 · 5 / 2 = 10</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context nines Two names for one number
0.4999… with the 9s going on for ever equals 0.5 exactly. One way to see it: 0.999… = 3 · 0.333… = 3 · (1/3) = 1. So a number can have two decimal expansions, one ending in endless 0s and one in endless 9s. If the diagonal argument built such a number, it could differ from a listed number in every digit and still equal it in value. Using only the digits 4 and 5 means the new number has one expansion, so it really is different.
:::

::: context halting The same trick, for programs
Suppose a program `halts(p, x)` could decide whether program p stops on input x. Build `trouble(p)`: if `halts(p, p)` says yes, loop for ever; otherwise stop. Does `trouble(trouble)` stop? It stops exactly when it does not. That is Cantor's set D again, with programs in place of elements, and it shows no such `halts` can exist. The Theory of Computation course proves it in full.
:::

::: context godel Numbers that stand for proofs
In 1931 Kurt Gödel gave every formula and every proof of arithmetic a natural number, its Gödel number, using an injective encoding built from prime powers, much like the encodings in this lesson. Statements about proofs became statements about numbers, and he built a statement that says of itself "I cannot be proved". That is his incompleteness theorem, one more diagonal argument, and it set limits on what any system of proof can do.
:::
--- task
Write Cantor's pairing function and its inverse, exactly, for natural numbers of any size.

- `pair(x, y)` returns (x + y)(x + y + 1) / 2 + y. Use whole-number division so the result is an `int`.
- `unpair(z)` returns the tuple `(x, y)` with `pair(x, y) == z`. Find the diagonal d, the largest whole number with d(d + 1)/2 ≤ z, using `math.isqrt` (the float `math.sqrt` gives wrong answers for large z). Then y = z − d(d + 1)/2 and x = d − y.
- Both raise `ValueError` if given a negative number.

For example, `pair(2, 1)` is `7` and `unpair(7)` is `(2, 1)`. A check unpairs 10⁴⁰.
--- starter
def pair(x, y):
    return 0


def unpair(z):
    return (0, 0)
--- solution
import math


def pair(x, y):
    if x < 0 or y < 0:
        raise ValueError("pair takes natural numbers")
    d = x + y
    return d * (d + 1) // 2 + y


def unpair(z):
    if z < 0:
        raise ValueError("unpair takes a natural number")
    d = (math.isqrt(8 * z + 1) - 1) // 2
    y = z - d * (d + 1) // 2
    return (d - y, y)
--- hint
`pair` is the formula with `//` for the division by 2. d(d + 1) is always even, so nothing is lost.
--- hint
For `unpair`, the diagonal is `d = (math.isqrt(8 * z + 1) - 1) // 2`. Both steps round down, and together they give the largest d with d(d + 1)/2 ≤ z.
--- hint
Then `y` is how far `z` is past the start of diagonal d, and `x` is what is left of d: `y = z - d * (d + 1) // 2`, `x = d - y`.
--- check case | The first diagonals
[pair(0, 0), pair(1, 0), pair(0, 1), pair(2, 0), pair(1, 1), pair(0, 2), pair(2, 1)]
=> [0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 7]
--- check case | unpair goes back
[unpair(0), unpair(1), unpair(2), unpair(7), unpair(31)]
=> [(0, 0), (1, 0), (0, 1), (2, 1), (4, 3)]
--- check test | pair is a bijection from the first 100 diagonals onto 0 … 5049
sorted(pair(x, d - x) for d in range(100) for x in range(d + 1)) == list(range(5050))
--- check test | unpair then pair gives z back, for every z below 20,000
all(pair(*unpair(z)) == z and min(unpair(z)) >= 0 for z in range(20000))
--- check test | pair then unpair gives the pair back
all(unpair(pair(x, y)) == (x, y) for x in range(120) for y in range(120))
--- check test | Exact for huge numbers
(lambda p: pair(*p) == 10 ** 40 and min(p) >= 0)(unpair(10 ** 40)) and unpair(pair(10 ** 30, 10 ** 30 + 7)) == (10 ** 30, 10 ** 30 + 7)
?? math.sqrt works with floats, which keep about 16 digits. Use math.isqrt, which is exact for integers of any size.
--- check test | Results are ints
type(pair(3, 4)) is int and all(type(v) is int for v in unpair(12345))
--- check test | Negative numbers raise ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: pair(-1, 2)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: pair(0, -3)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: unpair(-1))

+++ practice | A second pairing: powers of two
--- task
Every positive integer can be written in exactly one way as 2ˣ · (2y + 1): a power of two times an odd number. (Divide out all the 2s; what is left is odd.) That gives another bijection from ℕ × ℕ to ℕ:

pair2(x, y) = 2ˣ · (2y + 1) − 1.

Write `pair2(x, y)` and its inverse `unpair2(z)`, which returns the tuple `(x, y)`. To invert, add 1 to `z`, count how many times 2 divides it (that is x), and read y from the odd part that is left.

For example, `pair2(2, 1)` is `11` (4 · 3 − 1), and `unpair2(0)` is `(0, 0)`.
--- starter
def pair2(x, y):
    return 0


def unpair2(z):
    return (0, 0)
--- solution
def pair2(x, y):
    return 2 ** x * (2 * y + 1) - 1


def unpair2(z):
    n = z + 1
    x = 0
    while n % 2 == 0:
        n //= 2
        x += 1
    return (x, (n - 1) // 2)
--- hint
`unpair2`: let `n = z + 1`. While `n` is even, halve it and add 1 to `x`.
--- hint
When the loop stops, `n` is odd, so it is `2 * y + 1` for `y = (n - 1) // 2`.
--- check case | The examples
(pair2(2, 1), unpair2(11), unpair2(0))
=> (11, (2, 1), (0, 0))
--- check case | The first few positions
[unpair2(z) for z in range(8)]
=> [(0, 0), (1, 0), (0, 1), (2, 0), (0, 2), (1, 1), (0, 3), (3, 0)]
--- check test | Round trips both ways
all(pair2(*unpair2(z)) == z for z in range(5000)) and all(unpair2(pair2(x, y)) == (x, y) for x in range(20) for y in range(50))
--- check test | Injective on a block of pairs
len({pair2(x, y) for x in range(30) for y in range(30)}) == 900
--- check case | Large values
(unpair2(2 ** 200 * 7 - 1), pair2(100, 0))
=> ((200, 3), 2 ** 100 - 1)

+++ practice | The diagonal escape
--- task
Here is Cantor's diagonal argument on a finite list. `rows` is a list of n strings, each made of the characters `"0"` and `"1"`, and each at least n characters long.

Write `diagonal_escape(rows)` that returns a string of exactly n characters, each `"0"` or `"1"`, whose character at position i is different from the character at position i of `rows[i]`. Such a string differs from every row within its first n characters, so it is not the first n characters of any row.

For example, `diagonal_escape(["011", "110", "101"])` is `"100"`. `diagonal_escape([])` is `""`.
--- starter
def diagonal_escape(rows):
    return "0" * len(rows)
--- solution
def diagonal_escape(rows):
    return "".join("1" if rows[i][i] == "0" else "0" for i in range(len(rows)))
--- hint
Only one character of each row matters: row i at position i.
--- hint
Flip each diagonal character, `"0"` to `"1"` and `"1"` to `"0"`, and join the flipped characters in order.
--- check case | The example
diagonal_escape(["011", "110", "101"])
=> "100"
--- check case | No rows, one row
(diagonal_escape([]), diagonal_escape(["0"]), diagonal_escape(["1111"]))
=> ("", "1", "0")
--- check test | Rows longer than n: only the diagonal matters
diagonal_escape(["00000", "11111", "01010"]) == "101"
--- check test | Differs from every row, on many lists
(lambda R: all((lambda d: len(d) == len(rows) and all(d[i] != rows[i][i] for i in range(len(rows))) and all(d != r[:len(rows)] for r in rows))(diagonal_escape(rows)) for rows in R))([["".join("01"[(i * 7 + j * j + k) % 3 % 2] for j in range(n + 2)) for i in range(n)] for n in range(1, 40) for k in range(3)])

+++ practice | Every string gets a number
--- task
Shortlex order lists every finite string over an alphabet: first by length, then alphabetically. Over the alphabet `"ab"` it goes `""`, `"a"`, `"b"`, `"aa"`, `"ab"`, `"ba"`, `"bb"`, `"aaa"`, … , so the position of `"ab"` is 4.

Write two functions that turn this list into a bijection between ℕ and the strings. `alphabet` is a string of distinct characters in alphabetical order, at least one of them.

- `nth_string(n, alphabet)` returns the string at position `n`.
- `string_position(s, alphabet)` returns the position of the string `s`, whose characters all come from `alphabet`.

It must be fast for strings of 100 characters, so do not walk the list. Use this: with k characters there are kᴸ strings of each length L, and within one length the strings are like L-digit numbers in base k, with the first character as digit 0.

For example, over `"ab"`, `nth_string(4, "ab")` is `"ab"` and `string_position("bb", "ab")` is `6`.
--- starter
def nth_string(n, alphabet):
    return ""


def string_position(s, alphabet):
    return 0
--- solution
def nth_string(n, alphabet):
    k = len(alphabet)
    length = 0
    while n >= k ** length:
        n -= k ** length
        length += 1
    chars = []
    for _ in range(length):
        chars.append(alphabet[n % k])
        n //= k
    return "".join(reversed(chars))


def string_position(s, alphabet):
    k = len(alphabet)
    before = sum(k ** length for length in range(len(s)))
    within = 0
    for ch in s:
        within = within * k + alphabet.index(ch)
    return before + within
--- hint
The position of `s` is (how many strings are shorter than `s`) plus (its place among strings of its own length). Shorter strings number k⁰ + k¹ + … + k^(L−1).
--- hint
Its place within its length is `s` read as a base-k number, with `alphabet.index(ch)` as each digit's value. For `nth_string`, first subtract k⁰, k¹, … from `n` while you can, to find the length, then write what is left in base k with exactly that many digits.
--- check case | The examples
(nth_string(4, "ab"), string_position("bb", "ab"), string_position("ab", "ab"))
=> ("ab", 6, 4)
--- check case | The start of the list
[nth_string(n, "ab") for n in range(8)]
=> ["", "a", "b", "aa", "ab", "ba", "bb", "aaa"]
--- check case | A one-letter alphabet
(nth_string(5, "z"), string_position("zzz", "z"), nth_string(0, "z"))
=> ("zzzzz", 3, "")
--- check test | A bijection on the first 5,000 positions, over three letters
all(string_position(nth_string(n, "xyz"), "xyz") == n for n in range(5000)) and len({nth_string(n, "xyz") for n in range(5000)}) == 5000
--- check test | Shortlex order: by length, then alphabetically
(lambda L: all(len(a) < len(b) or (len(a) == len(b) and a < b) for a, b in zip(L, L[1:])))([nth_string(n, "abc") for n in range(2000)])
--- check test | A 100-character string, and back
(lambda s: nth_string(string_position(s, "0123456789"), "0123456789") == s)("9" * 50 + "0123456789" * 5)

+++ practice | Where a fraction sits
--- task
List the positive rationals by walking diagonals. Diagonal s holds the fractions p/q with p + q = s, for s = 2, 3, 4, … . Within a diagonal, go in order of increasing p. Skip any fraction not in lowest terms (where gcd(p, q) > 1), because the same number appeared earlier in lowest terms. The list starts:

1/1, 1/2, 2/1, 1/3, 3/1, 1/4, 2/3, 3/2, 4/1, 1/5, 5/1, …

(2/2 was skipped on diagonal 4, and 2/4, 3/3 and 4/2 on diagonal 6.)

Write `rational_position(p, q)` that returns the position of the number p/q in this list, counting from 0. First reduce p/q to lowest terms: 2/4 is the same number as 1/2, so it has position 1. If `p` or `q` is 0 or negative, raise `ValueError`.

For example, `rational_position(1, 1)` is `0`, `rational_position(3, 2)` is `7` and `rational_position(4, 2)` is `2`.
--- starter
def rational_position(p, q):
    return p + q - 2
--- solution
from math import gcd


def rational_position(p, q):
    if p <= 0 or q <= 0:
        raise ValueError("p and q must be positive")
    g = gcd(p, q)
    p, q = p // g, q // g
    position = 0
    for s in range(2, p + q):
        for a in range(1, s):
            if gcd(a, s - a) == 1:
                position += 1
    for a in range(1, p):
        if gcd(a, p + q - a) == 1:
            position += 1
    return position
--- hint
Reduce first with `math.gcd`. Then count the fractions in lowest terms that come before p/q: all of those on the earlier diagonals, then those on its own diagonal with a smaller numerator.
--- hint
On diagonal s, the fractions are a/(s − a) for a from 1 to s − 1. Count one only when `gcd(a, s - a) == 1`.
--- check case | The examples
(rational_position(1, 1), rational_position(3, 2), rational_position(4, 2))
=> (0, 7, 2)
--- check case | Fractions not in lowest terms
(rational_position(2, 4), rational_position(3, 3), rational_position(10, 15))
=> (1, 0, 6)
--- check case | The start of the list
[rational_position(p, q) for p, q in [(1, 2), (2, 1), (1, 3), (3, 1), (1, 4), (2, 3), (4, 1), (1, 5), (5, 1)]]
=> [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9, 10]
--- check test | Zero and negatives raise ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: rational_position(0, 3)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: rational_position(2, -1)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: rational_position(-1, -1))
--- check test | The reduced fractions on the first 60 diagonals fill positions 0, 1, 2, … with no gaps
(lambda P: sorted(P) == list(range(len(P))))([rational_position(a, s - a) for s in range(2, 62) for a in range(1, s) if __import__("math").gcd(a, s - a) == 1])

+++ practice | Debug: two integers, one number
--- task
The list 0, 1, −1, 2, −2, 3, −3, … is a bijection between ℕ and ℤ. `nat_to_int(n)` returns the integer at position `n`, and it is correct. `int_to_nat(z)` should be its inverse: it should return the position of the integer `z`, so that `int_to_nat(nat_to_int(n)) == n` for every `n`.

But `int_to_nat(1)` and `int_to_nat(-1)` both return 2, so it is not even injective. Fix `int_to_nat`.
--- starter
def nat_to_int(n):
    if n % 2 == 1:
        return (n + 1) // 2
    return -(n // 2)


def int_to_nat(z):
    if z > 0:
        return 2 * z
    return -2 * z
--- solution
def nat_to_int(n):
    if n % 2 == 1:
        return (n + 1) // 2
    return -(n // 2)


def int_to_nat(z):
    if z > 0:
        return 2 * z - 1
    return -2 * z
--- hint
Write out the list with positions: 0 is at 0, 1 at 1, −1 at 2, 2 at 3, −2 at 4. Where do the positive integers sit?
--- hint
A positive k is at the odd position 2k − 1; −k and 0 are at the even position 2k.
--- check case | 1 and −1 get different positions
(int_to_nat(1), int_to_nat(-1), int_to_nat(0))
=> (1, 2, 0)
--- check test | The inverse of nat_to_int, for n below 10,000
all(int_to_nat(nat_to_int(n)) == n for n in range(10000))
--- check test | And the other way round
all(nat_to_int(int_to_nat(z)) == z for z in range(-5000, 5001))
--- check case | Large integers
(int_to_nat(10 ** 20), int_to_nat(-10 ** 20))
=> (2 * 10 ** 20 - 1, 2 * 10 ** 20)

+++ practice | One number for a whole list
--- task
A ground system wants a single integer key for a short list of sensor counts (natural numbers). Nested Cantor pairing gives a bijection between **all** finite lists of naturals and ℕ:

- the empty list has code 0;
- a list whose first item is `a` and the rest is `rest` has code `1 + pair(a, code of rest)`,

where `pair(x, y) = (x + y) * (x + y + 1) // 2 + y` is Cantor's pairing function.

Write `encode_list(xs)` and `decode_list(z)`. Decoding reverses it: code 0 is `[]`; any other code z gives `(a, r) = unpair(z - 1)`, and the list is `a` followed by the decoding of `r`. Decoding stops because r is smaller than z: pair(a, r) is at least r, so r ≤ z − 1.

Write your own `pair` and `unpair` (use `math.isqrt`). Codes grow very fast, since each extra item roughly squares the code, so lists will have at most 8 items.

For example, `encode_list([])` is `0`, `encode_list([2])` is `4`, and `decode_list(4)` is `[2]`.
--- starter
def encode_list(xs):
    return 0


def decode_list(z):
    return []
--- solution
import math


def pair(x, y):
    d = x + y
    return d * (d + 1) // 2 + y


def unpair(z):
    d = (math.isqrt(8 * z + 1) - 1) // 2
    y = z - d * (d + 1) // 2
    return (d - y, y)


def encode_list(xs):
    code = 0
    for a in reversed(xs):
        code = 1 + pair(a, code)
    return code


def decode_list(z):
    items = []
    while z > 0:
        a, z = unpair(z - 1)
        items.append(a)
    return items
--- hint
Encoding works from the back of the list: the code of the last item's "rest" is 0 (the empty list). Walk the list in reverse, replacing `code` with `1 + pair(a, code)` each time.
--- hint
Decoding works from the front: while `z` is not 0, unpair `z - 1` into the next item and the code of the rest.
--- check case | The examples
(encode_list([]), encode_list([2]), decode_list(4), decode_list(0))
=> (0, 4, [2], [])
--- check case | Order matters
(encode_list([1, 0]), encode_list([0, 1]))
=> (5, 6)
--- check test | Every code below 3,000 decodes to a list that encodes back to it
all(encode_list(decode_list(z)) == z for z in range(3000))
--- check test | Every list of up to 3 items below 6 round-trips, with no two sharing a code
(lambda Ls: all(decode_list(encode_list(L)) == L for L in Ls) and len({encode_list(L) for L in Ls}) == len(Ls))([list(t) for k in range(4) for t in __import__("itertools").product(range(6), repeat=k)])
--- check test | Eight items, with large values
(lambda L: decode_list(encode_list(L)) == L)([5, 0, 12345, 7, 0, 0, 99, 3])

+++ question | Row by row
--- ask
Why does listing ℕ × ℕ as (0, 0), (1, 0), (2, 0), (3, 0), … not show that ℕ × ℕ is countable?
--- choice
Because ℕ × ℕ is uncountable.
--- choice correct
Because the pair (0, 1) never gets a finite position: the first row never ends.
--- choice
Because some pairs appear twice.
--- choice
Because the list is not in increasing order.
--- why
Countable means some list gives every element a finite position. This list is stuck in the first row for ever, so (0, 1) and every later row are never reached. The diagonal walk fixes it: every diagonal is finite.

+++ question | Why the diagonal escapes
--- ask
In the diagonal argument, d(n) = 1 − sₙ(n). Why is d different from s₅?
--- choice
Because d was built after the list was made.
--- choice
Because d differs from s₅ in every bit.
--- choice correct
Because bit 5 of d is the opposite of bit 5 of s₅.
--- choice
Because s₅ is in the list and d is infinite.
--- why
d was built to disagree with the n-th sequence at position n. So it disagrees with s₅ at position 5, whatever the other bits are. One differing bit is enough to make two sequences different.

+++ question | Counting programs
--- ask
Is the set of all Python programs countable?
--- choice correct
Yes: each program is a finite string over a finite alphabet, and those can be listed in shortlex order.
--- choice
No: there are infinitely many programs.
--- choice
No: programs can take any real number as input.
--- choice
Only programs shorter than some fixed length.
--- why
Infinite does not mean uncountable. Listing strings by length, then alphabetically, gives every finite string a finite position, and every program is one. That is why most yes-or-no problems (an uncountable set) have no program that solves them.

=== disc-12 | Relations
--- teach
The last two lessons built functions out of ordered pairs: a function is a set of pairs in which every input appears exactly once. Drop that rule, and allow any set of pairs at all, and you get a **relation**. "Is less than", "divides", "has the same remainder mod 3", "must launch before" and "can talk to" are all relations. This lesson names the four properties that matter, and the two kinds of relation that appear everywhere in computing: **equivalence relations**, which group things, and **partial orders**, which rank them.

### A relation is a set of pairs

A **relation** R from a set A to a set B is a subset of A × B. When (a, b) ∈ R, you write a R b and say "a is related to b". A **relation on A** is a subset of A × A. In Python it is a set of tuples:

```python
nums = range(1, 7)
divides = {(a, b) for a in nums for b in nums if b % a == 0}
print(len(divides))                 # 14
print((2, 6) in divides, (6, 2) in divides)   # True False
```

The order matters: 2 divides 6, but 6 does not divide 2. Picture a relation on A as dots for the elements and an arrow from a to b for each pair. A pair (a, a) is an arrow from a dot back to itself, a **loop**. That picture is a [[directed graph|digraph]].

### Four properties

Let R be a relation on A.

| property | what it says | in words |
|---|---|---|
| **reflexive** | a R a for **every** a ∈ A | every dot has a loop |
| **symmetric** | if a R b, then b R a | every arrow has a partner going back |
| **antisymmetric** | if a R b and b R a, then a = b | no two different dots point at each other |
| **transitive** | if a R b and b R c, then a R c | every two-step path has a shortcut |

Some examples, on the integers unless stated:

| relation | reflexive | symmetric | antisymmetric | transitive |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| a = b | yes | yes | yes | yes |
| a ≤ b | yes | no | yes | yes |
| a < b | no | no | yes | yes |
| a divides b, on positive integers | yes | no | yes | yes |
| a and b have the same remainder mod 3 | yes | yes | no | yes |
| a and b differ by at most 1 | yes | yes | no | no |

The last row is not transitive: 1 and 2 differ by 1, and 2 and 3 do, but 1 and 3 differ by 2.

Checking a property in Python is a direct translation of its definition, with `all`:

```python
nums = range(1, 7)
divides = {(a, b) for a in nums for b in nums if b % a == 0}
print(all((n, n) in divides for n in nums))           # True: reflexive
print(all((b, a) in divides for a, b in divides))     # False: not symmetric
print(sorted(p for p in divides if (p[1], p[0]) not in divides)[:3])
# [(1, 2), (1, 3), (1, 4)]: pairs with no partner going back
```

Three details trip people up:

- **Reflexive needs every element of A**, including ones that appear in no pair. The relation {(1, 1), (2, 2)} is reflexive on {1, 2} but not on {1, 2, 3}.
- **Symmetric and antisymmetric are not opposites.** Equality is both. The relation {(1, 2), (2, 1), (1, 3)} is neither: (1, 2) has its partner, but (1, 3) does not.
- **Properties can hold vacuously.** The empty relation is symmetric, antisymmetric and transitive, because there is no pair to break the rule. (It is reflexive only on the empty set.)

### Equivalence relations

A relation that is reflexive, symmetric and transitive is an **equivalence relation**. It says "these two are the same, in the way I care about": the same remainder mod 3, the same length, the same ground station, the same file contents. It is often written a ∼ b (read "a is equivalent to b").

The **equivalence class** of a is the set of everything equivalent to it:

[a] = {x ∈ A : x ∼ a}.

For "same remainder mod 3" on {0, 1, …, 9} the classes are {0, 3, 6, 9}, {1, 4, 7} and {2, 5, 8}.

A **partition** of A is a collection of non-empty subsets of A, called **blocks**, such that every element of A is in exactly one block.

**Theorem.** The equivalence classes of an equivalence relation on A form a partition of A.

**Proof.** Every element a is in some class, its own, because a ∼ a (reflexive). So the classes are non-empty and cover A. It remains to show that two classes are either equal or disjoint. Suppose [a] and [b] share an element c. Then c ∼ a and c ∼ b. By symmetry a ∼ c, and with c ∼ b, transitivity gives a ∼ b. Now take any x ∈ [a]: x ∼ a and a ∼ b, so x ∼ b by transitivity, and x ∈ [b]. So [a] ⊆ [b]. The same argument with a and b swapped (using b ∼ a, by symmetry) gives [b] ⊆ [a]. So [a] = [b]. ∎

It works backwards too. Given a partition, "a and b are in the same block" is an equivalence relation. So equivalence relations and partitions are two descriptions of the same thing.

The most common way to get an equivalence relation in code is a **key**: a ∼ b when key(a) = key(b). That is always reflexive, symmetric and transitive, because equality of keys is. Grouping by the key gives the classes:

```python
from collections import defaultdict

words = ["io", "moon", "sun", "mars", "pi", "star"]
by_length = defaultdict(list)
for w in words:
    by_length[len(w)].append(w)
print(dict(by_length))
# {2: ['io', 'pi'], 4: ['moon', 'mars', 'star'], 3: ['sun']}
```

Every equivalence relation arises from a key: use "the class of a" as a's key. SQL's [[GROUP BY|group-by]] is this construction.

### Partial orders

A relation that is reflexive, **antisymmetric** and transitive is a **partial order**, often written ⪯ (read "precedes or equals"). A set with a partial order is a **poset**. Examples: ≤ on numbers, ⊆ on sets, "divides" on positive integers, and "task a must be done no later than task b" in a plan.

Two elements are **comparable** if a ⪯ b or b ⪯ a. "Partial" means some pairs may be **incomparable**: neither 2 nor 3 divides the other, and neither {1} ⊆ {2} nor {2} ⊆ {1}. A partial order in which every two elements are comparable is a **total order**, like ≤ on numbers.

A **[[Hasse diagram|hasse]]** draws a finite poset without clutter. Put smaller elements lower, and draw a line from a up to b only when a ≺ b (a ⪯ b and a ≠ b) with nothing strictly between them. Loops and shortcuts are left out, because reflexivity and transitivity put them back.

- a is **minimal** if no other element is below it: there is no b ≠ a with b ⪯ a.
- a is **least** if it is below everything: a ⪯ b for every b.

In the divisors of 12 under "divides", 1 is least and 12 is greatest. In {2, 3, 4, 6} under "divides", 2 and 3 are both minimal, and there is no least element. A poset can have several minimal elements, but at most one least one (if a and b were both least, a ⪯ b and b ⪯ a, so a = b by antisymmetry).

**Theorem.** Every finite non-empty poset has a minimal element.

**Proof.** Start at any element. If it is not minimal, some different element is below it; move there. Repeat. You can never return to an element you visited: if you walked x₁ ≻ x₂ ≻ … ≻ xₖ and xₖ = x₁, transitivity would give x₂ ⪯ x₁ and x₁ ⪯ x₂, so x₁ = x₂ by antisymmetry, but each step moved to a different element. So the walk visits distinct elements, and in a finite set it must stop. Where it stops is minimal. ∎

Repeat the theorem: take a minimal element out, and the rest is still a finite poset, so it has a minimal element too. Writing them down in the order you take them out gives a **total order that agrees with the partial order**, called a **linear extension**. For a plan with prerequisites, that is a valid schedule. A practice problem builds one.

A **strict** partial order, like <, is irreflexive (a R a never holds) and transitive. It is the non-strict one with the loops removed.

### Closures

A relation may lack a property you need. The **transitive closure** R⁺ is the smallest transitive relation that contains R: a R⁺ b exactly when there is a path of one or more arrows from a to b. If R is "there is a direct flight", R⁺ is "you can get there by some sequence of flights". One way to compute it: keep adding (a, c) whenever (a, b) and (b, c) are both there, until nothing changes. The **reflexive closure** adds every loop (a, a), and the **symmetric closure** adds every reversed pair.

### What it costs

Store R as a Python set of tuples, so that "is (a, b) in R?" takes about one step. Let n = |A|.

- **Reflexive:** n lookups. **Symmetric** and **antisymmetric:** one lookup per pair, O(|R|).
- **Transitive,** done naively: for every pair (a, b), look at every pair (c, d) with c = b. Trying all pairs of pairs is O(|R|²), about a billion steps for |R| = 30,000.
- **Transitive,** done well: build, for each a, the set of elements it points to, succ(a). R is transitive exactly when succ(b) ⊆ succ(a) for every pair (a, b). That costs the size of succ(b) per pair, at most O(|R| · n) in total, and far less for most relations.
- **Transitive closure:** Warshall's algorithm does it in O(n³) steps with three nested loops.

### Where it is used

- **Grouping.** GROUP BY, deduplication, and "which files have the same contents" are equivalence classes of a key.
- **Connectivity.** "Can reach each other" in a network is an equivalence relation, and the [[union-find|union-find]] structure maintains its classes as links are added.
- **Build systems and schedules.** Dependencies form a partial order; `make`, package managers and launch checklists all compute a linear extension.
- **Distributed systems.** "Event a happened before event b" is a partial order: two events on different machines with no message between them are incomparable, or **concurrent**. Vector clocks compute this order.
- **Types.** "Is a subtype of" is a partial order; "has the same type" is an equivalence.

**Watch out:** **"close enough" is not transitive.** Comparing floats with a tolerance, `abs(a - b) < 1e-9`, is reflexive and symmetric but not transitive, so it is **not** an equivalence relation. Grouping readings by it can put a and c in one group even though they are far apart, and the result depends on the order you process them. Group by a key instead, such as a rounded value. Also remember, when you check transitivity by hand, the case a R b and b R a: it requires a R a.

::: context digraph Relations as arrows
A directed graph is a set of dots, called vertices, and arrows between them, called edges. A relation on A is exactly a directed graph whose vertices are A. The properties become pictures: reflexive means a loop at every vertex, symmetric means arrows come in pairs, and transitive means every path of two arrows has a direct arrow too. The graph theory lessons later in the course use this picture all the time.
:::

::: context group-by GROUP BY is a partition
`SELECT station, COUNT(*) FROM passes GROUP BY station` splits the rows into equivalence classes, one per station, and returns one row per class. The key is the `station` column, so two rows are equivalent exactly when their stations are equal. Grouping by several columns uses the tuple of their values as the key, which is still an equivalence relation.
:::

::: context hasse A picture of "divides"
The Hasse diagram of the divisors of 12 under "divides". 1 divides everything, so it is at the bottom. A line joins two numbers when the lower one divides the upper one with nothing in between: 2 divides 4, but the line from 2 to 12 is left out, because it goes through 4 or 6. 4 and 6 are incomparable: neither divides the other.

:::

::: context union-find Keeping classes as links arrive
Union-find keeps a partition of n items and supports two operations: join the classes of a and b, and ask whether a and b are in the same class. Each class is a tree with a chosen representative at the root, and the answer is "yes" when two items have the same root. With two tricks, union by rank and path compression, each operation takes nearly constant time. It is how Kruskal's algorithm and many network tools track connectivity.
:::
--- task
Write `properties(pairs, elements)`. `elements` is a set, and `pairs` is a set of tuples `(a, b)`: a relation on `elements`. Return the set of the property names that hold, from `"reflexive"`, `"symmetric"`, `"antisymmetric"` and `"transitive"`.

- If a pair uses something that is not in `elements`, raise `ValueError`.
- Transitivity must be checked quickly: a check uses an equivalence relation with 30,000 pairs, where comparing every pair with every other pair would take far too long. Build, for each element, the set of elements it points to.

For example, `properties({(1, 2), (2, 1)}, {1, 2})` is `{"symmetric"}`: it is not transitive, because (1, 2) and (2, 1) would need (1, 1).
--- starter
def properties(pairs, elements):
    return set()
--- solution
def properties(pairs, elements):
    for a, b in pairs:
        if a not in elements or b not in elements:
            raise ValueError("a pair uses an element outside the set")
    after = {a: set() for a in elements}
    for a, b in pairs:
        after[a].add(b)
    found = set()
    if all((a, a) in pairs for a in elements):
        found.add("reflexive")
    if all((b, a) in pairs for a, b in pairs):
        found.add("symmetric")
    if all(a == b or (b, a) not in pairs for a, b in pairs):
        found.add("antisymmetric")
    if all(after[b] <= after[a] for a, b in pairs):
        found.add("transitive")
    return found
--- hint
Reflexive, symmetric and antisymmetric are each one `all(...)` over the elements or over the pairs, translated from the definitions.
--- hint
For transitivity, build `after`, a dict mapping each element to the set of elements it points to. If a points to b, then everything b points to must also be something a points to.
--- hint
So the relation is transitive exactly when `after[b] <= after[a]` for every pair `(a, b)`. Antisymmetric: for every pair with `a != b`, the reversed pair is missing.
--- check case | The example: the missing loop
properties({(1, 2), (2, 1)}, {1, 2})
=> {"symmetric"}
?? (1, 2) and (2, 1) are both in the relation, so transitivity requires (1, 1).
--- check case | Divides, on 1 to 6
properties({(a, b) for a in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7) if b % a == 0}, set(range(1, 7)))
=> {"reflexive", "antisymmetric", "transitive"}
--- check case | Same remainder mod 3: an equivalence relation
properties({(a, b) for a in range(9) for b in range(9) if a % 3 == b % 3}, set(range(9)))
=> {"reflexive", "symmetric", "transitive"}
--- check case | Less than
properties({(a, b) for a in range(4) for b in range(4) if a < b}, set(range(4)))
=> {"antisymmetric", "transitive"}
--- check case | Differ by at most 1
properties({(a, b) for a in range(6) for b in range(6) if abs(a - b) <= 1}, set(range(6)))
=> {"reflexive", "symmetric"}
--- check case | An element with no pairs spoils reflexivity
properties({(1, 1), (2, 2)}, {1, 2, 3})
=> {"symmetric", "antisymmetric", "transitive"}
--- check case | Empty relations hold vacuously
(properties(set(), {1, 2}), properties(set(), set()))
=> ({"symmetric", "antisymmetric", "transitive"}, {"reflexive", "symmetric", "antisymmetric", "transitive"})
--- check case | Neither symmetric nor antisymmetric
properties({(1, 2), (2, 1), (1, 3), (1, 1), (2, 2), (3, 3)}, {1, 2, 3})
=> {"reflexive"}
--- check test | A pair outside the set raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: properties({(1, 5)}, {1, 2}))
--- check test | 300 elements in 3 classes: 30,000 pairs
properties({(a, b) for a in range(300) for b in range(300) if a % 3 == b % 3}, set(range(300))) == {"reflexive", "symmetric", "transitive"}
?? Comparing every pair with every other pair is 900 million steps. Build a dict of what each element points to, and use subset tests.
--- check test | The same with one pair taken out
(lambda R: properties(R - {(0, 3)}, set(range(300))) == {"reflexive"})({(a, b) for a in range(300) for b in range(300) if a % 3 == b % 3})

+++ practice | The classes of an equivalence
--- task
`pairs` is an equivalence relation on the set `elements`, given as a set of tuples. Write `classes(pairs, elements)` that returns its equivalence classes as a list of lists: each class sorted, and the classes sorted by their first (smallest) element.

For example, with the relation "same remainder mod 3" on {0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5}, the answer is `[[0, 3], [1, 4], [2, 5]]`. With no elements, return `[]`.
--- starter
def classes(pairs, elements):
    return [sorted(elements)]
--- solution
def classes(pairs, elements):
    related = {a: {a} for a in elements}
    for a, b in pairs:
        related[a].add(b)
    seen = set()
    result = []
    for a in sorted(elements):
        if a not in seen:
            block = sorted(related[a])
            seen.update(block)
            result.append(block)
    return result
--- hint
The class of `a` is the set of every `b` with `(a, b)` in the relation. Collect those in a dict, one set per element.
--- hint
Walk the elements in sorted order. An element you have not seen yet is the smallest of a new class; add its class to the answer and mark all its members as seen.
--- check case | The example
classes({(a, b) for a in range(6) for b in range(6) if a % 3 == b % 3}, set(range(6)))
=> [[0, 3], [1, 4], [2, 5]]
--- check case | Equality: every class has one element
classes({(a, a) for a in "xyz"}, {"x", "y", "z"})
=> [["x"], ["y"], ["z"]]
--- check case | Everything equivalent, and nothing at all
(classes({(a, b) for a in range(4) for b in range(4)}, set(range(4))), classes(set(), set()))
=> ([[0, 1, 2, 3]], [])
--- check case | Same length, on words
classes({(a, b) for a in ["io", "sun", "pi", "mars", "ant"] for b in ["io", "sun", "pi", "mars", "ant"] if len(a) == len(b)}, {"io", "sun", "pi", "mars", "ant"})
=> [["ant", "sun"], ["io", "pi"], ["mars"]]
--- check test | 700 elements in 7 classes
(lambda E: classes({(a, b) for a in E for b in E if a % 7 == b % 7}, set(E)) == [list(range(r, 700, 7)) for r in range(7)])(range(700))

+++ practice | From blocks back to a relation
--- task
Every partition gives an equivalence relation: a ∼ b when a and b are in the same block. Write `relation_from_blocks(blocks)`. `blocks` is a list of sets. Return the relation as a set of tuples `(a, b)`, one for every a and b in the same block (including `(a, a)`).

If the blocks are not a partition of their union, raise `ValueError`: that happens when some block is empty, or when two blocks share an element.

For example, `relation_from_blocks([{1, 2}, {3}])` is `{(1, 1), (1, 2), (2, 1), (2, 2), (3, 3)}`.
--- starter
def relation_from_blocks(blocks):
    return set()
--- solution
def relation_from_blocks(blocks):
    seen = set()
    pairs = set()
    for block in blocks:
        if not block or not seen.isdisjoint(block):
            raise ValueError("not a partition")
        seen |= block
        pairs |= {(a, b) for a in block for b in block}
    return pairs
--- hint
Each block contributes every pair `(a, b)` with both `a` and `b` in it: a comprehension over two loops.
--- hint
To catch overlaps, keep the set of elements seen in earlier blocks, and check that each new block is disjoint from it (and not empty) before adding it.
--- check case | The example
relation_from_blocks([{1, 2}, {3}])
=> {(1, 1), (1, 2), (2, 1), (2, 2), (3, 3)}
--- check case | No blocks
relation_from_blocks([])
=> set()
--- check test | Overlapping blocks, or an empty block, raise ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: relation_from_blocks([{1, 2}, {2, 3}])) and raises(ValueError, lambda: relation_from_blocks([{1}, set()]))
--- check test | The size is the sum of the squares of the block sizes
len(relation_from_blocks([set(range(0, 10)), set(range(10, 13)), {99}])) == 100 + 9 + 1
--- check test | The result is reflexive, symmetric and transitive
(lambda R, E: all((a, a) in R for a in E) and all((b, a) in R for a, b in R) and all((a, d) in R for a, b in R for c, d in R if b == c))(relation_from_blocks([{"a", "b", "c"}, {"d"}, {"e", "f"}]), set("abcdef"))

+++ practice | Transitive closure
--- task
Write `transitive_closure(pairs)` that returns the transitive closure of the relation `pairs` (a set of tuples): the smallest transitive relation that contains it. `(a, c)` is in the closure exactly when you can get from `a` to `c` by following one or more pairs. Do not change `pairs`.

For example, `transitive_closure({(1, 2), (2, 3)})` is `{(1, 2), (2, 3), (1, 3)}`, and `transitive_closure({(1, 2), (2, 1)})` is `{(1, 2), (2, 1), (1, 1), (2, 2)}`: going 1 → 2 → 1 is a path from 1 to itself.
--- starter
def transitive_closure(pairs):
    return set(pairs)
--- solution
def transitive_closure(pairs):
    closure = set(pairs)
    while True:
        new = {(a, d) for a, b in closure for c, d in closure if b == c} - closure
        if not new:
            return closure
        closure |= new
--- hint
Start from a copy of `pairs`. Look for every two-step path (a, b), (b, d) whose shortcut (a, d) is missing, and add all of them.
--- hint
Adding shortcuts can create new two-step paths, so repeat until a round adds nothing. That final set is transitive, and everything in it was forced, so it is the smallest.
--- check case | The examples
(transitive_closure({(1, 2), (2, 3)}), transitive_closure({(1, 2), (2, 1)}))
=> ({(1, 2), (2, 3), (1, 3)}, {(1, 2), (2, 1), (1, 1), (2, 2)})
--- check case | Empty and already transitive
(transitive_closure(set()), transitive_closure({(1, 1), (1, 2)}))
=> (set(), {(1, 1), (1, 2)})
--- check case | A chain of 5
len(transitive_closure({(k, k + 1) for k in range(4)}))
=> 10
--- check test | A cycle of 6: everything reaches everything
transitive_closure({(k, (k + 1) % 6) for k in range(6)}) == {(a, b) for a in range(6) for b in range(6)}
--- check test | Does not change its input, and a chain of 40
(lambda R: (transitive_closure(R) == {(a, b) for a in range(40) for b in range(40) if a < b}) and len(R) == 39)({(k, k + 1) for k in range(39)})

+++ practice | Minimal and maximal
--- task
`pairs` is meant to be a partial order ⪯ on `elements`: reflexive, antisymmetric and transitive. Write `extremes(pairs, elements)` that returns a tuple `(minimal, maximal)` of two sets.

- An element a is **minimal** if no other element b has (b, a) in the relation, and **maximal** if no other element b has (a, b) in it. The loop (a, a) does not count.
- If the relation is not a partial order on `elements`, raise `ValueError`. Check all three properties, and reflexivity for every element.
- With no elements, return `(set(), set())`. An element comparable to nothing else is both minimal and maximal.

For example, for "divides" on {2, 3, 4, 6, 12}, the answer is `({2, 3}, {12})`.
--- starter
def extremes(pairs, elements):
    return (set(), set())
--- solution
def extremes(pairs, elements):
    reflexive = all((a, a) in pairs for a in elements)
    antisymmetric = all(a == b or (b, a) not in pairs for a, b in pairs)
    transitive = all((a, d) in pairs for a, b in pairs for c, d in pairs if b == c)
    if not (reflexive and antisymmetric and transitive):
        raise ValueError("not a partial order")
    minimal = {a for a in elements if not any(b != a and (b, a) in pairs for b in elements)}
    maximal = {a for a in elements if not any(b != a and (a, b) in pairs for b in elements)}
    return (minimal, maximal)
--- hint
First check the three properties and raise if any fails. The relations here are small, so checking transitivity over every pair of pairs is fine.
--- hint
Minimal: keep `a` when no other element `b` has `(b, a)` in the relation. Maximal is the same with the pair turned round. Remember `b != a`, or the loop (a, a) would make nothing minimal.
--- check case | The example: divides
extremes({(a, b) for a in [2, 3, 4, 6, 12] for b in [2, 3, 4, 6, 12] if b % a == 0}, {2, 3, 4, 6, 12})
=> ({2, 3}, {12})
--- check case | A total order has one of each
extremes({(a, b) for a in range(5) for b in range(5) if a <= b}, set(range(5)))
=> ({0}, {4})
--- check case | Only loops: everything is both
extremes({(a, a) for a in "pqr"}, {"p", "q", "r"})
=> ({"p", "q", "r"}, {"p", "q", "r"})
--- check case | No elements
extremes(set(), set())
=> (set(), set())
--- check test | Not a partial order raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: extremes({(1, 1), (2, 2), (1, 2), (2, 1)}, {1, 2})) and raises(ValueError, lambda: extremes({(1, 1)}, {1, 2})) and raises(ValueError, lambda: extremes({(1, 1), (2, 2), (3, 3), (1, 2), (2, 3)}, {1, 2, 3}))
?? Check reflexivity for every element, antisymmetry, and transitivity, before looking for extremes.
--- check case | Subsets of {1, 2} under ⊆
(lambda S: extremes({(a, b) for a in S for b in S if a <= b}, set(S)))([frozenset(), frozenset({1}), frozenset({2}), frozenset({1, 2})])
=> ({frozenset()}, {frozenset({1, 2})})

+++ practice | Debug: the transitivity check that forgets loops
--- task
`is_transitive(pairs)` should return `True` exactly when the relation `pairs` (a set of tuples) is transitive: whenever (a, b) and (b, d) are both in it, so is (a, d).

It returns `True` for `{(1, 2), (2, 1)}`, but that relation is not transitive: (1, 2) and (2, 1) require (1, 1). Fix it.
--- starter
def is_transitive(pairs):
    for a, b in pairs:
        for c, d in pairs:
            if b == c and a != d and (a, d) not in pairs:
                return False
    return True
--- solution
def is_transitive(pairs):
    for a, b in pairs:
        for c, d in pairs:
            if b == c and (a, d) not in pairs:
                return False
    return True
--- hint
Follow (1, 2) then (2, 1) through the loop. Which shortcut is required, and why does the code not look for it?
--- hint
The definition has no exception for a = d. Drop the extra condition.
--- check case | The missing loop
is_transitive({(1, 2), (2, 1)})
=> False
--- check case | With the loops it is transitive
is_transitive({(1, 2), (2, 1), (1, 1), (2, 2)})
=> True
--- check case | Empty, a chain, a chain without its shortcut
(is_transitive(set()), is_transitive({(1, 2), (2, 3), (1, 3)}), is_transitive({(1, 2), (2, 3)}))
=> (True, True, False)
--- check case | Less than on 0 to 9
is_transitive({(a, b) for a in range(10) for b in range(10) if a < b})
=> True

+++ practice | A launch-day schedule
--- task
Launch day has a set of `tasks` (strings), and `before` is a set of pairs `(x, y)` meaning task `x` must be finished before task `y` starts. Every name in `before` is in `tasks`.

Write `schedule(tasks, before)` that returns a list of all the tasks in an order that respects every pair. Build it the way the proof of linear extensions does: repeatedly take a task all of whose prerequisites are already scheduled, and when several are ready, take the one that comes first alphabetically. If at some point no task is ready but some are left, the pairs contain a cycle, and no schedule exists: return `None`.

It must handle 2,000 tasks. Count each task's unscheduled prerequisites, rather than rescanning all the pairs at every step.

For example, `schedule({"fuel", "arm", "vent", "go"}, {("fuel", "go"), ("arm", "go"), ("vent", "fuel")})` is `["arm", "vent", "fuel", "go"]`.
--- starter
def schedule(tasks, before):
    return sorted(tasks)
--- solution
def schedule(tasks, before):
    waiting = {t: 0 for t in tasks}
    unlocks = {t: [] for t in tasks}
    for x, y in before:
        waiting[y] += 1
        unlocks[x].append(y)
    ready = {t for t in tasks if waiting[t] == 0}
    order = []
    while ready:
        t = min(ready)
        ready.remove(t)
        order.append(t)
        for u in unlocks[t]:
            waiting[u] -= 1
            if waiting[u] == 0:
                ready.add(u)
    if len(order) < len(tasks):
        return None
    return order
--- hint
Keep, for each task, how many prerequisites are still unscheduled, and a list of the tasks it unlocks. The tasks with count 0 are ready.
--- hint
Repeatedly take `min(ready)`, append it, and lower the count of each task it unlocks; a task whose count reaches 0 becomes ready. If the loop ends with tasks left over, return `None`.
--- check case | The example
schedule({"fuel", "arm", "vent", "go"}, {("fuel", "go"), ("arm", "go"), ("vent", "fuel")})
=> ["arm", "vent", "fuel", "go"]
--- check case | No constraints: alphabetical
schedule({"c", "a", "b"}, set())
=> ["a", "b", "c"]
--- check case | A cycle
schedule({"a", "b", "c"}, {("a", "b"), ("b", "c"), ("c", "a")})
=> None
--- check case | Empty day
schedule(set(), set())
=> []
--- check test | 2,000 tasks in a long chain, named so that alphabetical order is backwards
(lambda n: schedule({"t%04d" % k for k in range(n)}, {("t%04d" % (k + 1), "t%04d" % k) for k in range(n - 1)}) == ["t%04d" % k for k in range(n - 1, -1, -1)])(2000)
--- check test | Every pair is respected on a larger plan
(lambda T, B: (lambda order: order is not None and len(order) == len(T) and all(order.index(x) < order.index(y) for x, y in B))(schedule(T, B)))({"s%03d" % k for k in range(300)}, {("s%03d" % a, "s%03d" % b) for a in range(300) for b in (a * 7 % 300, a * 13 % 300) if a < b})

+++ question | Close enough
--- ask
Rovers are "close" when they are within 1 metre of each other. Is "close" an equivalence relation on the rovers?
--- choice
Yes: it is reflexive, symmetric and transitive.
--- choice correct
No: it is not transitive. A can be close to B and B close to C while A and C are almost 2 metres apart.
--- choice
No: it is not symmetric.
--- choice
No: it is not reflexive.
--- why
Every rover is within 1 metre of itself, and closeness goes both ways, but closeness does not chain. Tolerance comparisons are the classic relation that looks like an equivalence and is not.

+++ question | Both at once
--- ask
Can a relation be both symmetric and antisymmetric?
--- choice
No: they are opposites.
--- choice correct
Yes: equality is both, and so is any relation made only of loops (a, a).
--- choice
Only the empty relation.
--- choice
Only on a set with one element.
--- why
Symmetric says every arrow has a partner going back; antisymmetric says two different elements never point at each other. A relation with no arrows between different elements satisfies both. Neither property is the negation of the other.

+++ question | Two classes that meet
--- ask
In the proof that equivalence classes form a partition, [a] and [b] share an element c, so c ∼ a and c ∼ b. Which properties give a ∼ b?
--- choice
Reflexivity alone.
--- choice correct
Symmetry turns c ∼ a into a ∼ c, then transitivity with c ∼ b gives a ∼ b.
--- choice
Transitivity alone, applied to c ∼ a and c ∼ b.
--- choice
Antisymmetry.
--- why
Transitivity needs a chain a ∼ c, c ∼ b, with the middle element in the right places. The facts come as c ∼ a and c ∼ b, so symmetry is needed first to turn one of them round.

=== disc-13 | Counting
--- teach
The last lessons measured sets by their size, |A|, and compared sizes with functions. This lesson is about finding |A| when A is far too big to list: how many callsigns, passwords, launch orders or crews there are. A handful of rules answer almost every such question, and Python checks each rule by brute force on small cases, which is how you catch a wrong formula before it matters.

### The sum rule

If a task can be done in one of m ways **or** in one of n other ways, with no way counted in both, there are m + n ways.

In set language: if A and B are **disjoint** finite sets, |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B|. The same holds for any number of pairwise disjoint sets. It is inclusion–exclusion from the sets lesson with the overlap equal to zero. If the options overlap, you must subtract the overlap, or you count it twice.

Example: a mission patch shows either one of 12 constellations or one of 7 planets. That is 12 + 7 = 19 designs.

### The product rule

If a task is done in steps, where the first step can be done in n₁ ways, and **whatever happened before**, the second in n₂ ways, and so on up to step k, then the whole task can be done in n₁ · n₂ · … · nₖ ways.

In set language it is |A₁ × A₂ × … × Aₖ| = |A₁| · |A₂| · … · |Aₖ|, from the sets lesson. The rule allows later steps to depend on earlier ones, as long as the **number** of choices does not.

Examples:

- A door code is 4 digits, repeats allowed: 10 · 10 · 10 · 10 = 10⁴ = 10,000 codes.
- A bit string of length n: 2ⁿ strings.
- A function from A to B: |B| choices for each of the |A| inputs, so |B|^|A| functions, as in the functions lesson.

In general, a **sequence of length k from n symbols, repeats allowed**, can be chosen in **nᵏ** ways. These are **arrangements with repetition**.

### Permutations

Now forbid repeats. How many ways can 3 of 8 experiments fill 3 time slots, in order? The first slot has 8 choices, the second 7 (any but the first), the third 6: 8 · 7 · 6 = 336.

A **k-permutation** of n things is an ordered list of k different things chosen from n. Their number is

P(n, k) = n · (n − 1) · … · (n − k + 1),

a product of k factors, by the product rule. With k = n, you arrange all n things, and the product n · (n − 1) · … · 2 · 1 is written **n!**, read "n factorial". So P(n, k) = n! / (n − k)!.

- **0! = 1.** There is exactly one way to arrange nothing: the empty list. It is also the [[empty product|empty-product]], the product of no numbers.
- P(n, k) = 0 when k > n: you cannot pick more different things than there are.
- P(|B|, |A|) counts the **injective** functions from A to B: the first input has |B| choices of output, the next |B| − 1, and so on.

### The division rule

Sometimes it is easier to count something with repeats, and then remove them. **If a count N counts every object you want exactly d times, the number of objects is N / d.**

Example: 5 crew sit around a round table, and only who sits next to whom matters, not which chair. Seat them in a row of 5 chairs: 5! = 120 ways. Now bend the row into a circle. Each circular seating comes from exactly 5 row seatings, one for each person who could be in the first chair. So there are 120 / 5 = 24 circular seatings, which is (5 − 1)!.

### Combinations

A **k-combination** of n things is a k-element **subset**: order does not matter. Its count is written C(n, k) or (n over k), and read "n choose k".

**Theorem.** C(n, k) = P(n, k) / k! = n! / (k! · (n − k)!).

**Proof,** by the division rule. Count the ordered lists of k different things: P(n, k). Each k-element subset appears in that count exactly k! times, once for each order of its k elements. So the number of subsets is P(n, k) / k!. ∎

Choosing 3 of 8 experiments to fly, with no order, is C(8, 3) = 336 / 6 = 56.

Useful facts, each with a one-line reason:

- **C(n, 0) = C(n, n) = 1.** One empty subset; one subset that is everything.
- **C(n, k) = C(n, n − k).** Choosing the k to take is the same as choosing the n − k to leave: taking complements is a bijection.
- **C(n, k) = 0 when k > n.**
- **C(n, 0) + C(n, 1) + … + C(n, n) = 2ⁿ.** Both sides count all subsets of an n-element set, the left side grouped by size.

**Computing it exactly.** Factorials get enormous: 1000! has 2,568 digits, although C(1000, 3) has only 9. A better way multiplies and divides alternately:

C(n, k) = (n − k + 1)/1 · (n − k + 2)/2 · … · (n − k + k)/k.

After i of these steps the running value is exactly C(n − k + i, i), which is a whole number, so dividing at each step with `//` never leaves a remainder. And because C(n, k) = C(n, n − k), you can always use the smaller of k and n − k: C(10⁶, 999,998) takes 2 steps, not 999,998.

### Arrangements of a word

How many different strings can be made by rearranging the letters of ORBIT? All 5 letters differ, so 5! = 120. What about LEVEL, which has two Ls and two Es?

Label the copies to make them different: L₁ E₁ V E₂ L₂. There are 5! = 120 orders. Now erase the labels. Each unlabelled word came from 2! · 2! = 4 labelled ones (swap the Ls or not, swap the Es or not). By the division rule there are 120 / 4 = 30 words.

**Theorem.** A word of n letters, in which the letter types appear n₁, n₂, …, nₘ times, has n! / (n₁! · n₂! · … · nₘ!) distinct arrangements.

### Choosing with repetition: stars and bars

Put 10 identical fuel units into 3 labelled tanks. How many ways? Only the amounts matter, so a way is a solution of x₁ + x₂ + x₃ = 10 in non-negative whole numbers.

Write each way as [[a row of 10 stars and 2 bars|stars]]. The bars cut the stars into 3 groups, from left to right: tank 1, tank 2, tank 3. For example `***|*****|**` is 3, 5 and 2, and `||**********` is 0, 0 and 10. Every way gives exactly one row, and every row gives exactly one way: a bijection. A row has 12 positions, and choosing which 2 of them hold bars decides it. So the answer is C(12, 2) = 66.

**Theorem.** The number of ways to choose k things from n kinds, repeats allowed and order ignored, is C(n + k − 1, k). (It is also the number of non-negative whole-number solutions of x₁ + … + xₙ = k: k stars and n − 1 bars.)

### The four basic models

| | order matters | order does not matter |
|---|---|---|
| **repeats allowed** | nᵏ | C(n + k − 1, k) |
| **no repeats** | P(n, k) | C(n, k) |

Before you count, ask the two questions in the table's margins: does order matter, and may things repeat?

### Python counts, and checks

The `math` module has all of these as exact integer functions: `math.factorial(n)`, `math.perm(n, k)` and `math.comb(n, k)`. The `itertools` module can list the objects themselves, which is how you check a formula on small cases: `permutations(items, k)` gives the ordered lists, `combinations(items, k)` the subsets, `combinations_with_replacement(items, k)` the choices with repeats, and `product` the sequences.

```python
from itertools import combinations_with_replacement, permutations
import math

kinds = "abc"
print(len(list(permutations("abcde", 3))), math.perm(5, 3))            # 60 60
print(len(list(combinations_with_replacement(kinds, 4))), math.comb(6, 4))   # 15 15
print(math.factorial(20))    # 2432902008176640000
```

Agreement on small cases is not a proof, but disagreement is a disproof, and it finds mistakes fast.

### What it costs

- **Listing is hopeless beyond small sizes.** 20! is about 2.4 · 10¹⁸. At a billion arrangements a second, listing them takes 77 years. Count with formulas; list only to check.
- **Formulas are cheap.** P(n, k) takes k multiplications, and C(n, k) about min(k, n − k) multiplications and divisions. The numbers are big integers, so each step costs more as they grow, but C(1000, 500), with 300 digits, takes well under a millisecond.
- **Factorial formulas waste work.** n! / (k! (n − k)!) with n = 10⁶ computes a number with 5.5 million digits to get an answer that may be small.
- **Floats are wrong here.** A float holds about 16 significant digits, so `math.factorial(25) / 2` is already rounded. Counts are integers: use `//`.

### Where it is used

- **Security.** A password's strength is the size of the set it came from. 8 lowercase letters give 26⁸ ≈ 2 · 10¹¹ passwords; a 128-bit key gives 2¹²⁸. The [[bits of entropy|entropy]] are the logarithm of the count.
- **Testing.** A form with 10 yes-or-no options has 2¹⁰ = 1,024 combinations; that count decides whether you test them all or only the pairs.
- **Probability,** in lesson 16, is counting: the chance of an event is (ways it happens) / (all ways), when all ways are equally likely.
- **Algorithms.** Brute force over all orders of n items is n!, over all subsets 2ⁿ. Counting is how you know it will not finish.
- **Planning.** Ways to choose 4 crew from 12 candidates, C(12, 4) = 495; ways to order 6 burns, 6! = 720.

**Watch out:** **overcounting**, especially with "at least one". How many 4-digit codes contain at least one 7? A tempting count: choose the position of the 7 (4 ways), then fill the other three positions freely (10³ ways), giving 4,000. But 7771 is counted three times, once for each 7 you could call "the" 7. The product rule needs each object to come from **exactly one** sequence of choices. The fix is to [[count the complement|complement]]: all codes minus codes with no 7, 10⁴ − 9⁴ = 3,439. Also watch the two model questions: counting ordered lists when you wanted sets is off by a factor of k!.

::: context empty-product Why 0! is 1
A product of no numbers is 1, the same way a sum of no numbers is 0: 1 is what you start from before multiplying anything in, as `math.prod([])` returns. With 0! = 1 the formulas work at the edges: C(n, 0) = n! / (0! · n!) = 1, one empty subset, and P(n, n) = n! / 0! = n!. Choosing a different value would force every formula to carry a special case.
:::

::: context stars A picture of stars and bars
Each row of 10 stars and 2 bars is one way to fill 3 tanks. The bars are dividers: the stars left of the first bar go in tank 1, those between the bars in tank 2, the rest in tank 3. Choosing the 2 bar positions among 12 decides everything.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 320 90" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <g font-size="18" fill="#1d6fd1" font-family="monospace">
    <text x="10" y="30">***|*****|**</text>
    <text x="10" y="70">||**********</text>
  </g>
  <g font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">
    <text x="190" y="30">tanks: 3, 5, 2</text>
    <text x="190" y="70">tanks: 0, 0, 10</text>
  </g>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context entropy Counting in bits
If a secret is chosen uniformly from N possibilities, it has log₂ N bits of entropy: an attacker who guesses in order needs N / 2 guesses on average. 4 random words from a list of 7,776 give 7,776⁴ ≈ 3.7 · 10¹⁵ phrases, about 52 bits. A 12-character password from 94 symbols has 94¹² ≈ 4.8 · 10²³, about 79 bits. Each extra bit doubles the attacker's work. Real passwords are not uniform, which is why their true entropy is lower.
:::

::: context complement Counting what you do not want
When "at least one" makes a direct count messy, count everything and subtract the objects with none. It is the sum rule turned round: every object either has at least one, or has none, and not both, so total = (at least one) + (none). The "none" case is usually a clean product. The same trick drives the birthday problem in probability: the chance that some two of 23 people share a birthday is 1 minus the chance that all 23 differ.
:::

--- task
Write `perm(n, k)` and `comb(n, k)` from scratch, returning exact integers. Do not use `math.comb`, `math.perm` or `math.factorial`.

- `perm(n, k)` is the number of ordered lists of k different things from n: n · (n − 1) · … · (n − k + 1).
- `comb(n, k)` is the number of k-element subsets of an n-element set. Use the step-by-step product (n − k + i) / i for i = 1 to k, dividing with `//` at each step, and use the smaller of `k` and `n - k`. It must be fast for `comb(10**6, 999997)` and exact for `comb(10**18, 2)`.
- Both return 0 when `k` is negative or bigger than `n`, and raise `ValueError` when `n` is negative.

For example, `perm(6, 2)` is `30` and `comb(6, 2)` is `15`.
--- starter
def perm(n, k):
    return 0


def comb(n, k):
    return 0
--- solution
def perm(n, k):
    if n < 0:
        raise ValueError("n must be at least 0")
    if k < 0 or k > n:
        return 0
    result = 1
    for i in range(k):
        result *= n - i
    return result


def comb(n, k):
    if n < 0:
        raise ValueError("n must be at least 0")
    if k < 0 or k > n:
        return 0
    k = min(k, n - k)
    result = 1
    for i in range(1, k + 1):
        result = result * (n - k + i) // i
    return result
--- hint
`perm` is a product of `k` factors: n, n − 1, … down to n − k + 1. Handle the edge cases first.
--- hint
In `comb`, first replace `k` by `min(k, n - k)`. Then start from 1 and, for i from 1 to k, multiply by `n - k + i` and then divide by `i` with `//`.
--- hint
Multiply before you divide. After step i the value is C(n − k + i, i), a whole number, so the division is exact. Dividing first would lose remainders.
--- check case | Small values
(perm(6, 2), comb(6, 2), perm(5, 5), comb(52, 5))
=> (30, 15, 120, 2598960)
--- check case | k = 0 and n = 0
(perm(10, 0), comb(10, 0), perm(0, 0), comb(0, 0))
=> (1, 1, 1, 1)
--- check case | k too big or negative gives 0
(perm(3, 4), comb(3, 4), perm(4, -1), comb(4, -1))
=> (0, 0, 0, 0)
--- check test | Negative n raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: comb(-1, 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: perm(-3, 2))
--- check test | Agrees with the library for every n below 60
all(comb(n, k) == __import__("math").comb(n, k) and perm(n, k) == __import__("math").perm(n, k) for n in range(60) for k in range(n + 1))
--- check test | Each row adds up to 2 to the power n
all(sum(comb(n, k) for k in range(n + 1)) == 2 ** n for n in range(40))
--- check case | Huge n, small k: exact
(comb(10 ** 18, 2), perm(10 ** 18, 2))
=> (499999999999999999500000000000000000, 999999999999999999000000000000000000)
--- check test | k close to n is fast
comb(10 ** 6, 999997) == 166666166667000000
?? Use C(n, k) = C(n, n − k), so this takes 3 steps instead of 999,997.
--- check test | A 300-digit answer
comb(1000, 500) == __import__("math").comb(1000, 500)
--- check source absent | Does not use the library's counting functions
math\.(comb|perm|factorial)|from\s+math\s+import[^\n]*\b(comb|perm|factorial)\b

+++ practice | Callsigns
--- task
A spacecraft callsign is `letters` capital letters (from the 26) followed by `digits` digits (from the 10). Digits may repeat. Letters may repeat only when `repeat_letters` is `True`; when it is `False`, all the letters must be different.

Write `count_callsigns(letters, digits, repeat_letters)` that returns how many callsigns there are. You may use `math.perm`.

For example, `count_callsigns(2, 3, True)` is `676000` and `count_callsigns(2, 3, False)` is `650000`. With 0 letters and 0 digits there is exactly one callsign, the empty one. With 27 different letters there are none.
--- starter
def count_callsigns(letters, digits, repeat_letters):
    return 26 * letters + 10 * digits
--- solution
import math


def count_callsigns(letters, digits, repeat_letters):
    if repeat_letters:
        letter_part = 26 ** letters
    else:
        letter_part = math.perm(26, letters)
    return letter_part * 10 ** digits
--- hint
The product rule: (ways to choose the letters) times (ways to choose the digits).
--- hint
Letters with repeats: 26 to the power `letters`. Without repeats: P(26, letters), which `math.perm` gives, and which is 0 when there are more than 26.
--- check case | The examples
(count_callsigns(2, 3, True), count_callsigns(2, 3, False))
=> (676000, 650000)
--- check case | Edge cases
(count_callsigns(0, 0, True), count_callsigns(0, 0, False), count_callsigns(27, 0, False), count_callsigns(26, 0, False))
=> (1, 1, 0, 403291461126605635584000000)
--- check case | Only digits, only letters
(count_callsigns(0, 4, False), count_callsigns(3, 0, True))
=> (10000, 17576)
--- check test | Agrees with listing: two different letters, then one digit
len([t for t in __import__("itertools").product("ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ", repeat=2) if t[0] != t[1]]) * 10 == count_callsigns(2, 1, False)

+++ practice | Rearranging a word
--- task
Write `arrangements(word)` that returns how many different strings can be made by rearranging all the letters of `word`. Letters that appear more than once make some rearrangements look the same. Use the formula n! / (n₁! · n₂! · …), where n is the length and each nᵢ is how many times one letter appears (`Counter` counts them). Do not list the rearrangements: a check uses a word of 40 letters.

For example, `arrangements("LEVEL")` is `30`, `arrangements("ORBIT")` is `120`, and `arrangements("")` is `1`.
--- starter
def arrangements(word):
    return len(word)
--- solution
import math
from collections import Counter


def arrangements(word):
    result = math.factorial(len(word))
    for count in Counter(word).values():
        result //= math.factorial(count)
    return result
--- hint
Start from the factorial of the length: the number of orders if every letter were different.
--- hint
Each letter that appears c times makes every word appear c! times over, so divide by c! for each letter, with `//`.
--- check case | The examples
(arrangements("LEVEL"), arrangements("ORBIT"), arrangements(""))
=> (30, 120, 1)
--- check case | MISSISSIPPI, and one repeated letter
(arrangements("MISSISSIPPI"), arrangements("aaaa"))
=> (34650, 1)
--- check test | Agrees with listing, for short words
all(arrangements(w) == len(set(__import__("itertools").permutations(w))) for w in ["abc", "aab", "abab", "banana", "aaabbc", "xyzzy"])
--- check case | A 40-letter word
arrangements("ab" * 20)
=> 137846528820
--- check test | Exact for a long mixed word
arrangements("telemetry" * 5) == __import__("math").factorial(45) // (__import__("math").factorial(15) * __import__("math").factorial(10) * __import__("math").factorial(5) ** 4)

+++ practice | Onto functions, counted
--- task
Write `count_onto(m, n)` that returns the number of surjective (onto) functions from a set of m elements to a set of n elements.

Use inclusion–exclusion over the outputs that are missed. The functions that miss a particular group of i outputs are the functions into the other n − i outputs: (n − i)ᵐ of them. So

count = Σ over i from 0 to n of (−1)ⁱ · C(n, i) · (n − i)ᵐ.

Check your work against the brute-force counts from the functions lesson: from 3 elements onto 2 there are 6, and from 5 onto 4 there are 240. Edge cases follow from the formula (with 0⁰ = 1, as Python computes it): `count_onto(0, 0)` is 1, and `count_onto(2, 3)` is 0. You may use `math.comb`.
--- starter
def count_onto(m, n):
    return n ** m
--- solution
import math


def count_onto(m, n):
    total = 0
    for i in range(n + 1):
        term = math.comb(n, i) * (n - i) ** m
        if i % 2 == 0:
            total += term
        else:
            total -= term
    return total
--- hint
Loop i from 0 to n. Each term is `math.comb(n, i) * (n - i) ** m`; add it when i is even, subtract it when i is odd.
--- hint
The i = 0 term is all nᵐ functions. The i = 1 terms remove the functions that miss one output, and so on, exactly as in inclusion–exclusion.
--- check case | Brute-force values from the functions lesson
(count_onto(3, 2), count_onto(4, 2), count_onto(5, 4), count_onto(3, 3))
=> (6, 14, 240, 6)
--- check case | Edge cases
(count_onto(0, 0), count_onto(2, 3), count_onto(0, 2), count_onto(2, 0), count_onto(4, 1))
=> (1, 0, 0, 0, 1)
--- check test | Equal sizes: onto means bijective, so n!
all(count_onto(n, n) == __import__("math").factorial(n) for n in range(15))
--- check test | Agrees with listing every function, for small sizes
all(count_onto(m, n) == sum(1 for t in __import__("itertools").product(range(n), repeat=m) if set(t) == set(range(n))) for m in range(6) for n in range(5))
--- check case | Large
count_onto(30, 10)
=> 629137189252738366241450572800

+++ practice | Fuel into tanks
--- task
A depot pours `total` identical fuel units into `tanks` labelled tanks. Every tank must receive at least `minimum` units, and the units are only counted, never told apart.

Write `distributions(total, tanks, minimum)` that returns the number of ways. First give every tank its `minimum`; then share out what is left freely, which is stars and bars: `left` units into `tanks` tanks is C(left + tanks − 1, tanks − 1). You may use `math.comb`.

Watch the edge cases:
- If less than `tanks * minimum` units are available, there are 0 ways.
- With 0 tanks there is 1 way if `total` is 0 (do nothing) and 0 ways otherwise.
- `total` and `minimum` can be 0.

For example, `distributions(10, 3, 0)` is `66`, and `distributions(10, 3, 2)` is `15`.
--- starter
def distributions(total, tanks, minimum):
    return 0
--- solution
import math


def distributions(total, tanks, minimum):
    if tanks == 0:
        return 1 if total == 0 else 0
    left = total - tanks * minimum
    if left < 0:
        return 0
    return math.comb(left + tanks - 1, tanks - 1)
--- hint
Handle `tanks == 0` first: the formula would ask for C(…, −1).
--- hint
After giving each tank its minimum, `left = total - tanks * minimum` units remain. A negative `left` means 0 ways; otherwise it is `math.comb(left + tanks - 1, tanks - 1)`.
--- check case | The examples
(distributions(10, 3, 0), distributions(10, 3, 2))
=> (66, 15)
--- check case | Not enough fuel, exactly enough fuel
(distributions(5, 3, 2), distributions(6, 3, 2))
=> (0, 1)
--- check case | No tanks
(distributions(0, 0, 0), distributions(4, 0, 1))
=> (1, 0)
--- check case | No fuel, one tank
(distributions(0, 4, 0), distributions(9, 1, 3), distributions(2, 1, 3))
=> (1, 1, 0)
--- check test | Agrees with listing every split, for small cases
all(distributions(t, k, m) == sum(1 for xs in __import__("itertools").product(range(t + 1), repeat=k) if sum(xs) == t and min(xs, default=m) >= m) for t in range(7) for k in range(4) for m in range(3))
--- check case | Large
distributions(10 ** 6, 4, 1000)
=> 164675648017826001

+++ practice | Debug: passwords with a digit
--- task
A password has `length` characters, each a lowercase letter or a digit: 36 symbols. `count_with_digit(length)` should return how many passwords contain **at least one digit**.

For length 2 it returns 720, but listing them gives 620. It overcounts: the password `"42"` is counted twice, once with each digit as "the" digit. Fix the count. (For length 0 the answer is 0: the empty password has no digit.)
--- starter
def count_with_digit(length):
    # choose the digit's position, then the digit, then fill the rest
    return length * 10 * 36 ** (length - 1)
--- solution
def count_with_digit(length):
    # all passwords, minus those with no digit at all
    return 36 ** length - 26 ** length
--- hint
