Count the complement: every password either has at least one digit, or has none.
--- hint
There are 36 to the power `length` passwords in all, and 26 to the power `length` with only letters.
--- check case | Length 2
count_with_digit(2)
=> 620
--- check case | Lengths 0 and 1
(count_with_digit(0), count_with_digit(1))
=> (0, 10)
--- check test | Agrees with listing, for length 3
count_with_digit(3) == sum(1 for p in __import__("itertools").product("abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz0123456789", repeat=3) if any(c.isdigit() for c in p))
--- check case | Length 12
count_with_digit(12)
=> 4642952381659934720

+++ practice | Rover routes
--- task
A rover drives on a grid from (0, 0) to (w, h), one step at a time, each step either east (x + 1) or north (y + 1). A route is its sequence of steps, so there are C(w + h, w) routes: choose which of the w + h steps go east.

Write `rover_routes(w, h, waypoint=None, hazard=None)` that counts the routes that visit the point `waypoint` (if one is given) and never visit the point `hazard` (if one is given). Points are `(x, y)` tuples. The grid can be huge, so count with formulas, not by listing routes. You may use `math.comb`.

Think it through with these facts:
- The number of routes from point p to point q is C(dx + dy, dx), where dx and dy are how far q is east and north of p; it is 0 if either is negative.
- A route visits points in order of increasing x + y, so it can pass the hazard before the waypoint, or after it, but not both.
- If the hazard is the start, the end or the waypoint itself, no route avoids it.

For example, `rover_routes(2, 2)` is `6`, `rover_routes(2, 2, hazard=(1, 1))` is `2`, and `rover_routes(2, 2, waypoint=(1, 0))` is `3`.
--- starter
def rover_routes(w, h, waypoint=None, hazard=None):
    return 0
--- solution
import math


def routes(p, q):
    dx = q[0] - p[0]
    dy = q[1] - p[1]
    if dx < 0 or dy < 0:
        return 0
    return math.comb(dx + dy, dx)


def rover_routes(w, h, waypoint=None, hazard=None):
    start = (0, 0)
    end = (w, h)
    if hazard in (start, end) or (waypoint is not None and hazard == waypoint):
        return 0
    if waypoint is None:
        total = routes(start, end)
        if hazard is not None:
            total -= routes(start, hazard) * routes(hazard, end)
        return total
    total = routes(start, waypoint) * routes(waypoint, end)
    if hazard is not None:
        total -= routes(start, hazard) * routes(hazard, waypoint) * routes(waypoint, end)
        total -= routes(start, waypoint) * routes(waypoint, hazard) * routes(hazard, end)
    return total
--- hint
Write a helper `routes(p, q)` for the number of routes between two points. Routes through a point are (routes to it) times (routes from it), by the product rule.
--- hint
To avoid the hazard, subtract the routes that visit it: the complement. With a waypoint as well, subtract the routes that visit both, which split into two groups that cannot overlap: hazard first, then waypoint; or waypoint first, then hazard.
--- hint
Deal with the special cases first: a hazard at the start, at the end, or on the waypoint leaves 0 routes. A waypoint off the grid gives 0 automatically, because one of the route counts is 0.
--- check case | The examples
(rover_routes(2, 2), rover_routes(2, 2, hazard=(1, 1)), rover_routes(2, 2, waypoint=(1, 0)))
=> (6, 2, 3)
--- check case | Hazard at the start, the end, or on the waypoint
(rover_routes(3, 3, hazard=(0, 0)), rover_routes(3, 3, hazard=(3, 3)), rover_routes(3, 3, waypoint=(1, 1), hazard=(1, 1)))
=> (0, 0, 0)
--- check case | Waypoint and hazard in both orders
(rover_routes(4, 4, waypoint=(2, 2), hazard=(1, 1)), rover_routes(4, 4, waypoint=(1, 1), hazard=(3, 2)))
=> (12, 22)
--- check case | Points off the grid
(rover_routes(3, 3, waypoint=(5, 0)), rover_routes(3, 3, hazard=(-1, 2)), rover_routes(0, 0))
=> (0, 20, 1)
--- check test | Agrees with listing every route, on small grids
(lambda C: all(rover_routes(w, h, W, H) == sum(1 for E in C(range(w + h), w) for cells in [{(sum(1 for j in range(i) if j in E), i - sum(1 for j in range(i) if j in E)) for i in range(w + h + 1)}] if (W is None or W in cells) and (H is None or H not in cells)) for w in range(4) for h in range(4) for W in [None, (1, 1), (0, 2), (2, 1)] for H in [None, (1, 1), (1, 0), (2, 2), (0, 1)]))(__import__("itertools").combinations)
--- check case | A huge grid
rover_routes(1000, 1000, waypoint=(500, 500), hazard=(250, 250)) == __import__("math").comb(1000, 500) ** 2 - __import__("math").comb(500, 250) ** 2 * __import__("math").comb(1000, 500)
=> True

+++ question | Choosing experiments
--- ask
A lander has room for 3 of 8 candidate experiments. Order does not matter. How many choices are there? Type a number.
--- answer
56
--- why
It is a subset of size 3 from 8: C(8, 3) = (8 · 7 · 6) / (3 · 2 · 1) = 336 / 6 = 56. Counting ordered lists would give 336, which counts every choice 3! = 6 times.

+++ question | Which model
--- ask
Five identical sample jars go into 3 labelled bins, any number per bin. Which count is right?
--- choice
3⁵ = 243, since each jar picks a bin.
--- choice correct
C(7, 2) = 21, by stars and bars: 5 stars and 2 bars.
--- choice
C(5, 3) = 10.
--- choice
P(5, 3) = 60.
--- why
The jars are identical, so only how many land in each bin matters: solutions of x₁ + x₂ + x₃ = 5. That is C(5 + 3 − 1, 3 − 1) = C(7, 2) = 21. 3⁵ would be right if the jars were labelled.

+++ question | Why divide by k!
--- ask
Why is C(n, k) = P(n, k) / k!?
--- choice
Because a subset of size k has k! elements.
--- choice correct
Because each k-element subset appears exactly k! times among the ordered lists of k different elements, once for each order.
--- choice
Because k! of the ordered lists contain repeats and must be removed.
--- choice
Because of the sum rule.
--- why
It is the division rule. P(n, k) counts ordered lists; forgetting the order turns exactly k! of them into the same subset, so dividing by k! counts each subset once.

=== disc-14 | The pigeonhole principle
--- teach
Last lesson counted things exactly. Often you do not need an exact count: you only need to know that something **must** exist. Two telemetry frames with the same checksum, two readings in the same range, a block of data whose sum is a multiple of the frame count. The **pigeonhole principle** proves such things with almost no calculation, and it sets hard limits on hashing, compression and data transfer. You already met its core in the functions lesson: if |A| > |B|, no function from A to B is injective.

### Too many pigeons

Picture 10 pigeons flying into 9 pigeonholes. However they choose, some hole ends up with at least 2 pigeons. You cannot say which hole, only that there is one.

**The pigeonhole principle.** If n + 1 or more objects are placed into n boxes, some box holds at least 2 objects.

**Proof,** by contradiction. Suppose every box held at most 1 object. Then the n boxes would hold at most n objects in total, but there are more than n. ∎

In the language of functions: placing objects in boxes is a function from objects to boxes, and "no box holds two" means the function is injective. The principle is the size theorem from the functions lesson: an injection from A to B needs |A| ≤ |B|.

Quick examples:

- Among any 13 crew members, two were born in the same month (13 people, 12 months).
- Any 8-bit counter that numbers 257 frames repeats a value (257 frames, 256 values).
- In any group of people, two have the same number of friends in the group. (With n people, the possible counts are 0 to n − 1, which is n boxes. But 0 and n − 1 cannot both occur: someone with n − 1 friends is friends with everyone, so no one has 0. So only n − 1 boxes are really used, for n people.)

### The generalised form

With more objects per box, you can say more. **⌈x⌉**, read "the ceiling of x", means x rounded up to a whole number: ⌈3.2⌉ = 4, and ⌈3⌉ = 3.

**The generalised pigeonhole principle.** If N objects are placed into k boxes, some box holds at least ⌈N / k⌉ objects.

**Proof,** by contradiction. Suppose every box held at most ⌈N / k⌉ − 1 objects. A number rounded up is less than one more than the number, so ⌈N / k⌉ < N / k + 1, which means ⌈N / k⌉ − 1 < N / k. Then the total would be less than k · (N / k) = N objects, but there are N. ∎

The bound cannot be improved: spread the objects as evenly as possible, and no box gets more than ⌈N / k⌉.

Examples:

- 100 readings sorted into 7 bands: some band has at least ⌈100 / 7⌉ = ⌈14.3⌉ = 15 readings.
- How many objects guarantee that some box has at least r, with k boxes? With k(r − 1) objects, each box could hold exactly r − 1, so that is not enough. With **k(r − 1) + 1** it is guaranteed. For 4 colours of socks, 4 · 2 + 1 = 9 socks guarantee 3 of one colour.

Ceilings in code should use whole numbers only. `math.ceil(n / k)` goes through a float, which is wrong for large numbers. Rounding up with integer division is exact:

```python
import math

n, k = 10 ** 20 + 1, 10
print((n + k - 1) // k)      # 10000000000000000001: exact
print(math.ceil(n / k))      # 10000000000000000000: the float lost the + 1
```

### Choosing the boxes

The principle itself is easy. The skill is choosing what the boxes are. Here are three classic arguments.

**Remainders.** Any n + 1 integers include two whose difference is divisible by n. The boxes are the n possible remainders 0, 1, …, n − 1 when dividing by n. Two of the n + 1 integers land in the same box, a ≡ b (read "a has the same remainder as b"), and then a − b is divisible by n.

**Odd parts.** Pick any n + 1 different numbers from {1, 2, …, 2n}. Then one of them divides another. Write each number as 2ᵃ · m with m odd, by dividing out every factor of 2 (you proved every number factors this way). The odd part m is one of 1, 3, 5, …, 2n − 1: only n boxes. So two of the numbers share an odd part: 2ᵃ · m and 2ᵇ · m with a < b, and the first divides the second. With only n numbers it can fail: n + 1, n + 2, …, 2n have no pair where one divides another, since the ratio of two of them is between 1 and 2.

**Prefix sums.** Any list of n integers has a non-empty block of consecutive entries whose sum is divisible by n. Let S₀ = 0 and let Sⱼ be the sum of the first j entries, for j = 1 to n. That is n + 1 **prefix sums**, and only n possible remainders when dividing by n. So two of them, Sᵢ and Sⱼ with i < j, have the same remainder. Their difference, Sⱼ − Sᵢ, is the sum of the entries from position i to position j − 1, and it is divisible by n. Notice the extra S₀ = 0: without it there are only n prefix sums, and the argument fails.

### Proofs that shrink the impossible

The principle also proves that some things **cannot** be done.

**No compressor shrinks every file.** A lossless compressor must be injective, or two files would unpack to the same thing. There are 2ⁿ files of exactly n bits. The files shorter than n bits number 2⁰ + 2¹ + … + 2ⁿ⁻¹ = 2ⁿ − 1, one fewer. So if every n-bit file were mapped to a shorter one, two would collide. Every compressor makes some files longer, or leaves them the same length:

```python
n = 16
shorter = sum(2 ** length for length in range(n))
print(2 ** n, shorter)     # 65536 65535: one box too few
```

**Repeats in a finite world.** Start at a state x₀ in a set of m states and apply a function f again and again: x₁ = f(x₀), x₂ = f(x₁), … . Among x₀, …, xₘ there are m + 1 values but only m states, so two are equal, say xᵢ = xⱼ with i < j. From then on the sequence repeats: xⱼ₊₁ = f(xⱼ) = f(xᵢ) = xᵢ₊₁, and so on for ever. Every such sequence runs along a tail and then loops round a cycle. A [[random number generator|prng]] with m internal states must repeat within m steps, for exactly this reason.

**Majorities overlap.** A group of 5 replicas accepts a write when 3 confirm it, and a read asks 3. Any 3 and any 3 must share a replica: if they were disjoint there would be 6 different replicas among 5. So every read sees the latest write. This [[quorum|quorum]] argument is |A| + |B| > n forcing A ∩ B ≠ ∅, and it is the heart of replicated databases:

```python
from itertools import combinations

replicas = range(5)
majorities = [set(c) for c in combinations(replicas, 3)]
print(all(a & b for a in majorities for b in majorities))   # True: every two meet
```

### A harder one: monotone subsequences

A **subsequence** keeps some entries of a sequence, in their original order. In 5, 2, 8, 3, 9, 1 the entries 2, 3, 9 are an increasing subsequence and 8, 3, 1 a decreasing one.

**Theorem (Erdős and Szekeres).** Every sequence of n² + 1 different numbers has an increasing subsequence or a decreasing subsequence of length n + 1.

**Proof.** Label each entry k with a pair (iₖ, dₖ): the length of the longest increasing subsequence that **starts** at entry k, and the length of the longest decreasing one. Suppose, for a contradiction, that no monotone subsequence has length n + 1. Then every iₖ and dₖ is between 1 and n, so there are at most n · n = n² possible labels. With n² + 1 entries, two entries s < t (in position) share a label. If the value at s is smaller than the value at t, put entry s in front of the longest increasing subsequence starting at t: that gives iₛ ≥ iₜ + 1, so the labels differ. If the value at s is bigger, the same with decreasing gives dₛ ≥ dₜ + 1. Either way the labels differ, which contradicts their being equal. ∎

The boxes here are the labels, and the objects are the entries. That is the pattern of every hard pigeonhole proof: find a label with fewer possible values than there are objects.

### What it costs

- **The principle gives existence, not location.** "Some two frames share a checksum" does not say which two. Finding them is an algorithm's job.
- **Finding a collision:** keep a dict from each box to the first object seen in it, and stop at the first box seen twice. That is O(N) time and O(k) space for N objects in k boxes, and by the principle it stops within k + 1 objects. Comparing every pair instead is O(N²).
- **The prefix-sum block** is found the same way in O(n): one pass, one dict of remainders.
- **The Erdős–Szekeres labels** can be computed in O(N²) by comparing each entry with the later ones, or in O(N log N) with patience sorting.

### Where it is used

- **Hashing.** More keys than slots means collisions for certain. The [[birthday paradox|birthday]] makes them likely much earlier.
- **Compression.** No algorithm compresses everything; good ones shrink the files people actually have, at the cost of slightly growing random ones.
- **Sequence numbers.** A sender with more packets in flight than sequence numbers cannot tell a new packet from an old one with the same number, so protocols limit the window to fit the number space.
- **Cycle detection.** Iterating a function on a finite set must loop; Floyd's tortoise-and-hare algorithm finds the loop in O(1) extra memory.
- **Distributed systems.** Majority quorums in Raft and Paxos overlap for exactly the reason above.

**Watch out:** **⌈N / k⌉ is not ⌊N / k⌋ + 1.** They agree unless k divides N. 12 socks in 4 drawers force a drawer with ⌈12 / 4⌉ = 3 socks, not 4: three in each drawer is possible. Also check that every object goes in exactly one box, and that there really are fewer boxes than objects. In the prefix-sum argument, forgetting S₀ leaves n sums for n remainders, and no collision is forced: the list [1, 1] with n = 2 has prefix sums 1 and 2, with different remainders 1 and 0, and the only block that works is the one starting at position 0, which needs S₀.

::: context prng Why random number generators repeat
A pseudo-random generator keeps a hidden state, and each call replaces it with f(state) and returns part of it. With a b-bit state there are at most 2ᵇ states, so by the pigeonhole principle the sequence must repeat within 2ᵇ calls, and from then on everything repeats. Python's Mersenne Twister has about 20,000 bits of state and a period of 2¹⁹⁹³⁷ − 1, far more than any program will ever use. A generator with a 16-bit state repeats after at most 65,536 numbers, which is short enough to spoil a simulation.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 300 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <g fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1">
    <circle cx="20" cy="95" r="9"/><circle cx="65" cy="95" r="9"/><circle cx="110" cy="95" r="9"/>
    <circle cx="160" cy="90" r="9"/><circle cx="215" cy="100" r="9"/><circle cx="255" cy="60" r="9"/>
    <circle cx="215" cy="20" r="9"/><circle cx="160" cy="30" r="9"/>
  </g>
  <g stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" fill="none">
    <line x1="29" y1="95" x2="56" y2="95"/><line x1="74" y1="95" x2="101" y2="95"/><line x1="119" y1="94" x2="151" y2="91"/>
    <line x1="169" y1="92" x2="206" y2="99"/><line x1="222" y1="94" x2="249" y2="67"/><line x1="253" y1="51" x2="222" y2="26"/>
    <line x1="206" y1="21" x2="169" y2="29"/><line x1="158" y1="39" x2="159" y2="81"/>
  </g>
  <g font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">
    <text x="20" y="115">x0</text><text x="65" y="115">x1</text><text x="110" y="115">x2</text>
  </g>
  <text x="65" y="70" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">tail</text>
  <text x="208" y="64" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">cycle</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context quorum Why majorities are safe
With n replicas, a write waits for W confirmations and a read asks R replicas. If R + W > n, every read set meets every write set, by the pigeonhole principle: a read set and a write set that shared nothing would need R + W different replicas. Systems such as Cassandra let you tune R and W; choosing a majority for both, more than n / 2 each, is the common setting, and it is also what Raft uses to elect leaders.
:::

::: context birthday Likely long before certain
The pigeonhole principle needs 367 people to guarantee a shared birthday, but with just 23 random people the chance is already over one half. In general, with k equally likely boxes, a collision becomes likely after about the square root of k objects. For a hash with 64-bit outputs that is about 4 billion keys, not 2⁶⁴. This is probability, not pigeonhole; the probability lessons compute it.
:::
--- task
Turn the prefix-sum argument into a fast function.

Write `divisible_block(nums)`. `nums` is a list of n integers (they may be negative). Return a tuple `(i, j)` with `0 <= i < j <= n` such that `sum(nums[i:j])` is divisible by n. The pigeonhole principle says one always exists.

- Return the block that **ends first**: the smallest possible `j`. For that `j` there is only one `i` that works. (Why? Every block with a divisible sum is a pair of prefix sums with the same remainder, so the smallest `j` is the first prefix sum whose remainder has been seen before.)
- If `nums` is empty, raise `ValueError`.
- It must run in O(n): a check uses 200,000 numbers whose first repeat comes at the very end.

For example, `divisible_block([3, 1, 4, 1, 5])` is `(1, 3)`: 1 + 4 = 5 is divisible by 5.
--- starter
def divisible_block(nums):
    return (0, len(nums))
--- solution
def divisible_block(nums):
    n = len(nums)
    if n == 0:
        raise ValueError("the list must not be empty")
    first_seen = {0: 0}
    total = 0
    for j in range(1, n + 1):
        total += nums[j - 1]
        r = total % n
        if r in first_seen:
            return (first_seen[r], j)
        first_seen[r] = j
--- hint
Walk the list once, keeping the running total, which is the prefix sum Sⱼ. Its remainder `total % n` is the box.
--- hint
Keep a dict from each remainder to the first j where it appeared. Start it with `{0: 0}`, for S₀ = 0; without it you miss blocks that start at position 0.
--- hint
The first time a remainder is already in the dict, return `(first_seen[r], j)`. Python's `%` gives a remainder from 0 to n − 1 even for negative totals, so negative numbers need no special care.
--- check case | The example
divisible_block([3, 1, 4, 1, 5])
=> (1, 3)
--- check case | A block that starts at 0
(divisible_block([4, 6, 9]), divisible_block([7]), divisible_block([2, -2]))
=> ((1, 2), (0, 1), (0, 1))
?? S₀ = 0 is a prefix sum too. Put remainder 0 in the dict before the loop.
--- check case | The whole list
(divisible_block([1, 1, 1, 1]), divisible_block([5, 5, 5, 5, 5, 5]))
=> ((0, 4), (0, 6))
--- check case | Negative numbers
divisible_block([-3, 8, 2, -7, 11, 4])
=> (0, 4)
--- check test | Empty list raises ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: divisible_block([]))
--- check test | Valid and ending first, on 300 lists checked against every block
all((lambda L, r: r[0] < r[1] and sum(L[r[0]:r[1]]) % len(L) == 0 and not any(sum(L[i:j]) % len(L) == 0 for j in range(1, r[1]) for i in range(j)))(L, divisible_block(L)) for L in [[(k * 37 + t * t * 11 + t) % 23 - 11 for k in range(1 + t % 17)] for t in range(300)])
--- check case | 200,000 numbers: the first repeat is at the end
(divisible_block([1] * 200000), divisible_block([1] * 199999 + [5]))
=> ((0, 200000), (4, 200000))
?? Checking every block is 20 billion sums. Keep a dict of the remainders seen so far.

+++ practice | Two with the same remainder
--- task
Write `same_remainder(nums, n)`. It returns a tuple `(i, j)` of positions with `i < j` and `nums[i] % n == nums[j] % n`, choosing the pair with the smallest `j` (for that `j`, the `i` is the earlier position with the same remainder). If there is no such pair, return `None`.

If `nums` has more than `n` numbers, the pigeonhole principle promises a pair (only `n` remainders are possible), and your function must find it within the first `n + 1` numbers. With `n` numbers or fewer there may be none. `n` is at least 1, and the numbers may be negative.

For example, `same_remainder([10, 4, 7, 22], 3)` is `(0, 1)`: 10 and 4 both leave remainder 1.
--- starter
def same_remainder(nums, n):
    return None
--- solution
def same_remainder(nums, n):
    first_at = {}
    for j, x in enumerate(nums):
        r = x % n
        if r in first_at:
            return (first_at[r], j)
        first_at[r] = j
    return None
--- hint
The boxes are the remainders. Walk the list with `enumerate`, remembering the first position where each remainder appeared.
--- hint
The first time a remainder is already in your dict, you have the pair. If the loop ends, return `None`.
--- check case | The example
same_remainder([10, 4, 7, 22], 3)
=> (0, 1)
--- check case | Negative numbers
same_remainder([-1, 4, 3, 2], 3)
=> (0, 3)
?? In Python, -1 % 3 is 2, the same as 2 % 3.
--- check case | No pair
(same_remainder([0, 1, 2], 3), same_remainder([], 4), same_remainder([9], 1))
=> (None, None, None)
--- check case | n = 1: everything has remainder 0
same_remainder([8, 3], 1)
=> (0, 1)
--- check test | More than n numbers always gives a pair within the first n + 1
all((lambda r: r is not None and r[1] <= n and (k * 7 + r[0] * r[0]) % n == (k * 7 + r[1] * r[1]) % n)(same_remainder([k * 7 + t * t for t in range(n + 5)], n)) for n in range(1, 60) for k in range(5))

+++ practice | One divides another
--- task
`nums` is a list of n + 1 different whole numbers, each from 1 to 2n (so n is `len(nums) - 1`, and n ≥ 1). By the odd-parts argument, two of them have the same odd part, and the smaller of those two divides the larger.

Write `dividing_pair(nums)`. Walk the list in order, working out each number's odd part (divide out all factors of 2). Stop at the first number whose odd part you have already seen, and return the tuple `(smaller, larger)` of it and the earlier number with that odd part.

For example, `dividing_pair([5, 3, 6, 4])` is `(3, 6)`: 3 and 6 both have odd part 3.
--- starter
def dividing_pair(nums):
    return (nums[0], nums[1])
--- solution
def dividing_pair(nums):
    by_odd_part = {}
    for x in nums:
        m = x
        while m % 2 == 0:
            m //= 2
        if m in by_odd_part:
            y = by_odd_part[m]
            return (min(x, y), max(x, y))
        by_odd_part[m] = x
--- hint
The odd part of x: keep halving while it is even. 12 → 6 → 3, so the odd part of 12 is 3.
--- hint
Keep a dict from odd part to the number that had it. At the first repeat, return the two numbers, smaller first.
--- check case | The example
dividing_pair([5, 3, 6, 4])
=> (3, 6)
--- check case | Powers of two share odd part 1
dividing_pair([8, 3, 5, 7, 2])
=> (2, 8)
--- check case | The smallest case, n = 1
dividing_pair([2, 1])
=> (1, 2)
--- check test | Always a real divisor pair, on many lists
all((lambda nums, r: r[0] < r[1] and r[1] % r[0] == 0 and r[0] in nums and r[1] in nums)(nums, dividing_pair(nums)) for nums in [sorted(range(1, 2 * n + 1), key=lambda v: (v * t) % 97)[:n + 1] for n in range(1, 40) for t in range(1, 8)])
--- check test | 100,001 numbers from 1 to 200,000
(lambda r: r[1] % r[0] == 0)(dividing_pair(list(range(200000, 99999, -1))))

+++ practice | Tail and cycle
--- task
A system has states numbered 0 to m − 1, and `step` is a list where `step[s]` is the state after state `s`. Starting from state `x0` and applying `step` again and again must eventually repeat, by the pigeonhole principle, and then it loops for ever: a **tail** of states visited once, then a **cycle**.

Write `tail_and_cycle(step, x0)` that returns a tuple `(tail, cycle)`: `tail` is how many steps it takes to first reach a state on the cycle, and `cycle` is the cycle's length.

Record the step at which each state is first visited. The first time you reach a state you have seen before, at step j, where it was first seen at step i: the tail is i and the cycle length is j − i. It must be O(m): a check uses a million states.

For example, with `step = [1, 2, 3, 4, 2]` and `x0 = 0` the states are 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 2, 3, 4, … , so the answer is `(2, 3)`.
--- starter
def tail_and_cycle(step, x0):
    return (0, len(step))
--- solution
def tail_and_cycle(step, x0):
    first_seen = {}
    x = x0
    t = 0
    while x not in first_seen:
        first_seen[x] = t
        x = step[x]
        t += 1
    return (first_seen[x], t - first_seen[x])
--- hint
Walk from `x0`, recording in a dict the step number at which each state is first visited.
--- hint
When you arrive at a state already in the dict, it is the first state of the cycle. Its recorded step is the tail length, and the current step minus that is the cycle length.
--- check case | The example
tail_and_cycle([1, 2, 3, 4, 2], 0)
=> (2, 3)
--- check case | A fixed point, and starting on the cycle
(tail_and_cycle([0], 0), tail_and_cycle([1, 2, 0], 1), tail_and_cycle([1, 1], 0))
=> ((0, 1), (0, 3), (1, 1))
--- check case | Everything is one cycle
tail_and_cycle([(s + 1) % 10 for s in range(10)], 4)
=> (0, 10)
--- check test | Tail plus cycle never exceeds m, on many small systems
all((lambda s, r: r[0] + r[1] <= len(s) and r[1] >= 1)(s, tail_and_cycle(s, 0)) for s in [[(k * k + t) % m for k in range(m)] for m in range(1, 40) for t in range(5)])
--- check case | A million states: a long tail into a long cycle
tail_and_cycle([s + 1 for s in range(999999)] + [400000], 0)
=> (400000, 600000)

+++ practice | How many must share
--- task
Write two functions with whole-number arithmetic only (no floats: a check uses numbers near 10²⁰).

- `must_hold(objects, boxes)` returns the number that the generalised pigeonhole principle guarantees: some box must hold at least ⌈objects / boxes⌉. `objects` is at least 0. If `boxes` is less than 1, raise `ValueError`.
- `objects_needed(boxes, r)` returns the smallest number of objects that guarantees some box holds at least `r`: `boxes * (r - 1) + 1`. `boxes` and `r` are at least 1; otherwise raise `ValueError`.

For example, `must_hold(100, 7)` is `15`, `must_hold(12, 4)` is `3`, and `objects_needed(4, 3)` is `9`.
--- starter
import math


def must_hold(objects, boxes):
    return math.ceil(objects / boxes)


def objects_needed(boxes, r):
    return boxes * r
--- solution
def must_hold(objects, boxes):
    if boxes < 1:
        raise ValueError("there must be at least one box")
    return (objects + boxes - 1) // boxes


def objects_needed(boxes, r):
    if boxes < 1 or r < 1:
        raise ValueError("boxes and r must be at least 1")
    return boxes * (r - 1) + 1
--- hint
Round up with integers: `(objects + boxes - 1) // boxes`. It is exact for any size, where `math.ceil(objects / boxes)` goes through a float.
--- hint
With `boxes * (r - 1)` objects, every box could hold exactly r − 1, so one more object is needed. Check the bad inputs first.
--- check case | The examples
(must_hold(100, 7), must_hold(12, 4), objects_needed(4, 3))
=> (15, 3, 9)
--- check case | Edge cases: no objects, one box, r = 1
(must_hold(0, 5), must_hold(9, 1), objects_needed(6, 1), objects_needed(1, 10))
=> (0, 9, 1, 10)
--- check case | Exactly divisible: no extra
(must_hold(20, 5), must_hold(21, 5))
=> (4, 5)
--- check test | Bad inputs raise ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: must_hold(5, 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: objects_needed(0, 3)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: objects_needed(3, 0))
--- check case | Numbers near 10²⁰
(must_hold(10 ** 20 + 1, 10), objects_needed(10 ** 20, 3))
=> (10000000000000000001, 200000000000000000001)
?? A float keeps about 16 digits, so objects / boxes loses the + 1. Use integer division.
--- check test | The two agree: objects_needed(k, r) objects force r, one fewer does not
all(must_hold(objects_needed(k, r), k) == r and (k * (r - 1) == 0 or must_hold(objects_needed(k, r) - 1, k) == r - 1) for k in range(1, 30) for r in range(1, 30))

+++ practice | Debug: the repeat that went missing
--- task
Telemetry frames carry an 8-bit checksum, so there are only 256 possible values. `first_repeat(checksums)` should return the positions `(i, j)`, with `i < j`, of the first repeated checksum: the smallest `j` whose value appeared earlier, with `i` that earlier position. It returns `None` if no value repeats.

With 257 frames the pigeonhole principle guarantees a repeat, but for some lists of 257 frames the function returns `None`. It happens when the only repeat involves the last frame. Fix it.
--- starter
def first_repeat(checksums):
    first_at = {}
    for j in range(len(checksums) - 1):
        c = checksums[j]
        if c in first_at:
            return (first_at[c], j)
        first_at[c] = j
    return None
--- solution
def first_repeat(checksums):
    first_at = {}
    for j in range(len(checksums)):
        c = checksums[j]
        if c in first_at:
            return (first_at[c], j)
        first_at[c] = j
    return None
--- hint
Which positions does the loop visit? Try a list of 2 frames with the same checksum.
--- hint
`range(len(checksums) - 1)` stops one short: the last frame is never looked at.
--- check case | 256 different values, then a repeat at the end
first_repeat(list(range(256)) + [17])
=> (17, 256)
--- check case | Two frames, the same checksum
first_repeat([9, 9])
=> (0, 1)
--- check case | No repeat, and empty
(first_repeat([1, 2, 3]), first_repeat([]))
=> (None, None)
--- check case | The first repeat wins
first_repeat([5, 1, 2, 1, 5])
=> (1, 3)

+++ practice | A monotone run in any sequence
--- task
By the Erdős–Szekeres theorem, every sequence of n² + 1 different numbers has an increasing or a decreasing subsequence of length n + 1.

Write `monotone_run(seq)`. `seq` is a list of different numbers, and n is the largest whole number with n² + 1 ≤ `len(seq)` (for an empty list there is nothing to find: return `[]`). Return a list of exactly n + 1 values from `seq`, in their original order, that is strictly increasing or strictly decreasing. Any correct answer is accepted.

Compute, for every position k, the length of the longest increasing subsequence that **ends** at k, and the longest decreasing one, each with a link to the entry before it. Some position reaches length n + 1 (the theorem's proof works with "ends at" as well as "starts at"); follow its links back. Lists have up to about 900 entries, so O(len²) work is fine.

For example, for `[5, 2, 8, 3, 9]`, n is 2, and `[2, 3, 9]`, `[2, 8, 9]` and `[5, 8, 9]` are all correct answers.
--- starter
def monotone_run(seq):
    return seq[:1]
--- solution
import math


def monotone_run(seq):
    if not seq:
        return []
    n = math.isqrt(len(seq) - 1)
    up = [1] * len(seq)
    down = [1] * len(seq)
    up_from = [-1] * len(seq)
    down_from = [-1] * len(seq)
    for k in range(len(seq)):
        for p in range(k):
            if seq[p] < seq[k] and up[p] + 1 > up[k]:
                up[k] = up[p] + 1
                up_from[k] = p
            if seq[p] > seq[k] and down[p] + 1 > down[k]:
                down[k] = down[p] + 1
                down_from[k] = p
    for lengths, links in ((up, up_from), (down, down_from)):
        for k in range(len(seq)):
            if lengths[k] >= n + 1:
                run = []
                while k != -1 and len(run) < n + 1:
                    run.append(seq[k])
                    k = links[k]
                return run[::-1]
    return []
--- hint
`n = math.isqrt(len(seq) - 1)` gives the largest n with n² + 1 ≤ len(seq). Keep four lists: the best increasing length ending at each position and where it came from, and the same for decreasing.
--- hint
For each position k, look at every earlier position p. If `seq[p] < seq[k]`, an increasing run ending at p can be extended by k; keep the longest and remember p. The same with `>` for decreasing.
--- hint
Find a position whose length is at least n + 1, follow the links back collecting values, keep n + 1 of them (the last n + 1 of the run, ending at that position), and reverse.
--- check test | The example gives a valid answer
monotone_run([5, 2, 8, 3, 9]) in ([2, 3, 9], [2, 8, 9], [5, 8, 9])
--- check case | Empty and one entry
(monotone_run([]), monotone_run([7]))
=> ([], [7])
--- check test | Valid on many sequences: right length, a subsequence, monotone
all((lambda s, r: (lambda n: len(r) == n + 1 and (lambda it: all(x in it for x in r))(iter(s)) and (all(a < b for a, b in zip(r, r[1:])) or all(a > b for a, b in zip(r, r[1:]))))(__import__("math").isqrt(len(s) - 1)))(s, monotone_run(s)) for s in [sorted(range(L), key=lambda v: (v * t + v * v) % (L + 7)) for L in range(1, 60) for t in range(1, 6)])
--- check test | A worst case: 900 entries with no run longer than 30
(lambda s: (lambda r: len(r) == 31 and (lambda it: all(x in it for x in r))(iter(s)) and (all(a < b for a, b in zip(r, r[1:])) or all(a > b for a, b in zip(r, r[1:]))))(monotone_run(s)))([b * 30 + (29 - a) for b in range(30) for a in range(30)] + [-1])

+++ question | Socks in drawers
--- ask
12 socks are put into 4 drawers. What is the largest number of socks that some drawer is guaranteed to hold? Type a number.
--- answer
3
--- why
⌈12 / 4⌉ = 3, and 3 is the best you can guarantee, because 3 socks in every drawer is possible. ⌊12 / 4⌋ + 1 = 4 is the classic wrong answer: it only equals the ceiling when 4 does not divide 12.

+++ question | Compressing everything
--- ask
Why can no lossless compressor make every 16-bit file shorter?
--- choice
Because 16 bits is already too small to compress.
--- choice correct
There are 2¹⁶ files of 16 bits but only 2¹⁶ − 1 shorter ones, so two files would get the same output and could not both be restored.
--- choice
Because compression only works on text.
--- choice
Because the compressor would need more than 16 bits of memory.
--- why
Decompression needs the compressor to be injective. An injection from a set of 65,536 files into a set of 65,535 shorter files is impossible by the pigeonhole principle, so some file must stay the same length or grow.

+++ question | Reading from a quorum
--- ask
A database keeps 5 replicas. Writes wait for 3 replicas; reads ask 3. Why does every read reach at least one replica with the latest write?
--- choice
Because 3 is more than half of 5, so the replicas vote on the value.
--- choice correct
Two sets of 3 replicas out of 5 must share one: disjoint, they would need 6 different replicas.
--- choice
Because the replicas copy the write to each other before the read.
--- choice
Because reads always go to the same replicas as writes.
--- why
It is the pigeonhole principle: 3 + 3 = 6 objects in 5 boxes means some replica is in both sets. In general a read of R and a write of W out of n always meet when R + W > n.

=== disc-15 | Binomial coefficients
--- teach
In the counting lesson you learned how many ways there are to choose k things from n when order does not matter, and last lesson you used counting to prove that some collision must happen. This lesson looks at those "choose" numbers themselves. They obey beautiful rules, and each rule has a proof that is a small story about choosing. You will learn to prove identities by counting, expand powers like (x + y)¹⁰ in one line, and compute the numbers exactly, fast, for n in the billions.

### What C(n, k) counts

For whole numbers n ≥ 0 and k, the **binomial coefficient** C(n, k), read "n choose k", is the number of k-element subsets of a set with n elements. Books also write it as n stacked over k inside tall brackets.

In everyday words: C(n, k) is the number of different crews of k you can pick from n candidates, when a crew is just a group, with no order and no roles.

Because it counts subsets, the definition already says what happens at the edges:

- C(n, 0) = 1: there is exactly one subset with no elements, the empty set.
- C(n, n) = 1: the only n-element subset is the whole set.
- C(n, k) = 0 when k > n or k < 0: there is no such subset. That is not an error, just a count of zero.

In the counting lesson you found the formula C(n, k) = n! / (k! · (n − k)!) for 0 ≤ k ≤ n, where n! ("n factorial") is 1 · 2 · … · n and 0! = 1. For example, C(5, 2) = 120 / (2 · 6) = 10. `itertools.combinations`, from the Python courses, lists the subsets themselves, so you can count them:

```python
from itertools import combinations

candidates = ["ada", "bo", "cy", "di", "eve"]
pairs = list(combinations(candidates, 2))
print(len(pairs))    # 10
print(pairs[:3])     # [('ada', 'bo'), ('ada', 'cy'), ('ada', 'di')]
```

Python also has the number directly: `math.comb(n, k)`. The task will ask you to compute it yourself, so that you know exactly what that function does and what it costs.

### Proving things by counting

This lesson proves every identity with a **combinatorial proof**. There are two kinds:

1. **Count one set in two ways.** If one way of counting a set gives the left-hand side and another way gives the right-hand side, the two sides are equal, because they are the size of the same set. This is called [[double counting|double-counting]].
2. **Find a bijection.** If there is a bijection from a set A to a set B (a function that is one-to-one and onto, from the functions lesson), then |A| = |B|.

Such a proof explains **why** an identity holds, not just that the algebra happens to work out. It is also often much shorter than the algebra.

### Symmetry

**Claim:** C(n, k) = C(n, n − k) for 0 ≤ k ≤ n.

**Proof, by a bijection.** Let S be a set of n elements. Send each k-element subset A to its complement S − A, which has n − k elements. Taking complements twice gives back A, so this map has an inverse, and a function with an inverse is a bijection. So the k-subsets and the (n − k)-subsets are equally many.

In words: choosing the 2 people who stay behind is the same as choosing the 3 who fly. For instance, C(10, 8) = C(10, 2) = 45.

### Pascal's rule

**Claim (Pascal's rule):** for n ≥ 1 and every integer k,

C(n, k) = C(n − 1, k − 1) + C(n − 1, k).

**Proof, by counting one set in two ways.** Let S have n elements, and pick out one of them; call it x (think of it as the commander). Every k-element subset of S is of exactly one of two kinds.

1. **It contains x.** Removing x leaves a (k − 1)-element subset of the other n − 1 elements, and adding x back undoes that. So this is a bijection, and there are C(n − 1, k − 1) subsets of this kind.
2. **It does not contain x.** Then it is a k-element subset of the other n − 1 elements, and there are C(n − 1, k) of those.

The two kinds do not overlap, and together they are every k-subset. By the sum rule, C(n, k) is their total. ∎

The rule also works at the edges, thanks to the zeros. With k = 0 it says C(n, 0) = C(n − 1, −1) + C(n − 1, 0) = 0 + 1 = 1. With k = n it says C(n, n) = C(n − 1, n − 1) + C(n − 1, n) = 1 + 0 = 1.

### Pascal's triangle

Write the numbers C(n, 0), C(n, 1), …, C(n, n) as **row n**, and stack the rows. Every row starts and ends with 1, and by Pascal's rule every other entry is the sum of the two entries just above it, to the left and to the right. This is **[[Pascal's triangle|pascal-history]]**:

```text
row 0:              1
row 1:            1   1
row 2:          1   2   1
row 3:        1   3   3   1
row 4:      1   4   6   4   1
row 5:    1   5  10  10   5   1
row 6:  1   6  15  20  15   6   1
```

For example, the 10 in the middle of row 5 is 4 + 6 from row 4. Symmetry is the triangle looking the same in a mirror.

Building rows 0 to n this way needs no multiplication at all. Row r has r − 1 entries that are sums, so rows 0 to n take about n²/2 additions: Θ(n²) of them. The numbers do grow, though. The middle of row n is about 2ⁿ divided by a small factor, so it has about n binary digits, and adding two such numbers costs about n steps. So the whole triangle costs about n³ bit operations.

### The binomial theorem

**Theorem:** for every n ≥ 0 and all numbers x and y,

(x + y)ⁿ = C(n, 0)·xⁿ + C(n, 1)·xⁿ⁻¹·y + C(n, 2)·xⁿ⁻²·y² + … + C(n, n)·yⁿ,

or, with a sum sign, the sum over k from 0 to n of C(n, k)·xⁿ⁻ᵏ·yᵏ. That is why these numbers are called binomial coefficients: a **binomial** is a sum of two terms, like x + y, and they are the coefficients of its powers.

**Proof, by counting.** Write (x + y)ⁿ as n brackets multiplied together: (x + y)(x + y)…(x + y). Multiplying them out means choosing either x or y from **each** bracket, multiplying the choices, and adding up the results over every possible way of choosing. There are 2ⁿ ways. A choice gives the term xⁿ⁻ᵏ·yᵏ exactly when it takes y from k of the brackets and x from the other n − k. How many choices do that? As many as there are ways to pick which k of the n brackets give y: C(n, k). So after collecting equal terms, xⁿ⁻ᵏ·yᵏ appears C(n, k) times. ∎

For n = 4 the theorem reads (x + y)⁴ = x⁴ + 4x³y + 6x²y² + 4xy³ + y⁴, with row 4 of the triangle as the coefficients. A theorem about all numbers cannot be proved by testing, but a test does catch a misremembered formula:

```python
from math import comb

x, y, n = 3, 2, 4
print((x + y) ** n)                                                    # 625
print(sum(comb(n, k) * x ** (n - k) * y ** k for k in range(n + 1)))   # 625
```

There is also a proof by induction on n that uses Pascal's rule in the step: multiply the expansion of (x + y)ⁿ⁻¹ by (x + y), and the two terms that land on xⁿ⁻ᵏ·yᵏ carry the coefficients C(n − 1, k) and C(n − 1, k − 1).

### Three identities you get for free

**Row sums.** Put x = y = 1 in the theorem: the sum of row n is 2ⁿ. The counting proof is one sentence: both sides count all the subsets of an n-element set, the left side sorted by size.

**Alternating sums.** Put x = 1 and y = −1: for n ≥ 1, C(n, 0) − C(n, 1) + C(n, 2) − … = (1 − 1)ⁿ = 0. So a nonempty set has exactly as many subsets of even size as of odd size. The bijection: fix an element x, and put x in if it is out, or take it out if it is in. That changes the size by one, so it swaps even and odd, and doing it twice changes nothing. For n = 0 the sum is 1, because the empty set has one subset, of size 0, which is even; there is no element to toggle.

**The captain identity.** k · C(n, k) = n · C(n − 1, k − 1) for n ≥ 1. Count the ways to choose a crew of k **with a captain** from n people. Way one: choose the crew, C(n, k) ways, then the captain from inside it, k ways. Way two: choose the captain first, n ways, then the other k − 1 crew members from the remaining n − 1 people, C(n − 1, k − 1) ways. Same set, so the counts are equal.

### Vandermonde's identity

C(m + n, r) = C(m, 0)·C(n, r) + C(m, 1)·C(n, r − 1) + … + C(m, r)·C(n, 0).

**Proof.** You have m pilots and n engineers and you choose a crew of r from all of them. Sort the crews by k, the number of pilots in them. A crew with exactly k pilots is a choice of k pilots and, independently, r − k engineers: C(m, k)·C(n, r − k) ways by the product rule. The values k = 0, 1, …, r cover every crew exactly once, so add. For instance, with m = 3, n = 4, r = 2: C(7, 2) = 21, and 1·6 + 3·4 + 3·1 = 21.

### Stars and bars

A bijection also counts something that does not look like subsets at all. In how many ways can you share out n identical fuel cells among k tanks, where a tank may get none? Draw each share-out as a row of n stars with k − 1 bars between them: the stars before the first bar go in tank 1, those between the first and second bar in tank 2, and so on. With n = 5 and k = 3:

```text
**|*|**    means  2, 1, 2
|****|*    means  0, 4, 1
*****||    means  5, 0, 0
```

Each share-out gives exactly one such row, and each row gives exactly one share-out, so this is a bijection. A row has n + k − 1 places, and it is fixed by choosing which k − 1 of them are bars. So the count is C(n + k − 1, k − 1). Here that is C(7, 2) = 21.

### Computing C(n, k) exactly and fast

The factorial formula is exact, but wasteful: C(1000000, 3) is about 1.7 × 10¹⁷, yet 1000000! has more than five million digits. There is a better way, built from the captain identity rewritten as

C(m, i) = C(m − 1, i − 1) · m / i.

Start from C(n − k, 0) = 1. Apply the identity with i = 1, 2, …, k, where each step raises the top and the bottom by one. After step i you have C(n − k + i, i), so after step k you have C(n, k). This is the **multiplicative formula**. Here it is for C(7, 3), starting from C(4, 0):

| step i | value before | times (4 + i) | divided by i | this is |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 5 | 5 | C(5, 1) |
| 2 | 5 | 30 | 15 | C(6, 2) |
| 3 | 15 | 105 | 35 | C(7, 3) |

**Why every division is exact.** The value after each step is a binomial coefficient, a count, so it is a whole number. So if you **multiply first and then divide**, the division leaves no remainder, and whole-number division `//` gives the exact answer with no fractions and no floats. Dividing first would be wrong: in step 2, 6 / 2 happens to be whole, but in step 3 of C(8, 3) you would need 8 / 3.

**What it costs.** k multiplications and k divisions. By symmetry you can use whichever of k and n − k is smaller, so the cost is Θ(min(k, n − k)) arithmetic steps. That makes C(10⁹, 10⁹ − 2) take two steps instead of a billion. The numbers themselves stay small: every value along the way is at most the answer, and C(n, k) ≤ 2ⁿ always, since it is one term of a row that sums to 2ⁿ. The largest entry of row n, the middle one, is about [[2ⁿ divided by the square root of πn/2|stirling]].

### Where it is used

- **Probability.** The next two lessons use C(n, k) constantly: the chance of exactly k successes in n independent tries is built on it.
- **Search spaces.** Testing every group of 3 flags out of 40 is C(40, 3) = 9,880 runs, fine. Every group of 20 is C(40, 20) ≈ 1.4 × 10¹¹, hopeless. Knowing the number first saves you from starting a job that cannot finish.
- **Error-correcting codes.** A telemetry word of n bits with at most t bits flipped by radiation can be damaged in C(n, 0) + C(n, 1) + … + C(n, t) ways. That number decides how many extra bits the code needs.
- **Grid paths.** A route with a east steps and b north steps is fixed by choosing which of the a + b steps go east: C(a + b, a) routes. Dynamic programming on grids, in DSA II, starts here.
- **Curves.** The smooth curves in drawing programs and in trajectory planners are [[Bézier curves|bezier]], whose blending weights are C(n, k)·(1 − t)ⁿ⁻ᵏ·tᵏ, the terms of the binomial theorem.

**Watch out:** four classic mistakes.

- Treating k > n or k < 0 as an error. The count is 0, and identities like Pascal's rule need those zeros. Python's `math.comb` returns 0 when k > n, but it raises `ValueError` for a negative argument, so guard negatives yourself.
- Using `/` and floats. `factorial(n) / (factorial(k) * factorial(n - k))` gives a float, and floats hold only about 16 significant digits, so C(57, 25) already comes out wrong: 9929472283517788.0 instead of 9,929,472,283,517,787. Stay with whole numbers and `//`.
- Dividing before multiplying. `r = r * m // i` is exact; `r *= m // i` is not.
- [[Fixed-width integers|int64]]. In C++ a `long long` overflows in the middle of row 67, and the product `r * m` in the multiplicative formula overflows even sooner than the answer does. Python's integers have no limit, which is why this course uses them.

::: context double-counting Two counts, one set
Double counting is one of the oldest proof tricks. Euler used it to prove the handshake lemma you will meet in graph theory: add up how many hands each guest shook, and you count every handshake exactly twice, so the total is even. The skill is choosing the set. A good rule: read the formula aloud as a recipe for choosing ("pick the crew, then the captain") and look for a second recipe that makes the same things. When the two recipes agree on what they produce, the formulas must agree on how many.
:::

::: context pascal-history Pascal's triangle is older than Pascal
Blaise Pascal wrote the first full treatment of the triangle in 1654, using it to settle questions about gambling, and the name stuck in Europe. But the triangle was known centuries earlier. The Indian scholar Halayudha described it in the 10th century, explaining older work by Pingala on poetic metres. The Persian mathematician al-Karaji and later Omar Khayyam used it, and in China it is called Yang Hui's triangle, after the 13th-century mathematician who published it, crediting Jia Xian from about 1050. It was rediscovered so often because it keeps appearing wherever people count choices.
:::

::: context stirling How big the middle gets
The middle entry of row n, for even n, is close to 2ⁿ / √(πn/2). For row 40 that estimate gives about 1.39 × 10¹¹, and the true value C(40, 20) is 137,846,528,820. The estimate comes from Stirling's formula, n! ≈ √(2πn)·(n/e)ⁿ, which you can plug into n!/((n/2)!)². So the middle entry is 2ⁿ shrunk only by a factor that grows like √n: nearly all of the 2ⁿ subsets have a size close to n/2. That fact returns in probability as the reason sums of many coin flips pile up near their average.
:::

::: context bezier Curves built from the binomial theorem
A Bézier curve of degree n is steered by n + 1 control points P₀, …, Pₙ. At time t between 0 and 1, the curve is at the weighted average of the points with weights C(n, k)·(1 − t)ⁿ⁻ᵏ·tᵏ. By the binomial theorem these weights add up to ((1 − t) + t)ⁿ = 1ⁿ = 1, which is exactly what makes it an average. Pierre Bézier used them to design car bodies at Renault in the 1960s. Today they draw the letters of every font on your screen and the smooth paths in vector drawing programs and robot motion planners.
:::

::: context int64 When the numbers stop fitting
A C++ `long long` holds whole numbers up to 2⁶³ − 1, about 9.22 × 10¹⁸. C(66, 33) = 7,219,428,434,016,265,740 fits, but C(67, 33) = 14,226,520,737,620,288,370 does not, and signed overflow in C++ is undefined behaviour: the program may print nonsense or worse. In the multiplicative formula the intermediate product `r * m` is i times the next value, so it overflows before the answer does. A common fix is to divide by the gcd of r and i before multiplying. Python's integers grow as needed, at the price of speed.
:::
--- task
Write two functions. Do not use `math.comb` or `math.factorial`: the point is to build them yourself.

- `pascal_rows(n)`: return rows 0 to n of Pascal's triangle as a list of lists. Row r is the list `[C(r, 0), C(r, 1), …, C(r, r)]`. Build each row from the row before it with Pascal's rule, using only addition. For `n < 0` return `[]`.
- `binomial(n, k)`: return C(n, k) for a whole number `n ≥ 0` and any whole number `k`, using the multiplicative formula with exact whole-number division. Return `0` when `k < 0` or `k > n`. Use symmetry, so that the loop runs min(k, n − k) times: a check asks for `binomial(10**9, 10**9 - 2)`, which a loop of a billion steps cannot finish in time.

For example, `pascal_rows(3)` is `[[1], [1, 1], [1, 2, 1], [1, 3, 3, 1]]`, and `binomial(8, 3)` is `56`.
--- starter
def pascal_rows(n):
    return []


def binomial(n, k):
    return 0
--- solution
def pascal_rows(n):
    rows = []
    row = [1]
    for r in range(n + 1):
        rows.append(row)
        row = [1] + [row[k - 1] + row[k] for k in range(1, len(row))] + [1]
    return rows


def binomial(n, k):
    if k < 0 or k > n:
        return 0
    k = min(k, n - k)
    result = 1
    for i in range(1, k + 1):
        result = result * (n - k + i) // i
    return result
--- hint
For `pascal_rows`, keep the current row. The next row is a 1, then the sum of each pair of neighbours in the current row, then another 1.
--- hint
For `binomial`, first return 0 outside 0 ≤ k ≤ n, then replace k with `min(k, n - k)`. Start a result at 1, which is C(n − k, 0).
--- hint
Step i, for i from 1 to k, turns C(n − k + i − 1, i − 1) into C(n − k + i, i): multiply by `n - k + i` first, then divide by `i` with `//`.
--- check case | The first four rows
pascal_rows(3)
=> [[1], [1, 1], [1, 2, 1], [1, 3, 3, 1]]
--- check case | Row 0 alone, and nothing for negative n
(pascal_rows(0), pascal_rows(-1))
=> ([[1]], [])
--- check case | Row 10
pascal_rows(10)[10]
=> [1, 10, 45, 120, 210, 252, 210, 120, 45, 10, 1]
--- check test | Every row r has r + 1 entries and sums to 2 to the power r, up to row 200
(lambda rows: len(rows) == 201 and all(len(rows[r]) == r + 1 and sum(rows[r]) == 2 ** r for r in range(201)))(pascal_rows(200))
--- check case | binomial on small values
(binomial(8, 3), binomial(5, 0), binomial(5, 5), binomial(0, 0), binomial(10, 8))
=> (56, 1, 1, 1, 45)
--- check case | Zero outside the row
(binomial(4, 5), binomial(4, -1), binomial(0, 1), binomial(3, -10))
=> (0, 0, 0, 0)
?? There is no 5-element subset of a 4-element set, and no subset with −1 elements. Return 0 for both.
--- check test | binomial agrees with the triangle for every entry up to row 120
(lambda rows: all(binomial(r, k) == rows[r][k] for r in range(121) for k in range(r + 1)))(pascal_rows(120))
--- check test | Pascal's rule holds, including at the edges
all(binomial(n, k) == binomial(n - 1, k - 1) + binomial(n - 1, k) for n in range(1, 40) for k in range(-2, n + 3))
--- check case | Past the edge of 64-bit integers: C(67, 33) and C(100, 50)
(binomial(67, 33), binomial(100, 50))
=> (14226520737620288370, 100891344545564193334812497256)
--- check case | C(10⁹, 2) and C(10⁹, 10⁹ − 2): symmetry makes the second one fast
(binomial(10**9, 2), binomial(10**9, 10**9 - 2))
=> (499999999500000000, 499999999500000000)
?? Replace k with min(k, n − k) before the loop, so C(10⁹, 10⁹ − 2) takes two steps.
--- check test | Row 1500 is exact: it sums to 2 to the power 1500
sum(binomial(1500, k) for k in range(1501)) == 2 ** 1500
--- check source absent | Does not use math.comb or math.factorial
\b(comb|factorial)\b

+++ practice | Rover routes
--- task
A rover drives on a grid of streets. Each move is one block **east** (`"E"`) or one block **north** (`"N"`). A route from its start to a point `e` blocks east and `n` blocks north is a string of exactly `e` letters `"E"` and `n` letters `"N"`.

Write two functions:

- `route_count(e, n)`: the number of such routes. It is C(e + n, e): a route is fixed by choosing which of its e + n moves go east. You may use `math.comb`.
- `routes(e, n)`: the list of all the routes, in alphabetical order (so `"E"` before `"N"`).

For `e = n = 0` there is one route, the empty string `""`. If `e` or `n` is negative there are no routes: return `0` and `[]`.

For example, `routes(2, 1)` is `["EEN", "ENE", "NEE"]` and `route_count(2, 1)` is `3`.
--- starter
from math import comb


def route_count(e, n):
    return comb(e + n, e)


def routes(e, n):
    return []
--- solution
from math import comb


def route_count(e, n):
    if e < 0 or n < 0:
        return 0
    return comb(e + n, e)


def routes(e, n):
    if e < 0 or n < 0:
        return []
    if e == 0 and n == 0:
        return [""]
    return ["E" + r for r in routes(e - 1, n)] + ["N" + r for r in routes(e, n - 1)]
--- hint
Every route starts with an `"E"` or an `"N"`. After an `"E"`, the rest is a route to (e − 1, n); after an `"N"`, a route to (e, n − 1). That is Pascal's rule, as a list.
--- hint
Write `routes` recursively. The base cases: a negative count gives `[]`, and (0, 0) gives `[""]`. Listing the `"E"` routes first keeps the list in alphabetical order.
--- check case | The example from the task
(routes(2, 1), route_count(2, 1))
=> (["EEN", "ENE", "NEE"], 3)
--- check case | Straight lines and the empty route
(routes(3, 0), routes(0, 2), routes(0, 0))
=> (["EEE"], ["NN"], [""])
--- check case | Negative counts
(routes(-1, 2), route_count(-1, 2), route_count(3, -2))
=> ([], 0, 0)
?? With a negative count there is no route at all: return 0 and [], before calling comb.
--- check test | The list has route_count entries, all different, in order, each with the right letters
all((lambda rs: len(rs) == route_count(e, n) and rs == sorted(set(rs)) and all(r.count("E") == e and r.count("N") == n for r in rs))(routes(e, n)) for e in range(6) for n in range(6))
--- check case | A 30 by 30 grid
route_count(30, 30)
=> 118264581564861424

+++ practice | Expanding a power
--- task
The binomial theorem with x replaced by a and y replaced by b·x says (a + b·x)ⁿ is the sum over k from 0 to n of C(n, k)·aⁿ⁻ᵏ·bᵏ·xᵏ.

Write `expand(a, b, n)` that returns the list of coefficients `[c0, c1, …, cn]` of (a + b·x)ⁿ, where `ck` is the number in front of xᵏ. `a` and `b` are whole numbers (possibly 0 or negative), and `n ≥ 0`. You may use `math.comb`. Python's `0 ** 0` is `1`, which is what the theorem needs.

For example, `expand(1, 2, 3)` is `[1, 6, 12, 8]`, because (1 + 2x)³ = 1 + 6x + 12x² + 8x³.
--- starter
from math import comb


def expand(a, b, n):
    return [comb(n, k) for k in range(n + 1)]
--- solution
from math import comb


def expand(a, b, n):
    return [comb(n, k) * a ** (n - k) * b ** k for k in range(n + 1)]
--- hint
The coefficient of xᵏ is C(n, k) times a to the power n − k times b to the power k.
--- hint
Build the list with a comprehension over k from 0 to n inclusive.
--- check case | The example from the task
expand(1, 2, 3)
=> [1, 6, 12, 8]
--- check case | (x − 1) to the power 4, written as (−1 + x)⁴
expand(-1, 1, 4)
=> [1, -4, 6, -4, 1]
--- check case | n = 0, and a zero term
(expand(5, 7, 0), expand(0, 3, 2), expand(2, 0, 3))
=> ([1], [0, 0, 9], [8, 0, 0, 0])
--- check test | Agrees with multiplying the brackets out one at a time
all(expand(a, b, n) == __import__("functools").reduce(lambda p, _: [x * a + y * b for x, y in zip(p + [0], [0] + p)], range(n), [1]) for a in range(-3, 4) for b in range(-3, 4) for n in range(8))
--- check test | Putting x = 1 gives (a + b) to the power n
all(sum(expand(a, b, n)) == (a + b) ** n for a in range(-4, 5) for b in range(-4, 5) for n in range(10))

+++ practice | Crews with at least one of each
--- task
A mission picks a crew of `size` people from `pilots` pilots and `engineers` engineers (all different people). The crew must contain **at least one pilot and at least one engineer**.

Write `mixed_crews(pilots, engineers, size)` that returns how many such crews there are. Count with the complement and inclusion-exclusion from the sets lesson: take all crews, then remove the crews with no pilot and the crews with no engineer, and add back the crews that have neither, since they were removed twice. You may use `math.comb`. All three arguments are whole numbers ≥ 0.

For example, `mixed_crews(3, 4, 3)` is `30`: C(7, 3) − C(4, 3) − C(3, 3) + C(0, 3) = 35 − 4 − 1 + 0.
--- starter
from math import comb


def mixed_crews(pilots, engineers, size):
    return comb(pilots + engineers, size) - comb(engineers, size) - comb(pilots, size)
--- solution
from math import comb


def mixed_crews(pilots, engineers, size):
    total = comb(pilots + engineers, size)
    no_pilot = comb(engineers, size)
    no_engineer = comb(pilots, size)
    neither = comb(0, size)
    return total - no_pilot - no_engineer + neither
--- hint
A crew with no pilot is a crew chosen only from the engineers, and a crew with no engineer is chosen only from the pilots.
--- hint
A crew with neither is chosen from nobody. There is exactly one such crew when `size` is 0 (the empty crew) and none otherwise, which is C(0, size). Leaving that term out breaks the `size = 0` case.
--- check case | The example from the task
mixed_crews(3, 4, 3)
=> 30
--- check case | A crew of nobody, and a crew of one, cannot be mixed
(mixed_crews(3, 4, 0), mixed_crews(3, 4, 1))
=> (0, 0)
?? For size 0, all three counts are 1, so you must add back C(0, 0) = 1.
--- check case | No pilots at all, or a crew bigger than everyone
(mixed_crews(0, 5, 2), mixed_crews(2, 2, 5))
=> (0, 0)
--- check case | Everyone goes
(mixed_crews(2, 3, 5), mixed_crews(0, 3, 3))
=> (1, 0)
--- check test | Agrees with listing every crew
all(mixed_crews(p, e, s) == sum(1 for c in __import__("itertools").combinations(range(p + e), s) if any(x < p for x in c) and any(x >= p for x in c)) for p in range(5) for e in range(5) for s in range(p + e + 2))

+++ practice | Sharing fuel cells with a minimum
--- task
A depot shares out `cells` identical fuel cells among `tanks` different tanks, and every tank must get **at least** `minimum` cells. All cells must be used.

Write `fuel_splits(cells, tanks, minimum)` that returns the number of ways, for whole numbers `cells ≥ 0`, `tanks ≥ 0` and `minimum ≥ 0`. You may use `math.comb`.

First give every tank its `minimum`. What is left is shared with no minimum, which is stars and bars: `left` cells into `tanks` tanks in C(left + tanks − 1, tanks − 1) ways. Watch the edges:

- if the minimums alone need more cells than there are, the answer is `0`;
- with `tanks = 0` there is one way to share out 0 cells (do nothing) and no way to share out more.

For example, `fuel_splits(10, 3, 2)` is `15`: after giving 2 to each tank, 4 cells are left, and C(6, 2) = 15.
--- starter
from math import comb


def fuel_splits(cells, tanks, minimum):
    left = cells - tanks * minimum
    return comb(left + tanks - 1, tanks - 1)
--- solution
from math import comb


def fuel_splits(cells, tanks, minimum):
    left = cells - tanks * minimum
    if left < 0:
        return 0
    if tanks == 0:
        return 1 if left == 0 else 0
    return comb(left + tanks - 1, tanks - 1)
--- hint
If `left` is negative, return 0 straight away: `math.comb` raises an error on negative numbers.
--- hint
With `tanks = 0` the formula asks for C(left − 1, −1), which is not what you want. Handle it on its own: one way if `left` is 0, otherwise none.
--- check case | The example from the task
fuel_splits(10, 3, 2)
=> 15
--- check case | No minimum is plain stars and bars
(fuel_splits(5, 3, 0), fuel_splits(0, 4, 0), fuel_splits(7, 1, 0))
=> (21, 1, 1)
--- check case | Not enough cells for the minimums
(fuel_splits(5, 3, 2), fuel_splits(0, 1, 1))
=> (0, 0)
?? 3 tanks of at least 2 need 6 cells, and there are only 5. Return 0 before calling comb.
--- check case | No tanks at all
(fuel_splits(0, 0, 3), fuel_splits(4, 0, 0))
=> (1, 0)
--- check test | Agrees with listing every share-out
all(fuel_splits(c, t, m) == sum(1 for s in __import__("itertools").product(range(c + 1), repeat=t) if sum(s) == c and all(x >= m for x in s)) for c in range(8) for t in range(4) for m in range(4))

+++ practice | Debug: the row that goes wrong at row 3
--- task
`pascal_row(n)` should return row `n` of Pascal's triangle, for `n ≥ 0`, using a single list that it updates in place. Rows 0, 1 and 2 come out right, but `pascal_row(3)` returns `[1, 3, 4, 1]` instead of `[1, 3, 3, 1]`, and later rows are worse.

Find the bug and fix it. Keep the single list: the fix is one line.
--- starter
def pascal_row(n):
    row = [1]
    for r in range(1, n + 1):
        row.append(1)
        for k in range(1, r):
            row[k] = row[k] + row[k - 1]
    return row
--- solution
def pascal_row(n):
    row = [1]
    for r in range(1, n + 1):
        row.append(1)
        for k in range(r - 1, 0, -1):
            row[k] = row[k] + row[k - 1]
    return row
--- hint
Pascal's rule needs the **old** values of `row[k - 1]` and `row[k]`. Going from left to right, by the time you reach position k, position k − 1 already holds its new value.
--- hint
Go from right to left instead: `range(r - 1, 0, -1)`. Then `row[k - 1]` has not been updated yet when you use it.
--- check case | The example from the task
pascal_row(3)
=> [1, 3, 3, 1]
--- check case | The first rows
(pascal_row(0), pascal_row(1), pascal_row(2))
=> ([1], [1, 1], [1, 2, 1])
--- check case | Row 8
pascal_row(8)
=> [1, 8, 28, 56, 70, 56, 28, 8, 1]
--- check test | Rows up to 60 agree with math.comb
all(pascal_row(n) == [__import__("math").comb(n, k) for k in range(n + 1)] for n in range(61))

+++ practice | How many telemetry codewords can there be?
--- task
A probe sends telemetry as words of `n` bits. Radiation can flip bits. A code is a chosen set of **codewords**, and it **corrects t errors** if every received word with at most `t` flipped bits can be traced back to exactly one codeword.

Around each codeword, the words that differ from it in at most `t` bits form its **ball**. For the code to correct `t` errors, the balls of different codewords must not overlap. A word differs from a codeword in exactly i bits in C(n, i) ways (choose which i bits flip), so each ball holds C(n, 0) + C(n, 1) + … + C(n, t) words (stopping at C(n, n) if t > n). All the balls fit inside the 2ⁿ possible words, so a code can have at most 2ⁿ divided by the ball size codewords, rounded down. This is the **Hamming bound**.

Write three functions. You may use `math.comb`.

- `ball_size(n, t)`: the number of words within t flips of a given word, for `n ≥ 0`, `t ≥ 0`.
- `hamming_bound(n, t)`: `2**n // ball_size(n, t)`.
- `min_bits(messages, t)`: the smallest `n ≥ 0` for which `hamming_bound(n, t) >= messages`. No code with fewer bits can hold that many messages and still correct `t` errors. `messages ≥ 1`.

For example, `ball_size(7, 1)` is `8`, `hamming_bound(7, 1)` is `16`, and `min_bits(16, 1)` is `7`. (A code that reaches this bound exists: the Hamming code with 16 words of 7 bits.)
--- starter
from math import comb


def ball_size(n, t):
    return comb(n, t)


def hamming_bound(n, t):
    return 2 ** n // ball_size(n, t)


def min_bits(messages, t):
    return 0
--- solution
from math import comb


def ball_size(n, t):
    return sum(comb(n, i) for i in range(min(t, n) + 1))


def hamming_bound(n, t):
    return 2 ** n // ball_size(n, t)


def min_bits(messages, t):
    n = 0
    while hamming_bound(n, t) < messages:
        n += 1
    return n
--- hint
The ball holds the word itself (0 flips), plus C(n, 1) words with one flip, and so on up to t flips. Sum C(n, i) for i from 0 to min(t, n).
--- hint
For `min_bits`, try n = 0, 1, 2, … and stop at the first n whose bound reaches `messages`. The loop always stops: each extra bit at least doubles 2ⁿ while the ball grows more slowly.
--- check case | The example from the task
(ball_size(7, 1), hamming_bound(7, 1), min_bits(16, 1))
=> (8, 16, 7)
--- check case | The binary Golay code fills the space perfectly: 23 bits, 3 errors
(ball_size(23, 3), hamming_bound(23, 3))
=> (2048, 4096)
--- check case | No errors to correct, and more errors than bits
(ball_size(5, 0), hamming_bound(5, 0), ball_size(4, 9), hamming_bound(4, 9))
=> (1, 32, 16, 1)
?? With t larger than n, every word is in the ball: stop the sum at C(n, n).
--- check case | Zero bits hold one message
(ball_size(0, 2), min_bits(1, 0), min_bits(1, 3))
=> (1, 0, 0)
--- check case | Bits needed for 1,000 messages with 0, 1 and 2 corrected errors
(min_bits(1000, 0), min_bits(1000, 1), min_bits(1000, 2))
=> (10, 14, 18)
--- check test | ball_size agrees with counting words for small n
all(ball_size(n, t) == sum(1 for w in range(2 ** n) if bin(w).count("1") <= t) for n in range(9) for t in range(10))

+++ question | Why the two counts add
--- ask
In the proof of Pascal's rule, the k-subsets are split into those that contain x and those that do not. Why may the two counts be added?
--- choice correct
The two kinds do not overlap and together they include every k-subset, so the sum rule applies.
--- choice
Because C(n − 1, k − 1) and C(n − 1, k) are both smaller than C(n, k).
--- choice
Because x was chosen at random, so each kind is half of the subsets.
--- choice
Because the two kinds are the same size, by symmetry.
--- why
The sum rule counts a set that is split into disjoint pieces: its size is the sum of the pieces' sizes. Each k-subset either contains x or it does not, never both, so the pieces are disjoint and cover everything. They are usually not the same size: for k = 1 there is one subset containing x and n − 1 without it.

+++ question | A coefficient from the theorem
--- ask
What is the coefficient of x³ in (2 + x)⁵? Type the number.
--- answer
40
--- why
By the binomial theorem the term with x³ is C(5, 3)·2⁵⁻³·x³ = 10 · 4 · x³ = 40x³. Forgetting the power of 2 gives 10, the most common wrong answer.

+++ question | Why the division is exact
--- ask
The multiplicative formula computes `r = r * (n - k + i) // i` in a loop. Why does `//` never throw away a remainder?
--- choice
Because i always divides n − k + i.
--- choice correct
Because after each step r equals C(n − k + i, i), a count of subsets, which is a whole number.
--- choice
Because Python's `//` rounds to the nearest whole number, which is exact enough.
--- choice
Because the factorials cancel before the division happens.
--- why
i need not divide n − k + i: in C(8, 3), step 3 multiplies by 8 and divides by 3. What makes the division exact is that the product r·(n − k + i) is i times a binomial coefficient, so it is a multiple of i. That is why the multiplication must come first.

=== disc-16 | Discrete probability
--- teach
Last lesson you counted subsets with C(n, k) and proved identities by counting. This lesson turns counting into **chance**. When every outcome of an experiment is equally likely, a probability is just a count divided by a count. You will learn the rules that every probability obeys, how new information changes a probability, when two events have nothing to do with each other, and Bayes' theorem, which turns "how likely is this alarm, if there is a fault?" into "how likely is a fault, now that the alarm has gone off?"

### Sample spaces and events

An **experiment** is anything with an uncertain result: rolling a die, drawing a part from a crate, sending a packet over a noisy link. Its **sample space**, written Ω (the Greek capital omega), is the set of all its possible **outcomes**. In this course Ω is always finite.

An **event** is a subset of Ω: a set of outcomes. "The event happens" means the outcome that actually came up is in that set.

Rolling two dice, one red and one blue, has 36 outcomes: the pairs (red, blue) with each number from 1 to 6. The event "the sum is 7" is the set {(1, 6), (2, 5), (3, 4), (4, 3), (5, 2), (6, 1)}. Since events are sets, everything from the sets lesson applies: "A or B" is the union A ∪ B, "A and B" is the intersection A ∩ B, and "not A" is the complement, written Ā.

### A probability on the outcomes

A **probability function** gives every outcome ω a number P(ω) with

- P(ω) ≥ 0 for every outcome, and
- the P(ω) of all the outcomes adding up to 1.

The **probability of an event** E is the sum of P(ω) over the outcomes ω in E. A probability is a number from 0 (impossible) to 1 (certain).

The most common case is the **uniform** probability, where every outcome gets the same share, 1/|Ω|. Then

P(E) = |E| / |Ω|,

the number of outcomes in E divided by the number of all outcomes. For two fair dice, P(sum is 7) = 6/36 = 1/6. Probability with equally likely outcomes **is** counting, which is why the counting lessons came first.

Not every sample space is uniform. If you record only the **sum** of two dice, the sample space is {2, 3, …, 12}, and those 11 outcomes are not equally likely: P(2) = 1/36 while P(7) = 6/36. The safe habit is to choose outcomes that really are equally likely, such as the 36 ordered pairs, and count those.

### Exact answers with Fraction

Floats cannot hold 1/6 or 1/10 exactly, so they turn "equal" into "nearly equal". Python's `fractions` module has a type, `Fraction`, that stores a whole-number top and bottom and always keeps them in lowest terms, so arithmetic with it is exact:

```python
from fractions import Fraction

print(Fraction(6, 36))                     # 1/6
print(Fraction(1, 10) + Fraction(2, 10))   # 3/10
print(0.1 + 0.2)                           # 0.30000000000000004
print(Fraction(1, 3) * 3 == 1)             # True
```

Fractions mix with whole numbers (`1 - Fraction(1, 6)` is `5/6`), and `float(x)` turns one into a decimal when you want to see its size. This course computes probabilities as fractions, so that a check can say "exactly 1/6".

Counting the sevens among all 36 rolls:

```python
from fractions import Fraction
from itertools import product

rolls = list(product(range(1, 7), repeat=2))
sevens = [r for r in rolls if r[0] + r[1] == 7]
print(len(rolls), len(sevens))              # 36 6
print(Fraction(len(sevens), len(rolls)))    # 1/6
```

### Rules every probability obeys

Everything below follows from the definition, with no extra assumptions.

**Complement:** P(Ā) = 1 − P(A). Proof: every outcome is in exactly one of A and Ā, so the sum over all outcomes, which is 1, splits into P(A) + P(Ā).

**Union:** P(A ∪ B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A ∩ B). Proof: the sum P(A) + P(B) counts each outcome in A ∪ B once, except the outcomes in both A and B, which it counts twice. Subtracting P(A ∩ B) counts those once. This is inclusion-exclusion from the sets lesson, with weights instead of counts.

**The union bound:** P(A ∪ B) ≤ P(A) + P(B), because the part you subtract is never negative. It is crude, but it needs to know nothing about how A and B overlap. It works for any number of events, and it is how engineers bound "the chance that something, anywhere, goes wrong".

The complement is often the easy way in. The **[[birthday problem|birthday]]** asks: with n people and 365 equally likely birthdays, what is the chance that two share a birthday? "Two share" has many messy cases; "all different" has one. The first person can have any birthday, the second any of the other 364, and so on, so the chance that all n are different is (365 · 364 · … · (365 − n + 1)) / 365ⁿ, and the chance of a shared birthday is 1 minus that. It passes 1/2 at n = 23 and is about 0.99 at n = 57.

### Conditional probability

Often you learn something before you know the outcome. The **conditional probability of A given B**, written P(A | B), is

P(A | B) = P(A ∩ B) / P(B),   when P(B) > 0.

In everyday words: you are told B happened, so throw away every outcome outside B, and ask what share of what is left lies in A. Dividing by P(B) rescales the survivors so that they add up to 1 again. With equally likely outcomes it is a count again: P(A | B) = |A ∩ B| / |B|. When P(B) = 0 it is not defined; there is nothing left to take a share of.

Two dice again. You are told that at least one die shows a 6. What is the chance that both do? The event "at least one 6" has 11 outcomes: six with a red 6, six with a blue 6, minus (6, 6), which is in both lists. Only (6, 6) has both, so the answer is 1/11, not 1/6. Keeping track of exactly which outcomes survive is the whole skill.

### Multiplying along a story

Rearranging the definition gives the **multiplication rule**:

P(A ∩ B) = P(B) · P(A | B).

It lets you compute the chance of a story step by step. A crate holds 10 parts, 2 of them faulty, and you draw two without putting the first back. The chance that the first is faulty is 2/10. Given that, 9 parts are left and 1 is faulty, so the second is faulty with chance 1/9. Both: 2/10 · 1/9 = 1/45. Counting agrees: of the C(10, 2) = 45 equally likely pairs, one is the faulty pair. For longer stories, keep multiplying: P(A ∩ B ∩ C) = P(A) · P(B | A) · P(C | A ∩ B).

### Total probability

Events B₁, …, Bₘ **partition** Ω when every outcome is in exactly one of them: the cases of a proof by cases. If each has P(Bᵢ) > 0, then for every event A,

P(A) = P(A | B₁)·P(B₁) + … + P(A | Bₘ)·P(Bₘ).

**Proof.** The pieces A ∩ B₁, …, A ∩ Bₘ do not overlap and together make A, so P(A) is the sum of their probabilities. By the multiplication rule each piece is P(A | Bᵢ)·P(Bᵢ). ∎

This is "split into cases" for probabilities. If 60% of a fleet's thrusters come from supplier X with a 2% failure rate, and 40% from supplier Y with a 5% rate, a random thruster fails with probability 0.02 · 0.6 + 0.05 · 0.4 = 0.032.

### Bayes' theorem

The multiplication rule works both ways round: P(A ∩ B) = P(B)·P(A | B) and also P(A ∩ B) = P(A)·P(B | A). Set the two equal and divide by P(A):

**Bayes' theorem:** P(B | A) = P(A | B) · P(B) / P(A),   when P(A) > 0,

and P(A) usually comes from total probability. It turns the probability you know, of the evidence given the cause, into the one you want, of the cause given the evidence.

A detector checks parts. 1% of parts are faulty. It flags 99% of faulty parts, and it also flags 5% of good ones. A part is flagged: how likely is it to be faulty? Picture 10,000 parts:

| | flagged | not flagged | total |
|---|---|---|---|
| faulty | 99 | 1 | 100 |
| good | 495 | 9,405 | 9,900 |

Of the 594 flagged parts, 99 are faulty: P(faulty | flagged) = 99/594 = 1/6. Bayes' theorem gives the same, 0.99 · 0.01 / (0.99 · 0.01 + 0.05 · 0.99). The detector is "99% accurate", yet five flags in six are false alarms, because good parts vastly outnumber faulty ones. Ignoring how rare the cause is, the [[base rate|base-rate]], is one of the most common reasoning errors there is.

### Independence

Events A and B are **independent** when

P(A ∩ B) = P(A) · P(B).

When P(B) > 0 this says the same as P(A | B) = P(A): learning that B happened does not change the chance of A. The product form is the official definition because it is symmetric and still makes sense when a probability is 0.

Independence is a fact about the numbers, and it can surprise you. With two dice, "the red die is even" (probability 1/2) and "the sum is 7" (probability 1/6) are independent: the intersection {(2, 5), (4, 3), (6, 1)} has probability 3/36 = 1/12 = 1/2 · 1/6. But "the red die is even" and "the sum is 8" are not: 3/36 is not 1/2 · 5/36.

For more than two events, **mutually independent** means the product rule holds for **every** group of two or more of them. Checking only the pairs is not enough. Toss two fair coins, and let A = "the first is heads", B = "the second is heads", and C = "the two coins match". Each has probability 1/2, and each pair has probability 1/4 = 1/2 · 1/2, so they are pairwise independent. But A ∩ B ∩ C = {heads, heads} has probability 1/4, not 1/8: once you know A and B, you know C for certain.

### Independent experiments

Where do the 36 equally likely outcomes of two dice come from? From the assumption that the dice do not influence each other. In general, when two experiments are run independently, the combined experiment has the pairs (x, y) as outcomes, with

P((x, y)) = P₁(x) · P₂(y).
