This is the **product space**. For two fair dice every pair gets 1/6 · 1/6 = 1/36. For a coin that lands heads with probability 1/3, tossed twice, the outcome (heads, tails) gets 1/3 · 2/3 = 2/9. In a product space, any event about the first experiment alone is independent of any event about the second alone, which is exactly what "run independently" should mean.

### Simulation: a check, not a proof

A computer can also estimate a probability by running the experiment many times and counting. Python's `random` module makes pseudo-random choices. A `random.Random(seed)` object with a fixed seed gives the [[same sequence every run|seeds]], which makes a simulation repeatable:

```python
import random

rng = random.Random(2024)
trials = 100_000
wins = 0
for _ in range(trials):
    rolls = [rng.randint(1, 6) for _ in range(4)]
    if 6 in rolls:
        wins += 1
print(wins / trials)    # 0.51834, close to the exact 0.5177
```

That is the chance of at least one 6 in four rolls. The exact answer, by the complement and independence, is 1 − (5/6)⁴ = 671/1296 ≈ 0.5177, the question that started probability theory in a [[1654 exchange of letters|fermat-pascal]]. A simulation's error shrinks only like 1/√(number of trials): 100,000 trials give about two correct decimal places, and each extra digit costs 100 times more trials. So simulation is excellent for catching a wrong formula and useless for proving a right one.

### What it costs

Listing a sample space and summing over it is exact but grows fast: n dice have 6ⁿ outcomes, so 20 dice have about 3.7 × 10¹⁵, far too many to list. The rules above are how you avoid listing: complements, the multiplication rule, independence and counting formulas turn an exponential sum into a few multiplications. Exact fractions are not free either: in the birthday computation the bottom of the fraction is 365ⁿ, whose number of digits grows in proportion to n, so each exact step gets slower as n grows.

### Where it is used

- **Randomised algorithms.** Quicksort with a random pivot, hash tables and random primality tests are analysed with exactly these rules.
- **Reliability.** Redundant sensors, spare thrusters and backup links are judged by computing failure probabilities, usually assuming failures are independent. That assumption is where real systems break, when one cause takes out several parts at once: a [[common-cause failure|common-cause]].
- **Bayesian inference.** Spam filters, medical tests, fault diagnosis and a rover's estimate of its own position all update beliefs with Bayes' theorem as evidence arrives.
- **Hashing and identifiers.** The birthday problem says when random IDs or hash values start to collide: with d possible values, after about √d items.
- **Security.** "An attacker guesses the 128-bit key with probability 2⁻¹²⁸ per try" is a probability statement, and so is every argument that a protocol is safe.

**Watch out:** four traps.

- P(A | B) is not P(B | A). "Most faulty parts get flagged" does not mean "most flagged parts are faulty". Always ask how common the cause is to begin with.
- Disjoint is not independent. If A and B cannot both happen and each has positive probability, they are as dependent as events can be: knowing A happened tells you B did not. Independent events with positive probabilities always overlap.
- Outcomes that are not equally likely. Counting sums of dice as if 2 to 12 were equally likely is wrong. Count outcomes that really are.
- Floats for exact questions. `a / n * (b / n) == c / n` can be `False` when the true values are equal. Use `Fraction` when the question is "exactly equal?".

::: context birthday Birthdays and hash collisions
The birthday problem is really about collisions among random values. With d equally likely values, the chance of at least one collision passes 1/2 after about 1.18·√d items, not d/2 as intuition suggests, because the number of pairs grows like n². For 365 days that is 23 people. For a 32-bit hash it is about 77,000 items, which is why 32-bit checksums cannot serve as unique IDs for large datasets. Cryptographic hashes use 256 bits so that √d is 2¹²⁸, far beyond any computer. Attacks that exploit this are called birthday attacks.
:::

::: context base-rate Ignoring the base rate
The base rate is how common the cause is before any evidence. Studies going back to the 1970s show that people, including doctors, mostly ignore it: told that a test is 99% accurate, they think a positive result means 99%. With a rare condition, most positives are false, as the table shows. The reliable fix is to translate percentages into counts out of a round number, like 10,000 parts, before reasoning. The same error appears in court as the prosecutor's fallacy: confusing "the chance of this evidence if innocent" with "the chance of innocence given this evidence".
:::

::: context seeds Pseudo-random numbers
A computer following instructions cannot be truly random. `random.Random` runs a fixed rule, the Mersenne Twister, that turns a starting number, the seed, into a long sequence that looks random and passes statistical tests. The same seed always gives the same sequence, which is what you want for repeatable tests and simulations. Never use it for secrets: anyone who sees enough of its output can work out the rest. For passwords, keys and tokens Python has the `secrets` module, which draws on the operating system's source of unpredictable data.
:::

::: context fermat-pascal Where probability began
In 1654 a French writer, the Chevalier de Méré, asked Blaise Pascal why betting on "at least one 6 in four rolls" won money while "at least one double 6 in 24 rolls of two dice" seemed to lose. Pascal wrote to Pierre de Fermat, and their letters worked out the answers: 671/1296 ≈ 0.518 for the first bet, and 1 − (35/36)²⁴ ≈ 0.491 for the second. In those letters they also split the stakes of an unfinished game fairly, which needed exactly the counting in this lesson. It is usually taken as the birth of probability theory.
:::

::: context common-cause When independence fails
Two sensors that each fail with probability 1 in 1,000 fail together with probability 1 in a million, but only if their failures are independent. If both share a power supply, a cable run or a software bug, one event can take out both, and the true chance can be close to 1 in 1,000. Such common-cause failures dominate the real risk of redundant systems. That is why safety-critical designs use diverse redundancy: different suppliers, different algorithms and separate routing, so that the independence assumption in the arithmetic is closer to the truth.
:::
--- task
A **probability space** is given as a dictionary that maps each outcome to its probability, a `Fraction`. An **event** is given as a function that takes an outcome and returns `True` or `False`. For example, a fair die is `{1: Fraction(1, 6), 2: Fraction(1, 6), …, 6: Fraction(1, 6)}`, and "even" is `lambda x: x % 2 == 0`.

Write four functions. Every probability they return must be exact (a `Fraction` or a whole number, never a float).

- `prob(space, event)`: P(event), the sum of the probabilities of the outcomes where `event` is true. An event that never happens has probability 0.
- `given(space, a, b)`: P(a | b). If P(b) is 0, raise `ValueError`.
- `independent(space, a, b)`: `True` if P(a ∩ b) = P(a) · P(b) exactly, otherwise `False`.
- `product_space(first, second)`: the space of two experiments run independently. Its outcomes are the pairs `(x, y)`, with `x` an outcome of `first` and `y` one of `second`, and the probability of `(x, y)` is the product of theirs.

For example, with `die` the fair die, `prob(die, lambda x: x >= 5)` is `Fraction(1, 3)`, and `product_space(die, die)` has 36 outcomes, each with probability `Fraction(1, 36)`.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def prob(space, event):
    return 0


def given(space, a, b):
    return 0


def independent(space, a, b):
    return False


def product_space(first, second):
    return {}
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def prob(space, event):
    return sum((p for outcome, p in space.items() if event(outcome)), Fraction(0))


def given(space, a, b):
    pb = prob(space, b)
    if pb == 0:
        raise ValueError("the condition has probability 0")
    return prob(space, lambda o: a(o) and b(o)) / pb


def independent(space, a, b):
    return prob(space, lambda o: a(o) and b(o)) == prob(space, a) * prob(space, b)


def product_space(first, second):
    return {(x, y): p * q for x, p in first.items() for y, q in second.items()}
--- hint
`prob` adds up `p` for each `outcome, p` in `space.items()` where `event(outcome)` is true. Start the sum at `Fraction(0)` so the answer is exact even for an empty event.
--- hint
For "a and b", build a new event on the spot: `lambda o: a(o) and b(o)`. Then `given` is P(a and b) divided by P(b), after checking that P(b) is not 0.
--- hint
`product_space` is a dictionary comprehension with two `for` parts: one over the items of `first`, one over the items of `second`, with key `(x, y)` and value `p * q`.
--- check case | One die: at least 5, and even
(lambda die: (prob(die, lambda x: x >= 5), prob(die, lambda x: x % 2 == 0)))({k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 7)})
=> (Fraction(1, 3), Fraction(1, 2))
--- check case | An event that never happens, and one that always does
(lambda die: (prob(die, lambda x: x > 6), prob(die, lambda x: True)))({k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 7)})
=> (0, 1)
--- check case | A space that is not uniform
(lambda s: (prob(s, lambda x: x != "nominal"), prob(s, lambda x: x.startswith("fault"))))({"nominal": Fraction(9, 10), "fault-a": Fraction(1, 20), "fault-b": Fraction(1, 30), "unknown": Fraction(1, 60)})
=> (Fraction(1, 10), Fraction(1, 12))
--- check test | Two dice: 36 pairs, each 1/36
(lambda die: (lambda two: len(two) == 36 and set(two) == {(a, b) for a in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)} and all(p == Fraction(1, 36) for p in two.values()))(product_space(die, die)))({k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 7)})
--- check case | Two dice: the sum is 7
(lambda die: prob(product_space(die, die), lambda o: o[0] + o[1] == 7))({k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 7)})
=> Fraction(1, 6)
--- check case | Both dice show 6, given that at least one does
(lambda die: given(product_space(die, die), lambda o: o == (6, 6), lambda o: 6 in o))({k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 7)})
=> Fraction(1, 11)
?? "At least one 6" has 11 outcomes, and only (6, 6) of them has two sixes.
--- check case | Sum at least 10, given that the red die shows 6
(lambda die: given(product_space(die, die), lambda o: o[0] + o[1] >= 10, lambda o: o[0] == 6))({k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 7)})
=> Fraction(1, 2)
--- check test | Conditioning on an impossible event raises ValueError
(lambda die: raises(ValueError, lambda: given(die, lambda x: x == 1, lambda x: x == 7)))({k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 7)})
--- check case | Red even and sum 7 are independent; red even and sum 8 are not
(lambda two: (independent(two, lambda o: o[0] % 2 == 0, lambda o: sum(o) == 7), independent(two, lambda o: o[0] % 2 == 0, lambda o: sum(o) == 8)))({(a, b): Fraction(1, 36) for a in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)})
=> (True, False)
--- check case | Disjoint events with positive probability are not independent
(lambda die: independent(die, lambda x: x <= 2, lambda x: x >= 5))({k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 7)})
=> False
--- check test | A biased coin tossed twice: probabilities multiply, and the two tosses are independent
(lambda two: two[("H", "T")] == Fraction(2, 9) and two[("H", "H")] == Fraction(1, 9) and sum(two.values()) == 1 and independent(two, lambda o: o[0] == "H", lambda o: o[1] == "H"))(product_space({"H": Fraction(1, 3), "T": Fraction(2, 3)}, {"H": Fraction(1, 3), "T": Fraction(2, 3)}))
--- check test | First toss heads, second heads, and "they match" are pairwise independent, but the third is decided by the first two
(lambda s, A, B, C: independent(s, A, B) and independent(s, A, C) and independent(s, B, C) and given(s, C, lambda o: A(o) and B(o)) == 1)(product_space({"H": Fraction(1, 2), "T": Fraction(1, 2)}, {"H": Fraction(1, 2), "T": Fraction(1, 2)}), lambda o: o[0] == "H", lambda o: o[1] == "H", lambda o: o[0] == o[1])

+++ practice | The distribution of a dice total
--- task
Write `total_distribution(dice, sides)` that rolls `dice` fair dice, each numbered 1 to `sides`, and returns a dictionary mapping each possible total to its exact probability as a `Fraction`. List every outcome with `itertools.product` and count: all `sides ** dice` outcomes are equally likely. Only totals that can happen appear as keys.

With `dice = 0` there is exactly one outcome, rolling nothing, with total 0, so the answer is `{0: Fraction(1)}`.

For example, `total_distribution(2, 4)` is `{2: Fraction(1, 16), 3: Fraction(1, 8), 4: Fraction(3, 16), 5: Fraction(1, 4), 6: Fraction(3, 16), 7: Fraction(1, 8), 8: Fraction(1, 16)}`.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def total_distribution(dice, sides):
    return {t: Fraction(1, sides * dice - dice + 1) for t in range(dice, sides * dice + 1)}
--- solution
from collections import Counter
from fractions import Fraction
from itertools import product


def total_distribution(dice, sides):
    counts = Counter(sum(roll) for roll in product(range(1, sides + 1), repeat=dice))
    outcomes = sides ** dice
    return {total: Fraction(c, outcomes) for total, c in counts.items()}
--- hint
The starter makes every total equally likely, which is the classic mistake. Count how many of the equally likely rolls give each total instead.
--- hint
`Counter(sum(roll) for roll in product(range(1, sides + 1), repeat=dice))` counts rolls by total. Divide each count by `sides ** dice`, as a `Fraction`.
--- check case | The example from the task
total_distribution(2, 4)
=> {2: Fraction(1, 16), 3: Fraction(1, 8), 4: Fraction(3, 16), 5: Fraction(1, 4), 6: Fraction(3, 16), 7: Fraction(1, 8), 8: Fraction(1, 16)}
--- check case | Two ordinary dice: 7 is the most likely total
(lambda d: (d[7], d[2], d[12], max(d, key=d.get)))(total_distribution(2, 6))
=> (Fraction(1, 6), Fraction(1, 36), Fraction(1, 36), 7)
--- check case | No dice, and one die
(total_distribution(0, 6), total_distribution(1, 3))
=> ({0: Fraction(1)}, {1: Fraction(1, 3), 2: Fraction(1, 3), 3: Fraction(1, 3)})
--- check test | Three dice: 16 totals from 3 to 18, adding up to exactly 1, with 10 and 11 each 1/8
(lambda d: sorted(d) == list(range(3, 19)) and sum(d.values()) == 1 and d[10] == d[11] == Fraction(1, 8))(total_distribution(3, 6))

+++ practice | The birthday problem, exactly
--- task
`n` people each have a birthday chosen independently and uniformly from `days` equally likely days.

Write two functions that give exact answers as `Fraction`s:

- `p_shared(n, days)`: the probability that at least two of the n people share a birthday. Use the complement: the chance that all n birthdays are different is the product of (days − i)/days for i = 0, 1, …, n − 1. With 0 or 1 people it is 0; with more people than days it is 1.
- `smallest_group(days, target)`: the smallest `n` with `p_shared(n, days) >= target`, for a `Fraction` `target` with 0 < target ≤ 1. Update the product one person at a time as n grows, rather than recomputing it from scratch for every n.

For example, `p_shared(2, 365)` is `Fraction(1, 365)` and `smallest_group(365, Fraction(1, 2))` is `23`.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def p_shared(n, days):
    return Fraction(n, days)


def smallest_group(days, target):
    return 0
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def p_shared(n, days):
    all_different = Fraction(1)
    for i in range(n):
        all_different *= Fraction(days - i, days)
    return 1 - all_different


def smallest_group(days, target):
    n = 0
    all_different = Fraction(1)
    while 1 - all_different < target:
        all_different *= Fraction(days - n, days)
        n += 1
    return n
--- hint
In `p_shared`, start `all_different` at `Fraction(1)` and multiply by `Fraction(days - i, days)` for each i in `range(n)`. The answer is 1 minus that. With more people than days, one factor is 0, so it comes out as 1 by itself.
--- hint
In `smallest_group`, keep the product for the current n. Adding person number n + 1 multiplies it by `Fraction(days - n, days)`. Stop as soon as 1 minus the product reaches the target.
--- check case | Two people, and nobody
(p_shared(2, 365), p_shared(0, 365), p_shared(1, 365))
=> (Fraction(1, 365), 0, 0)
--- check case | More people than days
(p_shared(4, 3), p_shared(366, 365))
=> (1, 1)
--- check test | 22 people stay below one half, 23 go above it
p_shared(22, 365) < Fraction(1, 2) < p_shared(23, 365)
--- check case | The group sizes for one half and for 99 in 100
(smallest_group(365, Fraction(1, 2)), smallest_group(365, Fraction(99, 100)))
=> (23, 57)
--- check case | Certainty needs one more person than there are days
(smallest_group(365, Fraction(1)), smallest_group(1, Fraction(1)))
=> (366, 2)
?? With `days` days a collision is certain only when there are `days + 1` people.
--- check case | Four equally likely values
(p_shared(3, 4), smallest_group(4, Fraction(1, 2)))
=> (Fraction(5, 8), 3)

+++ practice | Faulty parts in a sample
--- task
A crate holds `total` parts, `faulty` of them faulty. An inspector draws `drawn` parts at random without putting any back, so every set of `drawn` parts is equally likely.

Write two functions that give exact answers as `Fraction`s. You may use `math.comb`.

- `p_exactly(total, faulty, drawn, k)`: the probability that exactly `k` of the drawn parts are faulty. Count: choose k of the faulty parts and drawn − k of the good ones, out of C(total, drawn) equally likely samples. It is 0 when that is impossible (for example `k` negative, or more than `faulty`, or more than `drawn`).
- `p_caught(total, faulty, drawn)`: the probability that at least one drawn part is faulty. Use the complement.

For example, `p_exactly(10, 2, 3, 1)` is `Fraction(7, 15)`: C(2, 1) · C(8, 2) / C(10, 3) = 2 · 28 / 120.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction
from math import comb


def p_exactly(total, faulty, drawn, k):
    return Fraction(comb(faulty, k), comb(total, drawn))


def p_caught(total, faulty, drawn):
    return 1 - p_exactly(total, faulty, drawn, 1)
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction
from math import comb


def p_exactly(total, faulty, drawn, k):
    if k < 0 or k > drawn:
        return Fraction(0)
    return Fraction(comb(faulty, k) * comb(total - faulty, drawn - k), comb(total, drawn))


def p_caught(total, faulty, drawn):
    return 1 - p_exactly(total, faulty, drawn, 0)
--- hint
A sample with exactly k faulty parts is a choice of k faulty parts **and** drawn − k good parts: multiply C(faulty, k) by C(total − faulty, drawn − k).
--- hint
`math.comb` already gives 0 when the bottom is bigger than the top, but it raises an error for negative numbers, so return 0 first when k is negative or bigger than `drawn`. "At least one" is 1 minus "exactly none".
--- check case | The example from the task
p_exactly(10, 2, 3, 1)
=> Fraction(7, 15)
--- check case | Impossible counts
(p_exactly(10, 2, 3, 3), p_exactly(10, 2, 3, -1), p_exactly(10, 2, 3, 4))
=> (0, 0, 0)
--- check test | For each sample, the probabilities of k = 0 to drawn add up to 1
all(sum(p_exactly(t, f, d, k) for k in range(d + 1)) == 1 for t in range(1, 9) for f in range(t + 1) for d in range(t + 1))
--- check case | Catching a fault
(p_caught(10, 2, 2), p_caught(100, 5, 10), p_caught(10, 0, 5), p_caught(10, 8, 3))
=> (Fraction(17, 45), Fraction(79137, 190120), 0, 1)
--- check test | Agrees with listing every sample
all(p_exactly(t, f, d, k) == Fraction(sum(1 for s in __import__("itertools").combinations(range(t), d) if sum(1 for x in s if x < f) == k), __import__("math").comb(t, d)) for t in range(1, 8) for f in range(t + 1) for d in range(t + 1) for k in range(-1, d + 2))

+++ practice | At least k of n systems work
--- task
A probe carries `n` independent units, and unit i works with probability `probs[i]` (a `Fraction`). The probe survives if **at least** `k` units work.

Write `p_at_least(probs, k)` that returns the exact probability, as a `Fraction`, that at least `k` of the units work.

Build the distribution of the number of working units one unit at a time. Keep a list `dist` where `dist[j]` is the probability that exactly j of the units seen so far work; with no units, `dist` is `[Fraction(1)]`. Adding a unit that works with probability p: exactly j work afterwards if j worked before and the new one fails, or j − 1 worked before and the new one works.

Edge cases: `k <= 0` gives 1 (always true), and `k` greater than the number of units gives 0. The list can be empty.

For example, `p_at_least([Fraction(1, 2), Fraction(1, 2), Fraction(1, 2)], 2)` is `Fraction(1, 2)`.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def p_at_least(probs, k):
    result = Fraction(1)
    for p in probs[:k]:
        result *= p
    return result
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def p_at_least(probs, k):
    dist = [Fraction(1)]
    for p in probs:
        new = [Fraction(0)] * (len(dist) + 1)
        for j, q in enumerate(dist):
            new[j] += q * (1 - p)
            new[j + 1] += q * p
        dist = new
    return sum(dist[max(k, 0):], Fraction(0))
--- hint
For each unit, make a new list one longer than `dist`, full of zeros. For each j, the old `dist[j]` adds `dist[j] * (1 - p)` to position j (the new unit fails) and `dist[j] * p` to position j + 1 (it works).
--- hint
At the end, add up `dist[k]`, `dist[k + 1]`, … to the end. Slicing from `max(k, 0)` handles `k <= 0`, and a slice past the end is empty, which handles k greater than n.
--- check case | The example from the task
p_at_least([Fraction(1, 2), Fraction(1, 2), Fraction(1, 2)], 2)
=> Fraction(1, 2)
--- check case | Units that differ: two of three
p_at_least([Fraction(9, 10), Fraction(4, 5), Fraction(1, 2)], 2)
=> Fraction(17, 20)
--- check case | k of 0 or less, and k beyond the number of units
(p_at_least([Fraction(1, 3)], 0), p_at_least([Fraction(1, 3)], -2), p_at_least([Fraction(1, 3)], 2))
=> (1, 1, 0)
?? At least 0 units always work. At least 2 of 1 unit never do.
--- check case | No units at all
(p_at_least([], 0), p_at_least([], 1))
=> (1, 0)
--- check case | Units that always or never work
(p_at_least([Fraction(1), Fraction(0), Fraction(1)], 2), p_at_least([Fraction(1), Fraction(0), Fraction(1)], 3))
=> (1, 0)
--- check test | Agrees with listing all 2ⁿ ways the units can turn out
(lambda ps: all(p_at_least(ps, k) == sum((__import__("math").prod(p if w else 1 - p for p, w in zip(ps, ws)) for ws in __import__("itertools").product([0, 1], repeat=len(ps)) if sum(ws) >= k), Fraction(0)) for k in range(-1, len(ps) + 2)))([Fraction(1, 2), Fraction(2, 3), Fraction(3, 7), Fraction(9, 10), Fraction(1, 5), Fraction(5, 6)])

+++ practice | Debug: the independence test that fails on dice
--- task
`independent(outcomes, a, b)` gets a list of **equally likely** outcomes and two events (functions that return `True` or `False`), and should return `True` exactly when P(a ∩ b) = P(a) · P(b).

For two dice, "the red die is not a 6" and "the blue die is not a 6" are independent: 25/36 = 5/6 · 5/6. But the function returns `False` for them.

Find the bug and fix it so that the test is exact. The fix changes how the three probabilities are computed.
--- starter
def independent(outcomes, a, b):
    n = len(outcomes)
    pa = sum(1 for o in outcomes if a(o)) / n
    pb = sum(1 for o in outcomes if b(o)) / n
    pab = sum(1 for o in outcomes if a(o) and b(o)) / n
    return pab == pa * pb
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def independent(outcomes, a, b):
    n = len(outcomes)
    pa = Fraction(sum(1 for o in outcomes if a(o)), n)
    pb = Fraction(sum(1 for o in outcomes if b(o)), n)
    pab = Fraction(sum(1 for o in outcomes if a(o) and b(o)), n)
    return pab == pa * pb
--- hint
Print `25 / 36` and `(30 / 36) * (30 / 36)`. The two floats differ in the last digit, because 5/6 has no exact float.
--- hint
Compute each probability as `Fraction(count, n)` instead of `count / n`. Fractions multiply exactly.
--- check case | The example from the task
(lambda rolls: independent(rolls, lambda o: o[0] != 6, lambda o: o[1] != 6))([(r, b) for r in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)])
=> True
--- check case | Dependent events are still reported as dependent
(lambda rolls: (independent(rolls, lambda o: o[0] % 2 == 0, lambda o: sum(o) == 8), independent(rolls, lambda o: o[0] == 1, lambda o: o[0] == 2)))([(r, b) for r in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)])
=> (False, False)
--- check case | A twenty-sided die: at most 4, and not in {1, 5, 6, 7, 8}
independent(list(range(1, 21)), lambda x: x <= 4, lambda x: x not in (1, 5, 6, 7, 8))
=> True
--- check test | Every pair of "red at most i" and "blue at most j" events is independent
(lambda rolls: all(independent(rolls, lambda o: o[0] <= i, lambda o: o[1] <= j) for i in range(7) for j in range(7)))([(r, b) for r in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)])

+++ practice | Diagnosing a fault
--- task
A spacecraft's fault monitor knows a list of possible conditions (for example `"ok"`, `"valve"`, `"sensor"`), each with a **prior** probability. For each condition it also knows, for each symptom, the probability that the symptom shows up when that condition holds. It assumes that, once the condition is fixed, the symptoms show up independently of each other. (This is the **naive Bayes** assumption.)

Write `diagnose(priors, likelihoods, observed)` that returns a dictionary mapping each condition to its exact **posterior** probability, as a `Fraction`, given what was observed:

- `priors` maps each condition to its prior probability; they add up to 1.
- `likelihoods` maps each condition to a dictionary that maps each symptom to the probability that the symptom is **present** under that condition.
- `observed` maps some of the symptoms to `True` (seen) or `False` (checked and not seen). Symptoms not in `observed` were not checked and are ignored.

For each condition, multiply its prior by P(symptom present | condition) for each symptom observed `True`, and by 1 minus that for each observed `False`. By Bayes' theorem and total probability, the posteriors are these products divided by their sum. If the sum is 0 (the observations are impossible under every condition), raise `ValueError`.

For example, with priors ok 9/10 and valve 1/10, and a pressure-drop symptom with probability 1/20 under ok and 3/5 under valve, observing a pressure drop gives valve a posterior of (1/10 · 3/5) / (9/10 · 1/20 + 1/10 · 3/5) = 4/7.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def diagnose(priors, likelihoods, observed):
    return dict(priors)
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def diagnose(priors, likelihoods, observed):
    weights = {}
    for condition, prior in priors.items():
        w = Fraction(prior)
        for symptom, seen in observed.items():
            p = likelihoods[condition][symptom]
            w *= p if seen else 1 - p
        weights[condition] = w
    total = sum(weights.values())
    if total == 0:
        raise ValueError("these observations are impossible")
    return {condition: w / total for condition, w in weights.items()}
--- hint
Work out one unnormalised weight per condition: its prior, times the probability of each observed symptom being the way it was seen under that condition.
--- hint
A symptom observed `False` contributes 1 − P(present | condition). Then divide every weight by the sum of all the weights, after checking that the sum is not 0.
--- check case | The example from the task
diagnose({"ok": Fraction(9, 10), "valve": Fraction(1, 10)}, {"ok": {"drop": Fraction(1, 20)}, "valve": {"drop": Fraction(3, 5)}}, {"drop": True})
=> {"ok": Fraction(3, 7), "valve": Fraction(4, 7)}
--- check case | Nothing observed leaves the priors unchanged
diagnose({"ok": Fraction(9, 10), "valve": Fraction(1, 10)}, {"ok": {"drop": Fraction(1, 20)}, "valve": {"drop": Fraction(3, 5)}}, {})
=> {"ok": Fraction(9, 10), "valve": Fraction(1, 10)}
--- check case | A symptom checked and not seen
diagnose({"ok": Fraction(9, 10), "valve": Fraction(1, 10)}, {"ok": {"drop": Fraction(1, 20)}, "valve": {"drop": Fraction(3, 5)}}, {"drop": False})
=> {"ok": Fraction(171, 179), "valve": Fraction(8, 179)}
--- check case | Three conditions and two symptoms
diagnose({"ok": Fraction(17, 20), "valve": Fraction(1, 10), "sensor": Fraction(1, 20)}, {"ok": {"drop": Fraction(1, 50), "noise": Fraction(1, 10)}, "valve": {"drop": Fraction(4, 5), "noise": Fraction(1, 10)}, "sensor": {"drop": Fraction(1, 2), "noise": Fraction(9, 10)}}, {"drop": True, "noise": True})
=> {"ok": Fraction(17, 322), "valve": Fraction(40, 161), "sensor": Fraction(225, 322)}
--- check test | Impossible observations raise ValueError
raises(ValueError, lambda: diagnose({"ok": Fraction(1, 2), "valve": Fraction(1, 2)}, {"ok": {"drop": Fraction(0)}, "valve": {"drop": Fraction(0)}}, {"drop": True}))
--- check test | A condition that cannot produce a seen symptom drops to 0, and the rest add up to 1
(lambda post: post["ok"] == 0 and sum(post.values()) == 1)(diagnose({"ok": Fraction(1, 2), "valve": Fraction(1, 4), "sensor": Fraction(1, 4)}, {"ok": {"drop": Fraction(0)}, "valve": {"drop": Fraction(1, 3)}, "sensor": {"drop": Fraction(2, 3)}}, {"drop": True}))

+++ question | The flagged part
--- ask
1% of parts are faulty. A detector flags 99% of faulty parts and 5% of good parts. A part is flagged. Which is closest to the probability that it is faulty?
--- choice
99%
--- choice
95%
--- choice correct
17%
--- choice
1%
--- why
Out of 10,000 parts, 100 are faulty and 99 of them are flagged; 9,900 are good and 495 of them are flagged. Only 99 of the 594 flagged parts are faulty, which is 1/6, about 17%. 99% is P(flagged | faulty), the reverse conditional.

+++ question | Disjoint events
--- ask
Events A and B cannot both happen, and each has probability 1/3. Are they independent?
--- choice
Yes: they have nothing to do with each other.
--- choice correct
No: P(A ∩ B) is 0, but P(A) · P(B) is 1/9.
--- choice
Yes: independence and disjointness mean the same thing.
--- choice
There is not enough information to tell.
--- why
Independence needs P(A ∩ B) = P(A) · P(B). Disjoint events have P(A ∩ B) = 0, so with positive probabilities they are never independent. Knowing that A happened tells you for certain that B did not.

+++ question | Two sixes
--- ask
Two fair dice are rolled, and you are told that at least one shows a 6. What is the probability that both do? Type it as a fraction, like 1/2.
--- answer
1/11
--- why
"At least one 6" has 11 equally likely outcomes: 6 with a red 6 and 6 with a blue 6, minus (6, 6), which was counted twice. Only one of the 11 has two sixes.

=== disc-17 | Random variables and expectation
--- teach
Last lesson you computed the probability of events: "the sum is 7", "a part is faulty". Often you want a number instead of a yes or no: how many packets are lost, how many comparisons a sort makes, how long until a signal gets through. This lesson gives those numbers a name, random variables, and a way to summarise each one with a single number, its expected value. Its most useful tool, linearity of expectation, answers questions in one line that would take pages by listing outcomes.

### Random variables

A **random variable** is a function X from the sample space Ω to the real numbers: it gives every outcome ω a number X(ω). Despite the name it is [[neither random nor a variable|rv-name]]; it is a plain function, as in the functions lesson. The randomness is all in which outcome comes up.

With two dice, where an outcome is a pair (red, blue):

- S, the **sum**, gives (2, 5) the number 7;
- M, the **maximum**, gives (2, 5) the number 5.

In Python a random variable is just a function of the outcome, such as `lambda o: o[0] + o[1]`.

"X = x" is an event: the set of outcomes ω with X(ω) = x. Its probability is written P(X = x). The table of these probabilities, one for each value x that X can take, is the **distribution** of X. For the maximum of two dice, P(M = 1) = 1/36 (only (1, 1)), and P(M = 6) = 11/36.

### Indicator variables

The simplest random variables have only two values. The **indicator** of an event A, written I_A, is 1 on the outcomes in A and 0 on the others. It turns a yes-or-no question into a number you can add up. In Python, `int(event(o))` is the indicator: `int(True)` is 1 and `int(False)` is 0.

### Expected value

The **expected value** (or **expectation**, or **mean**) of X is the weighted average of its values, each outcome weighted by its probability:

E[X] = the sum over all outcomes ω of P(ω) · X(ω).

For one fair die, E[X] = (1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 + 6)/6 = 7/2. The expected value need not be a value X can take: no die shows 3.5. What it means is the long-run average. If you roll many times, the average of the rolls settles near 3.5, a fact called the [[law of large numbers|lln]].

There is a second formula: E[X] = the sum over the values x of x · P(X = x). **Why both agree:** group the outcomes by the value X gives them. The outcomes with X(ω) = x together contribute x times the sum of their probabilities, which is x · P(X = x). Grouping only reorders a finite sum. So you can use whichever is easier: the outcomes or the distribution.

For the maximum of two dice:

```python
from fractions import Fraction
from itertools import product

rolls = list(product(range(1, 7), repeat=2))
print(Fraction(sum(max(r) for r in rolls), len(rolls)))   # 161/36, about 4.47
```

### Linearity of expectation

**Theorem:** for any random variables X and Y on the same sample space and any number c,

E[X + Y] = E[X] + E[Y]   and   E[c · X] = c · E[X].

**Proof.** Write out the sum. E[X + Y] is the sum over ω of P(ω)·(X(ω) + Y(ω)). Multiply out each term and split the sum in two: the sum of P(ω)·X(ω), plus the sum of P(ω)·Y(ω). Those are E[X] and E[Y]. The constant c comes out of every term the same way. ∎

Read the proof again and notice what it does **not** need: independence. X and Y can depend on each other in any way at all. For two dice the sum has expected value 7/2 + 7/2 = 7, and you never need the distribution of the sum. It works even for red + red, which is as dependent as it gets: E[2 · red] = 2 · 7/2 = 7. The same holds for a sum of any number of variables, by induction on how many there are.

### Counting with indicators

First, E[I_A] = 1 · P(A) + 0 · P(Ā) = P(A): **the expected value of an indicator is the probability of its event.**

Now suppose X counts how many of the events A₁, …, Aₙ happen. Then X = I_A₁ + … + I_Aₙ, since each event that happens adds 1. By linearity,

E[X] = P(A₁) + … + P(Aₙ).

This is the most useful trick in the lesson: to find an expected count, write the count as a sum of indicators and add up probabilities. Each probability is usually easy, even when the distribution of X is a mess.

**Coins.** Toss n coins, each heads with probability p. Heads in toss i has probability p, so the expected number of heads is n·p.

**Swapped labels.** A technician relabels n sensor cables in a completely random order: all n! orders equally likely. How many cables, on average, get their own label back? Cable i does exactly when the order puts label i on it, which happens in (n − 1)! of the n! orders, so with probability 1/n. Adding the n indicators: **1**, for every n. Getting that from the distribution needs the [[derangement numbers|hat-check]]; with indicators it is two lines.

**Shared birthdays.** Among n people, how many **pairs** share a birthday, on average? There are C(n, 2) pairs, and each pair matches with probability 1/365. So the expected number is C(n, 2)/365, which passes 1 at n = 28.

### Distributions to know

**Bernoulli(p):** a single trial that succeeds with probability p; its indicator has expected value p.

**Binomial(n, p):** the number X of successes in n independent trials, each succeeding with probability p. Then

P(X = k) = C(n, k) · pᵏ · (1 − p)ⁿ⁻ᵏ.

**Why:** one particular pattern of results with k successes and n − k failures has probability pᵏ(1 − p)ⁿ⁻ᵏ, because the trials are independent, so the probabilities multiply. There are C(n, k) patterns, one for each choice of which k trials succeed, and the patterns are disjoint. By the binomial theorem the probabilities add up to (p + (1 − p))ⁿ = 1, as they must. The expected value is n·p, by the indicators above.

**Geometric(p):** keep trying until the first success; X is the number of tries. P(X = k) = (1 − p)ᵏ⁻¹ · p: k − 1 failures, then a success. This needs an infinite sample space (there is no limit on the number of tries), but the same rules carry over. There is a neat way to find E[X]. For a random variable whose values are whole numbers 0, 1, 2, …,

E[X] = P(X ≥ 1) + P(X ≥ 2) + P(X ≥ 3) + …

**Why:** X is the sum of the indicators of "X ≥ 1", "X ≥ 2", "X ≥ 3", and so on. If X is 3, exactly the first three are 1. So linearity gives the formula. For the geometric, X ≥ k means the first k − 1 tries failed, with probability (1 − p)ᵏ⁻¹. The sum 1 + (1 − p) + (1 − p)² + … is a geometric series, from the precalculus module, and equals 1/(1 − (1 − p)) = **1/p**. A link that loses 1 packet in 5 needs, on average, 1/(4/5) = 5/4 [[transmissions|arq]] per packet.

### Products need independence

Random variables X and Y are **independent** when P(X = x and Y = y) = P(X = x) · P(Y = y) for every pair of values x, y. Then

E[X · Y] = E[X] · E[Y].

**Why:** by the second formula, E[XY] is the sum over pairs of values of x·y·P(X = x and Y = y). With independence each term is x·P(X = x) times y·P(Y = y), and the double sum factors into the product of the two single sums.

Without independence it fails. Let X be the indicator of heads on one fair coin, and let Y = X. Then XY = X, so E[XY] = 1/2, but E[X]·E[Y] = 1/4.

A related trap: E[f(X)] is usually not f(E[X]). For one die, E[X²] = (1 + 4 + 9 + 16 + 25 + 36)/6 = 91/6, about 15.17, while E[X]² = 49/4 = 12.25.

### Variance: how spread out

Two random variables can have the same mean and behave very differently. The **variance** measures the spread around the mean μ = E[X]:

Var(X) = E[(X − μ)²],

the expected squared distance from the mean. Its square root is the **standard deviation**, in the same units as X. By linearity there is a shorter formula:

Var(X) = E[X²] − μ².

**Why:** (X − μ)² = X² − 2μX + μ². Take expectations, using linearity and the fact that μ is a constant: E[X²] − 2μ·E[X] + μ² = E[X²] − 2μ² + μ² = E[X²] − μ².

For one die, Var(X) = 91/6 − 49/4 = 35/12. A constant has variance 0. For independent X and Y, Var(X + Y) = Var(X) + Var(Y); the proof expands the square and uses E[XY] = E[X]·E[Y]. So a binomial, a sum of n independent indicators each with variance p − p², has variance n·p·(1 − p).

### Markov's inequality

The mean alone already limits how often a variable can be large. **Markov's inequality:** if X is never negative, then for every a > 0,

P(X ≥ a) ≤ E[X] / a.

**Proof.** Compare X with a · I, where I is the indicator of "X ≥ a". On outcomes where X ≥ a, a · I is a, which is at most X. Elsewhere a · I is 0, which is at most X because X is never negative. So X ≥ a · I on every outcome, and taking expected values keeps the inequality (each term of the sum does): E[X] ≥ a · P(X ≥ a). Divide by a. ∎

So if a randomised algorithm takes T steps on average, the chance that one run takes 10T steps or more is at most 1/10. Applying Markov's inequality to (X − μ)² gives **Chebyshev's inequality**: P(|X − μ| ≥ t) ≤ Var(X)/t², so a small variance keeps X near its mean.

### What it costs

Computing E[X] from the definition means visiting every outcome: 6ⁿ for n dice, n! for a random order of n cables. Linearity replaces that with one easy probability per indicator: n terms for the cables instead of n! outcomes. That is not a small saving; it is the difference between impossible and instant, and it is why average-case analysis of algorithms is done this way.

### Where it is used

- **Average-case analysis.** Quicksort with random pivots makes about 2n ln n comparisons on average; the proof writes the count as a sum of one indicator per pair of items. A hash table with n keys in m buckets has expected chain length n/m, by indicators.
- **Randomised algorithms.** Expected running time, plus Markov's inequality, turns "fast on average" into "fast with high probability": run it, and restart if it takes too long.
- **Networks.** Expected retransmissions (geometric), expected queue lengths and expected delays size links and buffers.
- **Decisions.** Mission planners compare options by expected cost or expected science return, and use the variance to see the risk.
- **Machine learning.** Training minimises the expected error over the data, and a random sample estimates that expectation.

**Watch out:** four classic slips.

- Thinking linearity needs independence. It never does. Products and variances of sums do.
- Taking E[f(X)] = f(E[X]). The mean of the squares is not the square of the mean, and the average of 1/X is not 1 over the average.
- Reading the expected value as the most likely value. The expected number of heads in 3 tosses is 1.5, which never happens.
- Indicators that miss or double-count. Make sure that each thing you count adds exactly 1 to exactly one indicator, for example one indicator per **pair**, with i < j, not per ordered pair.

::: context rv-name Why "random variable"
The name comes from the early 20th century, when probability was mostly about measurements that vary randomly, like errors in astronomy. In the 1930s Andrey Kolmogorov put probability on the footing used today: a sample space, a probability on events, and random variables as functions on the sample space. The old name stayed. Thinking of X as a function is what makes things like X + Y clear: it is the function whose value at ω is X(ω) + Y(ω). That is also exactly how you write it in Python.
:::

::: context lln The law of large numbers
If you repeat an experiment independently many times, the average of the results gets close to E[X] with probability close to 1. This is the law of large numbers, proved by Jakob Bernoulli in 1713 for coin tosses, and it follows from Chebyshev's inequality in this lesson: the average of n independent copies has variance Var(X)/n, which shrinks to 0. It is why simulations work, why casinos profit on average, and why the expected value is the right summary for a choice you will make many times.
:::

::: context hat-check The hat-check problem
The classic version: n people check their hats, and the hats are handed back in a random order. The number of orders where nobody gets their own hat is the derangement number Dₙ, which is close to n!/e, so the chance that nobody gets their own hat approaches 1/e ≈ 0.368 as n grows. Pierre de Montmort studied it in 1708. Working out the full distribution of the number of matches needs inclusion-exclusion over all subsets of people. The expected number of matches, 1, needs only linearity.
:::

::: context arq Retransmitting until it arrives
Protocols like TCP resend a packet until it is acknowledged, a scheme called automatic repeat request. If each attempt is lost independently with probability q, the number of attempts is geometric with p = 1 − q, so the expected number is 1/(1 − q). At 20% loss that is 1.25 sends per packet. At 90% loss it is 10, and the link has effectively lost 90% of its capacity. Deep-space links are so slow that waiting to resend is expensive, so they add error-correcting codes instead, trading extra bits for fewer retransmissions.
:::
--- task
As in the last lesson, a probability space is a dictionary mapping each outcome to its probability (a `Fraction`). A **random variable** is a function that takes an outcome and returns a number.

Write three functions that give exact answers:

- `expectation(space, X)`: E[X], the sum of P(ω) · X(ω) over all outcomes ω.
- `distribution(space, X)`: a dictionary that maps each value X takes on an outcome of the space to P(X = that value).
- `variance(space, X)`: Var(X) = E[(X − μ)²], where μ = E[X].

For example, with `die = {k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 7)}`, `expectation(die, lambda x: x)` is `Fraction(7, 2)` and `variance(die, lambda x: x)` is `Fraction(35, 12)`. With the space of two dice, `distribution` of the maximum maps 6 to `Fraction(11, 36)`.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def expectation(space, X):
    return 0


def distribution(space, X):
    return {}


def variance(space, X):
    return 0
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def expectation(space, X):
    return sum((p * X(o) for o, p in space.items()), Fraction(0))


def distribution(space, X):
    dist = {}
    for o, p in space.items():
        value = X(o)
        dist[value] = dist.get(value, Fraction(0)) + p
    return dist


def variance(space, X):
    mu = expectation(space, X)
    return expectation(space, lambda o: (X(o) - mu) ** 2)
--- hint
`expectation` is one sum over `space.items()`: add `p * X(o)` for each outcome `o` with probability `p`, starting from `Fraction(0)`.
--- hint
For `distribution`, go through the outcomes and add each one's probability to the entry for its value, using `dist.get(value, Fraction(0))` for a value you have not seen yet.
--- hint
For `variance`, find μ first. Then the variance is the expectation of a new random variable, `lambda o: (X(o) - mu) ** 2`.
--- check case | One die: the mean and the variance
(lambda die: (expectation(die, lambda x: x), variance(die, lambda x: x)))({k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 7)})
=> (Fraction(7, 2), Fraction(35, 12))
--- check case | The distribution of a loaded three-sided die
(lambda die: distribution(die, lambda x: x))({k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 3)} | {3: Fraction(2, 3)})
=> {1: Fraction(1, 6), 2: Fraction(1, 6), 3: Fraction(2, 3)}
--- check case | Two dice: the maximum
(lambda two: (expectation(two, max), distribution(two, max)[6], distribution(two, max)[1], len(distribution(two, max))))({(a, b): Fraction(1, 36) for a in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)})
=> (Fraction(161, 36), Fraction(11, 36), Fraction(1, 36), 6)
--- check case | Two dice: the sum has mean 7 and variance 35/6
(lambda two: (expectation(two, sum), variance(two, sum)))({(a, b): Fraction(1, 36) for a in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)})
=> (Fraction(7), Fraction(35, 6))
--- check test | The distribution of the sum adds up to 1, with 11 values and P(sum = 7) = 1/6
(lambda d: sum(d.values()) == 1 and len(d) == 11 and d[7] == Fraction(1, 6))(distribution({(a, b): Fraction(1, 36) for a in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)}, sum))
--- check case | An indicator's expectation is its event's probability
(lambda two: expectation(two, lambda o: int(6 in o)))({(a, b): Fraction(1, 36) for a in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)})
=> Fraction(11, 36)
--- check test | Linearity: E[3X − 2Y + 5] = 3E[X] − 2E[Y] + 5 on an uneven space, with Y depending on X
(lambda s, X, Y: expectation(s, lambda o: 3 * X(o) - 2 * Y(o) + 5) == 3 * expectation(s, X) - 2 * expectation(s, Y) + 5)({"a": Fraction(1, 2), "b": Fraction(1, 3), "c": Fraction(1, 6)}, lambda o: {"a": 4, "b": -1, "c": 9}[o], lambda o: {"a": 16, "b": 1, "c": 81}[o])
--- check case | A constant has variance 0; E[X²] is not E[X]²
(lambda die: (variance(die, lambda x: 5), expectation(die, lambda x: x * x), expectation(die, lambda x: x) ** 2))({k: Fraction(1, 6) for k in range(1, 7)})
=> (0, Fraction(91, 6), Fraction(49, 4))
--- check test | Var(X) = E[X²] − E[X]² on an uneven space with negative values
(lambda s, X: variance(s, X) == expectation(s, lambda o: X(o) ** 2) - expectation(s, X) ** 2)({1: Fraction(1, 10), 2: Fraction(3, 10), 3: Fraction(2, 5), 4: Fraction(1, 5)}, lambda o: 7 - 3 * o * o)
--- check case | Values that repeat across outcomes are merged
(lambda s: distribution(s, lambda o: o % 3))({k: Fraction(1, 10) for k in range(10)})
=> {0: Fraction(2, 5), 1: Fraction(3, 10), 2: Fraction(3, 10)}

+++ practice | The binomial distribution
--- task
A probe sends `n` packets, and each one gets through independently with probability `p` (a `Fraction`). Let X be the number that get through.

Write `binomial_pmf(n, p)` that returns the list `[P(X = 0), P(X = 1), …, P(X = n)]` of exact `Fraction`s, using P(X = k) = C(n, k) · pᵏ · (1 − p)ⁿ⁻ᵏ. You may use `math.comb`. For `n = 0` the list is `[Fraction(1)]`.

Then write `binomial_mean(n, p)` that returns E[X] computed **from your list**, as the sum of k · P(X = k). (The checks compare it with n · p.)

For example, `binomial_pmf(2, Fraction(1, 3))` is `[Fraction(4, 9), Fraction(4, 9), Fraction(1, 9)]`.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction
from math import comb


def binomial_pmf(n, p):
    return [Fraction(comb(n, k), 2 ** n) for k in range(n + 1)]


def binomial_mean(n, p):
    return n * p
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction
from math import comb


def binomial_pmf(n, p):
    p = Fraction(p)
    return [comb(n, k) * p ** k * (1 - p) ** (n - k) for k in range(n + 1)]


def binomial_mean(n, p):
    return sum((k * q for k, q in enumerate(binomial_pmf(n, p))), Fraction(0))
--- hint
The starter uses probability 1/2 for every packet. Put p to the power k and (1 − p) to the power n − k into each term.
--- hint
For the mean, `enumerate(binomial_pmf(n, p))` gives pairs `(k, P(X = k))`. Add up `k * q`.
--- check case | The example from the task
binomial_pmf(2, Fraction(1, 3))
=> [Fraction(4, 9), Fraction(4, 9), Fraction(1, 9)]
--- check case | No packets, and packets that always or never arrive
(binomial_pmf(0, Fraction(1, 2)), binomial_pmf(3, Fraction(1)), binomial_pmf(2, Fraction(0)))
=> ([Fraction(1)], [0, 0, 0, 1], [1, 0, 0])
--- check test | The probabilities add up to 1 and the mean is n·p
all(sum(binomial_pmf(n, p)) == 1 and binomial_mean(n, p) == n * p for n in range(12) for p in [Fraction(1, 2), Fraction(1, 7), Fraction(5, 6), Fraction(0), Fraction(1)])
--- check test | Agrees with listing every pattern of arrivals
all(binomial_pmf(n, p) == [sum((__import__("math").prod(p if b else 1 - p for b in bits) for bits in __import__("itertools").product([0, 1], repeat=n) if sum(bits) == k), Fraction(0)) for k in range(n + 1)] for n in range(7) for p in [Fraction(1, 3), Fraction(3, 4)])
--- check case | Ten packets on a link that delivers 9 in 10
(binomial_pmf(10, Fraction(9, 10))[10], binomial_mean(10, Fraction(9, 10)))
=> (Fraction(3486784401, 10000000000), 9)

+++ practice | Empty buckets in a hash table
--- task
A hash table has `n` buckets. `m` keys are placed one at a time, each into a bucket chosen uniformly at random and independently of the others.

Use indicators and linearity to write two functions that return exact `Fraction`s:

- `expected_empty(m, n)`: the expected number of buckets that end up with no key. Bucket j is empty when all m keys miss it.
- `expected_single(m, n)`: the expected number of buckets that end up with **exactly one** key. Bucket j has exactly one key when one particular key lands in it (m choices of which key) and the other m − 1 keys miss it.

Both are for whole numbers `m ≥ 0` and `n ≥ 1`.

For example, `expected_empty(2, 2)` is `Fraction(1, 2)` and `expected_single(2, 2)` is `Fraction(1, 1)`: half the time both keys share a bucket (1 empty, 0 single), and half the time they split (0 empty, 2 single).
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def expected_empty(m, n):
    return Fraction(max(n - m, 0))


def expected_single(m, n):
    return Fraction(min(m, n))
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def expected_empty(m, n):
    miss = 1 - Fraction(1, n)
    return n * miss ** m


def expected_single(m, n):
    miss = 1 - Fraction(1, n)
    return n * m * Fraction(1, n) * miss ** (m - 1) if m > 0 else Fraction(0)
--- hint
One key misses a given bucket with probability 1 − 1/n, so all m keys miss it with probability (1 − 1/n) to the power m. There are n buckets, and each contributes its probability.
--- hint
For exactly one key: m choices of which key, times 1/n for it to land there, times (1 − 1/n) to the power m − 1 for all the others to miss. With m = 0 no bucket has one key.
--- check case | The example from the task
(expected_empty(2, 2), expected_single(2, 2))
=> (Fraction(1, 2), 1)
--- check case | No keys: every bucket is empty, none has one key
(expected_empty(0, 5), expected_single(0, 5))
=> (5, 0)
--- check case | One bucket
(expected_empty(3, 1), expected_single(1, 1), expected_single(3, 1))
=> (0, 1, 0)
--- check test | Agrees with listing all n to the m placements
all(expected_empty(m, n) == Fraction(sum(sum(1 for b in range(n) if b not in pl) for pl in __import__("itertools").product(range(n), repeat=m)), n ** m) and expected_single(m, n) == Fraction(sum(sum(1 for b in range(n) if pl.count(b) == 1) for pl in __import__("itertools").product(range(n), repeat=m)), n ** m) for m in range(6) for n in range(1, 5))
--- check test | 100 keys in 100 buckets leave about 36.6 empty, close to 100/e
abs(float(expected_empty(100, 100)) - 36.603) < 0.001

+++ practice | Expected inversions after a shuffle
--- task
An **inversion** in a list is a pair of positions i < j where the item at i is **bigger** than the item at j. A sorted list has none.

A list of readings `values` is shuffled so that every one of its orders (as positions, all `len(values)!` of them) is equally likely. Write `expected_inversions(values)` that returns the exact expected number of inversions after the shuffle, as a `Fraction`.

Use one indicator per pair of positions. Two **different** values end up in the wrong order with probability 1/2, by symmetry: swapping them turns each order into one where they are the other way round. Two **equal** values never form an inversion. So the answer is half the number of pairs of items whose values differ. You may use `collections.Counter` and `math.comb`.

For example, `expected_inversions([3, 1, 2])` is `Fraction(3, 2)`, and `expected_inversions([5, 5, 7])` is `Fraction(1)`.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction
from math import comb


def expected_inversions(values):
    return Fraction(comb(len(values), 2), 2)
--- solution
from collections import Counter
from fractions import Fraction
from math import comb


def expected_inversions(values):
    pairs = comb(len(values), 2)
    equal_pairs = sum(comb(c, 2) for c in Counter(values).values())
    return Fraction(pairs - equal_pairs, 2)
--- hint
The starter treats every pair as able to be inverted. Pairs of equal values can never be, so subtract them before halving.
--- hint
If a value appears c times, it makes C(c, 2) pairs of equal items. `Counter(values).values()` gives every c.
--- check case | The example from the task
(expected_inversions([3, 1, 2]), expected_inversions([5, 5, 7]))
=> (Fraction(3, 2), 1)
--- check case | Empty, one item, and all equal
(expected_inversions([]), expected_inversions([4]), expected_inversions([2, 2, 2, 2]))
=> (0, 0, 0)
?? Pairs of equal values can never be inverted, so a list of equal values always has 0 inversions.
--- check case | n different values give n(n − 1)/4
(expected_inversions(list(range(10))), expected_inversions(list(range(101))))
=> (Fraction(45, 2), 2525)
--- check test | Agrees with averaging over every shuffle, with and without repeats
all(expected_inversions(v) == Fraction(sum(sum(1 for i in range(len(v)) for j in range(i + 1, len(v)) if perm[i] > perm[j]) for perm in __import__("itertools").permutations(v)), __import__("math").factorial(len(v))) for v in [[1, 2, 3, 4], [2, 2, 1], [3, 1, 3, 1, 2], [9, 9, 9, 1], ["b", "a", "c", "a"]])

+++ practice | Collecting every kind of sample
--- task
A rover picks up rock samples. Each sample is one of `n` kinds, each kind equally likely, independently of earlier samples. The rover already holds `have` different kinds and keeps collecting until it holds `want` different kinds (`0 ≤ have ≤ want ≤ n`).

While the rover holds i different kinds, each new sample is a new kind with probability (n − i)/n, so the number of samples until the next new kind is geometric with mean n/(n − i). By linearity, the expected total is the sum of n/(n − i) for i = have, have + 1, …, want − 1.

Write `expected_samples(n, have, want)` that returns this exactly as a `Fraction`. If `have == want` the answer is 0 (nothing to do), including when `n` is 0.

For example, `expected_samples(6, 0, 6)` is `Fraction(147, 10)`: on average 14.7 rolls of a die to see every face.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def expected_samples(n, have, want):
    return sum(Fraction(n, n - i) for i in range(have, want + 1))
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def expected_samples(n, have, want):
    return sum((Fraction(n, n - i) for i in range(have, want)), Fraction(0))
--- hint
The range of i should stop **before** `want`: once the rover holds `want` kinds it stops. With i = n the fraction would divide by zero.
--- hint
Give `sum` a start of `Fraction(0)` so that an empty range gives an exact 0.
--- check case | The example from the task
expected_samples(6, 0, 6)
=> Fraction(147, 10)
--- check case | Nothing left to collect
(expected_samples(6, 6, 6), expected_samples(0, 0, 0), expected_samples(5, 2, 2))
=> (0, 0, 0)
?? When have equals want, the sum is empty. Stop the range before want.
--- check case | The first kind takes one sample; the last of 10 takes 10 on average
(expected_samples(10, 0, 1), expected_samples(10, 9, 10))
=> (1, 10)
--- check case | Starting partway, and stopping partway
(expected_samples(4, 2, 4), expected_samples(4, 0, 2))
=> (6, Fraction(7, 3))
--- check test | All 50 kinds take 50 times the sum of 1/k for k from 1 to 50
expected_samples(50, 0, 50) == 50 * sum(Fraction(1, k) for k in range(1, 51))

+++ practice | Debug: the variance that is too big
--- task
`variance(values, probs)` gets a list of values and a list of their probabilities (same length, `Fraction`s adding up to 1) and should return Var(X) = E[X²] − E[X]² exactly.

For a fair coin with values 0 and 1 it returns `1/2 - 1/2 = 0`, which happens to look plausible, but for a fair die it returns `Fraction(35, 3)` instead of `Fraction(35, 12)`. Find the bug and fix it. It is one line.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def variance(values, probs):
    mean = sum((p * x for x, p in zip(values, probs)), Fraction(0))
    second = sum((p * x * x for x, p in zip(values, probs)), Fraction(0))
    return second - mean
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def variance(values, probs):
    mean = sum((p * x for x, p in zip(values, probs)), Fraction(0))
    second = sum((p * x * x for x, p in zip(values, probs)), Fraction(0))
    return second - mean ** 2
--- hint
Write out the formula: the variance is E[X²] minus the **square** of E[X]. Which part of the last line is not squared?
--- hint
For a fair coin, E[X] = 1/2 and E[X]² = 1/4, so the true variance is 1/4, not 0.
--- check case | The example from the task: a fair die
variance([1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6], [Fraction(1, 6)] * 6)
=> Fraction(35, 12)
--- check case | A fair coin
variance([0, 1], [Fraction(1, 2), Fraction(1, 2)])
=> Fraction(1, 4)
--- check case | A certain value has no spread
variance([7], [Fraction(1)])
=> 0
--- check case | An uneven distribution with negative values
variance([-2, 0, 3], [Fraction(1, 4), Fraction(1, 2), Fraction(1, 4)])
=> Fraction(51, 16)

+++ practice | How many comparisons does quicksort make?
--- task
Randomised quicksort sorts n different items like this: pick a pivot uniformly at random, compare it with every other item to split them into smaller and larger, then sort each side the same way.

Name the items by their rank, 1 (smallest) to n. Two items with ranks i < j are compared **at most once**, and only if one of them is a pivot while both are still on the same side. Consider the ranks i, i + 1, …, j. They stay together until the first time one of them is picked as a pivot. If that first pivot is i or j, the two are compared; if it is one strictly between them, they are split apart and never compared. Each of the j − i + 1 ranks is equally likely to be first, so **P(i and j are compared) = 2/(j − i + 1)**.

By linearity, the expected number of comparisons is the sum of 2/(j − i + 1) over all pairs i < j. Pairs with the same distance d = j − i have the same probability, and there are n − d of them.

Write `expected_comparisons(n)` that returns this sum exactly as a `Fraction`, for `n ≥ 0`. Group the pairs by distance so that the loop runs about n times: a check uses n = 3000, where a loop over all 4.5 million pairs of exact fractions is far too slow.

For example, `expected_comparisons(3)` is `Fraction(8, 3)`.
--- starter
from fractions import Fraction


def expected_comparisons(n):
    return Fraction(n * (n - 1), 2)
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def expected_comparisons(n):
    return sum((Fraction(2 * (n - d), d + 1) for d in range(1, n)), Fraction(0))
--- hint
Distance d runs from 1 to n − 1. There are n − d pairs at distance d, each compared with probability 2/(d + 1).
--- hint
So the answer is the sum over d of (n − d) · 2/(d + 1), as a `Fraction`, starting from `Fraction(0)` so that n = 0 and n = 1 give 0.
--- check case | The example from the task
expected_comparisons(3)
=> Fraction(8, 3)
--- check case | Nothing to compare, and one comparison
(expected_comparisons(0), expected_comparisons(1), expected_comparisons(2))
=> (0, 0, 1)
--- check test | Matches an actual quicksort averaged over every order of up to 7 items
all(expected_comparisons(n) == Fraction(sum((lambda f: f(f, list(perm)))(lambda f, l: 0 if len(l) <= 1 else len(l) - 1 + f(f, [x for x in l[1:] if x < l[0]]) + f(f, [x for x in l[1:] if x > l[0]])) for perm in __import__("itertools").permutations(range(n))), __import__("math").factorial(n)) for n in range(8))
--- check case | Ten items
expected_comparisons(10)
=> Fraction(30791, 1260)
--- check test | 3,000 items: equals the closed form 2(n + 1)Hₙ − 4n, where Hₙ is the sum of 1/k
(lambda n: expected_comparisons(n) == 2 * (n + 1) * sum(Fraction(1, k) for k in range(1, n + 1)) - 4 * n)(3000)

+++ question | Does linearity need independence?
--- ask
X is the number on the red die and Y = 7 − X, which depends completely on X. What is E[X + Y], and why?
--- choice correct
7, because E[X + Y] = E[X] + E[Y] = 7/2 + 7/2 holds for any random variables, dependent or not.
--- choice
It cannot be found with linearity, because X and Y are not independent.
--- choice
49/4, because E[X] · E[Y] = 7/2 · 7/2.
--- choice
0, because the dependence cancels out.
--- why
Linearity of expectation needs no independence: its proof just splits a sum. Here X + Y is 7 on every outcome anyway, so its expected value is 7. What needs independence is the product rule E[XY] = E[X]·E[Y].

+++ question | Markov's bound
--- ask
A random variable X is never negative and E[X] = 3. What is the largest value P(X ≥ 12) could possibly have? Type it as a fraction.
--- answer
1/4
--- why
Markov's inequality gives P(X ≥ 12) ≤ 3/12 = 1/4, and it can be reached: let X be 12 with probability 1/4 and 0 otherwise. Then E[X] = 3 and P(X ≥ 12) = 1/4 exactly.

+++ question | The mean of a product
--- ask
X is the indicator of heads on a fair coin, and Y = X. Which is true?
--- choice
E[XY] = E[X] · E[Y] = 1/4, as always.
--- choice correct
E[XY] = 1/2, which is not E[X] · E[Y] = 1/4, because X and Y are not independent.
--- choice
E[XY] = 1, because X and Y are always equal.
--- choice
E[XY] cannot be computed without more information.
--- why
XY = X·X = X, since X is 0 or 1, so E[XY] = E[X] = 1/2. The product rule E[XY] = E[X]·E[Y] needs independence, and X is as dependent on itself as a variable can be.

=== disc-18 | Number theory: divisibility and Euclid's algorithm
--- teach
The last three lessons were about chance. The next three are about whole numbers, and they end with RSA, the public-key cipher that protected most of the web for decades. Its security rests on facts about divisibility, and its keys are made with the algorithm in this lesson. You used `math.gcd` in the lesson on proof by contradiction. Now you will build it, prove it correct, see why it is astonishingly fast, and extend it to find the numbers x and y that make ax + by equal to the gcd.

### Divisibility

For integers a and b, **a divides b**, written a | b, when b = a · k for some integer k. You also say a is a **divisor** (or factor) of b, and b is a **multiple** of a. So 3 | 12, since 12 = 3 · 4, and −3 | 12 too, but 5 does not divide 12. Careful: a | b is a statement, true or false, while a / b is a number.

Some facts follow straight from the definition:

- Every integer divides 0, since 0 = a · 0.
- If a | b and b | c, then a | c: if b = ak and c = bl, then c = a(kl).
- **If a | b and a | c, then a divides bx + cy for all integers x and y.** Proof: write b = ak and c = al. Then bx + cy = akx + aly = a(kx + ly), and kx + ly is an integer. ∎

The last fact, "a divisor of two numbers divides every combination of them", is the engine of this whole lesson. A sum bx + cy with integer x and y is called a **linear combination** of b and c.

### Division with remainder

**Theorem (division with remainder).** For all integers a and b with b > 0, there is exactly one pair of integers q and r with

a = q · b + r   and   0 ≤ r < b.

q is the **quotient** and r the **remainder**, written a mod b.

**Proof of existence.** Let q be the largest integer with q · b ≤ a; one exists, because the multiples of b go down forever and up forever in steps of b. Let r = a − q·b. Then r ≥ 0, because q·b ≤ a. And r < b, because otherwise (q + 1)·b ≤ a would hold, and q would not be the largest.

**Proof of uniqueness.** Suppose a = qb + r = q'b + r', with both remainders between 0 and b − 1. Subtracting gives r − r' = (q' − q)·b, so b divides r − r'. But r − r' lies strictly between −b and b, and the only multiple of b there is 0. So r = r', and then q = q'. ∎

Python's `//` and `%` give exactly this q and r when b > 0, even when a is negative. `divmod` gives both at once:

```python
print(divmod(17, 5))     # (3, 2)    17 = 3·5 + 2
print(divmod(-7, 2))     # (-4, 1)   −7 = −4·2 + 1, and the remainder is not negative
print(-7 % 2 == 1)       # True
```

Many other languages, including C, C++ and Java, [[round the other way|trunc-mod]]: in C++, `-7 / 2` is −3 and `-7 % 2` is −1.

### The greatest common divisor

The **greatest common divisor** gcd(a, b) of two integers, not both 0, is the largest integer that divides both. For example, the divisors of 12 are 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12, the divisors of 18 are 1, 2, 3, 6, 9, 18, and the largest one they share is 6. Signs make no difference, since −d divides a exactly when d does, so gcd(a, b) = gcd(|a|, |b|). Also gcd(a, 0) = |a|, because everything divides 0. For gcd(0, 0), where every integer is a common divisor, the convention is 0.

Two integers are **coprime** (or relatively prime) when their gcd is 1: they share no factor except 1. For example, 8 and 15 are coprime, though neither is prime.

Trying every candidate d from min(|a|, |b|) downwards works, but for two 100-digit numbers it would take about 10¹⁰⁰ tries. Euclid found something much better, [[more than 2,000 years ago|euclid-elements]].

### Euclid's key fact

**Lemma.** For integers a and b with b > 0: gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, a mod b).

**Proof.** Write a = qb + r with r = a mod b. We show that the pairs (a, b) and (b, r) have exactly the same common divisors, so their largest common divisor is the same.

- If d divides a and b, then d divides r = a − q·b, a linear combination of a and b. So d divides b and r.
- If d divides b and r, then d divides a = q·b + r, a linear combination of b and r. So d divides a and b. ∎

### Euclid's algorithm

So replace the pair (a, b) by (b, a mod b), again and again, until the second number is 0. Then the answer is the first number, because gcd(g, 0) = g. For gcd(252, 198):

| a | b | a = q·b + r | new pair |
|---|---|---|---|
| 252 | 198 | 252 = 1·198 + 54 | (198, 54) |
| 198 | 54 | 198 = 3·54 + 36 | (54, 36) |
| 54 | 36 | 54 = 1·36 + 18 | (36, 18) |
| 36 | 18 | 36 = 2·18 + 0 | (18, 0) |

So gcd(252, 198) = 18. In Python, with a tuple assignment that updates both numbers at once:

```python
def gcd(a, b):
    a, b = abs(a), abs(b)
    while b != 0:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

print(gcd(252, 198), gcd(-12, 18), gcd(7, 0), gcd(0, 0))   # 18 6 7 0
```

**Why it is correct.** The loop invariant is: gcd(a, b) equals the gcd of the original inputs. It holds at the start (taking absolute values changes nothing), and each pass keeps it by the lemma. **Why it stops:** b is never negative and gets strictly smaller on every pass, because a % b < b. A sequence of whole numbers that keeps shrinking cannot go on forever, so eventually b = 0, and then the invariant says a is the answer.

### Why it is fast

**Lemma.** If 0 < b ≤ a, then a mod b < a/2.

**Proof.** Two cases. If b ≤ a/2, then a mod b < b ≤ a/2. If b > a/2, then a fits b only once, so a mod b = a − b < a − a/2 = a/2. ∎

After two passes of the loop, the first number of the pair is the old a mod b, so the first number at least halves every two passes. Halving a number n can happen only about log₂ n times before it reaches 1, so Euclid's algorithm takes at most about 2 · log₂(min(a, b)) + 2 passes: **O(log min(a, b))** divisions. That is proportional to the number of **digits**, not to the size of the number. Two 100-digit numbers take at most a few hundred steps.

The worst case is two neighbouring **Fibonacci numbers** (1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13, …, each the sum of the two before). Every quotient is then 1, so each step makes the smallest possible progress: gcd(13, 8) goes through (8, 5), (5, 3), (3, 2), (2, 1), (1, 0). Proving that Fibonacci numbers are the worst case was [[one of the first analyses of an algorithm's running time|lame]].

Compare the **subtraction** version, which replaces (a, b) by (a − b, b). It is correct by the same lemma, but gcd(10⁴⁰, 3) takes about 3 × 10³⁹ subtractions: it would outlast the universe. The division does all those subtractions in one step.

### Bézout's identity

Euclid's algorithm gives more than the gcd.

**Theorem ([[Bézout's identity|bezout]]).** For integers a and b, not both 0, there are integers x and y with

a·x + b·y = gcd(a, b).

What is more, gcd(a, b) is the **smallest positive** number of the form a·x + b·y.

**Proof, by extending the algorithm.** Run Euclid's algorithm, and write every number it produces as a linear combination of the original a and b. Call the numbers r₀ = a, r₁ = b, r₂, r₃, … where each new one is r_{i+1} = r_{i−1} − q_i · r_i. Keep a pair of coefficients (s_i, t_i) for each with

r_i = a · s_i + b · t_i.

- For r₀ = a use (1, 0), and for r₁ = b use (0, 1).
- If r_{i−1} and r_i are combinations with coefficients (s_{i−1}, t_{i−1}) and (s_i, t_i), then r_{i+1} = r_{i−1} − q_i·r_i is a combination with coefficients (s_{i−1} − q_i·s_i, t_{i−1} − q_i·t_i). The coefficients follow the same rule as the remainders.

When the remainder reaches 0, the last nonzero remainder is the gcd, and its coefficients are the x and y. That proves they exist. For "smallest": any common divisor d of a and b divides every a·x + b·y, so every positive combination is a multiple of gcd(a, b) and at least that big. The gcd is itself a combination, so it is the smallest. ∎

Here is the **extended Euclidean algorithm** on 252 and 198. Each row's s and t come from the two rows above it, using the q of the row above:

| r | q | s | t | check: 252·s + 198·t |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 252 | | 1 | 0 | 252 |
| 198 | 1 | 0 | 1 | 198 |
| 54 | 3 | 1 | −1 | 54 |
| 36 | 1 | −3 | 4 | 36 |
| 18 | 2 | 4 | −5 | 18 |
| 0 | | | | |

So 252 · 4 + 198 · (−5) = 1008 − 990 = 18. The cost is the same O(log) number of steps as plain Euclid, with a few more multiplications per step. The x and y are not unique: 252 · (4 + 11) + 198 · (−5 − 14) is also 18, since 252 · 11 = 198 · 14.

### What Bézout gives you

**Every common divisor divides the gcd.** If d | a and d | b, then d divides a·x + b·y = gcd(a, b). So the gcd is not just the largest common divisor; all the others divide it.

**Euclid's lemma.** If p is prime and p | a·b, then p | a or p | b. **Proof.** Suppose p does not divide a. The only positive divisors of p are 1 and p, so gcd(p, a) = 1, and Bézout gives 1 = p·x + a·y. Multiply by b: b = p·b·x + a·b·y. p divides both terms (the second because p | ab), so p divides b. ∎ The same proof shows more generally: **if n | a·b and gcd(n, a) = 1, then n | b.**

**Unique factorisation.** The strong induction lesson proved that every integer n ≥ 2 is a product of primes. Euclid's lemma gives the other half of the **fundamental theorem of arithmetic**: the factorisation is unique, apart from the order of the factors. If p₁p₂…p_k = q₁q₂…q_m with all of them prime, then p₁ divides the right side, so (by Euclid's lemma, applied again and again) it divides some q_j, and a prime dividing a prime is equal to it. Cancel it from both sides, and repeat on the shorter product. That is an induction on k.

**The least common multiple.** lcm(a, b), the smallest positive integer that both a and b divide, equals a·b / gcd(a, b) for positive a and b. (In the prime factorisations, the gcd takes the smaller power of each prime and the lcm the larger, and min + max = the sum.) Compute it as `a // gcd(a, b) * b`, dividing first so the numbers stay small.

### Equations in whole numbers

An equation where only integer answers count is called **Diophantine**. The simplest kind is a·x + b·y = c.

**Theorem.** With g = gcd(a, b) (a and b not both 0), a·x + b·y = c has an integer solution exactly when g | c. If (x₀, y₀) is one solution, then all the solutions are

x = x₀ + (b/g)·t,   y = y₀ − (a/g)·t,   for every integer t.

**Proof.** If there is a solution, g divides a·x + b·y = c. Conversely, if c = g·m, multiply Bézout's a·x + b·y = g by m. The formula gives solutions, since the extra terms a·(b/g)·t and −b·(a/g)·t cancel. It gives all of them: if (x, y) is any solution, subtracting gives a·(x − x₀) = −b·(y − y₀). Divide by g: (a/g)·(x − x₀) = −(b/g)·(y − y₀). Now a/g and b/g are coprime, so by the general form of Euclid's lemma, b/g divides x − x₀. Write x − x₀ = (b/g)·t, and the equation gives y − y₀ = −(a/g)·t. ∎

Example: can 6x + 15y = 9 be solved? gcd(6, 15) = 3 divides 9, so yes: x = −1, y = 1 works, and so does every x = −1 + 5t, y = 1 − 2t. But 6x + 15y = 10 has no integer solution at all, since 3 does not divide 10.

### Where it is used

- **Cryptography.** RSA keys are made with the extended Euclidean algorithm, as you will see in two lessons, and it runs every time a key pair is generated.
- **Modular arithmetic.** The next lesson uses Bézout to divide "on a clock", which is the basis of hashing, checksums and ciphers.
- **Schedules.** Two beacons that transmit every 12 and every 18 minutes transmit together every lcm(12, 18) = 36 minutes. Real-time systems compute this [[hyperperiod|hyperperiod]] to know how long a schedule runs before it repeats.
- **Ratios.** Fractions in lowest terms, gear ratios, and audio resampling from 44,100 to 48,000 samples a second, which is the ratio 147 : 160 after dividing by the gcd, 300.
- **Computer algebra.** The same algorithm finds the gcd of two polynomials, which is used in error-correcting codes.

**Watch out:** four classic mistakes.

- Updating one number before the other. `a = b` followed by `b = a % b` computes `b % b`, which is always 0. Use the tuple assignment `a, b = b, a % b`, which evaluates the right side first. The coefficients s and t need the same care.
- Trusting `%` with negative numbers in another language. Python's remainder has the sign of the divisor; C, C++ and Java give −1 for −7 % 2. Take absolute values first, or fix a negative remainder by adding b.
- Forgetting the zero cases. gcd(a, 0) = |a|, gcd(0, 0) = 0, and with negative inputs the gcd is still positive.
- Computing the lcm as `a * b // gcd(a, b)` in a language with fixed-width integers: the product can overflow even when the lcm fits. Divide first.

::: context trunc-mod Two ways to round a quotient
When −7 is divided by 2, the true quotient is −3.5, and a language must choose which whole number to call the quotient. Python rounds down (towards minus infinity), giving −4 and remainder 1, so the remainder always has the divisor's sign. C, C++, Java, JavaScript and Rust round towards zero, giving −3 and remainder −1. Both satisfy a = q·b + r. Python's choice matches the mathematics in this lesson and makes `i % n` a safe index into a list of length n even for negative i; in C++ you would write `((i % n) + n) % n`.
:::

::: context euclid-elements The oldest algorithm still in daily use
Euclid's Elements, written in Alexandria around 300 BC, describes the algorithm in Book VII, Propositions 1 and 2, with lengths instead of numbers: repeatedly take the shorter line segment away from the longer. Donald Knuth called it "the granddaddy of all algorithms, because it is the oldest nontrivial algorithm that has survived to the present day". It runs today inside every web browser and phone, each time a cryptographic key is created or checked.
:::

::: context lame The first analysis of an algorithm
In 1844 the French mathematician Gabriel Lamé proved that the number of division steps in Euclid's algorithm is at most five times the number of decimal digits in the smaller number. His proof shows that the slowest inputs of a given size are neighbouring Fibonacci numbers, which grow by a factor of about 1.618 at each step. It is often called the first result in computational complexity: a proof, about all inputs, of how long an algorithm can take, more than a century before computers.
:::

::: context bezout Who found it
The identity is named after Étienne Bézout, who proved a version of it for polynomials in 1779. For whole numbers it was known earlier: Claude Gaspard Bachet de Méziriac described how to find x and y in 1624, and the Indian mathematician Aryabhata gave a method for such equations around the year 500. In mathematics a result is often named after someone who was not first, a pattern so common it has a name of its own, Stigler's law.
:::

::: context hyperperiod Repeating schedules
A flight computer might run the attitude loop every 10 ms, read the star tracker every 25 ms and log telemetry every 40 ms. The whole pattern of tasks repeats every lcm(10, 25, 40) = 200 ms, called the hyperperiod. Checking that every deadline is met over one hyperperiod checks it forever, because what follows is an exact copy. Engineers choose periods with small lcms for this reason: periods of 10, 20 and 40 ms have a hyperperiod of 40, while 7, 11 and 13 ms give 1,001.
:::
--- task
Write two functions. Use a loop, not recursion: the checks use numbers with hundreds of digits.

- `ext_gcd(a, b)`: for any integers `a` and `b` (negative or zero allowed), return a tuple `(g, x, y)` where `g = gcd(a, b)` is never negative and `a * x + b * y == g`. For `a = b = 0`, `g` is 0 and any `x` and `y` will do. Do not use `math.gcd`.
- `solve_linear(a, b, c)`: return one pair of integers `(x, y)` with `a * x + b * y == c`, or `None` if there is none. Use `ext_gcd`. When `a = b = 0`, there is a solution only if `c` is 0.

For example, `ext_gcd(252, 198)` could return `(18, 4, -5)`, `solve_linear(6, 15, 9)` could return `(-6, 3)`, and `solve_linear(6, 15, 10)` is `None`.
--- starter
def ext_gcd(a, b):
    return (0, 0, 0)


def solve_linear(a, b, c):
    return None
--- solution
def ext_gcd(a, b):
    old_r, r = a, b
    old_s, s = 1, 0
    old_t, t = 0, 1
    while r != 0:
        q = old_r // r
        old_r, r = r, old_r - q * r
        old_s, s = s, old_s - q * s
