        old_t, t = t, old_t - q * t
    if old_r < 0:
        old_r, old_s, old_t = -old_r, -old_s, -old_t
    return (old_r, old_s, old_t)


def solve_linear(a, b, c):
    g, x, y = ext_gcd(a, b)
    if g == 0:
        return (0, 0) if c == 0 else None
    if c % g != 0:
        return None
    m = c // g
    return (x * m, y * m)
--- hint
Keep three pairs, each an "old" value and a current value: the remainders (start `a`, `b`), the s coefficients (start 1, 0) and the t coefficients (start 0, 1). Loop while the current remainder is not 0.
--- hint
Each pass computes `q = old_r // r`, then updates all three pairs the same way with tuple assignment: `old_r, r = r, old_r - q * r`, and likewise for s and t. At the end the gcd and its coefficients are in the "old" slots. If the gcd came out negative (negative inputs can do that), flip the sign of all three.
--- hint
In `solve_linear`, a solution exists only if g divides c. Then multiply Bézout's x and y by c // g. Handle g = 0 first, so you never divide by zero.
--- check test | The example from the task: gcd(252, 198) = 18 with a valid x and y
(lambda g, x, y: g == 18 and 252 * x + 198 * y == 18)(*ext_gcd(252, 198))
--- check test | Coprime numbers give 1
(lambda g, x, y: g == 1 and 17 * x + 5 * y == 1)(*ext_gcd(17, 5))
--- check test | Negative inputs still give a positive gcd
all((lambda g, x, y: g == 6 and a * x + b * y == 6)(*ext_gcd(a, b)) for a, b in [(-12, 18), (12, -18), (-12, -18), (18, -12)])
?? If the loop ends with a negative gcd, negate g, x and y together: the equation still holds.
--- check test | Zeros: gcd(a, 0) = |a|, gcd(0, b) = |b|, gcd(0, 0) = 0
all((lambda g, x, y: g == e and a * x + b * y == g)(*ext_gcd(a, b)) for a, b, e in [(7, 0, 7), (0, 5, 5), (-9, 0, 9), (0, -4, 4), (0, 0, 0)])
--- check test | 2,000 pairs with seeded random signs and sizes agree with math.gcd
(lambda rng: all((lambda a, b: (lambda g, x, y: g == __import__("math").gcd(a, b) and a * x + b * y == g)(*ext_gcd(a, b)))(rng.randint(-10**6, 10**6), rng.randint(-10**6, 10**6)) for _ in range(2000)))(__import__("random").Random(18))
--- check test | Neighbouring Fibonacci numbers with over 200 digits: the worst case, still fast
(lambda f: (lambda g, x, y: g == 1 and f[1000] * x + f[999] * y == 1)(*ext_gcd(f[1000], f[999])))(__import__("functools").reduce(lambda l, _: l + [l[-1] + l[-2]], range(1000), [0, 1]))
?? Euclid takes about 1,000 steps here. A recursive version would come close to Python's recursion limit, so use a loop.
--- check test | A 40-digit number and 3: division, not repeated subtraction
(lambda g, x, y: g == 1 and 10**40 * x + 3 * y == 1)(*ext_gcd(10**40, 3))
--- check test | Two 300-digit numbers with a large common factor
(lambda rng: (lambda k, a, b: (lambda g, x, y: g == __import__("math").gcd(a, b) and g % k == 0 and a * x + b * y == g)(*ext_gcd(a, b)))(*(lambda k: (k, k * rng.randrange(10**150), k * rng.randrange(10**150)))(rng.randrange(10**149, 10**150))))(__import__("random").Random(7))
--- check test | solve_linear finds a solution when the gcd divides c
all((lambda s: s is not None and a * s[0] + b * s[1] == c)(solve_linear(a, b, c)) for a, b, c in [(6, 15, 9), (6, 15, -21), (252, 198, 18 * 7), (5, 0, 35), (0, 4, -8), (-3, 7, 1), (1, 1, 0)])
--- check case | No solution when the gcd does not divide c
(solve_linear(6, 15, 10), solve_linear(4, 0, 6), solve_linear(-8, 12, 2))
=> (None, None, None)
--- check test | Both coefficients zero: only c = 0 works
(lambda s: s is not None and 0 * s[0] + 0 * s[1] == 0)(solve_linear(0, 0, 0)) and solve_linear(0, 0, 5) is None
?? With a = b = 0 the gcd is 0. Check for it before computing c % g, which would divide by zero.

+++ practice | When do the beacons line up?
--- task
Several beacons each transmit on a fixed period: beacon i transmits at minutes 0, `periods[i]`, 2 · `periods[i]`, and so on. All of them transmit together at minute 0, and next together at the least common multiple of all the periods.

Write `next_alignment(periods)` that returns that minute, for a list of positive integers. For an empty list return `1`. Build the answer one period at a time, using lcm(a, b) = a // gcd(a, b) · b. You may use `math.gcd`.

For example, `next_alignment([12, 18])` is `36`, and `next_alignment([10, 25, 40])` is `200`.
--- starter
def next_alignment(periods):
    result = 1
    for p in periods:
        result = result * p
    return result
--- solution
from math import gcd


def next_alignment(periods):
    result = 1
    for p in periods:
        result = result // gcd(result, p) * p
    return result
--- hint
Multiplying all the periods gives a common multiple, but usually not the least one: 12 · 18 = 216, while 36 already works.
--- hint
Keep a running answer, starting at 1. Each new period p replaces it with lcm(answer, p) = answer // gcd(answer, p) * p.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(next_alignment([12, 18]), next_alignment([10, 25, 40]))
=> (36, 200)
--- check case | Empty, one period, and repeated periods
(next_alignment([]), next_alignment([7]), next_alignment([6, 6, 6]))
=> (1, 7, 6)
--- check case | Coprime periods multiply; divisors add nothing
(next_alignment([7, 11, 13]), next_alignment([10, 20, 40]), next_alignment([1, 9]))
=> (1001, 40, 9)
--- check case | The periods 1 to 20
next_alignment(list(range(1, 21)))
=> 232792560
--- check test | Every period divides the answer, and no smaller positive number works, for seeded random lists
(lambda rng: all((lambda ps, m: all(m % p == 0 for p in ps) and not any(all(k % p == 0 for p in ps) for k in range(1, m)))(ps, next_alignment(ps)) for ps in [[rng.randint(1, 12) for _ in range(rng.randint(1, 4))] for _ in range(60)]))(__import__("random").Random(3))

+++ practice | The steps of Euclid's algorithm
--- task
Write `euclid_trace(a, b)` that returns the steps of Euclid's algorithm on whole numbers `a ≥ 0` and `b ≥ 0` as a list of tuples `(a, b, q, r)`, one per division, where `a = q * b + r` and `0 <= r < b`. The next step divides `b` by `r`, and the trace ends with the step whose remainder is 0. If `b` is 0 there are no divisions, so return `[]`.

For example, `euclid_trace(252, 198)` is `[(252, 198, 1, 54), (198, 54, 3, 36), (54, 36, 1, 18), (36, 18, 2, 0)]`. If `a < b`, the first step has `q = 0` and just swaps them: `euclid_trace(3, 5)` starts with `(3, 5, 0, 3)`.
--- starter
def euclid_trace(a, b):
    steps = []
    while b != 0:
        steps.append((a, b, a // b, a % b))
        a = b
        b = a % b
    return steps
--- solution
def euclid_trace(a, b):
    steps = []
    while b != 0:
        q, r = divmod(a, b)
        steps.append((a, b, q, r))
        a, b = b, r
    return steps
--- hint
The starter's last two lines update `a` first, and then `a % b` uses the new `a`, which is `b` itself, so the remainder is always 0.
--- hint
Compute `q, r = divmod(a, b)` once, record the step, then move on with `a, b = b, r` in one tuple assignment.
--- check case | The example from the task
euclid_trace(252, 198)
=> [(252, 198, 1, 54), (198, 54, 3, 36), (54, 36, 1, 18), (36, 18, 2, 0)]
--- check case | The smaller number first
euclid_trace(3, 5)
=> [(3, 5, 0, 3), (5, 3, 1, 2), (3, 2, 1, 1), (2, 1, 2, 0)]
--- check case | Zero cases
(euclid_trace(7, 0), euclid_trace(0, 7), euclid_trace(0, 0))
=> ([], [(0, 7, 0, 0)], [])
--- check test | Neighbouring Fibonacci numbers: every quotient is 1 except the last, and F(k + 2), F(k + 1) take k steps
(lambda f: all((lambda t: len(t) == k and all(s[2] == 1 for s in t[:-1]))(euclid_trace(f[k + 2], f[k + 1])) for k in range(1, 60)))(__import__("functools").reduce(lambda l, _: l + [l[-1] + l[-2]], range(70), [0, 1]))
--- check test | Every step is a correct division, for seeded random pairs, and the last nonzero remainder is the gcd
(lambda rng: all((lambda a, b, t: all(s[0] == s[2] * s[1] + s[3] and 0 <= s[3] < s[1] for s in t) and t[-1][1] == __import__("math").gcd(a, b) and t[-1][3] == 0)(a, b, euclid_trace(a, b)) for a, b in [(rng.randint(0, 10**9), rng.randint(1, 10**9)) for _ in range(300)]))(__import__("random").Random(11))

+++ practice | The smallest nonnegative solution
--- task
A probe's position can be nudged by thruster pulses: one kind moves it `a` millimetres forward, the other `b` millimetres. A negative number of pulses means firing that thruster in reverse. To move exactly `c` millimetres you need integers x and y with a·x + b·y = c.

Write `smallest_x(a, b, c)` for positive integers `a` and `b` and any integer `c`. Return the solution `(x, y)` with the **smallest x that is 0 or more**, or `None` if there is no integer solution at all.

Use the lesson's theorem: with g = gcd(a, b) and one solution (x₀, y₀), every solution is x = x₀ + (b/g)·t, y = y₀ − (a/g)·t. So the smallest x ≥ 0 is x₀ mod (b/g), with Python's `%`, which is never negative for a positive divisor. You will need your own extended Euclid (you may copy the idea from the lesson's table).

For example, `smallest_x(6, 15, 9)` is `(4, -1)`: 6 · 4 − 15 = 9, and x must go up or down in steps of 15/3 = 5.
--- starter
def smallest_x(a, b, c):
    return None
--- solution
def ext_gcd(a, b):
    old_r, r = a, b
    old_s, s = 1, 0
    old_t, t = 0, 1
    while r != 0:
        q = old_r // r
        old_r, r = r, old_r - q * r
        old_s, s = s, old_s - q * s
        old_t, t = t, old_t - q * t
    return old_r, old_s, old_t


def smallest_x(a, b, c):
    g, x0, y0 = ext_gcd(a, b)
    if c % g != 0:
        return None
    x0 *= c // g
    step = b // g
    x = x0 % step
    y = (c - a * x) // b
    return (x, y)
--- hint
First find g, x₀ and y₀ with the extended Euclidean algorithm. If g does not divide c there is no solution. Otherwise multiply x₀ by c // g to get a solution of a·x + b·y = c.
--- hint
x can move only in steps of b // g, so the smallest x ≥ 0 is `x0 % (b // g)`. Then work out y from the equation: y = (c − a·x) // b, which divides exactly.
--- check case | The example from the task
smallest_x(6, 15, 9)
=> (4, -1)
--- check case | No solution
(smallest_x(6, 15, 10), smallest_x(4, 10, 3))
=> (None, None)
--- check case | Zero and negative targets
(smallest_x(6, 15, 0), smallest_x(3, 5, -1), smallest_x(7, 1, 20))
=> ((0, 0), (3, -2), (0, 20))
--- check test | Huge numbers, where trying x = 0, 1, 2, … would never finish
(lambda a, b, c: (lambda s: s is not None and a * s[0] + b * s[1] == c and 0 <= s[0] < b)(smallest_x(a, b, c)))(10**30 + 7, 10**29 + 3, 10**40 + 1)
--- check test | Agrees with a search over x for small inputs
all(smallest_x(a, b, c) == next(((x, (c - a * x) // b) for x in range(0, b + 1) if (c - a * x) % b == 0), None) for a in range(1, 9) for b in range(1, 9) for c in range(-20, 21))

+++ practice | Counting the ways to hit a target
--- task
Supply drops come in crates of `a` kilograms and crates of `b` kilograms, and you cannot send a negative number of crates. Write `count_ways(a, b, c)` that returns how many pairs of whole numbers `(x, y)` with `x >= 0` and `y >= 0` satisfy a·x + b·y = c, for positive integers `a` and `b` and any integer `c`.

Do not search over all x: `c` can have 30 digits. Instead, find the smallest x ≥ 0 that gives an integer y (as in the thruster problem). If its y is negative there are no solutions. Otherwise every further solution adds b/g to x and takes a/g off y, so the count is y // (a/g) + 1.

Edge cases the checks test: `c = 0` has exactly one way (no crates), a negative `c` has none, and a `c` that g does not divide has none. You may use `math.gcd`, and Python's `pow(x, -1, m)` if you know it, but your own extended Euclid is fine too.

For example, `count_ways(3, 5, 30)` is `3`: (10, 0), (5, 3) and (0, 6).
--- starter
def count_ways(a, b, c):
    return c // (a * b) + 1
--- solution
def ext_gcd(a, b):
    old_r, r = a, b
    old_s, s = 1, 0
    while r != 0:
        q = old_r // r
        old_r, r = r, old_r - q * r
        old_s, s = s, old_s - q * s
    return old_r, old_s


def count_ways(a, b, c):
    if c < 0:
        return 0
    g, x0 = ext_gcd(a, b)
    if c % g != 0:
        return 0
    x = (x0 * (c // g)) % (b // g)
    y = (c - a * x) // b
    if y < 0:
        return 0
    return y // (a // g) + 1
--- hint
Find the smallest x ≥ 0 with a·x ≡ c in steps of b: that is the extended Euclid x₀, times c // g, taken `% (b // g)`. Then y = (c − a·x) // b.
--- hint
If that y is negative, even the smallest x overshoots, so return 0. Otherwise you can keep taking a // g off y until it would go negative: that is y // (a // g) more solutions, plus the one you have.
--- check case | The example from the task
count_ways(3, 5, 30)
=> 3
--- check case | Nothing to send, and a negative target
(count_ways(3, 5, 0), count_ways(3, 5, -15))
=> (1, 0)
?? c = 0 has exactly one solution, x = y = 0. A negative c has none.
--- check case | Targets that cannot be reached
(count_ways(3, 5, 7), count_ways(6, 10, 15), count_ways(4, 6, 2))
=> (0, 0, 0)
--- check case | Equal crate sizes and crates of 1
(count_ways(4, 4, 12), count_ways(1, 1, 5), count_ways(1, 3, 9))
=> (4, 6, 4)
--- check test | Agrees with a search for small inputs
all(count_ways(a, b, c) == sum(1 for x in range(0, c // a + 1) if (c - a * x) % b == 0) for a in range(1, 8) for b in range(1, 8) for c in range(0, 60))
--- check case | A 30-digit target
count_ways(7, 11, 10**30)
=> 12987012987012987012987012987

+++ practice | Debug: the extended Euclid that lies
--- task
`ext_gcd(a, b)` should return `(g, x, y)` with `g = gcd(a, b)` and `a * x + b * y == g`, for whole numbers `a, b ≥ 0`. The g it returns is always right, and so is y, but the x is wrong: `ext_gcd(252, 198)` returns an x for which 252·x + 198·y is not 18.

Find the bug and fix it. It is in how one pair of values is updated.
--- starter
def ext_gcd(a, b):
    old_r, r = a, b
    old_s, s = 1, 0
    old_t, t = 0, 1
    while r != 0:
        q = old_r // r
        old_r, r = r, old_r - q * r
        old_s = s
        s = old_s - q * s
        old_t, t = t, old_t - q * t
    return (old_r, old_s, old_t)
--- solution
def ext_gcd(a, b):
    old_r, r = a, b
    old_s, s = 1, 0
    old_t, t = 0, 1
    while r != 0:
        q = old_r // r
        old_r, r = r, old_r - q * r
        old_s, s = s, old_s - q * s
        old_t, t = t, old_t - q * t
    return (old_r, old_s, old_t)
--- hint
Compare the lines for s with the lines for r and t. After `old_s = s`, the next line uses the **new** `old_s`, so it computes s − q·s instead of old_s − q·s.
--- hint
Update both s values at once with a tuple assignment, exactly like the r and t lines.
--- check test | The example from the task
(lambda g, x, y: g == 18 and 252 * x + 198 * y == 18)(*ext_gcd(252, 198))
--- check test | Coprime pairs
all((lambda g, x, y: g == 1 and a * x + b * y == 1)(*ext_gcd(a, b)) for a, b in [(17, 5), (5, 17), (1000003, 999983), (89, 55)])
--- check test | Zero on either side
all((lambda g, x, y: g == e and a * x + b * y == e)(*ext_gcd(a, b)) for a, b, e in [(9, 0, 9), (0, 9, 9), (0, 0, 0)])
--- check test | Seeded random pairs agree with math.gcd
(lambda rng: all((lambda a, b: (lambda g, x, y: g == __import__("math").gcd(a, b) and a * x + b * y == g)(*ext_gcd(a, b)))(rng.randint(0, 10**12), rng.randint(0, 10**12)) for _ in range(500)))(__import__("random").Random(5))

+++ practice | One recipe for many step sizes
--- task
A docking arm can move by several fixed step sizes, forward or back. With steps `nums = [n₁, n₂, …, n_k]`, a move of c₁·n₁ + c₂·n₂ + … + c_k·n_k is possible for any integers c₁, …, c_k. The smallest positive move possible is the gcd of all the step sizes (Bézout's identity, extended to many numbers).

Write `bezout_many(nums)` that returns a tuple `(g, coeffs)` where `g` is the gcd of all the numbers (never negative) and `coeffs` is a list of integers, one per number, with the sum of `coeffs[i] * nums[i]` equal to `g`.

Fold the list with the extended Euclidean algorithm: keep the gcd g of the numbers seen so far and coefficients for it. For the next number n, find u and v with g·u + n·v = gcd(g, n). Then the new coefficients are the old ones each multiplied by u, followed by v. Start with g = 0 and no coefficients: gcd(0, n) = |n|.

For an empty list return `(0, [])`. Numbers may be negative or zero.

For example, `bezout_many([6, 10, 15])` returns g = 1 with coefficients such as `[-4, 1, 1]`: −24 + 10 + 15 = 1.
--- starter
from math import gcd


def bezout_many(nums):
    g = 0
    for n in nums:
        g = gcd(g, n)
    return (g, [1] * len(nums))
--- solution
def ext_gcd(a, b):
    old_r, r = a, b
    old_s, s = 1, 0
    old_t, t = 0, 1
    while r != 0:
        q = old_r // r
        old_r, r = r, old_r - q * r
        old_s, s = s, old_s - q * s
        old_t, t = t, old_t - q * t
    if old_r < 0:
        old_r, old_s, old_t = -old_r, -old_s, -old_t
    return (old_r, old_s, old_t)


def bezout_many(nums):
    g = 0
    coeffs = []
    for n in nums:
        g, u, v = ext_gcd(g, n)
        coeffs = [c * u for c in coeffs] + [v]
    return (g, coeffs)
--- hint
If the coefficients so far give g, then multiplying them all by u gives g·u. Adding v times the new number gives g·u + n·v, which is the new gcd.
--- hint
Your `ext_gcd` must return a gcd that is never negative, even for negative inputs: flip the signs of all three results if it comes out negative. With g = 0 at the start, `ext_gcd(0, n)` gives |n|.
--- check test | The example from the task
(lambda g, cs: g == 1 and len(cs) == 3 and sum(c * n for c, n in zip(cs, [6, 10, 15])) == 1)(*bezout_many([6, 10, 15]))
--- check case | An empty list
bezout_many([])
=> (0, [])
--- check test | One number, including a negative one and zero
all((lambda g, cs: g == abs(n) and len(cs) == 1 and cs[0] * n == g)(*bezout_many([n])) for n in [12, -12, 0, 1])
--- check test | Zeros and negatives mixed in
all((lambda g, cs: g == e and len(cs) == len(ns) and sum(c * n for c, n in zip(cs, ns)) == e)(*bezout_many(ns)) for ns, e in [([0, 0], 0), ([0, -9, 6], 3), ([-4, -6, -10], 2), ([35, 0, 21, 15], 1)])
?? The gcd must never be negative, even when every number is.
--- check test | Seeded random lists: the gcd is right and the recipe works
(lambda rng: all((lambda ns: (lambda g, cs: g == __import__("math").gcd(*ns) and sum(c * n for c, n in zip(cs, ns)) == g)(*bezout_many(ns)))([rng.randint(-10**6, 10**6) * rng.choice([1, 6, 30]) for _ in range(rng.randint(1, 6))]) for _ in range(300)))(__import__("random").Random(29))

+++ question | Why the remainder keeps the gcd
--- ask
Why does gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, a mod b)?
--- choice
Because a mod b is always smaller than b.
--- choice correct
Because a mod b = a − q·b, so a number divides both a and b exactly when it divides both b and a mod b: the two pairs have the same common divisors.
--- choice
Because a and a mod b always have the same divisors.
--- choice
Because the gcd of any two numbers is the smaller one's remainder.
--- why
A common divisor of a and b divides the combination a − q·b, and a common divisor of b and r divides q·b + r = a. So the sets of common divisors are equal, and so are their largest members. "a mod b is smaller" is why the algorithm stops, not why it is correct.

+++ question | A negative remainder
--- ask
What does Python print for `print(-7 % 3)`? Type the number.
--- answer
2
--- why
Python's remainder is never negative when the divisor is positive: −7 = −3 · 3 + 2. C++ and Java would print −1, because they round the quotient −2.33 towards zero instead of down.

+++ question | How many steps
--- ask
Roughly how many division steps can Euclid's algorithm need, at most, on two numbers of 100 decimal digits?
--- choice
About 10¹⁰⁰, one per number up to the smaller one.
--- choice
About 10⁵⁰, the square root of the numbers.
--- choice correct
A few hundred: the count grows with the number of digits.
--- choice
Always fewer than 10.
--- why
The first number at least halves every two steps, so the count is O(log n). Lamé proved it is at most five times the number of digits of the smaller number: at most 500 here, and about 480 for the worst case, neighbouring Fibonacci numbers.

=== disc-19 | Modular arithmetic
--- teach
Last lesson you divided with remainder, computed gcds with Euclid's algorithm and found Bézout's x and y. This lesson does arithmetic with the remainders themselves, the way a clock does: 9 o'clock plus 5 hours is 2 o'clock. It is the arithmetic of hash functions, checksums, random number generators, and every public-key cipher. You will learn when you may divide on a clock, how to compute a number to a 600-digit power in a few thousand steps, and Fermat's little theorem, the fact that makes RSA work.

### Congruence

Fix a whole number n ≥ 1, the **modulus**. Integers a and b are **congruent modulo n**, written

a ≡ b (mod n),

when n divides a − b. In everyday words: a and b land on the same mark of a clock with n marks, numbered 0 to n − 1. So 17 ≡ 5 (mod 12), because 17 − 5 = 12, and −1 ≡ 11 (mod 12), because −1 − 11 = −12. Carl Friedrich Gauss [[introduced the notation|gauss]].

**Congruent means same remainder.** a ≡ b (mod n) exactly when a mod n = b mod n. **Proof.** Write a = qn + r and b = q'n + r' with remainders r and r' between 0 and n − 1. Then a − b = (q − q')n + (r − r'). So n divides a − b exactly when n divides r − r'. But r − r' lies strictly between −n and n, where the only multiple of n is 0. So n | (a − b) exactly when r = r'. ∎

So in Python, `a % n == b % n` tests congruence, and `a % n` picks the **standard representative**, the one in 0 … n − 1.

Congruence modulo n is an **equivalence relation**, as in the relations lesson. It is reflexive (n divides a − a = 0), symmetric (if n divides a − b, it divides b − a), and transitive (if n divides a − b and b − c, it divides their sum a − c). Its equivalence classes, the **residue classes**, are the n sets "leaves remainder 0", "leaves remainder 1", …, "leaves remainder n − 1". The set of standard representatives {0, 1, …, n − 1} is called **ℤₙ**, read "the integers mod n".

### Arithmetic respects congruence

**Theorem.** If a ≡ a' and b ≡ b' (mod n), then

a + b ≡ a' + b',   a − b ≡ a' − b',   and   a · b ≡ a' · b'   (mod n).

**Proof for products.** a·b − a'·b' = a·(b − b') + b'·(a − a'). Both b − b' and a − a' are multiples of n, so the right side is a multiple of n. Sums and differences are easier: (a + b) − (a' + b') = (a − a') + (b − b'). ∎

This means you may replace any number by its remainder **at any point** in a calculation made of +, − and ×, and the final remainder does not change. That keeps the numbers small:

```python
a, b, n = 987654321, 123456789, 1000
print((a * b) % n)                  # 269
print(((a % n) * (b % n)) % n)      # 269: 321 · 789 = 253269
```

Powers follow, since a power is repeated multiplication. What is the last digit of 7¹⁰⁰? Last digit means mod 10. 7⁴ = 2401 ≡ 1 (mod 10), so 7¹⁰⁰ = (7⁴)²⁵ ≡ 1²⁵ = 1: the last digit is 1. The same idea gives the old trick of [[casting out nines|nines]]: 10 ≡ 1 (mod 9), so every number is congruent to the sum of its digits, mod 9.

### Division is different

Try to cancel a factor: 2 · 3 = 6 and 2 · 8 = 16 are congruent mod 10, but 3 and 8 are not. Cancelling a common factor can fail.

**Cancellation law.** If c·a ≡ c·b (mod n) and gcd(c, n) = 1, then a ≡ b (mod n). **Proof.** n divides c·a − c·b = c·(a − b). Since n and c share no factor, the general form of Euclid's lemma from the last lesson says n divides a − b. ∎ In the example, gcd(2, 10) = 2, not 1, so the law does not apply.

### Inverses

An **inverse of a modulo n** is an integer x with a·x ≡ 1 (mod n). It plays the part of 1/a: multiplying by x undoes multiplying by a. For example, 3 · 5 = 15 ≡ 1 (mod 7), so 5 is an inverse of 3 modulo 7, and "dividing by 3" mod 7 means multiplying by 5.

**Theorem.** a has an inverse modulo n exactly when gcd(a, n) = 1. The inverse is then unique modulo n.

**Proof.** If gcd(a, n) = 1, Bézout gives a·x + n·y = 1, so a·x = 1 − n·y ≡ 1 (mod n). Conversely, if a·x ≡ 1, then a·x = 1 + k·n for some k, so every common divisor of a and n divides a·x − k·n = 1, and the gcd is 1. For uniqueness, if a·x ≡ 1 and a·x' ≡ 1, then x ≡ x·(a·x') = (x·a)·x' ≡ x'. ∎

The proof is also the algorithm: run the extended Euclidean algorithm on a and n, and take x mod n. That costs O(log n) steps. Python can do it too: `pow(a, -1, n)` returns the inverse in 0 … n − 1, and raises `ValueError` when there is none.

When the modulus is a prime p, every a that p does not divide has an inverse, since gcd(a, p) = 1. So in ℤₚ you can divide by anything except 0, just as with fractions. (A number system like that is called a **field**.)

### Fast exponentiation

Cryptography needs aᵉ mod n where e has hundreds of digits. Multiplying by a, e times, is out of the question: for e ≈ 2²⁰⁴⁸ it would take longer than the age of the universe many times over. But you do not need e multiplications.

Squaring doubles an exponent: from a you get a², then a⁴, a⁸, a¹⁶, …, one multiplication each. And every exponent is a sum of powers of two, its binary digits. For example 13 = 8 + 4 + 1 (binary 1101), so a¹³ = a⁸ · a⁴ · a¹. So: keep squaring, and multiply into the result the powers whose binary digit is 1. This is **square-and-multiply**, or **fast exponentiation**. Read the exponent's binary digits from the right: when the exponent is odd, its last digit is 1; halving it with `//` drops that digit.

The loop keeps three numbers: a result, a base and an exponent, starting at 1, a mod n and e. Each pass:

1. if the exponent is odd, multiply the result by the base (mod n);
2. square the base (mod n);
3. halve the exponent, rounding down.

When the exponent reaches 0, the result is the answer. Here it is for 5¹³ mod 11:

| pass | exponent (binary) | odd? | result | base after squaring |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| start | 13 (1101) | | 1 | 5 |
| 1 | 13 → 6 | yes | 1 · 5 = 5 | 25 mod 11 = 3 |
| 2 | 6 → 3 | no | 5 | 9 |
| 3 | 3 → 1 | yes | 5 · 9 = 45 mod 11 = 1 | 81 mod 11 = 4 |
| 4 | 1 → 0 | yes | 1 · 4 = 4 | 16 mod 11 = 5 |

So 5¹³ ≡ 4 (mod 11). (Check: 5⁵ = 3125 = 284 · 11 + 1, so 5¹⁰ ≡ 1 and 5¹³ ≡ 5³ = 125 ≡ 4.)

**Why it is correct.** Let E be the original exponent. The **loop invariant** is: result · base^exp ≡ a^E (mod n), where exp is the current exponent.

- At the start it reads 1 · a^E, which is true.
- If exp is even, the result stays and base^exp = (base²)^(exp/2), so replacing base by base² and exp by exp/2 keeps the product the same.
- If exp is odd, base^exp = base · (base²)^((exp − 1)/2). Moving one factor of base into the result, then squaring the base and halving exp (rounding down gives (exp − 1)/2), again keeps the product.
- Reducing mod n along the way changes nothing, by the theorem above.

When exp = 0, base⁰ = 1, so the result is a^E mod n. **It stops** because exp halves each pass.

**What it costs.** One pass per binary digit of e, so about log₂ e passes, with at most two multiplications of numbers below n each. A 2048-bit exponent takes at most 4,096 multiplications instead of 2²⁰⁴⁸. Reducing mod n every time matters just as much: without it, a^E has about E · log₂ a binary digits, far more than any memory can hold. Python's built-in three-argument `pow(a, e, n)` computes exactly this, with some extra tricks for speed.

### Fermat's little theorem

**Theorem (Fermat).** If p is prime and p does not divide a, then

a^(p−1) ≡ 1 (mod p).

For example, 3⁶ = 729 = 104 · 7 + 1, so 3⁶ ≡ 1 (mod 7).

**Proof.** Look at the p − 1 numbers a·1, a·2, …, a·(p − 1), each taken mod p.

1. **None is 0.** p does not divide a, and it does not divide any k from 1 to p − 1, so by Euclid's lemma it does not divide a·k.
2. **No two are equal.** If a·j ≡ a·k (mod p), then j ≡ k by the cancellation law, since gcd(a, p) = 1. For j and k between 1 and p − 1 that means j = k.

So these are p − 1 different values, all in {1, 2, …, p − 1}, which has only p − 1 members. They must be exactly 1, 2, …, p − 1, in some order: multiplying by a just shuffles the nonzero remainders. Multiply all of them together:

a^(p−1) · (1 · 2 · … · (p − 1)) ≡ 1 · 2 · … · (p − 1)   (mod p).

The product (p − 1)! is coprime to p, because p divides none of its factors, so cancel it. What remains is a^(p−1) ≡ 1. ∎

Multiplying both sides by a gives a form that holds for **every** integer a, including multiples of p: **aᵖ ≡ a (mod p)**.

Three uses follow at once, each with its hypothesis "p is prime and p does not divide a":

- **Inverses:** a · a^(p−2) = a^(p−1) ≡ 1, so a^(p−2) mod p is the inverse of a.
- **Smaller exponents:** a^e ≡ a^(e mod (p−1)) (mod p), because every block of p − 1 factors of a is ≡ 1. The exponent is reduced mod **p − 1**, not mod p.
- **Proving a number composite:** if a^(n−1) mod n is not 1 for some a between 2 and n − 1, then n is **not** prime, even though you have found no factor of it. This is the **Fermat test**. The converse fails: in the proof-by-cases lesson you met composite "liars" that pass for some a, and [[Carmichael numbers|carmichael]] such as 561 = 3 · 11 · 17 pass for every a coprime to them. The next lesson says how real systems test primes anyway.

The next lesson generalises Fermat's theorem from a prime p to any modulus, which is exactly what RSA needs.

### Where it is used

- **Hashing.** A hash table puts key k in bucket k mod m, and string hashes such as the rolling hash of Rabin-Karp are polynomials evaluated mod a prime.
- **Check digits.** ISBNs, bank account numbers (IBAN, mod 97) and card numbers carry a digit chosen so that a weighted sum is ≡ 0 modulo some number. A mistyped digit breaks the congruence.
- **Machine arithmetic.** A 64-bit unsigned integer [[wraps around|wrap]]: its arithmetic is arithmetic mod 2⁶⁴. Ring buffers index with `i % capacity`.
- **Pseudo-random numbers.** Many generators step x → (a·x + c) mod m.
- **Cryptography.** RSA, Diffie-Hellman key exchange and digital signatures all run fast exponentiation mod huge numbers, and elliptic-curve cryptography does its arithmetic in ℤₚ.

**Watch out:** four classic mistakes.

- Dividing as if mod n were ordinary arithmetic. `(a // b) % n` is not "a divided by b mod n". Multiply by the inverse of b instead, and only when gcd(b, n) = 1.
- Forgetting to reduce inside the loop. `a ** e % n` is correct but builds the whole of aᵉ first; for big e it runs out of time and memory.
- Reducing the exponent mod p instead of mod p − 1, or forgetting that Fermat needs p not dividing a. If p | a, then aᵉ ≡ 0 for every e ≥ 1.
- Negative numbers in C, C++ or Java, where `-3 % 7` is −3, not 4. Python's `%` already gives a result in 0 … n − 1.

::: context gauss Gauss and the ≡ sign
Carl Friedrich Gauss introduced the ≡ notation in "Disquisitiones Arithmeticae", published in 1801 when he was 24. He chose a sign close to = on purpose: congruences can be added, subtracted and multiplied like equations, and he wanted the notation to invite that. The book turned a scattered collection of results about whole numbers into a single subject, number theory. It also proved that every positive integer is a sum of at most three triangular numbers, which Gauss had noted in his diary as "EYPHKA! num = Δ + Δ + Δ".
:::

::: context nines Casting out nines
Since 10 ≡ 1 (mod 9), every power of 10 is ≡ 1, so a number like 4,725 = 4·1000 + 7·100 + 2·10 + 5 is ≡ 4 + 7 + 2 + 5 = 18 ≡ 0 (mod 9). Before calculators, clerks checked sums and products this way: reduce each number to its digit sum mod 9, do the same arithmetic on the small numbers, and compare. A mismatch proves an error. A match does not prove the answer right: swapping two digits keeps the digit sum, so that mistake slips through. That is why ISBN-10 uses weighted digits mod 11 instead.
:::

::: context carmichael Numbers that fool Fermat
A Carmichael number is a composite n with a^(n−1) ≡ 1 (mod n) for every a coprime to n. Robert Carmichael found the smallest, 561, in 1910; the next are 1105, 1729, 2465 and 2821. In 1994 Alford, Granville and Pomerance proved there are infinitely many. So the Fermat test alone can never prove a number prime. The Miller-Rabin test adds one more check, on the square roots of 1 met along the way, and no composite number fools it for more than a quarter of the bases.
:::

::: context wrap Arithmetic on a 64-bit clock
A processor's 64-bit register holds 0 to 2⁶⁴ − 1. When an unsigned addition or multiplication goes past the top, the extra high bits are thrown away, which is the same as reducing mod 2⁶⁴: the numbers wrap around a clock with 2⁶⁴ marks. Because arithmetic respects congruence, a long unsigned calculation that overflows many times still gives the right answer mod 2⁶⁴. Hash functions rely on this. In C and C++ that holds only for unsigned types; overflowing a signed integer is undefined behaviour.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 220 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <circle cx="110" cy="100" r="70" fill="none" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="110" y="22" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">0</text>
  <text x="166" y="50" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">1</text>
  <text x="186" y="112" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2</text>
  <text x="150" y="170" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">3</text>
  <text x="70" y="170" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">4</text>
  <text x="34" y="112" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">5</text>
  <text x="54" y="50" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">6</text>
  <path d="M150 60 A55 55 0 0 1 150 140" fill="none" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="150,140 143,131 156,132" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="110" y="104" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">mod 7: 1 + 2 = 3</text>
  <text x="110" y="194" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">and 6 + 1 wraps to 0</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
Write two functions, without Python's `pow` and without `**`: the point is to build them yourself.

- `mod_pow(base, exp, n)`: return base^exp mod n (base to the power exp), a number from 0 to n − 1, by square-and-multiply. `base` is any integer (it may be negative or bigger than n), `exp ≥ 0` and `n ≥ 1`. It must be fast for huge exponents: a check uses an exponent of 10¹⁸, and another a 1,000-digit modulus.
- `mod_inverse(a, n)`: return the inverse of `a` modulo `n` as a number from 0 to n − 1, or `None` if `a` has no inverse (when gcd(a, n) is not 1). Use the extended Euclidean algorithm from the last lesson. For `n = 1` every number is ≡ 0, and the answer is 0.

For example, `mod_pow(5, 13, 11)` is `4`, `mod_inverse(3, 7)` is `5`, and `mod_inverse(4, 10)` is `None`.
--- starter
def mod_pow(base, exp, n):
    return 1


def mod_inverse(a, n):
    return None
--- solution
def mod_pow(base, exp, n):
    result = 1 % n
    base %= n
    while exp > 0:
        if exp % 2 == 1:
            result = result * base % n
        base = base * base % n
        exp //= 2
    return result


def mod_inverse(a, n):
    old_r, r = a % n, n
    old_s, s = 1, 0
    while r != 0:
        q = old_r // r
        old_r, r = r, old_r - q * r
        old_s, s = s, old_s - q * s
    if old_r != 1:
        return 0 if n == 1 else None
    return old_s % n
--- hint
In `mod_pow`, start with `result = 1 % n` (so that n = 1 gives 0) and `base %= n`. Loop while the exponent is above 0: when it is odd, multiply the result by the base; then square the base; then halve the exponent with `//`. Reduce mod n after every multiplication.
--- hint
In `mod_inverse`, run the extended Euclidean algorithm on `a % n` and `n`, keeping only the s coefficients. If the last nonzero remainder (the gcd) is 1, the inverse is that s, taken `% n` to land in 0 … n − 1.
--- hint
Watch the edges: a gcd other than 1 means no inverse, except when n = 1, where the answer is 0.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(mod_pow(5, 13, 11), mod_inverse(3, 7), mod_inverse(4, 10))
=> (4, 5, None)
--- check test | Agrees with the built-in pow for every small case
all(mod_pow(b, e, n) == pow(b, e, n) for b in range(-6, 13) for e in range(0, 20) for n in range(1, 14))
--- check case | Exponent 0, modulus 1, and a base that is a multiple of n
(mod_pow(7, 0, 5), mod_pow(7, 0, 1), mod_pow(7, 5, 1), mod_pow(14, 3, 7))
=> (1, 0, 0, 0)
?? Anything mod 1 is 0, even 7⁰ = 1. Start the result at 1 % n.
--- check case | Negative bases
(mod_pow(-2, 3, 7), mod_pow(-1, 1001, 10))
=> (6, 9)
--- check case | An exponent of 10¹⁸: square-and-multiply, not one multiplication per step
(mod_pow(3, 10**18, 1000000007), mod_pow(2, 10**18 + 1, 10**9))
=> (pow(3, 10**18, 1000000007), pow(2, 10**18 + 1, 10**9))
--- check test | A 1,000-digit modulus and a 1,000-digit exponent
(lambda rng: (lambda b, e, n: mod_pow(b, e, n) == pow(b, e, n))(rng.randrange(10**999), rng.randrange(10**999), rng.randrange(10**999, 10**1000)))(__import__("random").Random(19))
--- check test | Inverses modulo 101: every nonzero number has one
all(mod_inverse(a, 101) is not None and a * mod_inverse(a, 101) % 101 == 1 and 0 <= mod_inverse(a, 101) < 101 for a in range(1, 101))
--- check test | Inverses exist exactly when the gcd is 1, for every n up to 40
all((mod_inverse(a, n) is None) == (__import__("math").gcd(a, n) != 1) and (mod_inverse(a, n) is None or a * mod_inverse(a, n) % n == 1 % n) for n in range(2, 41) for a in range(0, 2 * n))
--- check case | Modulus 1, a zero, and negative numbers
(mod_inverse(5, 1), mod_inverse(0, 7), mod_inverse(-3, 7), mod_inverse(10, 7))
=> (0, None, 2, 5)
--- check test | A 300-digit modulus
(lambda rng: (lambda n, a: (lambda x: x is not None and a * x % n == 1)(mod_inverse(a, n)))(*(lambda n: (n, rng.randrange(1, n)))(2 ** 1279 - 1)))(__import__("random").Random(2))

\bpow\s*\(|\*\*

+++ practice | ISBN check digits
--- task
An ISBN-10 is 10 characters: nine digits d₁ … d₉ and a check character d₁₀. The check is chosen so that

1·d₁ + 2·d₂ + 3·d₃ + … + 10·d₁₀ ≡ 0 (mod 11).

Because 10 ≡ −1 (mod 11), that is the same as d₁₀ ≡ 1·d₁ + 2·d₂ + … + 9·d₉ (mod 11). The check can be 10, which is written `"X"`. (Because 11 is prime, this catches every single wrong digit and every swap of two neighbouring digits.)

Write two functions:

- `isbn_check(first9)`: given a string of 9 digits, return the check character as a one-character string: `"0"` to `"9"`, or `"X"` for 10.
- `isbn_valid(code)`: given a string, return `True` if it is exactly 10 characters, the first 9 are digits, the last is a digit or `"X"`, and the weighted sum is ≡ 0 (mod 11). Otherwise return `False`.

For example, `isbn_check("030640615")` is `"2"`, and `isbn_valid("0306406152")` is `True`.
--- starter
def isbn_check(first9):
    total = sum(int(d) for d in first9)
    return str(total % 10)


def isbn_valid(code):
    return code[-1] == isbn_check(code[:9])
--- solution
def isbn_check(first9):
    total = sum(i * int(d) for i, d in enumerate(first9, start=1))
    check = total % 11
    return "X" if check == 10 else str(check)


def isbn_valid(code):
    if len(code) != 10 or not code[:9].isdigit():
        return False
    last = code[9]
    if last == "X":
        value = 10
    elif last.isdigit():
        value = int(last)
    else:
        return False
    total = sum(i * int(d) for i, d in enumerate(code[:9], start=1)) + 10 * value
    return total % 11 == 0
--- hint
Weight digit i (counting from 1) by i: `enumerate(first9, start=1)` gives the pairs. The check is the weighted sum mod 11, with 10 shown as `"X"`.
--- hint
In `isbn_valid`, reject anything of the wrong length or with a non-digit in the first nine places before doing arithmetic. Turn a final `"X"` into 10, then test whether the full weighted sum (the last character weighted by 10) is divisible by 11.
--- check case | The example from the task
(isbn_check("030640615"), isbn_valid("0306406152"))
=> ("2", True)
--- check case | A check character of X, and of 0
(isbn_check("080442957"), isbn_valid("080442957X"), isbn_check("000000000"))
=> ("X", True, "0")
--- check case | One wrong digit, and two neighbours swapped
(isbn_valid("0306406153"), isbn_valid("0306406125"), isbn_valid("3006406152"))
=> (False, False, False)
--- check case | Malformed codes
(isbn_valid("030640615"), isbn_valid("03064061522"), isbn_valid("03064O6152"), isbn_valid("030640615x"), isbn_valid("X306406152"))
=> (False, False, False, False, False)
--- check test | Every seeded random 9 digits plus its check is valid, and changing any one digit breaks it
(lambda rng: all((lambda s: isbn_valid(s + isbn_check(s)) and all(not isbn_valid(s[:i] + d + s[i + 1:] + isbn_check(s)) for i in range(9) for d in "0123456789" if d != s[i]))("".join(rng.choice("0123456789") for _ in range(9))) for _ in range(100)))(__import__("random").Random(10))

+++ practice | Solving a congruence
--- task
Write `solve_congruence(a, b, n)` that returns the sorted list of **all** x with 0 ≤ x < n and a·x ≡ b (mod n), for integers `a` and `b` and `n ≥ 1`.

Do not try every x: `n` can be huge. Use this theorem, which follows from the lesson. Let g = gcd(a, n).

- If g does not divide b, there is no solution: return `[]`.
- Otherwise divide a, b and n by g. Now a/g is invertible modulo n/g, and x₀ = (b/g) · (inverse of a/g) mod n/g is a solution. The solutions below n are exactly x₀, x₀ + n/g, x₀ + 2n/g, …: g of them.

You may use `math.gcd` and `pow(a, -1, m)`, which gives the inverse of a modulo m (for m = 1 it gives 0).

For example, `solve_congruence(6, 4, 10)` is `[4, 9]`: 6 · 4 = 24 and 6 · 9 = 54 are both ≡ 4 (mod 10).
--- starter
def solve_congruence(a, b, n):
    return [pow(a, -1, n) * b % n]
--- solution
from math import gcd


def solve_congruence(a, b, n):
    g = gcd(a, n)
    if b % g != 0:
        return []
    m = n // g
    x0 = (b // g) * pow(a // g, -1, m) % m
    return [x0 + k * m for k in range(g)]
--- hint
`pow(a, -1, n)` fails when gcd(a, n) is not 1. Find g = gcd(a, n) first; if b % g is not 0, return `[]`.
--- hint
Otherwise work modulo m = n // g, where a // g is invertible. x₀ = (b // g) · inverse(a // g, m) % m, and the solutions are x₀ + k·m for k from 0 to g − 1, which are already sorted.
--- check case | The example from the task
solve_congruence(6, 4, 10)
=> [4, 9]
--- check case | No solution
(solve_congruence(6, 5, 10), solve_congruence(0, 3, 7))
=> ([], [])
--- check case | a is 0 or a multiple of n: everything or nothing
(solve_congruence(0, 0, 4), solve_congruence(12, 0, 6), solve_congruence(12, 1, 6))
=> ([0, 1, 2, 3], [0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5], [])
?? When a ≡ 0 (mod n), every x works if b ≡ 0 and none does otherwise. gcd(0, n) = n handles this.
--- check case | Negative numbers and modulus 1
(solve_congruence(-3, 2, 7), solve_congruence(5, -1, 7), solve_congruence(4, 9, 1))
=> ([4], [4], [0])
--- check test | Agrees with trying every x for small inputs
all(solve_congruence(a, b, n) == [x for x in range(n) if (a * x - b) % n == 0] for a in range(-10, 25) for b in range(-5, 15) for n in range(1, 25))
--- check test | A 40-digit modulus with gcd 6
(lambda n, a, b: (lambda xs: len(xs) == 6 and all(0 <= x < n and (a * x - b) % n == 0 for x in xs) and xs == sorted(xs))(solve_congruence(a, b, n)))(6 * (10**39 + 3), 6 * 1234567891011, 6 * 42)

+++ practice | Numbers that fool Fermat
--- task
The Fermat test says: if a^(n−1) mod n is not 1 for some a, then n is composite. A composite n that still gives 1 for **every** a between 2 and n − 1 with gcd(a, n) = 1 is a **Carmichael number**.

Write two functions. Use Python's three-argument `pow(a, e, n)`, which computes aᵉ mod n by square-and-multiply, and `math.gcd`.

- `fermat_witness(n)`: for an odd integer `n ≥ 3`, return the smallest a from 2 to n − 1 with gcd(a, n) = 1 and a^(n−1) mod n not equal to 1, or `None` if there is none.
- `carmichael_up_to(limit)`: the sorted list of Carmichael numbers below `limit`. They are odd composites whose `fermat_witness` is `None`. Test compositeness by trial division; primes never count.

For example, `fermat_witness(15)` is `2` (2¹⁴ mod 15 = 4), `fermat_witness(13)` is `None`, and `carmichael_up_to(1000)` is `[561]`.
--- starter
from math import gcd


def fermat_witness(n):
    if pow(2, n - 1, n) != 1:
        return 2
    return None


def carmichael_up_to(limit):
    return [n for n in range(3, limit, 2) if fermat_witness(n) is None]
--- solution
from math import gcd, isqrt


def fermat_witness(n):
    for a in range(2, n):
        if gcd(a, n) == 1 and pow(a, n - 1, n) != 1:
            return a
    return None


def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    for d in range(2, isqrt(n) + 1):
        if n % d == 0:
            return False
    return True


def carmichael_up_to(limit):
    return [n for n in range(3, limit, 2) if not is_prime(n) and fermat_witness(n) is None]
--- hint
`fermat_witness` must try every a from 2 up, skipping those that share a factor with n, and return the first a with `pow(a, n - 1, n) != 1`. Trying only a = 2 misses composites that fool base 2, like 341.
--- hint
A prime never has a witness, by Fermat's theorem, so `carmichael_up_to` must leave primes out explicitly. Most composites are caught at a = 2, so the loop is fast.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(fermat_witness(15), fermat_witness(13), carmichael_up_to(1000))
=> (2, None, [561])
--- check case | 341 = 11 · 31 fools base 2 but not base 3
(pow(2, 340, 341), fermat_witness(341))
=> (1, 3)
--- check case | Primes never have a witness
(fermat_witness(3), fermat_witness(97), fermat_witness(7919))
=> (None, None, None)
--- check case | The Carmichael numbers below 3,000
carmichael_up_to(3000)
=> [561, 1105, 1729, 2465, 2821]
--- check case | Nothing below 561
(carmichael_up_to(561), carmichael_up_to(3))
=> ([], [])

+++ practice | A power of a power
--- task
Write `tower_mod(a, b, c, p)` that returns a^(b^c) mod p: a to the power (b to the power c), reduced mod p. Here `p` is a prime, `a ≥ 0`, `b ≥ 1` and `c ≥ 0`, so the exponent b^c is at least 1. c can be as large as 10¹⁸, so b^c itself can never be computed.

Use Fermat's little theorem. If p does not divide a, then a^e ≡ a^(e mod (p − 1)) (mod p), and e mod (p − 1) = b^c mod (p − 1) can be found with `pow(b, c, p - 1)`. But Fermat's theorem needs p not dividing a: if p divides a, the answer is 0, since the exponent is at least 1. You may use Python's three-argument `pow`.

For example, `tower_mod(2, 3, 2, 7)` is `1`: the exponent is 3² = 9, and 2⁹ = 512 = 73 · 7 + 1.
--- starter
def tower_mod(a, b, c, p):
    return pow(a, pow(b, c, p), p)
--- solution
def tower_mod(a, b, c, p):
    if a % p == 0:
        return 0
    e = pow(b, c, p - 1)
    return pow(a, e, p)
--- hint
The starter reduces the exponent mod p. Fermat's theorem says a^(p−1) ≡ 1, so exponents repeat every p − 1, not every p.
--- hint
Handle a ≡ 0 (mod p) first: the answer is 0. Otherwise `pow(a, pow(b, c, p - 1), p)`. When the reduced exponent comes out 0, a⁰ = 1 is right, because a^(p−1) ≡ 1 too.
--- check case | The example from the task
tower_mod(2, 3, 2, 7)
=> 1
--- check test | Agrees with computing b^c directly for small cases
all(tower_mod(a, b, c, p) == pow(a, b ** c, p) for p in [2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13] for a in range(0, 30) for b in range(1, 6) for c in range(0, 5))
--- check case | a divisible by p
(tower_mod(7, 5, 3, 7), tower_mod(0, 2, 10, 5), tower_mod(14, 1, 0, 7))
=> (0, 0, 0)
?? Fermat's theorem needs p not dividing a. If p divides a, every positive power of a is 0 mod p.
--- check case | The modulus 2
(tower_mod(3, 5, 7, 2), tower_mod(4, 5, 7, 2))
=> (1, 0)
--- check case | An exponent of 10¹⁸, modulo a large prime
tower_mod(3, 2, 10**18, 1000000007)
=> pow(3, pow(2, 10**18, 1000000006), 1000000007)

+++ practice | Debug: square-and-multiply that stops early
--- task
`mod_pow(base, e, n)` should return baseᵉ mod n by square-and-multiply, for `e ≥ 0` and `n ≥ 1`. It is fast, but wrong: `mod_pow(3, 5, 7)` returns `3` instead of `5`, and `mod_pow(2, 1, 5)` returns `1` instead of `2`.

Find the bug and fix it. It is one character in one line.
--- starter
def mod_pow(base, e, n):
    result = 1
    base %= n
    while e > 1:
        if e % 2 == 1:
            result = result * base % n
        base = base * base % n
        e //= 2
    return result % n
--- solution
def mod_pow(base, e, n):
    result = 1
    base %= n
    while e > 0:
        if e % 2 == 1:
            result = result * base % n
        base = base * base % n
        e //= 2
    return result % n
--- hint
Follow the loop for e = 1. The exponent's last binary digit is 1, so the base should be multiplied into the result. Does the loop run at all?
--- hint
The highest binary digit of e is always 1, and it is handled on the pass where e is 1. The loop must keep going until e reaches 0.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(mod_pow(3, 5, 7), mod_pow(2, 1, 5))
=> (5, 2)
--- check case | Exponent 0 and modulus 1
(mod_pow(9, 0, 4), mod_pow(9, 3, 1))
=> (1, 0)
--- check test | Agrees with the built-in pow for small cases
